===== ICLE-US-IND-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: The Exxon Valdez Oil Spill

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a compelling topic, and your personal outrage at the Exxon Valdez disaster comes through clearly. This emotional investment is a strength—it shows you care about the subject. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection or narrative than an academic argument. To strengthen it, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, organize your evidence more systematically, and revise for clarity and precision. Below are specific areas to work on.

Thesis and Argument

Your opening claim—that "money is the root of all evil" and that you personally find this true—is very broad. What specific *argument* are you making about Exxon? Is it that:
Corporate negligence, driven by profit motives, caused preventable environmental disaster?
Corporations should be held legally/financially accountable for environmental damage?
Regulatory oversight of the oil industry was (and remains) inadequate?

Right now, the essay touches on all of these but doesn't commit to one. Choose a single, focused claim and make it your thesis in the first paragraph. For example: "The Exxon Valdez disaster demonstrates that voluntary corporate responsibility is insufficient to prevent environmental catastrophe, and that stronger regulatory accountability is necessary." This gives your essay a clear argumentative spine.

Structure and Organization

The essay moves somewhat unevenly between narrative (what you personally felt watching the news), historical description (what happened during the spill), and argument (what should happen now). Consider organizing with clear sections:

Introduction – thesis statement, brief context
Background – what happened (the facts of the spill, kept concise)
Analysis – *why* it happened (negligence, lack of double hulls, poor maintenance, delayed response) and *why this matters* (connect to your thesis about corporate accountability)
Argument – what should be done, and why this solution follows logically from your analysis
Conclusion – restate thesis, broader implications

Right now, personal reactions ("I loathed the corporation," "I grew angry") appear in the middle of factual reporting. Consider using personal reaction primarily in the introduction to establish your stake in the topic, then shift to a more analytical, evidence-based voice for the body.

Evidence and Support

This is the area needing the most attention:

The placeholder "" is still in your draft—you need to fill this in with what Exxon spokespersons actually said. This is important evidence for your argument about corporate negligence.
Several claims need citation or more specific evidence: the exact volume of oil spilled, the settlement details ("close to two years... Exxon finally made monetary amends"), and the claim about ship maintenance being revealed in "notes and court transcriptions." Where did you learn this? Cite your sources.
You state that double-hulling became "common practice" afterward—this is a good point connecting cause and effect, but it needs a source and more explanation of *why* this matters to your argument (ie, it proves the disaster was preventable).
Note a logical inconsistency: you say Exxon "made monetary amends" but later say you "would like to see Exxon set aside monies." If a settlement already occurred, clarify what it covered and why it was (in your view) insufficient. This will make your call to action more precise and credible.

Language and Style Issues

A few specific edits:
"I feel this statement is personally true" – revise to something more academic: "I argue that this statement holds true in the case of..."
"mind boggling" and "beyond belief" are informal; use more precise language, eg, "difficult to fully comprehend" or "unprecedented in scale."
"Prince William sound" should be capitalized: "Prince William Sound."
Watch verb tense consistency—you shift between past and present when discussing ongoing effects. This is fine if intentional (past event, present consequences), but make sure the shifts are clear and deliberate.
"the Captain in charge the vessel" – missing "of": "in charge of the vessel."
Avoid absolute claims like "biggest Public Relations blunder by any firm in the nation" unless you can support this with a source; consider softening to "one of the most significant."

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in the missing quotation/placeholder with real content.
Write a one-sentence thesis and place it at the end of your first paragraph.
Separate narrative/personal reaction from factual reporting and argument.
Add citations for factual claims (spill volume, settlement, double-hull regulation).
Proofread for informal phrasing and minor grammar issues (articles, capitalization).

You have strong personal motivation and a solid topic—now it's about channeling that energy into a disciplined, evidence-based argument. I'd be happy to look at your revision once you've addressed these points.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a relatable topic and support your argument with a personal example, which is a strong start. However, the essay currently reads more like a narrative (a story about one person) than an argumentative essay that defends a claim with varied evidence and reasoning. Below are suggestions to strengthen the argument, organization, and language.

Content and Argument Development

Thesis clarity: Your thesis — "crime does not pay" — is stated, but it needs to be introduced more directly and early. Consider starting with a clear claim: *"Although crime may offer quick riches, it ultimately destroys the lives of those who pursue it and the people who depend on them."* This gives your reader a roadmap for the essay.

Over-reliance on one example: The story about your acquaintance is compelling, but an argumentative essay is stronger when it draws on multiple types of evidence — for example, statistics on recidivism, other real or hypothetical cases, or even a brief counterargument (some people *do* get away with crime for a while — why does this matter for your thesis?). Addressing the counterargument and refuting it would make your essay more persuasive.

Analysis, not just narration: Much of the second paragraph tells *what happened* to the family, but does not explain *why* this proves your thesis. After the story, add a paragraph that steps back and explicitly connects the details (arrest, lost home, broken trust) to your central claim about the true "cost" of crime — financial, emotional, and social.

Conclusion needs development: Your final paragraph restates the thesis but doesn't expand it. Consider ending with a broader reflection: what lesson should readers take away? Is there advice for someone tempted by "quick riches"?

Organization

Right now, you have three paragraphs: introduction/generalization, story, and conclusion. Consider organizing this way instead:
  - Paragraph 1: Introduce the problem (people seeking quick wealth through crime) and state your thesis clearly.
  - Paragraph 2: Briefly explain *why* people are tempted by crime (impatience, environment, dissatisfaction with slow/legal paths).
  - Paragraph 3: Present your extended example as evidence, focusing on the consequences.
  - Paragraph 4: Analyze what the example shows about crime's true "cost."
  - Paragraph 5: Conclude by connecting back to the thesis and offering a final insight.

There is a strange placeholder  in your first paragraph — make sure to remove or replace this before submitting; it looks like an error left from drafting.

Language and Grammar

You have a good vocabulary range, but there are recurring errors that affect clarity. Focus on these patterns:

Spelling: "catigory" → *category*; "beleive" → *believe*; "expectable" → likely meant *acceptable* or *normal*.
Subject-verb agreement: "His family way very proud" → *was* very proud.
Word form confusion: "not being will to go about" → *willing*; "an easier way" (you wrote "and easier way" — likely a typo for "an").
Apostrophes for possession: "societies rules" → *society's rules*; "father's/husband's picture" is correct, but be consistent with this pattern elsewhere.
Run-on and comma-splice sentences: Several sentences are joined with commas where a period or conjunction is needed. For example: *"He decided to get rich quick and he did"* — good, but *"This went on for about a year. The one day..."* should be *"...for about a year. Then one day..."*

Suggestion: After revising content and organization, do a separate proofreading pass focused only on grammar and spelling. Reading your essay aloud can help you catch missing words or awkward phrasing.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis clearly and early.
Add analysis connecting your example to the thesis (don't just narrate).
Consider a brief counterargument for a more persuasive essay.
Reorganize into more focused paragraphs.
Proofread carefully for spelling, agreement, and punctuation errors.

You have real potential here — the story is vivid and the moral message is clear. With sharper organization and more analytical depth, this essay will feel much more persuasive and academically strong. Keep revising!

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This is a thoughtful, ambitious essay that tackles a genuinely complex question: how American society reconciles its stated values with its economic behavior. Your central insight—that the *value* of work and the *pay* for work are governed by different, sometimes contradictory, cultural logics—is sophisticated and worth developing. The child-care example is well-chosen and emotionally resonant. However, the essay would benefit from clearer structural signposting, tighter sentence construction, and a more decisive resolution of the "chicken-and-egg" problem you raise at the end.

Strengths

Conceptual sophistication: You distinguish between equality of *task* and equality of *societal value*, which is a nuanced move many writers miss.
Good use of a concrete case: Child care work grounds the abstract argument in something readers can visualize and feel invested in.
Awareness of complexity: You resist an easy policy fix (government or employer intervention) and acknowledge its limitations rather than pretending a simple solution exists.

Areas for Improvement

Missing/Unclear Thesis Statement

The opening line refers to "the idea that ," which suggests the actual claim you're responding to has been dropped or is a placeholder. Before revising further, make sure this sentence explicitly states the position you are examining (eg, "equal work deserves equal pay"). Readers need this anchor immediately, since your whole essay pivots on interrogating it.

The Circular Ending Needs to Be Addressed Directly

Your conclusion identifies a genuine paradox: society won't value child care work until it pays better, but it won't pay better until it's valued. This is actually one of the most interesting parts of your essay—but you present it as an "irony" and then stop, leaving the reader without guidance on how to think about it. Consider:
Naming this explicitly as a chicken-and-egg problem or self-reinforcing cycle.
Suggesting what *could* break the cycle (cultural shifts, unionization, subsidized wages, symbolic recognition) even if you don't endorse a single solution. You don't need to solve it, but gesture toward where change might begin.

Sentence-Level Clarity

Several sentences are overloaded with subordinate clauses, which makes your argument harder to follow than it needs to be. For example:

> "It is difficult to imagine, however, a society where workers are encouraged to take employment seriously - to say nothing of finding satisfaction from employment - and where the work force is filled with a diversity of willing workers, without considering the fundamental value of child care providers."

Try breaking this into two sentences:
> "It is difficult to imagine a healthy workforce—one where workers take their jobs seriously and find satisfaction in them—without also considering who cares for children while parents work. A diverse labor force depends on reliable child care."

Look for other long sentences (there are several per paragraph) and ask whether splitting them would help.

Grammar and Word Choice

A few specific corrections:
"profess to the belief that" → "profess to believe that" or "hold the belief that"
"pereniel" → "perennial"
"man with family responsibilities" → "men with family responsibilities"
"child care industry - which may not be the ideal solution" — the dash here creates ambiguity about what "may not be ideal": government involvement, or the resolution itself? Rephrase for clarity: "...without government or employer involvement in the child care industry, though such involvement may not be an ideal solution."

I'd recommend having a native-speaking peer or writing tutor do a final line edit, since these are minor but frequent enough to distract from your argument.

Paragraph Transitions

The move from "Americans value children" to "Americans don't value child care work" (paragraph 2) happens quickly. Consider adding a transitional sentence that makes the contrast explicit: "Yet this professed concern for children's welfare does not extend to those who perform the daily labor of caring for them." This will make your logical move more visible to readers.

Consider Adding a Counterargument

Your essay would be strengthened by briefly acknowledging an objection—for instance, someone might argue that market wages reflect supply and demand (many people are willing to do child care, so wages stay low) rather than a values judgment. Addressing this would let you clarify whether you're making a *moral* claim, an *economic* claim, or both.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Restore/clarify the thesis statement at the start.
Shorten and simplify long sentences.
Fix grammatical errors (listed above).
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs.
Develop the ending's paradox instead of just naming it as ironic.
Consider one counterargument to sharpen your claim.

You have real intellectual substance here—the revision work is mostly about clarity and follow-through, not rethinking your argument.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely important and thought-provoking question—the disparity between how society values market labor versus domestic labor. You have a clear thesis and some compelling rhetorical moments. Below is feedback organized by area to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Strengths

Your opening paragraph does good work establishing the problem and stating your thesis clearly. The rhetorical question "But what is 'equal work'?" is an effective pivot that drives the essay forward, and your comparison between a teacher and a neurosurgeon versus a homemaker is a vivid, memorable illustration of your central concern.

Areas for Revision

Thesis and Structure
Your thesis proposes that "some sort of compensation should be made for 'home-making service.'" However, your concluding paragraph offers a *different* solution—paying the paycheck to the couple/family rather than the individual. This is a shift from direct compensation for homemaking to redistribution of existing marketplace income. These are two distinct policy ideas, and the essay would be stronger if you signaled this evolution in thinking earlier, or if you chose one proposal and developed it more fully. Consider revising your thesis to hint at this "redistribution" idea, or use your conclusion to explicitly connect it back to your original claim.

Development of the Central Argument
The essay raises many important questions but doesn't fully answer them. For instance, you ask "what is equal work?" but never really resolve how we *would* measure the value of caregiving against neurosurgery—you assert it's "immense" and "immeasurable," which is persuasive rhetorically but leaves the argument underdeveloped analytically. Consider: What specific mechanisms exist (or could exist) for valuing this work? Are there real-world models—Social Security credits for caregivers, universal basic income proposals, wages-for-housework movements—you could reference to ground your argument in the real world rather than staying in the hypothetical?

Evidence and Support
You mention "study after study" tracing societal ills to lack of family guidance, but no specific study or source is cited. Similarly, your claim that "no culture on this globe" pays homemakers a salary is a strong, sweeping statement that would benefit from at least one concrete example or source. As an argumentative essay, your claims will be more persuasive with specific evidence rather than general assertions.

Consider Counterarguments
Right now, the essay presents only one side. A more persuasive argumentative essay would acknowledge objections: Why *hasn't* society compensated homemakers? Is it purely due to sexism/media bias, as you suggest, or are there practical objections (eg, who pays? How do you value different homemaking styles? Does payment change the intrinsic/relational nature of family care)? Engaging with these tensions—even briefly—will make your argument feel more rigorous and complete.

Language and Style Notes
"an neurosurgeon" → "a neurosurgeon" (minor typo)
"so immense as to be practically immeasurable" — the word "so" needs "as" nearby but check the full sentence; consider rephrasing for clarity: "This contribution to society is so immense that it is practically immeasurable."
"it, of course, the worker is married" — this sentence has a grammatical error; I believe you mean "if, of course, the worker is married." Please double check this construction.
Your phrase "with a capital C" is a nice touch of voice, but ensure this informal tone is consistent throughout, or reserve it for select moments for stylistic effect.
The phrase "doctors, lawyers, and Indian chiefs" is a well-known idiom, but be aware that some readers may find "Indian chiefs" outdated or potentially insensitive in this context; consider whether an alternative phrase would serve you better with a broader audience.

Final Suggestions

Clarify and possibly unify your thesis with your concluding proposal.
Add at least 2-3 concrete examples, statistics, or references to real programs/studies.
Address at least one counterargument to strengthen your credibility.
Proofread carefully for the grammatical issues noted above.

You have a strong foundation and a compelling, humane argument here. With more concrete support and a tighter connection between your thesis and conclusion, this essay could be quite persuasive. Good work—keep developing these ideas!

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You're tackling a genuinely interesting philosophical question—whether "money is the root of all evil" holds up as a meaningful claim—and your opening move (imagining the logical consequence: "the poor would be saints") is a strong, provocative hook. The essay shows real conceptual ambition. However, the argument currently loses the reader in several places due to unclear logical transitions, underdeveloped definitions, and an incomplete section. Below are the main areas to address in revision.

Clarify the Core Argument Early

Your thesis—that the saying is "meaningless or impractical" because desire and evil "cannot be appropriated objectively"—is buried at the end of a dense first paragraph. Consider stating this claim more directly near the beginning, perhaps in a single, clear sentence, and then use the rest of paragraph one to unpack *why* desire resists measurement. Right now the reader has to work hard to extract your central claim from sentences like "Desire cannot be measured on some utilitarian scale of pleasure. It increases and decreases with the acruement of wealth." These are assertions; briefly explain the reasoning behind them (eg, why *would* desire increase with wealth rather than decrease, as common sense might suggest?).

The Happiness/Virtue Paragraph Needs a Logical Bridge

This is the weakest link in the essay. You define happiness as "a lack of desire for further money, a contentedness with all one has," and then treat virtue as happiness's "counterpart." But the connection between *contentment* and *virtue* is asserted, not argued. A reader will ask: why should someone who is content necessarily be virtuous? Many content people are simply indifferent, not good. You need at least one sentence explicitly justifying this equation, or the paragraph will read as a non sequitur.

Similarly, the claim that follows—"The desire for money would—must—coincide and flourish with a desire for virtue if virtue... is to thrive"—is difficult to parse. Consider rewriting this as a clearer conditional: *If X, then Y, because Z.* As written, the logic collapses under its own qualifications and dashes.

Missing Content and Structural Gap

There is a placeholder  before your final paragraph. Whatever example, transition, or historical illustration you intended to insert there is crucial—it currently makes the final paragraph feel like a non sequitur, appearing to conclude "history shows the wealthy are as often oppressors as saviors" without any evidence or example actually presented. Since this historical claim is doing real argumentative work (it's your strongest piece of evidence against the saying), it deserves full development, not a placeholder. Consider adding a concrete example (a historical figure, a philanthropist-turned-exploiter, etc) to anchor the claim.

Paragraph Transitions

Each paragraph currently opens somewhat abruptly ("The problem, however, becomes immediately clear," "'Happiness' as a psychological idea can be shown to exist..."). Add a transitional clause connecting each new paragraph back to the previous argument, so the reader can track how the happiness discussion, the virtue discussion, and the historical claim all serve your central thesis about the unmeasurability of desire and evil.

Language and Mechanics

There are several typos and grammatical slips to correct in your next draft:
"privelaged" → privileged
"acruement" → accrual / accumulation
"wheighed" → weighed
"serrendipitously" → serendipitously
"a least" → "at least"
"where in which" → choose one ("in a world where..." or "in a world in which...")
"It is not subtle argument" → "It is not a subtle argument"

Additionally, watch your use of dashes for emphasis (eg, "would—must—coincide," "desire—much less evil—"). Used sparingly, this device is effective; overused, it fragments your sentences and makes the logic harder to follow. Consider replacing one or two of these with full clauses.

Style Suggestion

Your prose has an appealing, slightly formal, essayistic voice—reminiscent of philosophical argumentation. To strengthen it, favor slightly shorter sentences at key argumentative junctures (thesis statements, paragraph transitions) and reserve your longer, more complex sentences for elaboration. This will make your central claims easier to locate and follow.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
State your thesis more directly early on.
Justify the happiness = virtue equation.
Fill in the  gap with concrete historical evidence.
Smooth transitions between paragraphs.
Proofread for the typos listed above.

You have the makings of a sharp, original argument here—the revision work is mainly about making your logical steps explicit and supplying evidence where you currently rely on assertion.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay engages with a genuinely interesting question—whether Marx's egalitarian principle ("from each according to his ability, to each according to his need," presumably the missing quotation) can realistically function in Western, individualist society. You bring in family, religion, and philosophy to test this idea, which shows ambitious thinking. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected reflections than a tightly argued essay. Below are the main areas to address.

Missing Quotations and Undefined Terms

You have three bracketed placeholders () where quotations should appear. These are essential—without them, a reader cannot verify what "Marx's principle" actually says, what the New Testament teaches, or what "idea" you are critiquing in paragraph 4. Please insert the actual quotations and cite them properly. Right now, the essay's central claims are literally missing their evidence.

Thesis Clarity

Your thesis needs to be sharpened and stated explicitly, ideally at the end of the introduction. Currently, you assert that egalitarianism is natural to the family, is reinforced by religion, but ultimately fails as a broader social ethic due to individualism and moral relativism. This is a reasonable argument, but it needs to be stated as a single, clear claim early on—something like: "While Marx's egalitarian ideal finds natural expression in the family and in religious tradition, it cannot be successfully extended to society as a whole because Western culture prioritizes individual autonomy over collective obligation." State this clearly, then organize each paragraph to build toward it.

Structural and Logical Gaps

The move from family (paragraph 2) to religion (paragraph 3) to the "idea" you are critiquing (paragraph 4) is not signposted. Add transitional sentences that explain *why* you are moving from one institution to the next (eg, "If the family instills egalitarian values informally, religious institutions attempt to formalize them at a societal level").
Paragraph 4 introduces "problems" with a notion that is never clearly defined (because the quotation is missing). Before listing objections, state plainly what claim you are objecting to.
The rhetorical questions in paragraph 4 ("who is to define...?", "Must I go instead...?") are effective for provoking thought, but you never answer them. Follow up with your own position, even tentatively, so the paragraph doesn't feel like a list of unresolved doubts.
The paragraph on "freedom" (paragraph 5) restates the individual-vs-society tension abstractly but doesn't connect back to the egalitarian question. Clarify how individualism specifically undermines Marx's principle in practice.
The paragraph on moral relativism introduces a new concept quite late. Consider whether this deserves fuller development or could be folded into your discussion of individualism, since both point to the same root cause: the erosion of shared external moral authority.

Conclusion

Your final line is a good start but arrives too abruptly. Restate your thesis in more developed form, and briefly summarize *why* (family loyalty, religious internalization, individualism, relativism) the egalitarian ideal fails to scale up in Western culture. Right now the conclusion is only one sentence—expand it to at least a full paragraph.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to fix in revision:
"on e" → "one" (paragraph 1, typo)
"who it to define" → "who is to define" (paragraph 4)
"form the family" → "from the family" (paragraph 7)
Watch subject-verb agreement: "Society also has social institutions & practices in order which preserve..." is unclear—consider revising to "Society also maintains social institutions and practices that preserve the egalitarian concept."
Avoid ampersands (&) in formal academic writing; write "and" in full.
"The individual, in turn, incorporates the norm into rational thought" is vague—clarify what you mean by this process.
Some sentences are quite short and declarative in sequence (paragraph 2); consider varying sentence length and combining related ideas for better flow.

Suggestions for Revision

Insert and properly cite all three quotations.
Write a clear one- or two-sentence thesis statement at the end of your introduction.
Add transition sentences between paragraphs to make the logical progression explicit.
Define the "idea" you critique in paragraph 4 before listing objections to it.
Answer your own rhetorical questions, even briefly.
Expand the conclusion to synthesize your argument rather than simply asserting a verdict.
Proofread carefully for typos and grammatical slips.

Final Note

The intellectual content here is promising—you're clearly thinking seriously about the tension between collective ideals and individualist culture. The revision task is mostly about *making your reasoning visible*: filling in missing evidence, stating your claims directly, and guiding the reader step-by-step through your logic rather than assuming they will follow your associations. Good luck with your revision!

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback on "Does Crime Pay?"

Overall Impression

This is a thoughtful, well-organized essay with a genuinely interesting central idea: applying business/investment logic to criminal activity. Your extended metaphor comparing crime to a small business is creative and gives the essay a distinctive voice. However, the essay would benefit from a clearer thesis, more concrete evidence, and a stronger conclusion. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Strengths

Strong organizing structure: You break down "loss" into clear categories (capital/time, health, legal), which helps the reader follow your reasoning.
Original framework: The comparison to a small business owner facing competition from "larger criminal corporations" is a clever and memorable way to frame the argument.
Logical development: Each paragraph builds on the previous one, moving from simple financial calculation to more complex psychological and legal costs.

Areas for Revision

Clarify Your Thesis

Right now, your essay defines terms carefully but never commits to a clear position until the very end—and even then, the position is hedged ("In some cases it may..."). Ask yourself: what is your actual argument? Is it that crime *rarely* pays once all costs are considered? That it *depends* on the criminal's alternative opportunities? Consider stating this thesis explicitly in your introduction, so readers know what claim you will be defending. Right now the essay reads more like an *exploration* of the question than an *argument* answering it.

Strengthen the Conclusion

The final paragraph introduces a new comparison (crime vs. legitimate business) that isn't fully developed earlier in the essay. It also ends on a humorous note ("no taxes") that undercuts the seriousness of your argument. If the joke is intentional and ironic, it could work—but currently it makes the conclusion feel abrupt rather than clinching. Consider using the conclusion to explicitly answer your opening question and synthesize your three types of "loss" into a final judgment.

Add Concrete Evidence

Your essay relies entirely on hypothetical scenarios (the jewelry store theft, the gunshot wound) rather than real data, statistics, or documented cases. Argumentative essays are strengthened by outside evidence—for example, criminology research on recidivism, incarceration rates, or economic studies on the "opportunity cost" of criminal careers. Even one or two concrete examples (a specific criminal case, a cited statistic on the average earnings of drug dealers, etc) would significantly strengthen your credibility and move the essay from speculation toward argumentation.

Develop the "Legitimate Job Comparison" Earlier

You introduce a compelling idea in paragraph 3—that whether crime "pays" depends on what a criminal could earn legally—but you never return to develop this point. This is actually one of your most sophisticated insights (it gets at opportunity cost, a real economic concept). Consider expanding this idea with an example: What might a high-school dropout earn in a minimum-wage job versus dealing drugs? What might a college-educated embezzler have earned in a legitimate career? This would give your abstract argument more real-world texture.

Language and Style Notes

Typo: "equiptment" should be "equipment."
Run-on sentences: The first sentence of the essay is a run-on with multiple clauses connected by commas. Consider breaking it into two sentences for clarity: "If a person gains $100 in a jewelry store theft, he may still end up in debt. For example, he might use ten dollars of gas to escape, cut his hand on broken glass (costing $50 in medical bills), and spend another $50 on a hotel room."
Awkward phrasing: "then crimes can earn less to justify time spent" is unclear. Do you mean "a crime need not earn as much to be worthwhile"? Revise for precision.
Comparative error: In the conclusion, "he does better the legitimate businessman" is missing a word—should be "he does *better than* the legitimate businessman."
Word choice: "pay-offs," "hideout," and "small businessman criminal" are colloquial and fit your informal, almost tongue-in-cheek tone—this seems intentional and works well, but be consistent about how formal or informal you want the essay to sound throughout.

Suggested Next Steps

Draft a one-sentence thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Find at least one or two pieces of real-world evidence (statistics or case studies) to support your claims about costs and gains.
Revise the conclusion so it directly answers the question posed in your title, rather than introducing a new, undeveloped comparison.
Proofread carefully for the typo and grammatical issues noted above.

Overall, you have a promising and original argument here—the revision work now is about *committing* to a clear claim and *supporting* it with real evidence, rather than only well-reasoned hypotheticals. Good luck with your revision!

===== ICLE-US-IND-0008.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: "Is Money the Root of All Evil?"

Overall Impression

You've tackled a classic philosophical question with genuine conviction, and your central argument—distinguishing between money itself and the *love* of money—is a solid, defensible thesis. Your essay shows real thought about how this distinction plays out in families, individuals, and societies. However, the essay needs work in several areas: structural organization, paragraph development, sentence-level grammar, and consistency in tone. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Structure and Organization

Your essay currently reads like a series of related but loosely connected observations rather than a tightly organized argument. Consider this structure:

Introduction: State the claim you're responding to ("money is the root of all evil"), and present your thesis clearly.
Body 1: Establish why money itself is necessary/neutral (your second and third paragraphs).
Body 2: Explain the distinction between money and the *love* of money.
Body 3: Provide extended examples (family, business, nation, individual) with more development.
Body 4: Address the cross-cultural/universal nature of this problem.
Conclusion: Return to the original question and answer it directly, as you do in your final paragraph.

Right now, your strongest concluding move ("Is money the root of all evil? No...") appears only at the very end. Consider signaling this thesis more clearly in your introduction so readers know where the essay is heading.

Paragraph Development

Several paragraphs contain a single idea stated once without elaboration or evidence. For example:

> "It is a cross-cultural thing. We can be speaking of the American dollar, the Japanese yen or the Spanish peseta."

This is a good insight, but it's underdeveloped. Consider adding a specific example: *why* is greed universal across cultures? Is there a historical or contemporary example you could cite?

Similarly, your paragraph about the four-year-old child is a vivid, concrete example—this is one of your strongest moments in the essay. Try to develop other abstract claims (like the family breaking apart, or the company going under) with similarly concrete detail. Right now these are mentioned in a single sentence; expanding one of them into 3-4 sentences with a specific scenario would strengthen your argument considerably.

Grammar and Word Choice

Here are recurring issues to address:

Subject-verb agreement / verb forms: "It can be sued for good" should be "used." "It's not long before" — check that "it's" (contraction of "it is") vs. "its" (possessive) are used correctly; you have this reversed in "a life that already has it's needs met" (should be "its needs met").

Capitalization after questions: "is money the root of all evil? No." — capitalize "Is" since it begins a new sentence.

Spelling: "capitivates" should be "captivates."

Article usage: "This is a different concept all together" should be "altogether" (one word, meaning "entirely").

Pronoun consistency: You shift between "he," "men," and more neutral phrasing. Consider using more inclusive language throughout (eg, "individuals," "people," or "they") for consistency and to strengthen the essay's universal claims.

Redundancy: "Money, in some form or other, is required in order to carry out business trading, commerce & exchange of property" — the ampersand (&) should be spelled out as "and" in academic writing.

Tone and Register

Your essay sometimes shifts between formal academic argument and a more sermon-like or conversational tone (eg, "How many times have we seen on the evening news..."). This rhetorical question is effective for engagement, but make sure your overall essay maintains a consistent, analytical register appropriate for argumentative writing. Reduce reliance on rhetorical questions in favor of analysis and evidence.

Strengths to Preserve

Your central distinction (money vs. love of money) is intellectually sound and well-reasoned.
The image of the child learning to hoard money is vivid and effective—it grounds an abstract argument in something recognizable.
Your conclusion directly and confidently answers the question posed, which gives the essay a satisfying sense of closure.

Next Steps

Revise your introduction to state your thesis explicitly and early.
Choose 2-3 of your examples (family, business, nation, or child) and develop them with more specific, concrete detail.
Proofread carefully for the grammar issues noted above, particularly "its/it's" and verb forms.
Read your essay aloud to catch places where the tone shifts from analytical to conversational, and revise for consistency.

With these revisions, your essay's clear and thoughtful argument will be much more persuasive and polished.

Overall Impression

Below are specific suggestions.

Missing Phrase and Thesis Clarity

The placeholder  in your opening sentence needs to be filled in with the actual phrase you are analyzing ("Crime does not pay"). As it stands, a reader encountering this for the first time may be confused. More importantly, state your thesis clearly and early. Right now, your position—that the phrase is "wishful thinking" rather than a "truism"—only appears in the final sentence. Consider forecasting this claim in your introduction so readers know from the start what argument they are following. For example, you might end your first paragraph with a sentence like: "Yet a closer look at how crime is actually punished and profited from suggests that this saying is less a truth than a comforting fiction."

Strengthen and Verify Evidence

Several of your examples need more precision and documentation:

Michael Milken (you wrote "Milliken") should be spelled correctly, and you should cite where you learned these facts (a news article, a book, etc). Right now the claims about his earnings and prison experience are asserted without support, which weakens their persuasive power for an academic reader.

The Nixon example is compelling but could be developed further. You might explain *how* he "managed to become a representative of the nation again"—was it a specific rehabilitation of his image, a particular event, or public forgetting? A sentence or two of elaboration would make this example more persuasive.
The Indiana CHINS example is interesting but somewhat under-explained for readers unfamiliar with the acronym or the specific policy debate. Consider adding a sentence that clarifies who benefits financially and how this connects back to your central claim.

Your essay moves through several different *kinds* of argument:
Individual criminals who profit despite punishment (Milken, Nixon)
A statistical/economic argument about murder specifically
A systemic argument about the incarceration industry

This is a sophisticated structure, but the transitions between these types of argument need to be made more explicit. Right now, the murder statistic appears somewhat abruptly in paragraph 3 and then is not returned to. Consider adding a sentence that explicitly signals the shift: for instance, "Beyond individual cases, the economics of punishment itself raise questions about whether crime truly fails to pay." This would help readers see that you are building a *cumulative* case rather than simply listing separate examples.

Similarly, the final paragraph moves quickly from the CHINS example to your conclusion. You might restate, briefly, how each type of evidence (individual profit, statistical incentive, systemic profit) supports your final claim.

"Michael Milliken" → "Michael Milken" (spelling)

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in the missing phrase and state your thesis explicitly near the start.
Add citations or sources for statistical and biographical claims.
Strengthen transitions between the three types of argument (individual, statistical, systemic).
Briefly acknowledge counterexamples where crime does *not* pay.

You have a genuinely interesting and well-organized line of reasoning here—the main task now is to make your thesis more visible and your evidence more credible and better connected.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay engages with an interesting and debatable question—whether financial reward should be tied to individual effort—and you clearly have a personal, thoughtful perspective on it. The writing has energy and conviction. However, the essay would benefit from a sharper thesis, more rigorous development of ideas, engagement with counterarguments, and attention to grammar and word choice. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis appears in the first paragraph: "men and women should be financially reward for their individual efforts." This is a reasonable claim, but it needs to be more precise. What does "individual effort" mean exactly—hours worked? Skill level? Risk taken? Innovation? Right now the claim is broad enough that a reader isn't sure what specific policy or principle you're defending (eg, merit-based pay, capitalism versus socialism, opposition to universal basic income). Consider revising your thesis to state clearly *why* effort-based reward is preferable to alternatives, and preview the reasons you'll give.

Also, be careful with phrases like "I feel" and "I would venture to guess." Academic argumentative writing is strongest when it presents claims as reasoned conclusions supported by evidence, rather than personal feelings. Try replacing "I feel it is vital that..." with something like "This essay argues that..." or "Society functions best when..."

Structure and Organization

The essay moves from past to present to a hypothetical scenario to a general reflection on rewards. This chronological/hypothetical structure is understandable, but each paragraph needs a clearer topic sentence that ties back to your thesis, so the reader always knows how the paragraph is building your case.

For example, paragraph two (about early societies) makes a good point—that reward has historically been tied to effort—but it functions mostly as background. Consider adding a sentence that explicitly connects this history to your argument: "This historical pattern suggests that reward-for-effort is not an arbitrary social construct but a natural incentive system."

The final paragraph feels more like a set of reflections than a conclusion. A strong conclusion should restate your thesis in a fresh way and briefly synthesize your main supporting points (history, present technology, the hypothetical equal society) before ending with a memorable final statement.

Development and Evidence

Right now, your strongest evidence is a hypothetical thought experiment (the "society where all men and women were treated equal"). While thought experiments can be persuasive, an academic essay is strengthened by concrete, real-world evidence: historical examples, economic data, or references to actual policy debates (eg, comparisons between capitalist and socialist economies, studies on motivation and productivity). Even one or two real examples would make your argument far more convincing than speculation ("I would venture to guess," "Worst case scenario").

Additionally, consider addressing a counterargument directly. For instance: some people cannot work due to disability, discrimination, or lack of opportunity—should reward still be tied strictly to effort in these cases? Engaging with this complicates and strengthens your position, showing you've considered opposing views rather than only imagining extreme scenarios (the world "destroyed").

Language, Style, and Grammar

There are several recurring grammar issues worth reviewing:

"should be financially reward" → "should be financially rewarded"
"took up of majority of each person's day" → "took up the majority of each person's day"
"we have many machines to held us meet" → "to help us meet"
"Competitiveness and jealously would set in" → "jealousy"
"how advanced of a society" → "how advanced a society"
"Some are content with needs, others want more, much more!" — the exclamation point and short fragments are more suited to conversational or persuasive speech than academic prose; consider integrating this idea into a fuller sentence.

Also watch for inconsistent verb tense and subject-verb agreement (eg, "Persons with great technical minds are using their talent on the job and paying someone to do their labor" — this sentence's logic is a bit unclear; clarify who is doing what).

Try to reduce your use of second person ("you had to plant," "you had to build") in favor of a more consistent point of view (either general "people" or first-person plural "we"), since shifting between them can confuse the reader about your intended audience.

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to state a specific, arguable claim.
Add topic sentences that explicitly link each paragraph back to that thesis.
Replace speculative language ("I would venture to guess," "it would appear") with more assertive, evidence-based claims.
Incorporate at least one concrete historical or economic example.
Address a counterargument (eg, structural inequality, disability, unequal opportunity) to show balanced reasoning.
Proofread carefully for verb forms and word choice errors listed above.
Strengthen the conclusion by synthesizing your argument rather than introducing new philosophical musings.

Final Note

Your ideas show genuine engagement with the topic, and your writing voice is clear and confident. With a tighter thesis, more concrete evidence, and careful editing, this essay could become a much more persuasive piece of argumentative writing. Keep developing your ideas—you're off to a solid start!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult philosophical question—whether compensation should reflect "contribution to society"—and you bring some vivid, memorable examples to the discussion (the 1920s investors, the migrant worker versus the wealthy tipper). These concrete images are one of the strongest features of your writing. However, the essay would benefit significantly from a clearer structure, a more precise thesis, and more careful attention to logical development. Below are specific areas to address.

Clarify Your Thesis and Central Claim

Your opening paragraph states that "contribution to society" is subjective and therefore compensation cannot be equal—but this claim needs to be argued more explicitly and consistently. By the final paragraph, you seem to be making a related but distinct claim: that we cannot align pay with contribution without either forcing the wealthy to work harder or redistributing wealth in ways society won't accept. These are two different arguments (one about subjectivity/definition, one about practical/political feasibility). Decide which is your main thesis, and make sure every paragraph supports it directly. Right now, a reader finishes the essay unsure which claim you most want us to accept.

Strengthen the Logical Connections Between Paragraphs

The essay currently reads as a series of loosely related observations rather than a step-by-step argument. For example:
Paragraph 2 (the 1920s) introduces the idea that some critique the wealthy as "parasites," but you never state your own position on this—do you agree or disagree with these authors?
Paragraph 3 raises the migrant worker example, which is powerful, but it isn't clearly connected back to your thesis. Are you arguing that this inequality is unjust, or just describing it?
Paragraph 4 introduces "Democracy" and the idea of equal opportunity, but this concept appears suddenly and isn't defined or connected to the earlier examples.

Suggestion: After each paragraph, ask yourself: "How does this specific point prove my thesis?" Add a transition sentence that makes the connection explicit rather than leaving it for the reader to infer.

Develop Counterarguments More Fairly

You gesture toward opposing views (the "hard view" that low pay is one's own fault) but dismiss them quickly as based on a "faulty premise" without fully explaining *why* the premise is faulty or engaging with the strongest version of that argument. A stronger essay would:
State the opposing view fully and fairly.
Explain precisely what evidence or reasoning undermines it.
Then pivot back to your own position with added credibility.

The Doctor Example Needs Reworking

The point about doctors is interesting but logically shaky. You suggest that because doctors don't lower their fees, they must believe their price is fair—but this conflates "what the market will bear" with "what is morally fair," which is precisely the distinction your essay is trying to explore. This example could be much stronger if you explicitly acknowledged this tension rather than treating it as settled.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Word choice/spelling: "Democracry" → *Democracy*; "responsibly" → *responsibility*; "money" repeated awkwardly in "make much too money" → *make too much money*.
Article usage: "In the 1920s in American" → *in America*.
Subject-verb agreement: "A migrant work may slave away" → *A migrant worker*; "unless we wish to impose on the personal lives... or give people... money that society does not believe they deserve, we cannot give pay equal to contribution" — this sentence is grammatically dense; consider breaking it into two sentences for clarity.
Punctuation with quotation marks: "equally'" has mismatched quotation marks—check for a stray apostrophe.
Sentence fragments: "The answer this question lies..." is missing "to" — *The answer to this question lies...*

Paragraph Organization (Structural Suggestion)

Consider this revised structure:
Introduction: State thesis clearly (subjectivity of "contribution," OR practical impossibility of matching pay to contribution—pick one primary angle).
Historical example: 1920s investors—but explicitly state what this proves about your thesis.
Counterexample: Migrant worker—explicitly contrast with the wealthy investor to show the *range* of the problem.
Address counterargument: The "equal opportunity" premise—engage seriously, then refute.
Practical example: Doctors—clarify what this shows about market price vs. moral fairness.
Conclusion: Return to thesis, drawing together your examples into a final synthesized claim.

Summary

You have compelling raw material here—historical context, vivid contrasts, and a genuinely thought-provoking question. Your revision should focus on (1) settling on one precise thesis, (2) explicitly connecting each example back to that thesis, and (3) treating the counterargument with more depth before refuting it. Cleaning up the grammar issues noted above will also help your argument read more smoothly. I'd be glad to look at a revised draft once you've reworked the structure.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting and provocative thesis—that crime can be profitable in America—and you support it with a range of engaging examples. Below are suggestions to help strengthen the argument, organization, and language.

Strengths

You've chosen a genuinely debatable claim and organized your essay around three clear categories of support (historical examples, monetary rewards, and the justice system). Your examples are vivid and specific—the Boston Tea Party, O.J. Simpson, Prohibition—which makes the essay engaging to read.

Areas for Revision

Clarify Your Central Argument

Your thesis conflates several different ideas: (a) crimes that were only later punished retroactively (Boston Tea Party), (b) people who profit from *notoriety* rather than the crime itself (O.J. Simpson, Divine Brown), and (c) people who profit *directly* from illegal acts (bootleggers). These are not the same phenomenon. Consider narrowing your thesis to one clear claim—for instance, "crime and infamy can generate financial profit in American culture"—and then organize your evidence to support that single point consistently.

Address the Obvious Counterargument

A sharp reader will immediately object: most criminals are *not* rich or famous—they are imprisoned, poor, or dead. Your essay would be far more persuasive if you acknowledged this reality explicitly and then explained why the exceptions matter (eg, "While the majority of criminals face punishment without reward, American culture has created a system that occasionally transforms crime into celebrity and profit"). Right now, the essay overstates its case ("crime does not pay" being false) without acknowledging that this is true only in specific, unusual circumstances.

Distinguish Between "Profit from Crime" and "Profit from Fame Tied to Crime"

This is your biggest structural issue. Autobiographies and movie deals are profits from *storytelling about* a crime, mediated by publishers and the media—not profits from the *crime itself*. Bootlegging profits, by contrast, come directly from the illegal transaction. You should either separate these into distinct categories with distinct explanations, or explicitly argue that *both* count as "crime paying" and explain why.

Strengthen the Boston Tea Party / Benedict Arnold Examples

These examples work against your thesis more than for it: they show that history's *judgment* of an act changes, not that the *people who committed it* profited financially. Consider either cutting these examples or reframing your argument to also include how the retrospective judgment shapes American attitudes toward crime.

Revise the Conclusion

Your final paragraph asserts that crime statistics are "rising continuously" — this is a factual claim you have not supported with any evidence. Since crime rates in the U.S. actually declined significantly in the 1990s, this claim could seriously undermine your credibility with an informed reader. Either provide a source or remove this specific claim and focus your conclusion on the cultural/systemic argument instead.

Language and Mechanics

"vents" should be "events" (paragraph 2)
"the guilty are punished, and no one profits" — consider varying sentence structure at the start; you have several short, choppy sentences early on.
"No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money, if it were not why would people commit them" — this sentence has a comma splice and a subject-verb agreement issue ("them" should be "it," referring to "crime"). Revise to: "No one can argue that crime is not a means of making money; if it were not, why would people commit it?"
"Los Vegas" should be "Las Vegas"
"speak-easies" is the correct spelling (not "speak-easies" — check this)
"tes." at the start of the final paragraph appears to be a typo/formatting error—please proofread your final draft carefully.
The phrase "National Enquirer brand of fame" is a nice touch, but make sure your audience will recognize the reference; consider a brief explanatory clause if writing for an international or academic audience.
"criminals autobiographies" should be "criminals' autobiographies" (missing apostrophe for possession)

Overall Suggestion

Your strongest material is in paragraphs 3 and 4 (monetary rewards and the justice system), where the connection between crime and profit is most direct. Consider leading with this material, then use the historical paragraph as a shorter supporting section rather than your opening argument, since it currently raises questions your thesis doesn't fully answer.

Word count suggestion: Focus your revision on tightening the historical section and adding a paragraph or two acknowledging the counterargument. This will make your essay more persuasive and analytically rigorous.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Feminism and Women's Roles

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely complex topic, and your essay shows real engagement with it—you moved beyond a simple "for or against" stance to explore internal disagreements among women themselves. This is a promising strategy for a nuanced argument. However, the essay currently reads as a set of personal opinions rather than a supported argumentative essay. Below are specific areas to strengthen in revision.

Clarify Your Thesis

Right now, your position shifts throughout the essay. You begin by agreeing women faced real discrimination, then argue feminists have gone "too far" and don't represent all women, then argue men and women are naturally suited to different roles. These are three different claims. Decide which is your *main* argument and make it explicit in your introduction. For example: "While feminism achieved important progress, it has overreached by assuming all women want the same things." Everything else should support this single claim.

Support Claims with Evidence

This is the essay's biggest weakness. Several strong claims are presented with no evidence:

"Women just can't fight in a war very well. They are not strong enough."
"Now many employers are the victims [of harassment claims]."
"Divorce is at fifty percent and rising, women are having more heart attacks, being more stressful."
"God would have created us all the same if this was not true."

Academic argument requires you to show *why* a claim is true, using research, statistics with citations, historical examples, or expert opinion—not just assertion. For instance, you could cite actual research on gender differences in physical strength standards, or data on divorce rates and their causes (which are debated among sociologists, not simply attributable to "modernization"). The claim linking women's stress/heart attacks to leaving traditional roles is a causal claim that needs strong evidence to avoid seeming like speculation.

Similarly, the appeal to "God" as evidence works only for readers who share your religious framework, and it functions as a natural-order argument ("nature cannot be changed") that many readers—and much of history—would dispute (social roles have changed dramatically over time). If you want to use this reasoning, acknowledge it's a belief-based argument, or replace it with argument grounded in biology/sociology research if that's your true reasoning.

Strengthen Logical Structure

Several points would benefit from being unpacked more carefully:

The military combat example is your strongest illustration of internal disagreement among women, but you undercut it by calling opposing feminists "crazy"—this is a personal judgment, not an argument. Instead, present the actual reasoning on both sides (physical standards, unit cohesion arguments vs. equal opportunity arguments) and explain why you find one more persuasive.
The paragraph on sexual harassment makes a sharp generalization ("many employers are the victims") without explaining the reasoning or citing examples/data. This claim, especially, needs careful qualification—it risks sounding dismissive of legitimate harassment claims unless you show specific evidence of the trend you're describing.

Watch Your Word Choice and Tone

Phrases like "these people were crazy" or "impossible to do" are too absolute and informal for academic writing. Consider more measured phrasing: "I disagree with this position because..." Academic tone maintains respect for opposing views even while arguing against them.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring errors to fix in editing:

"verses" → should be versus
"piers" → should be peers
"The are not strong enough" → They are not strong enough
Watch subject-verb agreement: "Women should be created equal, but they can only do what nature intended they to do" → should be "them to do"
Several sentences run together without proper punctuation (comma splices): eg, "This makes it hard, because if they don't, they are looked down upon by their piers" — consider restructuring for clarity.
Vary sentence openings; several paragraphs begin with "These/This," which becomes repetitive.

Conclusion Needs Rebuilding

Your final paragraph raises interesting points (a future woman president, women running companies) but doesn't tie back clearly to your thesis. A strong conclusion should restate your central argument and explain its broader significance—why does this debate matter for how we think about gender roles today?

Suggested Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis statement and check that every paragraph supports it.
For each major claim, find one source (article, study, or statistic) to support it.
Revise emotionally loaded language into more analytical, respectful phrasing.
Proofread for the grammar issues noted above.

You have an interesting angle here—the tension between formal feminist goals and individual women's varied preferences. Sharpening your evidence and structure will make this argument much more persuasive.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this draft. You clearly have a strong personal conviction about your topic, and your writing shows creativity and a natural storytelling voice. However, as an argumentative essay, the piece needs significant revision in terms of structure, argument development, and academic tone. Below are the main areas to focus on.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your opening claim—"crime does not pay"—is a reasonable thesis, but it gets lost almost immediately. The essay shifts between several related but distinct ideas: (1) crime escalates because offenders test limits, (2) punishment severity depends on the harm caused, and (3) a long hypothetical story about a robbery gone wrong. These ideas are connected, but you never explicitly state *how* they support your central claim. Ask yourself: what is the one thing I want my reader to believe by the end of this essay? Write that as a clear, one-sentence thesis, and make sure every paragraph explicitly returns to it.

Structure and Paragraphing

Right now, the essay reads more like a spoken narrative than a structured argument. An academic argumentative essay typically needs:
An introduction with a clear thesis
Body paragraphs, each built around one main point, supported by evidence or reasoning
A conclusion that ties the argument together

Your second paragraph, for instance, introduces the idea that punishment severity depends on harm caused—but this point is never developed. Instead, it launches into a very long hypothetical story that takes over the rest of the essay. Consider: does this story serve your thesis, or does it become the main event? Right now, it's the latter. I'd recommend cutting the story down to 3-4 sentences that illustrate your point, then use the space you save to actually *argue* your case with reasoning.

Over-reliance on Anecdote/Hypothetical

The convenience-store story is vivid, but it's not evidence—it's a fictional scenario you invented. In academic argumentative writing, you're expected to support claims with real evidence: statistics on recidivism, criminology research, case law, or at least logical reasoning drawn from credible sources. A hypothetical example can *illustrate* a point, but it cannot *prove* one. Right now, your entire argument rests on personal belief and an invented story, which will weaken your credibility with an academic reader. Try researching real cases or data about how crimes escalate or how sentencing works, and use those instead.

Tone and Register

Phrases like "run like hell," "guess what happens," "sounds like trouble to me," and "big mess" are conversational and appropriate for spoken storytelling, but not for academic argumentative writing. Academic essays require a more formal, objective register. I recommend reading through and asking: "Would I say this in a formal presentation to a professor?" If not, revise it to sound more analytical. For example, instead of "Sounds like trouble to me," you might write: "This example illustrates how a minor crime can escalate into a much more serious offense with severe legal consequences."

Grammar and Mechanics

There are frequent spelling and typographical errors (eg, "committs," "indivudual," "convenient store" instead of "convenience store," "strats," "hurridly," "persistant," "deterent," "whrn"). I recommend running your essay through a spell-checker and reading it aloud slowly to catch these. Also watch for:
Subject-verb agreement issues ("With this attitude someone could definitely headed for destruction" → "could definitely be headed")
Run-on sentences (several of your sentences exceed 60-70 words and combine multiple ideas without clear punctuation—consider breaking these into shorter, clearer sentences)
Comma splices, especially in the narrative paragraph

Missing Conclusion

The essay ends abruptly with the hypothetical story's resolution. There is no paragraph that returns to your original thesis ("crime does not pay") and explains what the reader should take away from your argument. A strong conclusion should restate your thesis in light of the evidence you've presented and perhaps suggest broader implications.

Suggested Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis and revise your intro to build directly toward it.
Cut the hypothetical story to a brief, focused example (3-5 sentences).
Add at least one body paragraph with real evidence or reasoning (not narrative).
Revise tone throughout for formality.
Proofread carefully for spelling and sentence-length issues.
Write a conclusion that circles back to your thesis.

You have a genuine argument here—now it needs a stronger academic frame to support it. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: "Money is the Root of All Evil"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a thought-provoking topic and taken an interesting angle: rather than simply agreeing or disagreeing with the proverb, you argue that money's moral value depends on the *attitude* of the person who holds it. This is a promising thesis with real philosophical depth. However, the essay currently reads more like a first draft of ideas than a fully developed argument. Below are specific areas to strengthen.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your central claim—"Money of itself is neither good nor bad. One's attitude about money is the deciding factor"—is a strong, debatable thesis. I recommend stating this clearly in your introduction rather than burying it in paragraph two. Right now, the introduction spends most of its time defining money and describing its "power," and only hints at your real argument in the final sentence. Consider restructuring so that your thesis appears explicitly by the end of paragraph one.

Additionally, the essay would benefit from a counterargument. Since your claim is that money is neutral, you should acknowledge the opposing view more directly (that money itself corrupts) before refuting it. This would make your argument feel more rigorous and persuasive.

Evidence and Examples

This is the area that most needs development. You list categories of "evil" money-driven behavior—drug use, gambling, prostitution, materialism, prestige-seeking, greed—but you don't develop any of them with a concrete example. Academic essays are far more persuasive when abstract claims are illustrated. For instance:

Instead of just naming "gambling," briefly describe a case (real or hypothetical) of how the need for money destroyed someone's relationships or ethics.
When discussing greed, you could reference a well-known example (a corporate scandal, a historical figure, or even a literary character like Ebenezer Scrooge or Gordon Gekko) to ground your claim.

Right now, your paragraphs state ideas but don't *prove* them. Ask yourself after each claim: "How do I know this is true? What evidence supports it?"

Structure and Organization

The essay has three paragraphs but lacks a clear introduction-body-conclusion shape:

Paragraph 1 functions as an introduction but spends too much time defining money and not enough time previewing your argument.
Paragraph 2 is your strongest paragraph—it introduces your key claim and begins to explore it—but it tries to cover too much (addiction, materialism, prestige) without fully developing any single idea.
Paragraph 3 discusses greed but ends abruptly with "Money does good or evil—depending on who has the money," which reads like a thesis restatement, not a conclusion.

The essay needs a genuine concluding paragraph that synthesizes your argument, perhaps returning to the opening proverb and explaining how your essay has redefined or complicated it. Right now, the essay simply stops.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring grammar patterns to review:

Article usage: "the definition of term money" → "the definition of the term *money*." Non-native English speakers often struggle with articles (a/an/the); it may help to review rules for when definite/indefinite articles are required before nouns.
Subject-verb agreement and capitalization: "when the need for money becomes" should begin with a capital "When" since it starts a new sentence.
Apostrophe error: "Ones's attitude" should be "One's attitude" (only one apostrophe needed).
Missing article: "structure of a greedy persons life" → "the structure of a greedy person's life" (also note the missing apostrophe in "person's").
Run-on/comma splice: "Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive." This should likely be two sentences or joined with a semicolon or coordinating conjunction: "...to survive; a lack of money, however, may mean you can't survive."
Sentence fragments: "But there is another side to possessing money" is fine as a transition sentence, but make sure such short sentences are intentional stylistic choices, not accidental fragments.

I'd suggest reading your essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these small errors—they are minor individually but add up and distract the reader from your ideas.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clearer thesis statement at the end of paragraph one.
Add at least one concrete example or case per body paragraph.
Consider addressing a counterargument (eg, "Some believe money itself is corrupting because...").
Write a real conclusion that ties back to the opening proverb and offers a final insight.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this personal and passionate piece. You clearly have strong opinions on this topic, and your writing shows genuine engagement with a subject you've lived through across several decades. Below are some observations to help strengthen the essay for an academic context.

Strengths

Your essay has a clear emotional core and personal authority—you write from direct experience of both eras you're comparing (the 1960s and 1990s), which gives your argument a kind of ethos that many writers lack. You also make an effort at balance by acknowledging that some feminist goals (equal pay, breaking the glass ceiling, women's right to vote) are legitimate. This shows you're not simply dismissing the movement outright.

Areas for Revision

Clarify and narrow your thesis.
Your central claim seems to be: *"Chivalry has declined because of feminism, and this decline is regrettable."* However, this claim gets diluted by several tangents (equal pay, division of housework, historical suffrage) that, while related, pull focus away from your main argument about dating and courtship. Consider stating your thesis explicitly in the introduction—something like: "While many feminist achievements deserve support, the movement's effect on dating norms has been largely negative, leaving men uncertain and courtship diminished." This would give readers a clear roadmap.

Distinguish correlation from causation.
You attribute the decline of chivalry directly to feminism, but the essay itself notes many other possible factors (changing economic realities requiring two incomes, shifting parental values, generational change). Academic argumentation requires you to consider alternative explanations and explain why you believe feminism specifically is the cause, rather than one of several contributing factors. Right now, the connection is asserted rather than argued.

Move from anecdote to evidence.
Your evidence is almost entirely personal: your own dating experience, comments from friends, and a "significant other." While personal experience can be a valid starting point, an academic essay should also engage with broader evidence—sociological studies on dating norms, surveys on gender attitudes, or scholarly commentary on chivalry and feminism. This would make your claims about "women in general" or "men in general" more credible and less like sweeping generalizations.

Avoid overgeneralization.
Phrases like "women in general," "these feminist groups," and "you men out there" treat large, diverse groups as monolithic. Not all feminists share identical views on chivalry, and not all men from the 1960s were gentlemen (you acknowledge this briefly with "there are always exceptions," but then set it aside too quickly). Try to qualify your claims: "many," "some," "in my experience" rather than absolute statements. This will make your argument more persuasive to readers who might otherwise feel unfairly characterized.

Address counterarguments directly.
A strong argumentative essay anticipates objections. Why might some women feel that being helped with a chair or having a door opened seems patronizing rather than kind? Is there a version of "chivalry" that could be reframed as mutual courtesy rather than a return to older gender roles? Engaging seriously with this perspective—rather than dismissing it as "bunk"—would make your argument more persuasive and balanced.

Revise for tone and audience consistency.
The essay shifts from a general, reflective voice to a direct, informal address ("I'm not asking for the world from you men out there") in the conclusion. This sudden change in audience (from readers in general to "men") is confusing. Decide who you are speaking to throughout the essay and maintain that consistently.

Language and mechanics.
A few specific notes:
"Stigmatism" should be "stigma."
"Hanging around this continent for 40-plus years" is a colorful but very informal phrase; consider a more precise expression like "I have lived in this country for over 40 years."
Several sentences are quite long and contain multiple clauses joined with commas rather than periods or semicolons (eg, the sentence beginning "Men were gentlemen in the 60's..."). Breaking these into shorter sentences will improve clarity.
Watch subject-verb agreement and pronoun consistency, eg, "he/she comes home... which would leave more time" – make sure pronouns and verb tenses align throughout.

Summary

Your essay has a real point of view and lived experience behind it, which is valuable. To bring it to a stronger academic level, focus on: (1) a clearer, more focused thesis; (2) support beyond personal anecdote; (3) more careful, qualified language when describing groups; and (4) genuine engagement with opposing viewpoints. These changes will transform this from a strongly-felt personal reflection into a more persuasive and credible argumentative essay.
