Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting angle on the familiar saying that crime doesn't pay, and you clearly have several good ideas about the different "costs" of crime—financial, emotional, and social. However, the essay needs work in three main areas: maintaining a consistent argument, developing your points with evidence, and correcting language errors. Below are specific suggestions.

Clarify and Maintain Your Thesis

Your opening paragraph states that "crime actually costs money," which sets up an expectation that the whole essay will discuss *financial* costs. However, your third paragraph shifts to a *moral/emotional* argument (the criminal's remorse), which doesn't fit your stated thesis. This is a common issue in argumentative writing: each paragraph must clearly support the central claim.

Suggestion: Either broaden your thesis in the introduction to say something like "crime carries a cost—financial, emotional, and social—for everyone involved," or remove the paragraph about remorse and add another paragraph that reinforces the financial theme (for example, lost wages for crime victims, insurance costs, or costs to businesses from theft).

Strengthen Your Topic Sentences and Transitions

Each paragraph introduces a different perspective (the criminal, the law-abiding citizen, the ex-convict), which is a nice structural idea, but the transitions between paragraphs are abrupt. Consider explicitly signaling the shift, eg, "Beyond the criminal's own losses, the law-abiding citizen also bears the cost of crime." This will help your reader follow the logic of your argument as it moves from one group affected by crime to another.

Add Evidence and Examples

Right now, the essay relies entirely on general claims ("As the number of criminals increases so [too] will the tax money needed…") without any supporting data, statistics, or concrete examples. Academic argumentative writing is much more persuasive when backed by evidence. For instance:

Cite an approximate cost of incarcerating one prisoner per year.
Reference a study on how crime rates affect property values.
Give a specific example of a person denied a job or housing because of a criminal record.

Even one or two well-chosen statistics or examples per paragraph would significantly strengthen your argument.

Write a Conclusion

The essay currently ends with the paragraph about the "criminal" label, but there is no concluding paragraph that ties your points together and restates your thesis in light of the evidence you've presented. Every argumentative essay needs a conclusion that reminds the reader of your main claim and perhaps calls for reflection or action (eg, suggesting how society might reduce these costs).

Language and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to fix:

Spelling: "freinds" → *friends*; "acquantances" → *acquaintances*; "oppurtunity" → *opportunity*.
Homophone confusion: "to often" → *too often*; "so to will" → *so too will*; "their is no escape" → *there is no escape*.
Punctuation: "so even though an individual can try..." should begin with a capital letter (a new sentence), not lowercase "so."
Pronoun agreement: You often switch between singular "his or her" and plural "their" (eg, "that person carries a label with them"). Try to be consistent—either use "his or her...him or her" throughout, or consider using "they/their" consistently as a singular pronoun, which is now widely accepted, but be consistent in your choice.
Wordiness: Sentences like "This money may not come from the perpetrator of the crime but it will come from the individual's family, friends and acquaintances in the form of tax dollars" could be tightened: "This money often comes not from the perpetrator but from taxpayers, including the perpetrator's own family and friends."

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud after revising—this often helps catch missing words, awkward phrasing, and punctuation errors.

Strengths to Keep

Your rhetorical opening question is engaging and draws the reader in.
You've identified multiple stakeholders affected by crime (the criminal, taxpayers, property owners, employers), which shows good analytical thinking.
Your paragraph structure, while needing refinement, shows a clear attempt to organize ideas logically.

Next Steps

Revise your thesis to encompass all the types of "cost" you discuss, or narrow your paragraphs to match a single type of cost (financial).
Add at least one piece of evidence or a concrete example per paragraph.
Write a strong concluding paragraph.
Proofread carefully for spelling, homophones, and pronoun consistency.

With these revisions, your essay will have a much clearer, more persuasive argument. Keep up the good critical thinking—the ideas here are strong; they just need sharper organization and support.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Does Crime Pay?"

Thomas, thank you for sharing this essay. You clearly have real interest in and knowledge of your topic, and your voice comes through with energy and confidence. However, the essay needs significant revision to meet the expectations of academic argumentative writing. Below are the main areas to focus on.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Right now, your essay opens with a personal reflection rather than a clear thesis statement. As a reader, I finish paragraph one not knowing exactly what claim you will argue. You seem to be building toward the idea that *organized crime pays, while unorganized (petty) crime does not* — but this needs to be stated explicitly and early, ideally at the end of your introduction. A strong thesis might look something like: "While unorganized crime rarely pays off due to lack of resources and high visibility, organized crime is a highly profitable enterprise sustained by systemic corruption and money." State your position clearly so readers know what you're proving from the start.

Structure and Organization

Your essay follows a loose comparison (unorganized crime vs. organized crime), but the sections feel more like separate descriptions than a unified argument. Consider organizing body paragraphs around specific *points of comparison* — for example: definition, typical perpetrators, financial outcomes, and relationship to law enforcement. Discussing both types of crime side-by-side on each point (rather than one type per paragraph) will make your comparison sharper and easier to follow.

Additionally, the essay lacks a real conclusion. It should end by returning to your thesis and summarizing how your evidence supports it — not trail off into a biographical statement about yourself. That final paragraph (your age, major, and background) does not belong in an argumentative essay; save it for a separate introduction or bio assignment if required.

Evidence and Credibility

You reference your course ("Organized Crime & American Politics") and a statistic ($30 per person spent on illegal gambling), which is good — this shows you're drawing on real learning. However:

You need to cite your source for that statistic. Where does it come from? A textbook, a lecture, a report? Academic writing requires you to show where your facts originate, both for credibility and to avoid the appearance of plagiarism.
Some claims are generalizations without support, such as "these people are usually somewhat less educated." This is a stereotype, not a demonstrated fact — and it weakens your credibility. Either provide evidence for such claims or remove them.
You could strengthen your argument with a specific case study (a real gambling ring, a documented corruption case, a specific city) rather than staying at the level of general assertion.

Tone and Register

Your writing is engaging, but the tone is too informal for academic argument. Some specific issues:

Avoid rhetorical questions and phrases like "I will tell you about myself" that address the reader directly in a conversational way.
Avoid all-caps for emphasis ("BIG sum," "ANYTHING," "MONEY!"). Academic writing conveys emphasis through word choice and sentence structure, not capitalization.
Phrases like "both sides of the track" and "save the best for last" are colloquial. Replace with more formal academic phrasing.
The line "I cannot comment on whether there is a valid reason for committing a crime... they have no room to judge" introduces a moral argument that feels disconnected from your main claim about whether crime "pays." Either develop this idea fully as part of your argument or remove it to maintain focus.

Language and Grammar

A few recurring issues to review:

"The persons must be patient and will to deal with others" → should be "willing to deal."
"whomever coined this term" → should be "whoever," since it's the subject of the verb "coined."
Watch for run-on or fragment sentences, such as "Crime of passion; heat of the moment" — this is not a complete grammatical unit.
Some verb tenses shift inconsistently between past and present when describing general truths versus specific experiences. Keep general claims in present tense ("organized crime exists...") and personal experience in past tense.

Overall Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs around points of comparison rather than by "type" of crime alone.
Add citations for statistics and course-based facts.
Remove unsupported generalizations or add evidence for them.
Revise tone: eliminate all-caps, colloquialisms, and direct reader address.
Write a conclusion that returns to and reinforces your thesis; delete the closing autobiographical paragraph.

You have a genuinely interesting angle here — the contrast between "petty" and "organized" crime in terms of profitability and impunity is compelling. With clearer structure, more precise evidence, and a more formal tone, this essay can become a strong piece of argumentative writing.

Feedback Report: Narrative Essay on Crime and Justice

Overall Impression

This is a compelling personal narrative with a clear emotional arc and a strong thematic thread ("crime doesn't pay") that ties the beginning and end together effectively. You've chosen a topic that matters to you, and that investment shows in the vividness of certain details (eg, "everything, I mean everything, had been taken"). Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the essay's clarity, coherence, and argumentative power.

Strengths to Build On

Framing device: Opening and closing with your father's saying "crime doesn't pay" gives the essay a satisfying circular structure. This is a sophisticated technique—keep it.
Chronological clarity: The reader can follow the timeline of events without confusion.
Emotional honesty: Your fear, frustration, and eventual relief come through authentically, especially in the passage about feeling "punished" as a victim.

Areas for Revision

Clarify the Thesis/Argument
This is described as an *argumentative* essay, but right now it reads primarily as a narrative. Ask yourself: what is the actual claim you want to prove? Possible candidates:
Victims deserve a voice in plea bargain decisions.
The justice system often fails victims unless they actively advocate for themselves.
"Crime doesn't pay" is only true when the system is held accountable.

Whichever claim you choose, make it explicit—ideally in your introduction and again in your conclusion. Right now, the argument is implied through the story rather than stated directly. Consider adding a sentence like: "This experience taught me that justice is not automatic—victims must sometimes fight for it themselves." This would also justify why you're telling us this story.

Fix the Opening Sentence
The sentence "I always believed them and they saying that " contains a placeholder and a grammar error. Revise to something like: "I always believed them, and their words stayed with me." Also correct "the stealing was wrong" to "that stealing was wrong."

Grammar and Verb Tense Issues
Several recurring errors need attention:
"husbands firearms" → "husband's firearms" (possessive apostrophe)
"they thieves were getting away with a crime that they had no apparent reason to commit" — awkward phrasing; consider: "thieves were getting away with a crime, and I could think of no reason why they should."
"that these people did not have permission" — fine, but check subject-verb agreement throughout: "prosecutors office told us" → "the prosecutor's office told us"
"two men and one women" → "two men and one woman"
"I kept remembering what my father told be" → "told me"
"For whatever money they received... was not worth" — subject-verb agreement: "were not worth"

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or asking someone to read it to you, to catch these small errors.

Strengthen the Middle Section (Court Process)
This is where your *argument* should really emerge, since it's the turning point of the story. Currently, you summarize the plea bargain conflict quickly. Consider expanding this section with more specific details:
What exactly did you say to the prosecutor's office? A brief quote or paraphrase of your argument would strengthen your credibility as a persuasive figure in your own story.
Why do you believe plea bargains often fail victims? This is your chance to generalize beyond your personal experience toward a broader claim about the justice system.

Conclusion Needs More Analytical Weight
Right now, the conclusion mostly restates the outcome. Since this is an argumentative essay, use the conclusion to explicitly state what you want readers to *take away* and *do* with this information. For example: should victims speak up more? Should prosecutors consult victims before offering plea deals? This is where your essay can move from "here's what happened to me" to "here's what should change."

Minor Structural Suggestion
Consider breaking the essay into clearer sections with transitional phrases indicating time jumps ("Two weeks later," "After two months," etc)—you already do this in places, but making it more consistent will help readability.

Summary of Priority Fixes
Add an explicit thesis statement.
Fix grammar issues (possessives, subject-verb agreement, "woman" vs. "women").
Expand the court/prosecutor section with more argumentative substance.
Revise the conclusion to include a clear takeaway or call to action.

You have a strong story—now it's about sharpening it into a clear argument. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay. You clearly have strong opinions about feminism and write with passion and confidence. Below are some observations to help you strengthen the argument, structure, and language for a revision.

Thesis and Argument Development

Your central claim—that "feminism" has been misused and that modern radicals have hijacked a movement originally about equality—is a debatable, interesting thesis. However, it gets buried. The essay opens by explaining *why* the statement exists (implying you're responding to a prompt you haven't shown us), but you never clearly state the prompt or your own thesis in one crisp sentence. Consider opening with a direct claim: something like, "While the feminist movement's history is rooted in a legitimate demand for equality, its modern image has been distorted by a vocal minority, giving the movement's critics undeserved ammunition." This would give your reader a roadmap.

Additionally, your argument leans heavily on assertion rather than evidence. Phrases like "many people see the feminist movement moving in the wrong direction" or "many radical women have used it as an umbrella" are unsupported generalizations. Who are these people? Can you cite specific examples, statistics, or named figures/organizations (as you did with Susan B. Anthony) to illustrate the "radical" wing you're critiquing? Right now the essay relies on impressions rather than evidence, which weakens its persuasive power.

Stereotyping and Tone

This is the most important issue to address. The line "The concept many people today have of a 'feminist' is that of a big chunky lesbian who hates all men" is a harmful stereotype, and even though you say "this is hardly the case," repeating the stereotype without deeper critique can undermine your credibility and alienate readers. Academic argument benefits from *examining* a stereotype's origins and consequences rather than restating it colorfully. Consider replacing this with a more analytical description: eg, "Popular media often portrays feminists through reductive stereotypes that ignore the movement's diversity of goals and identities."

Similarly, phrases like "anarchists" and "obstinate in their belief that all men are evil" characterize an unnamed group in extreme terms. Since your broader point is that feminism has been unfairly caricatured, be careful not to caricature "radical feminists" in the same way. Try to distinguish between specific actions/arguments you disagree with (eg, a particular policy stance, a particular public statement) rather than describing "radical women" as a monolithic, irrational group.

Historical Accuracy

You attribute the founding of the feminist movement solely to Susan B. Anthony's belief in equality. This is a reasonable reference, but the feminist movement is not monolithic or single-founder; it has multiple waves (suffrage, 1960s-70s second wave, third wave, contemporary intersectional feminism) with different goals and internal debates. Acknowledging this complexity would strengthen your credibility and let you make a more precise argument: perhaps that *some* strands of contemporary feminism diverge from *some* historical goals, rather than suggesting the whole movement has strayed from a single, unified original purpose.

Structure and Cohesion

Each paragraph makes a reasonable point, but transitions between them are abrupt. For example, the jump from discussing Susan B. Anthony to describing modern stereotypes needs a bridging sentence explaining *how* we got from one to the other. Consider using topic sentences that explicitly connect back to your thesis, and transitional phrases ("This shift became visible when...", "As a result of this reframing...") to guide the reader.

Your conclusion effectively returns to your opening idea ("groups grow and change"), which is good, but it introduces new claims (feminists "becoming anarchists") without support, which should appear earlier with evidence, not as a late assertion.

Language and Mechanics

A few specific corrections:
"by this I do not mean" → capitalize "By"
"A women is just as much a citizen" → "A woman is..."
"which it seems like now" → awkward phrasing; consider "which it now seems to do"
Watch subject-verb agreement and run-on sentences, eg, the long sentence beginning "If women can shout..." would benefit from being split for clarity.
Avoid casual phrases like "home free" and "big chunky" in formal argumentative writing; academic register calls for more neutral diction.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
State your thesis explicitly and early.
Replace unsupported generalizations with specific examples or citations.
Remove or reframe the stereotype about feminists' appearance/sexuality.
Acknowledge the movement's historical diversity rather than treating it as monolithic.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs.
Proofread for grammar (capitalization, subject-verb agreement, sentence length).

You have a genuine, thoughtful perspective here—refining the evidence and tone will make it much more persuasive to a skeptical academic reader.

Feedback Report on Draft Essay: Women's Liberation Movement

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and debatable thesis: that the failures of feminist movements stem not from radical activists themselves, but from how outside observers perceive and remember them. This is a promising argumentative angle. However, the essay needs stronger organization, more precise evidence, and tighter logical connections between your claims and examples. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your thesis appears in paragraph 3 ("I contend that the fault does not lie with the radical members... but rather, in the perception of those observing the movement"), but it would benefit from being stated earlier and more emphatically—ideally by the end of your first paragraph. As it stands, readers must wait through two paragraphs of historical example before understanding your actual claim. Consider restructuring so your introduction ends with a clear thesis statement that readers can use as a roadmap.

Historical Evidence Needs Verification and Clarification

This is the essay's weakest point. Several factual and logical issues need attention:

Emily Dickinson is not conventionally classified as a feminist or part of the Women's Liberation Movement; she was a poet who lived a reclusive life. Including her as a "less aggressive feminist" without explanation weakens your credibility. If you have a specific argument for why she belongs in this category, you need to state it explicitly.
George Sand is flagged parenthetically as French, which raises the question of why she's used as evidence for the *American* movement you're discussing. Either justify her inclusion (perhaps as an international influence) or replace her with a clearer American example.
"Stein" is never given a first name or context. Readers will assume Gertrude Stein, but her connection to 1960s Women's Lib is unclear and requires explanation.
Dates are inconsistent: you mention "late nineteenth century" origins, then jump to "late 1960s" resurgence, then claim this is "still true twenty-five years later," which suggests you're writing from the 1990s. Make sure your timeline is coherent and that each example is chronologically and factually anchored.

Suggestion: For each historical figure, briefly explain *why* she fits your argument (radical vs. ordinary) and cite the connection explicitly. Vague name-dropping undermines your argument's persuasiveness.

Paragraph Structure and Organization

Several paragraphs are extremely short, particularly:
"So what am I getting at? Only that so-called feminists have not in and of themselves harmed the Women's movement."
"However, the popular perception of them has."

These one- or two-sentence paragraphs feel abrupt and underdeveloped. Consider merging them into a single, fuller paragraph that restates your thesis with more elaboration and perhaps a brief preview of your conclusion's implications.

Additionally, the essay would benefit from clearer transitions between paragraphs. Right now, paragraphs often begin with a time marker ("When Women's Lib really took off again...") without connecting back to the previous point. Try using transitional phrases that explicitly link cause and effect (eg, "This same pattern reappears in the 1960s, when...").

Logical Gaps

You assert that "radicals" caused the movement to be perceived negatively, but then argue the "fault" lies with observers, not radicals. This is a subtle but important distinction—make sure you're consistently arguing that the *perception* is the problem, not the radicals' existence or actions. Right now, some sentences (eg, "were noticed and ridiculed, thus defeating their cause, because of their aggressive action") suggest the radicals' behavior *caused* the failure, which contradicts your thesis. Revise these sentences to align with your argument: it's not that their actions were wrong, but that the audience chose to focus on them unfairly.

Grammar and Mechanics

Several errors need correction:
"Whenever a movement is began" → "is begun" or "begins"
"oppostion" → "opposition"
"therfore" → "therefore"
"readiliy" → "readily"
"the public it was being aimed" → "the public to whom it was being aimed" or "the public at whom it was aimed"
Watch subject-verb agreement and article usage throughout (eg, "and and other" is a typo).

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller errors before submission.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph restates the thesis but doesn't push the argument forward with any broader implication or call to action. Consider ending with a stronger statement about what should change—should the public reconsider how movements are historically remembered? Should activists change strategy? Give your reader something to think about beyond simple restatement.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
State thesis clearly and early.
Verify and clarify all historical references.
Merge underdeveloped paragraphs into fuller arguments.
Ensure examples logically support (not contradict) your thesis.
Proofread carefully for grammar and spelling.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here—focus your revision on clarity, evidence, and logical consistency, and this essay will be much stronger.

For your next draft, I'd recommend:

Fill in the missing sentence marked by —this is essential content.
Align your introduction and conclusion—either commit to the global comparison or narrow your focus to America alone.
Add specific evidence (statistics, dates, examples) to support generalized claims about progress.
Develop the counterargument about resentment toward feminists rather than mentioning it in passing.
Revise the conclusion so that it synthesizes your argument rather than simply asserting an opinion.

You have a solid foundation and clear command of the subject matter—now the task is to tighten the argument's logic and back up your claims with more concrete detail. I look forward to seeing your revision.

Argument Development

Your reasoning that "if money were the root of all evil, we would all be evil" is a strong logical move—keep it, but explain it more fully. You could strengthen this by explicitly naming the logical fallacy you're identifying (a hasty generalization, or a fallacy of composition: attributing evil to money itself rather than to how it's used).

The paragraph about agencies and donations is a good example, but it feels somewhat disconnected from your central argument. Make the connection explicit: explain *why* this example proves your thesis (ie, money used well produces good; therefore money itself cannot be evil).

Your conclusion introduces a new claim—that choice, not money, is the root of evil—but this deserves more development earlier in the essay. Right now it arrives somewhat abruptly. Consider dedicating a paragraph to explaining *why* choice, rather than money or other external factors (attitudes, greed, lack of morals), is the more fundamental cause. You could argue that greed and lack of morals are themselves the result of choices, which would tie your body paragraphs together more tightly.

Structure and Transitions

Your paragraphs move logically, but transitions between them could be smoother. For example, the paragraph beginning "Other things as well can be the root of evil" could open with a transition like: "Beyond money, several other factors can lead to evil actions." This helps guide the reader through your reasoning.

Consider also combining or reordering paragraphs 3 and 4, since both discuss how money-related behaviors (lying, cheating, greed) stem from deeper issues. This would help avoid repetition and tighten your argument.

Grammar and Word Choice

"I wold like" → "I would like" (typo)
"since the original subject is " → This needs to be a complete, specific sentence (eg, "since the original subject is *money*, not the love of money")
"Most money is attained in honest ways... how can honestly be associated with evil?" → "honestly" should be "honesty" (noun form needed here)
"If money was the root of all evil" → In formal writing, use the subjunctive: "If money *were* the root of all evil" (you use this correctly in your first paragraph—be consistent throughout)
"worth-while causes are sometimes being used" → Consider revising to "are sometimes used" for more natural phrasing
"will he choose to be morally decent" → Consider gender-neutral language throughout the essay (eg, "will a person choose...")

Style Suggestions

Avoid starting too many sentences with "If" — vary your sentence openings for better flow.
The phrase "lucrative means" is slightly imprecise; "lucrative" describes something profitable, but you're really listing *legitimate* or *legal* means of earning money. Consider "legitimate means" instead.
Your conclusion is strong as a rhetorical question, but consider adding one more sentence that restates your main argument explicitly before ending on the question, so the essay feels fully resolved.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Fix the placeholder/unclear sentence in paragraph 1 and state your thesis explicitly.
Strengthen the logical connection between your discussion of money and your conclusion about individual choice.
Proofread carefully for the specific grammar issues noted above.
Improve transitions between paragraphs to unify the argument.

With these revisions, your essay will have a much clearer argumentative thread and greater persuasive power. Good work overall—keep refining!

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this thought-provoking piece on money and power. You clearly have strong opinions and interesting ideas, but the essay needs significant revision in terms of organization, clarity, and language to communicate your argument effectively. Below is feedback organized by area.

Missing Context and Unclear Thesis

The biggest structural problem is that you never state the saying you are responding to. You write "It's difficult to justify this saying," but the reader has no idea what "this saying" is. Since this is an argumentative essay responding to a specific quotation, you must state it explicitly in your introduction (eg, "Money is the root of all evil" or whatever prompt you were given).

Additionally, your thesis shifts throughout the essay. Paragraph 1 argues money is necessary and powerful. Paragraph 2 pivots to a *different* saying about power corrupting. Paragraph 3 argues about the value of hard-earned money. Paragraph 4 introduces a master/slave metaphor. Paragraph 5 concludes with "money is for us to use, not for it to use us." Each of these is a reasonable idea, but together they read as four separate essays rather than one unified argument. Choose one central claim and make every paragraph support it. Based on your conclusion, it seems your real thesis is: "Money itself is neutral—its moral value depends on how people use it." If that's your argument, restructure the whole essay around it from the introduction onward.

Paragraph 2: Off-Topic Drift

This paragraph about "absolute power corrupts absolutely" doesn't connect clearly to money. Power and money are related, but you need a transition sentence explaining *why* you're discussing power here—perhaps "Money is a form of power, and just as power corrupts, money can corrupt those who acquire it without restraint." Without this bridge, the paragraph feels like a tangent.

Logical Gaps

Paragraph 1: You assert money is essential for survival, but this doesn't yet address whether money is "evil" or "the root of evil"—it only shows money is *necessary*. These are different claims. Clarify which one you're making.
Paragraph 3: The claim that hard-earned money is spent more wisely is an assertion without support. Consider adding an example (a personal anecdote, a common observation, or a hypothetical scenario) to make this persuasive.
Paragraph 4: The distinction between people who "live to use money" and those who "use money to live" is compelling but underdeveloped. Give a concrete example of each type of person to make the contrast vivid.

Organization: Suggested Structure

Consider this revised structure:
Introduction: State the saying you're responding to, and your thesis (money itself is not evil; its effect depends on how people use it).
Body 1: Money is a practical necessity in modern society (your current paragraph 1—but connect it to your thesis).
Body 2: The danger is not money itself, but the *pursuit of it without limits* (link to your power-corrupts idea here).
Body 3: How money is earned/used shapes its impact (hard-earned vs. easy money; master vs. slave metaphor).
Conclusion: Return explicitly to the original saying and explain how your argument agrees, disagrees, or complicates it.

Language and Mechanics

There are several spelling and grammar issues to fix:
"currupts" → corrupts
"sence" → sense
"cicle" → cycle
"begats" → begets (though even "begets" may be unusual here; consider "creates" or "produces")
"sucess" → success
"indespencable" → indispensable
"ones" → one's (possessive apostrophe needed throughout)
"somewhere to sleep" (currently "some where")
"Others rate success one heir more substantive accomplishments" — this sentence is unclear; I believe you mean something like "Others rate success on more substantive accomplishments." Please revise this sentence for clarity.
Watch subject-verb agreement: "This begats the will" → "This begets the will"

I recommend using a spell-checker and reading your essay aloud to catch these errors before submitting.

Strengths to Build On

Your master/slave metaphor for money in paragraph 4 is genuinely compelling and could be the emotional core of your essay if developed further. Your concluding paragraph is strong and clear—consider using its central idea ("money is for us to use, not for it to use us") as your thesis statement from the very beginning, then building every paragraph toward proving it.

Summary of Priority Revisions
State the saying you're analyzing in your introduction.
Choose one clear thesis and revise all paragraphs to support it.
Add transitions between paragraphs, especially before the power/corruption section.

Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic, and you develop a genuine argument with a clear personal stance at the end. The core idea—that money itself is neutral and it is human choice that determines whether it leads to good or evil—is a strong, defensible thesis. However, the essay would benefit from clearer organization, stronger evidence, and more precise language. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

---

Thesis and Structure

Right now, your main argument (money is not evil; how it is used determines outcome) does not appear until the final two paragraphs. Readers spend most of the essay assuming you *agree* that pursuing money leads to evil, then you reverse course. This is a valid rhetorical strategy (present one view, then complicate it), but you need to signal it explicitly. Consider:

Stating your thesis clearly in the introduction: something like, "While money is often blamed for corrupting people, I will argue that money itself is neutral—it is human choice and desire that create evil."
Adding a transition sentence before paragraph 5, such as "However, money itself cannot be blamed..." This tells the reader you are shifting from explaining *why people say* this to *refuting* it.

Also, the opening paragraph uses a placeholder () instead of stating the actual saying you are analyzing (presumably "money is the root of all evil"). Make sure to include the actual quotation, and even better, consider mentioning that the original biblical phrase is "the *love* of money is the root of all evil" (1 Timothy 6:10)—this distinction is central to your argument and would strengthen your credibility.

---

Development and Evidence

Your paragraphs about the dangers of money (paragraphs 2–4) rely on general claims—gambling, neglecting family, chasing "the next dollar"—but no concrete examples, statistics, or references support these claims. Academic essays are strengthened by specific evidence: a real or hypothetical case study, a statistic about gambling addiction, or a reference to a philosophical or religious text.

Similarly, in paragraphs 5–6, your claims about money helping the homeless or funding education are generalized. Consider adding a specific example (a charity, a historical case, a statistic) to make your argument more persuasive and academic in tone.

Also reconsider relying solely on a Christian framework to define "evil" and "good." Since this is a general argumentative essay (not specifically religious studies), you might briefly acknowledge that not all readers share this framework, or explicitly frame it as "from a Christian perspective..." This will make your argument feel more balanced and aware of audience.

---

Language and Grammar

There are several recurring errors worth reviewing:

Capitalization: "christian" → should be "Christian" (proper adjective).
Spelling: "subservant" → *subservient*; "mear" → *mere*.
Comparative structure: "you always want more that you have" → *more than you have*.
Verb agreement/tense: "Your family and friends will become unimportant and you can become consumed" — mixing "will" and "can" creates inconsistency; choose one modal and stay consistent (eg, "will become... will consume you").
Word choice: "provide opportunities to educated the uneducated" → *to educate uneducated people* (avoid awkward repetition of "educate/uneducated").
Repetition: The word "money" appears very frequently. Try substituting synonyms occasionally—*wealth, finances, riches, funds*—to improve style.
Contractions/casual tone: "It's all in how it is used and how you allow it to use you!" is a strong closing line, but the exclamation point and informal phrasing may feel too casual for an academic essay. Consider rephrasing slightly more formally while keeping the rhetorical force, eg, "Ultimately, it is not money that is evil, but how one chooses to use it—or allows it to be used."

---

Paragraph Cohesion

Each paragraph currently functions somewhat independently. Add topic sentences that connect back to your thesis, and use transitional phrases ("In contrast," "On the other hand," "This suggests that...") to guide the reader through your reasoning. This will make the shift from "money can lead to evil behavior" to "money itself is neutral" feel like a deliberate argumentative move rather than a contradiction.

---

Summary of Priority Revisions

State your thesis clearly and early.

Correct grammar and spelling issues noted above.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a good first draft with an interesting idea to a well-organized, persuasive academic argument. Keep working on it—the core insight is genuinely strong!

Feedback on "Crime Does Pay"

Thank you for sharing this provocative essay. You've chosen a bold thesis and support it with concrete comparisons, which shows creativity. However, the essay needs strengthening in several key areas: argument logic, evidence, counterargument, and language accuracy. Below are detailed suggestions.

Argument and Logic

Your central comparison—criminal income vs. legal wages—is interesting, but the reasoning has some serious gaps:

Unrealistic assumptions: You assume criminals always find work, are never caught, and always earn $300/week or $300/hour consistently. In reality, crime carries major risks: arrest, imprisonment, injury, or death. A strong argumentative essay must acknowledge these risks and explain why the benefits you describe would still outweigh them. Right now, the essay reads as if crime has *no* downside, which weakens your credibility.

Math inconsistency: In your final paragraph, you first say a criminal earns "$300.00 per hour" but earlier you said $300 per *week*. This creates a $1,170,000 figure that doesn't add up under either interpretation. Please recalculate carefully and be consistent. Errors like this can undermine the reader's trust in your entire argument.

Lack of a counterargument: A persuasive essay is stronger when it addresses the opposing view before dismissing it. For example, you might acknowledge: "Some would argue that criminals risk imprisonment, violence, or death, losing everything they've earned." Then explain why, in your view, the potential rewards still make crime worthwhile. This "concession and rebuttal" structure will make your argument feel more balanced and convincing, rather than one-sided.

Source of evidence: Where do your statistics (wages, tax deductions, criminal earnings) come from? Right now they appear to be invented or assumed. Even in an argumentative essay, using a real source (news article, government wage data, criminology study) for at least some numbers would greatly strengthen your credibility.

Organization

Your structure is basically sound: introduction with thesis → three points of comparison (job security, daily wages, retirement) → conclusion. To improve clarity:

Each body paragraph should open with a clear topic sentence stating the point of comparison (eg, "The first difference between criminal and legal work is job security.") Right now, paragraphs open with transitional fragments like "Then if we think about..." which read as unfinished sentences rather than topic sentences.

The phrase "And finally, the one thing the criminal does have in common with the average worker" is a good transition, but starting a paragraph with "And" is a stylistic weakness. Consider: "Finally, both criminals and workers must eventually retire, though the paths differ dramatically."

Add a short paragraph addressing risks/counterarguments before your conclusion. This will make the ending "Comparing both options, I'd definitely say crime does pay" feel more earned.

Language and Grammar

You have some recurring errors worth correcting:

"it's advantages" → should be "its advantages" (its = possessive; it's = it is)
"rather or not" → should be "whether or not"
"There is never a lack of work are a means for continuing employment" — this sentence is unclear; consider: "There is never a lack of work or a shortage of means for continued employment," or simplify to "Criminals never run out of opportunities to work."
"$300.00 per week before taxes" — check consistency with later "$300.00 per hour"
"invest half this money and earns interest they could potentially live" — subject-verb agreement: "invests," and consider smoothing to "If he or she invests half of this money and earns interest, they could potentially live on the interest income alone."
Watch for sentence fragments, especially at paragraph openings ("Then if we think about the work day...").

Suggestions for Revision

Add a counterargument paragraph: Address the risks of crime (arrest, violence, loss of freedom) and explain why you still believe the financial upside outweighs them.
Verify your math: Recalculate the retirement example so the numbers are internally consistent.
Cite at least one real source for wage or crime statistics to add credibility.
Revise topic sentences so each paragraph opens with a complete, clear statement of its main point.
Proofread for grammar, especially "its/it's," subject-verb agreement, and sentence fragments.

Final Note

Your essay has an engaging, controversial thesis, and your comparative structure (job security, wages, retirement) is a smart way to organize your points. With stronger evidence, corrected math, and a fair treatment of counterarguments, this essay could become a much more persuasive and academically rigorous piece. Keep developing your voice—it's clearly your own—but back it with careful reasoning and accurate data.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a provocative and socially significant topic—the economic logic that may drive some youth toward crime. You write with confidence and have a clear thesis ("crime pays") that you return to consistently. However, the essay would benefit from significant revision in several areas: evidence and credibility, logical structure, nuance, and mechanics.

Evidence and Credibility

This is the most pressing issue. Your argument relies entirely on personal observation ("drive down any street," "you will see") and unsupported assertions ("the shockingly low percentage of criminals who actually end up in jail"). For an academic argumentative essay, you need verifiable evidence: crime statistics, incarceration rates, studies on recidivism, economic research on urban poverty, or sociological studies on youth and crime. Without this, your claims—however plausible—read as anecdotal generalization rather than argument. Consider: What percentage of criminals actually avoid jail? What does research say about the relationship between poverty and crime? Citing sources (even 2-3 solid ones) would transform this from an opinion piece into a persuasive academic essay.

Overgeneralization and Stereotyping

Be cautious with claims like "many youths are well aware that crime pays" or descriptions of "young males and females, both black and white" driving luxury cars. These sweeping statements risk essentializing entire demographics and can undermine your credibility with readers who see them as stereotypes rather than argument. You also assume all luxury cars/clothing are crime-funded, which is a logical leap—some youth may have jobs, family wealth, or credit. Acknowledge alternative explanations, then argue why crime remains the more common or compelling one *based on evidence*.

Logical Gaps

Several claims need stronger causal reasoning:
You assert "the only danger for a criminal comes from other criminals," but this ignores violence victims face, health risks, family destruction, etc
The move from "police are underfunded" to "crime will continue to pay" needs a clearer logical bridge—perhaps statistics on clearance rates or funding trends over time.
Your point about jail space being reserved for violent offenders is a real policy issue (worth developing!) but currently rests on assertion, not data.

Consider the Counterargument

A stronger essay would acknowledge the human costs of the "crime pays" logic: incarceration's long-term effects on employment, family instability, violence, addiction, death. Right now, your essay almost reads as an endorsement of crime as rational strategy without weighing the risks and costs sufficiently. Even one paragraph engaging with the downsides (imprisonment's collateral damage, violence, lack of upward mobility over the long term) would make your argument feel more balanced and intellectually honest—and paradoxically more persuasive, because readers trust writers who show they've considered the other side.

Tone and Word Choice

Some phrasing undercuts your argument's seriousness:
"Rolling wads of money into their designer jeans" is vivid but overly casual/stylized for academic writing.
"The philosophy is simple: if you want it, go get it—at any cost" sounds like a slogan rather than analysis.
Brand-name lists (Karl Kani, Pele Pele, Cartier) may date the essay and feel more like cultural commentary than argument. Consider whether these details serve your thesis or distract from it.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few errors to correct:
"come from other criminals" → "comes from"
"wether" → "whether"
"no en in sight" → "no end in sight"
Extra period after "hire them. ."
"you are forced to work in a hot, cramped atmosphere, possibly with an overbearing boss who is stuck in a service job" — this sentence is a run-on; consider breaking it up.
Watch subject-verb agreement throughout ("he or she is often surrounded"—consistent, but check other instances).

Suggestions for Revision

Add 2-3 credible sources (studies, statistics) to support your central claims.
Narrow your focus—perhaps concentrate on one specific issue (eg, sentencing disparities for non-violent crime) rather than covering cars, clothing, policing, and jail capacity all at once.
Add a counterargument paragraph acknowledging the risks/costs of crime, then explain why you still find your thesis compelling.
Revise the conclusion to make a clearer call to action or policy implication—what should change, given your argument?

Overall: You have a strong voice and a bold, debatable thesis—qualities that matter in argumentative writing. Your next draft should focus on grounding your claims in evidence and demonstrating awareness of counterarguments, which will make your essay far more persuasive to an academic audience.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of Computers on Society

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and debatable topic, and your "catch-22" framing (easier physically, harder mentally) is a promising organizing principle. However, the essay would benefit from tighter logical structure, more concrete evidence, and revision of some claims that currently contradict themselves. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Logical Consistency

Your thesis states that computers make life "easier physically" and "harder mentally," but the body paragraphs don't fully support this dichotomy:

In your "physical" paragraph, you mention that computers have *eliminated jobs* — this seems like a negative, not evidence that life is "easier." Job loss appears again in your second "mental" section (reliability causing unemployment). Consider: is job automation a physical effect or a mental/emotional one? Right now it appears in both places, weakening the clarity of your framework.
Your first "mental" point argues that computer errors stem from human ignorance, but you also say "it is the person who told the computer what to do that truly made the error." This significantly undercuts your claim that computers make life "harder mentally" — if the error is really human error, what exactly is the computer's role? Clarify this: are you arguing that computers *enable* people to make more consequential errors by allowing them to bypass genuine understanding? That's actually a strong, specific claim — make it explicit rather than hedging.

Suggestion: Before revising, write a one-sentence version of your thesis and test each paragraph against it. Ask: "Does this paragraph directly support that sentence?"

Evidence and Development

Right now, most claims are asserted rather than demonstrated. Phrases like "computers have made office work easier and faster" or "mathematical models are absolutely riddled with errors" need support:

Add concrete examples. Instead of a general claim about office work, describe a specific process (eg, how spreadsheet software changed accounting reconciliation) and explain *why* it's faster.
Support the errors claim. You assert that math models are "riddled with errors" — can you cite even one example (a financial model, an engineering failure, a famous programming bug)? Even a hypothetical, clearly-marked illustration would strengthen this.
Avoid unsupported universal claims. "Everyone already knows... how abundant computers are" and "computers make errors all the time" are sweeping statements that a skeptical reader will question. Qualify them or provide evidence.

Paragraph Organization

The "mental effects" section covers two fairly different ideas (loss of mathematical understanding; job displacement causing psychological distress) under one heading. These could be split into two clearer, better-developed paragraphs, especially since the second point (unemployment/emotional response) overlaps with material already introduced in your first paragraph. Consider reorganizing as:

Physical/practical benefits (with concrete workplace examples)
Loss of genuine understanding due to over-reliance on computers (your strongest, most original point — expand this)
Social/psychological effects of automation-driven unemployment (a distinct issue, deserving its own space, not conflated with "mental" errors)

Sensitive Language

The parenthetical "(except for people in 2nd & 3rd world countries)" is dismissive and potentially offensive in academic writing. If you want to make a point about unequal global access to technology, it deserves a full sentence with reasoning or a source — not a parenthetical aside. Otherwise, remove it.

Sentence-Level Issues

Run-ons/comma splices: "The computer has eliminated many jobs through automation of factories; accounting, engineering, and other scientific programs, etc" — this sentence is unclear structurally. Are accounting and engineering *programs* separate from "factories," or examples of automation? Revise for clarity.
Awkward phrasing: "if that person knew what he should do, or better stated 'was able to do with a computer'" is confusing — simplify: "if that person had a deeper understanding of the underlying mathematics, rather than simply knowing which buttons to press."
Word choice: "whether computers are good or bad" is preferable to "if computers are good or bad" in your final line.
Watch pronoun consistency (use "they" or specify gender-neutral phrasing instead of defaulting to "he").

Conclusion

Your proposed solutions (better math education, curtailing "theoretical programming") appear suddenly without groundwork earlier in the essay. If these are your recommended solutions, foreshadow them briefly in the introduction or body so the conclusion feels earned rather than introduced from nowhere.

Priority for Revision

Clarify and test your central thesis against each paragraph
Add concrete examples/evidence throughout
Resolve the self-undermining logic in the errors paragraph
Remove or properly contextualize the "2nd & 3rd world" comment
Proofread for run-ons and awkward phrasing

Good luck with your revision — the core argument here is thought-provoking and worth refining!

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and relevant topic—the dual impact of computers on society—and you clearly have genuine opinions about this issue. The structure (positive effects, then negative effects) is logical and easy to follow. However, the essay would benefit from stronger organization, more developed arguments, and attention to grammar and word choice. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Content and Argumentation

Strengths: You identify a real tension—efficiency versus human connection—and this is a thoughtful angle. Your examples (ATMs, car sensors) are concrete and relatable.

Areas for improvement:

Thesis clarity: Your introduction states that computers have been "both bad and good," but this is quite general. Consider sharpening your thesis to preview *specifically* what aspects you'll discuss (eg, efficiency vs. human interaction, convenience vs. over-reliance). This gives readers a clearer roadmap.

Development of ideas: Several claims need more support or explanation:
   - "Jobs are being taken away from humans" — Can you give a specific example or brief explanation of *how* this happens?
   - "Computers are not able to express and truly feel emotions" — This is a significant claim. Why does this matter? Connect it more explicitly to your argument about impersonalization.
   - The final paragraph mentions "advances in medical technology" and "safety" very briefly. Since these are important counterpoints, they deserve at least a sentence of explanation rather than being tacked on as an afterthought.

Conclusion: Your final paragraph tries to do two jobs at once—summarizing and introducing new ideas (medical technology). Consider separating these: end the body paragraphs with all your points, then use the conclusion purely to synthesize and offer your final reflection or call to action.

Structure

Consider adding a clear transition sentence between your two body paragraphs to signal the shift from positive to negative effects (eg, "However, despite these benefits, computers have also created significant drawbacks.")
Each paragraph could be strengthened with a clearer topic sentence that states the paragraph's main claim before diving into examples.

Language and Grammar

You have some recurring errors worth addressing:

Spelling: "Interraction" → *interaction*; "obstical" → *obstacle*
Subject-verb agreement: "computers have also made live transpire" → likely meant "life"; "life styles" should probably be one word: *lifestyles*
Article usage: "We now have ATM" → "We now have ATMs" (plural needs the -s, and consider whether "the" is needed: "the ATM")
Sentence fragments: "Not to mention how it will change people's life styles in the future." This is a fragment—it lacks a main clause. Consider integrating it into the previous sentence or rewriting it as a complete sentence.
Comma splices/awkward punctuation: "These changes resulting from the invention of the computer, have been, I feel both bad and good." The commas here are overused and disrupt the flow. Try: "I feel these changes, resulting from the invention of the computer, have been both good and bad."
Word choice: "proceed with the same transactions" should likely be *conduct* or *process* transactions. "Interraction with humans in some places is extinct" — *extinct* is unusual here; perhaps *rare* or *nonexistent* fits better.
Capitalization for emphasis: Using "EVERYTHING" in all caps is informal for academic writing. Consider using italics sparingly, or simply strengthen your word choice (eg, "nearly everything") without relying on capitalization for emphasis.

Style

Avoid starting sentences with informal transitions like "Sure," and "Yes," in academic writing. These work in conversation but weaken the formality of an argumentative essay. Try alternatives like "Admittedly," or "While it is true that..."
The essay would benefit from more varied sentence openings—several sentences begin with "Computers have..." or similar constructions. Try varying your syntax for a more sophisticated style.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to be more specific about the two sides of your argument.
Add 1-2 concrete examples to strengthen the jobs/automation point.
Proofread carefully for spelling and article usage (a/an/the).
Separate your conclusion's new ideas (medical technology) into the body paragraph where they logically belong, then write a cleaner concluding paragraph.
Read your essay aloud—this will help you catch run-on sentences and awkward phrasing.

Final Note

You have good instincts for identifying a meaningful debate and organizing pro/con points. With more attention to grammar mechanics and slightly deeper development of your examples, this essay will be much stronger. Keep writing—your ideas are clearly there; now let's polish the delivery!

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a meaningful and important topic, and your passion for human equality and peace comes through clearly. Your essay has a strong ethical core, but it needs work in several areas: thesis clarity, organization, evidence/examples, and grammar/mechanics. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your introduction states that the "most significant discovery" is subjective and depends on the individual—but then you don't clearly argue why *unlearning prejudice* qualifies as a "discovery" in the way other 20th-century inventions (like antibiotics or the internet) do. This is actually an interesting and debatable claim, but you need to make this argument explicit. Consider revising your thesis to state directly: something like, "I will argue that the ongoing societal shift toward recognizing and unlearning prejudice constitutes one of the most significant 'discoveries' of the 20th century, because it fundamentally reshapes how humans relate to one another."

Right now, the essay describes *that* society is changing regarding discrimination, but it doesn't strongly defend *why this counts as a discovery* or *why it's more significant than other candidates*. Since your assignment seems to ask you to argue for significance, spend more time justifying this comparison.

Development and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay makes many claims but offers no concrete evidence, examples, or specific references:

*Which* discoveries are you comparing this to? Name 1-2 alternatives (eg, the internet, penicillin, nuclear technology) so readers understand your point of comparison.
*What* organizations or movements are you referring to when you say "organizations and forms are presently being held"? Can you name a specific example (a UN initiative, a civil rights movement, a specific study)?
*What* research supports the claim that "prejudice and discrimination are not consciously learned but must be unlearned"? This is a specific psychological/sociological claim—citing even one source (a psychologist, a study, a historical event) would strengthen it considerably.

Right now, the essay operates at a high level of abstraction. Adding even 2-3 concrete examples would make your argument much more persuasive.

Organization

Your three paragraphs move from (1) introduction/thesis, (2) historical context of discrimination and current progress, (3) brief conclusion. Consider a clearer structure:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly.
Body Paragraph 1: Explain the history/nature of the problem (discrimination as learned behavior).
Body Paragraph 2: Provide specific evidence of society "unlearning" this—name examples.
Body Paragraph 3: Explain *why* this matters more than other discoveries, or at least why it's comparably significant.
Conclusion: Synthesize your argument.

This would give your ideas more room to develop with evidence, rather than compressing everything into one dense middle paragraph.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address in revision:

Spelling: "discriminitive" → discriminatory; "excepted" → accepted; "enought" → enough; "sterotypes" → stereotypes; "burried" → buried; "avalibility" → availability; "existance" → existence; "peoples" → people's
Word choice/phrasing: "deeply breed sterotypes" → likely meant "deeply *bred* stereotypes" or "deeply ingrained stereotypes"; "Organizations and forms are presently being held" → unclear; do you mean "forums"?
Comma usage: Several sentences run multiple independent clauses together without proper punctuation (eg, "Cultural and racial equality is a new term that as a society we are striving for" — consider restructuring for clarity).
"more then" → should be "more than" (a common confusion between *then/than*)
Subject-verb agreement: check sentences like "Organizations and forms are presently being held to discover new ways" — passive construction is vague; consider active voice: "Organizations are now creating new strategies to address discrimination."

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these mechanical issues systematically.

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to explicitly argue why unlearning prejudice counts as a significant "discovery."
Add concrete examples—name specific movements, organizations, studies, or historical moments.
Restructure into 4-5 paragraphs with clearer topic sentences for each.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar, perhaps with a peer reviewer or writing center tutor.
Strengthen your conclusion by returning explicitly to your thesis and summarizing your strongest evidence.

Final Note

Your ideas are valuable and clearly come from genuine conviction. With more concrete evidence and tighter organization, this essay could be quite compelling. Keep developing your argument—you're off to a good start conceptually; now it's about strengthening the support and precision of your language.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay Draft

Thank you for sharing this draft. You're grappling with a genuinely interesting and complex topic—the gap between American ideals of equal opportunity and the lived reality of inequality in a fast-paced, media-saturated, individualistic society. Below are suggestions organized by area, along with specific line examples.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your central claim is buried inside a very long opening sentence. Right now, it reads as a list of related ideas (rules of society, equal opportunity, failure, media, changing values) rather than one clear, arguable claim. Ask yourself: What is the ONE thing I want to prove in this essay? Is it:
That the "American Dream" ideology masks real inequality?
That media reinforces a false narrative of equal opportunity?
That individualism causes society to ignore the disadvantaged?

These are all related, but they are different arguments. Choose one as your thesis, and let the others become supporting points. For example, a possible thesis: *"Although American culture promotes the idea that anyone can succeed through effort alone, this ideology ignores structural inequalities and is reinforced by media and a fast-paced, individualistic lifestyle that makes suffering invisible."* This kind of single, focused sentence should end your introduction.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

Currently, your two paragraphs feel like separate essays rather than connected steps in one argument:
Paragraph 1 introduces the ideology of success/failure and mentions media.
Paragraph 2 shifts to technology, pace of life, and individualism.

The connection between these ideas exists in your head, but you need to build the bridge for the reader. Consider adding a transition sentence like: *"This ideology is not only shaped by cultural values but also intensified by the technological changes of the last century."* Each paragraph should also open with a clear topic sentence stating its main point, rather than starting with a chronological marker ("And today...").

The final image—"parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated"—is a striking metaphor, but it appears without any setup. If you want to compare society to a machine, introduce this idea earlier and develop it, rather than dropping it in the last line.

Evidence and Development

You make strong claims ("everyone has an equal opportunity to succeed," "nothing was ever equal," "T.V. commercials & T.V. programs project models of how one should be") but don't yet support them with specific examples. For an argumentative essay, concrete evidence is essential:
*Which* TV commercials or programs? Can you describe a specific ad or show that models "success"?
*What* historical values are you referring to when you say the American founding values have "changed in meaning"? A brief historical reference (eg, to the Declaration of Independence's "all men are created equal") would strengthen this claim.
Consider citing a statistic, scholarly source, or news example about inequality or media influence to ground your argument in evidence rather than assertion.

Grammar and Mechanics

Since you're writing in a second language, here are some specific corrections to watch for in revision:

Run-on sentences: Your opening sentence needs to be broken into 2–3 shorter sentences for clarity.
Spelling: "comercials" → commercials; "nolonger" → no longer; "thier" → their; "unsuccessfull" → unsuccessful; "realitive" → relative; "substitued" → substituted.
Abbreviations: Avoid informal abbreviations like "T.V." and "20th cen" in formal writing—write out "television" and "the twentieth century."
Verb forms: "parts of the new machine that need oiled" → "need to be oiled" or "need oiling."
Article usage: Check phrases like "a current ideology" vs. "the current ideology"—consider which is more accurate given your argument.
Word choice: "attribute thier inadequacy to achieve" is a bit awkward; consider "attribute their failure to succeed" or "blame their lack of success on."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write one clear thesis sentence stating your specific argument.
Break up long sentences and add topic sentences to each paragraph.
Add a transition connecting the ideology paragraph to the technology/individualism paragraph.
Provide concrete examples or evidence for each major claim (specific ads, historical references, statistics).
Proofread carefully for spelling and verb form errors—reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.

You have a compelling and timely argument here. With a sharper thesis, stronger paragraph transitions, and more concrete evidence, this essay will be much more persuasive. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft!

Feedback on Your Essay on Technology and Society

Thank you for sharing this thought-provoking essay. You clearly have a personal stake in this topic, and your passion comes through in your writing. Below is feedback organized around argument structure, development, and language, followed by suggestions for revision.

Argument Structure and Focus

Your central metaphor—technology as both "friend and Nemesis"—is compelling and sets up an interesting tension. However, the essay's structure doesn't fully deliver on this promise. Here's the core issue: your introduction and second paragraph discuss technology broadly (as a double-edged tool), but your third and fourth paragraphs shift to discussing American educational shortcomings (lack of geography and language instruction). The connection between these two ideas—technology's dual nature and America's cultural insularity—is never explicitly drawn.

Ask yourself: How does technology specifically cause the problems you describe (ignorance of geography, limited language options)? Is technology the culprit, or is it educational policy, cultural attitudes, or something else? Right now, it reads as though you have two separate arguments: (1) technology is dangerous when combined with human misuse, and (2) American education fosters insularity. You need a clearer bridge. For instance, do you mean that technological self-sufficiency (constant connectivity, convenience, etc) has made Americans feel they don't need geographic or linguistic knowledge? If so, state this directly and develop it.

Development and Evidence

Your personal anecdotes (the cancer cure, the "Is Spain in Michigan?" story, your own delayed language learning) are vivid and effective for engaging readers. However, the essay relies *entirely* on personal experience. For an argumentative essay, especially at the university level, you should incorporate:
Statistics or studies on American geographic/linguistic literacy
References to educational policy debates (eg, foreign language requirements in U.S. schools)
Scholarly or journalistic perspectives on technology's role in cultural insularity (eg, algorithms creating "filter bubbles," social media's effect on attention spans)

This would strengthen your claims beyond the anecdotal and make your argument more persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Paragraph-Level Issues

Paragraph 2 makes an abstract claim ("when they become welded with certain aspects of and certain people in our societies, they become potentially the most dangerous things") but doesn't explain *how*. What aspects? What people? This needs concrete elaboration or examples before moving on.

Paragraph 3 introduces a sweeping generalization: "a great majority of Americans live in their own personal closets." This is a strong claim that needs support—consider softening it or providing evidence.

Conclusion: The essay currently lacks a proper conclusion. The final paragraph ends abruptly with a confusing sentence: "The effort of the United States has caused our minds to be heard, and a world that is growing while my country lags behind in its selfish self-satisfaction." This sentence is grammatically unclear (I suspect "heard" may be a typo for "herded" or "closed"?) and doesn't function as a conclusion. You need a closing paragraph that returns to your opening metaphor of technology as friend/enemy and synthesizes your argument, perhaps suggesting what should change.

Language and Style Notes

"Nemesis" doesn't need capitalization unless personifying it as a proper noun (which could work stylistically, but be consistent).
"Science and technology have had effects on the U.S. that cannot be described" — this phrase undermines your argument; if effects "cannot be described," why are you writing an essay describing them? Consider: "profound effects" or "effects I find difficult to summarize."
The phrase "I am a self-sufficient- I don't need any help" has a punctuation error (missing closing quotation mark, stray hyphen) and awkward phrasing. Consider revising to: "a self-sufficient, 'I don't need help from anyone' mentality."
Watch subject-verb agreement and sentence fragments, eg, "This thing, technology has been..." is a comma splice—consider "This thing—technology—has been..."

Suggestions for Revision

Clarify your thesis: Write one sentence that explicitly connects technology to American cultural/educational insularity. Place this at the end of your introduction.
Add a transitional sentence at the start of paragraph 3 explaining *why* you're shifting from technology-in-general to the American education system specifically.
Incorporate at least one external source (statistic, study, or expert opinion) to support your claims about American geographic/linguistic literacy.
Write a concluding paragraph that returns to your friend/nemesis metaphor and offers a final reflection or call to action.
Proofread for clarity, especially in complex sentences—reading aloud can help you catch confusing constructions.

Overall, you have a strong, personal foundation for this essay. With a clearer logical throughline and additional evidence, this could become a compelling piece. Keep developing this voice—it's engaging and sincere.

Feedback Report: "How Could People Live Without the Invention of the Computer?"

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses a relevant and relatable topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through. However, the essay currently reads more as a descriptive summary of computer use than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, develop a clearer line of argument, and provide more specific, concrete evidence. Below are detailed suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

An argumentative essay requires a clear claim that can be debated—something readers might disagree with. Currently, your thesis ("Computers have significantly changed people's lives") is more of an observation than an argument. Few readers would contest this statement.

Suggestion: Sharpen your claim into something more specific and arguable. For example:
"Although computers offer undeniable benefits, society's growing dependence on them has created a dangerous vulnerability."
"Computers have improved efficiency, but this convenience has come at the cost of critical human skills."

Your concluding paragraph actually hints at a more interesting argument—the *dependency* and *drawbacks* of computers—but this idea appears too late and is not developed. Consider moving this tension to your introduction and building your entire essay around it.

Structure and Paragraph Development

Right now, your paragraphs largely repeat the same point (computers are used everywhere and are helpful) without building toward a deeper argument. Each paragraph should serve a distinct purpose and advance your thesis.

Suggestions:
Paragraph 1 (Intro): State your specific, arguable thesis clearly.
Paragraph 2: Present the benefits of computers with specific evidence (see below).
Paragraph 3: Introduce the counterpoint—the drawbacks or dependency issue—which you currently only mention in your conclusion. This could become the heart of your argument.
Paragraph 4: Address a counterargument (eg, "Some argue computers only make life easier, but...") and refute it.
Conclusion: Synthesize your argument rather than restating the opening question.

Evidence and Specificity

Your essay relies on general statements ("computers can be found everywhere," "people rely greatly on computers") without concrete examples, statistics, or specific scenarios. Argumentative writing is strengthened by precise evidence.

Suggestions:
Instead of "Businesses use computers because they can be more productive," give a specific example: "Retail businesses use computer inventory systems to track stock in real time, reducing waste and lost sales."
Instead of "Schools use computers to teach children because it is fun and exciting," explain *how*—educational software, interactive learning platforms, etc
For your drawbacks paragraph, give a concrete scenario: "When a hospital's computer system crashed in [example], patient records became inaccessible for hours, delaying critical care."

Language and Grammar Notes

A few language issues to address in revision:

"calculators as usually found" → likely a typo; probably meant "calculators are usually found." Also, note that calculators and computers are different devices—clarify this distinction, especially since your third paragraph calls the calculator "the first computer," which is historically inaccurate and should be revised or removed.
"laboreous" → should be "laborious."
Repetition: The phrase "computers have become a part of everyday life" appears three times almost verbatim. Vary your language and avoid restating the same idea.
Comma splices/run-ons: Watch sentences like "Computers do many useful and wonderful things, but people have often experienced the drawbacks of such a wonderful invention." This is fine, but check similar constructions for clarity.
Rhetorical question in conclusion: Ending with "How did people live without the invention of the computer?" mirrors your title but doesn't provide closure. Consider ending with a stronger, more assertive statement that reflects your revised thesis.

Historical Accuracy

Be cautious with factual claims. Calling the calculator "the first computer" is misleading—mechanical calculators predate electronic computers, and the history of computing involves devices like the abacus, Babbage's Analytical Engine, and others. If you're not making a historical argument, it may be simplest to remove this parenthetical claim entirely.

Revision Priorities

Rewrite your thesis to make a specific, debatable claim.
Restructure paragraphs so each has a clear function tied to your argument.
Add concrete examples and evidence throughout.
Develop the "drawbacks" idea from your conclusion into a fuller counterargument section.
Proofread for the grammar and repetition issues noted above.

You have a solid foundation and an interesting tension (benefits vs. dependency) already present in your writing—now it's time to bring that tension to the forefront and argue it directly. Good luck with your revision!
