Feedback on Your Essay on Einstein and Relativity

Thank you for tackling such an ambitious topic. Your enthusiasm for the philosophical implications of Einstein's work is evident, and you're clearly trying to make a bold, original argument connecting relativity to global politics and human psychology. However, the essay currently struggles to communicate this argument clearly. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Right now, it's difficult to identify a single, clear claim that unifies the essay. You touch on several big ideas—the philosophical impact of relativity, the loss of "closure" in human knowledge, atomic weapons, and a call to shift global consciousness from fear to peace—but the connections between them are asserted rather than argued. 

Suggestion: Try writing a one-sentence thesis that captures your central claim. For example: *"Although Einstein's theory of relativity has been used destructively (atomic weapons) and has not been philosophically absorbed by humanity, a fuller understanding of relativity could shift global consciousness from fear to peace."* Once you have this sentence, make sure every paragraph works to support it directly.

Strengthen the Logical Connections

Several key moves in your argument need more explanation:

Relativity → loss of philosophical "closure": You assert that Einstein shattered the Socratic idea of complete, recoverable knowledge, but you don't explain *how* relativity does this. Is it because relativity shows knowledge is always relative to a reference point, so no single "God's eye view" exists? Say this explicitly.
Relativity → notions of time: Your paragraph on "earthtime" is intriguing but underdeveloped. What specifically did Einstein change about how you think about time? Give a concrete example.
Relativity → atomic bombs → fear → peace: This is the biggest logical leap. You need a bridging argument: *how* does understanding relativity itself (as opposed to just having the theory exist) lead humanity from fear to peace? Right now this reads as an assertion, not an argument. Consider explaining what Hawking's use of relativity implies about interconnectedness or perspective-taking, and how that could translate into political change.

Fact-Check Your Historical Claims

This is important: Japan and Germany were not the "winners" of WWII—they were defeated. The Allied powers, including the US and USSR, won. This factual error undermines your credibility on a topic central to your argument (the atomic bomb and its political aftermath). Please revise this section carefully, and make sure your account of the Cold War and its origins is historically accurate.

Distinguish Between Relativity and Quantum Mechanics

You mention that quantum mechanics "came with" relativity, but these are distinct (though related) areas of physics, and it's worth being precise about which one you're actually discussing throughout the essay, since your argument seems focused on relativity specifically.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

Each paragraph currently tries to do too much. Consider dedicating one paragraph to each of these ideas, with a clear topic sentence and supporting explanation:
Einstein's biographical/historical significance
The philosophical impact of relativity (loss of "closure")
The change in human notions of time
The use of relativity in atomic weapons
The political consequences (fear-based global systems)
Your proposed shift toward peace

Language and Mechanics

There are several errors to address in revision:
Spelling: "Einstien" → Einstein; "histories" → history's; "nostolgic" → nostalgic; "sidphantha" → likely Siddhartha (please clarify this reference, as it's unclear to readers unfamiliar with the allusion).
Punctuation: Avoid starting sentences with "And" or using semicolons where periods are needed (eg, "USA-USSR; with Japan and Germany...").
Word choice: "Underestimated" in "cannot be underestimated" likely should be "overestimated," since you mean the bomb's significance is very large, not negligible.
Clarify references: The bracketed insertions (eg, "[24 hours = day]," "[like sidphantha and his rock]") read as notes-to-self rather than integrated explanations. Please spell these out fully in your prose so readers unfamiliar with your references can follow.

Consider Your Audience

Some phrases ("indwell the topic," "philosopher's stone," "utopic dust") are poetic but may obscure your meaning for an academic reader. Ask yourself: could a reader unfamiliar with your personal associations follow this? If not, consider more direct language.

Summary of Priorities for Revision:
Write a clear one-sentence thesis.
Fix the WWII historical error.
Build explicit logical bridges between each paragraph's claims.
Proofread for spelling and grammar.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one clear focus.

You have an interesting and ambitious argument here—the revision work is about making your reasoning visible to the reader at each step. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic—the impact of television on society—and you've organized your argument in a fairly clear way: potential benefits, then actual negative effects, then conclusion. Below are some observations to help you strengthen the essay in a revision.

Strengths

You have a clear thesis: television could have been beneficial, but has instead become an "escape route" that promotes laziness and weakens family life. This is a solid, arguable claim, and your essay does return to it consistently, which shows good structural awareness.

Areas for Improvement

Thesis and Focus
Your introduction promises to discuss both "world views" and "lifestyle," but the essay's real focus is on the negative effects on family life and personal motivation. Consider narrowing your thesis early on so readers know exactly what argument to expect. Right now, the claim that TV "made the most impact" on this century feels like an overstatement that isn't fully defended—many other inventions (computers, the internet, cars) might have equally strong claims. Either qualify this claim ("one of the most significant inventions") or provide a brief justification for why television specifically deserves this distinction.

Evidence and Support
This is the area needing the most attention. Your claims are largely asserted rather than demonstrated. For example:
"Millions of Americans can be found to be staring at the T.V." — Can you cite a statistic or study here? Even an approximate figure (eg, "the average American watches X hours of TV per day") would strengthen your credibility.
"T.V. is a major cause of lack of togetherness" — This is a strong causal claim. Consider providing a specific example, anecdote, or research finding to support it, rather than stating it as self-evident.

Academic essays are more persuasive when they combine your own reasoning with outside evidence (studies, statistics, expert opinions). Try to find at least two or three concrete sources to integrate into your next draft.

Addressing Counterarguments
You do acknowledge television's potential benefits (educational programming, cultural exposure), which is good. However, you could deepen this by briefly mentioning specific examples of good television (eg, documentary series, news programs) and explaining more precisely why these positive potentials are rarely realized in practice. This would make your argument feel more balanced and thoughtful rather than one-sided.

Logical Connections
Some causal claims move quickly without enough reasoning. For instance: "The more time one spends watching TV, the less motivated he becomes." Why does this happen? Is it because TV provides passive entertainment that requires no effort, thus training people to avoid effortful activities? Spelling out the "why" behind your claims will make your argument more convincing.

Structure and Paragraph Focus
Your third paragraph currently combines two related but distinct ideas: (1) TV as entertainment vs. education, and (2) TV's effect on family togetherness and motivation. Consider splitting this into two paragraphs, each with its own clear topic sentence, so each idea can be developed more fully.

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats ideas from the introduction and body effectively, but try to end with a stronger final thought—perhaps a recommendation (how might television be used better?) or a broader implication (what does this suggest about how we should approach new technologies in the future?). This would give your essay a more forward-looking, memorable ending.

Language and Mechanics
There are several areas requiring editing attention:
Spelling errors: "integrety" → integrity; "dificulties" → difficulties; "becomed" → became
Grammar: "the one that has made the most impact" is fine, but check subject-verb agreement in a few places (eg, "society has becomed" → "has become")
Missing information: "00 years ago" appears to be a typo—did you mean "100 years ago"?
Word choice: "T.V." and "television" are used somewhat inconsistently; choose one style and use it throughout (many academic styles prefer spelling out "television" rather than abbreviating).
Pronoun agreement: "he becomes" (paragraph 3) assumes a male viewer; consider "one becomes" or "people become" for inclusivity and consistency with earlier "one" usage.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Narrow and clarify your thesis in the introduction.
Add concrete evidence (statistics, examples, sources) to support your claims about TV's negative effects.
Strengthen the counterargument section with specific examples of positive television content.
Split paragraph 3 for clearer organization.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar errors.

With these revisions, your essay will move from making assertions to building a well-supported, persuasive argument. You have a good foundation—now it's a matter of adding depth and precision. Keep up the strong effort!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Polio Vaccine

Thank you for sharing this essay on the significance of the polio vaccine. You've chosen a compelling topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. Below is feedback organized by key areas to help you strengthen the essay in revision.

Overall Argument and Structure

Your thesis suggests two main claims: (1) the vaccine changed population patterns, and (2) it served as a foundation for future medical advances. However, the essay only develops the first claim in real depth; the second point (that it "opened new doors" for medical science) is introduced only briefly in the final paragraph. Consider either:
Expanding the final paragraph substantially to give this claim equal treatment, or
Narrowing your thesis to focus solely on the population argument, which is your strongest material.

Right now, the essay feels front-loaded and ends somewhat abruptly. A stronger conclusion should synthesize your ideas and return to your opening claim about why the vaccine was "the greatest invention," rather than introducing a new idea about scientific method so late.

Clarity and Logical Development

Your discussion of the Baby Boom is the most interesting part of your essay, but the reasoning is difficult to follow. You want to argue that the vaccine didn't *cause* the boom but contributed to sustained population growth by keeping children alive. This is a nuanced and worthwhile point—make it more explicit. Consider restructuring this section as:
Common assumption: vaccine caused the Baby Boom
Your counterargument: correlation vs. causation
Your actual claim: the vaccine's role was in *sustaining* population growth rather than *initiating* it

Also, your claim that "disease plays a role in population control" and normalizes "an overpopulated world" is a bold, generalized statement that needs support. As written, it risks sounding like an unsupported opinion rather than a researched claim. Either provide evidence (historical examples, data) or soften the claim.

Evidence and Development

This essay would benefit enormously from concrete evidence:
Statistics: How many children died or were paralyzed by polio annually before the vaccine? What happened after?
Historical context: When exactly did Salk's vaccine become available (1955)? How does this align with the Baby Boom timeline (1946–1964)?
Comparison: You mention AIDS as a modern parallel—this is a great rhetorical move, but it's underdeveloped. A sentence or two more could strengthen this comparison.

Right now, the essay relies heavily on general assertions ("countless," "inequivalent to any other invention") without demonstrating *why* these claims are true. Ground your argument in specific facts.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring errors to address in revision:

Spelling: "mamed" should be "maimed" (appears twice).
Word choice: "inequivalent" is awkward—consider "unmatched" or "unparalleled."
Punctuation: You have several comma splices and fragments, eg, "For 20 year olds polio has never never been an issue, it is one of the many vaccines..." This should likely be two sentences or joined with a semicolon.
Repetition: "never never" appears to be a typo—remove the duplicate.
Capitalization for emphasis: Using "CAUSE" in capitals is informal for academic writing; instead, use italics or rephrase the sentence to emphasize this distinction through word order (eg, "did not cause the boom but rather...").
Dashes and parentheticals: Your use of dashes is inconsistent and sometimes confusing (eg, "for some of our parents, and grandparents this was what they feared"). Review this sentence for clarity—consider breaking it into two sentences.
Comma usage: Watch for unnecessary commas before conjunctions when not joining independent clauses (eg, "less children were dying, or being mamed").

Tone and Register

Phrases like "I'm sure in the past there were 'booms'" and "changed the future, and future's children" are conversational and imprecise for academic writing. Try to replace personal speculation ("I'm sure") with evidenced claims, and clarify phrases that sound poetic but lack precision.

Suggestions for Revision

Add a clear roadmap sentence in your introduction outlining both main points you'll discuss.
Develop the "foundation for medical advances" claim with specific examples (eg, how vaccine science influenced later immunizations).
Support your population claims with at least some historical data or dates.
Proofread carefully for spelling, spacing, and punctuation.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize both main arguments rather than introducing new territory.

You have a genuinely interesting argument here—the counterintuitive claim that the vaccine sustained rather than caused population growth is worth developing fully. With more evidence and tighter structure, this essay could be quite persuasive. Good luck with your revision!

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0012.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: The Impact of the Airplane

Overall Impression

You've chosen a strong, relevant topic and organized your essay in a clear, logical way—introduction, then separate paragraphs for travel, business, entertainment, and international relations. This structure makes your argument easy to follow. Your use of specific examples (Michigan to California, Michael Jackson's concert, Pearl Harbor) shows good effort to support your claims with real-world evidence. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the essay further.

Strengths

Clear thesis and roadmap: Your introduction effectively previews the four areas you'll discuss (travel, business, entertainment, politics).
Personal example: Mentioning your own trip from the U.S. to Spain is a nice touch that makes the essay more engaging and credible.
Concrete comparisons: The Michigan-to-California example (30-35 hours by car vs. 3 hours by air) is excellent—specific and persuasive.

Areas for Improvement

Grammar and Mechanics
There are several recurring errors to address:
Spelling: "efficeintly" → *efficiently*; "musicans" → *musicians*; "Habor" → *Harbor*; "choosen" → *chosen*
Ampersands: Avoid using "&" in formal academic writing. Write out "and" instead.
Verb tense/form errors: "has been drastically effected" should be "affected" (affect = verb, effect = noun); "travel times have been significantly shorten" → "shortened"; "than previously possibly" → "than was previously possible"
Subject-verb agreement: "if it took the President 1 week to get to where they were going" — mixing singular (President) with plural (they); consider "he or she" or rephrase to avoid the issue
Apostrophe use: "the do" should be "they do"

Suggestion: After revising, read your essay aloud slowly, or use a grammar-checking tool, to catch these small but frequent errors.

Paragraph Development
Your paragraphs are a good length, but some claims need more support:
In the business paragraph, you say international business has "flourished" and businesspeople can easily work internationally, but you don't explain *how* the airplane specifically enables this (eg, in-person meetings, flying goods, attending conferences). Adding one more sentence of explanation would strengthen this section.
In the entertainment paragraph, consider explaining *why* faster travel matters for entertainers—for instance, they can perform in multiple cities/countries within a short tour, reaching more fans.

Balance in the Final Paragraph
Your conclusion about international relations effectively shows both positive and negative effects, which adds nuance to your argument. However, the transition to negative effects feels abrupt. Consider adding a sentence that acknowledges this shift more smoothly, such as: "However, the airplane's ability to bring people together quickly has also made it a tool for conflict." This would guide the reader more clearly.

Conclusion
Your final paragraph doubles as a conclusion, but it's quite brief and ends the essay somewhat abruptly. Consider adding a few sentences that:
Briefly summarize the main areas you discussed (travel, business, entertainment, politics)
Offer a final reflection—perhaps on how the benefits of air travel generally outweigh its drawbacks, or how it has fundamentally changed how humans interact with the world

Word Choice and Register
A few phrases could be more precise or formal:
"touch on" → consider "discuss" or "examine" for a more academic tone
"positively and negatively effected" → "affected"
"in their perspective countries" → this should be "respective countries" (a common confusion between "perspective" and "respective")

Specific Line Edits to Consider

Paragraph 1: "overseas travel has been drastically effected" → "overseas travel has been drastically affected"
Paragraph 3: "much easier than previously possibly" → "much easier than was previously possible"
Paragraph 5: "it was brought about some negative changes" → "it has brought about some negative changes" (also fix subject-verb agreement)

Final Recommendations

Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar, especially affect/effect confusion and verb tense consistency.
Expand your business and entertainment paragraphs with slightly more explanation of *causation* (how exactly the airplane created these effects).
Add a proper concluding paragraph that synthesizes your argument rather than ending on the Pearl Harbor example.
Avoid the ampersand symbol (&) in formal writing—write "and" instead.

Overall, this is a solid draft with a clear argument and good examples. With careful proofreading and a stronger conclusion, this essay will be even more effective. Keep up the good work!

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0013.1.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay on the impact of AIDS on society. You've chosen a significant and thought-provoking topic. Below is feedback organized by category to help you strengthen your next draft.

Content and Argument Development

Your central claim—that AIDS has changed both behavior and social attitudes—is a reasonable and interesting thesis. However, the essay would benefit from clearer organization around this claim. Right now, you have two main ideas (stigma/prejudice, and behavioral change) but they aren't explicitly connected to a unified argument. Consider stating your thesis more directly in the introduction: for example, "AIDS has reshaped American society in two major ways: by fueling prejudice against marginalized groups, and by making people more cautious about sexual behavior."

Evidence needs strengthening. The Ryan White example is a strong, concrete illustration—use more details here. When did this happen? What specifically did the school district do? A brief citation or reference would add credibility.

Your claims about casual sex declining in the 1980s and relationships being "valued more" are asserted but not supported. What is this based on—your observation, a source, statistics? Since these are empirical claims, consider adding a source (even a general one, like a sociological study or news article) or softening the language to reflect that this is your impression rather than an established fact. Phrases like "I feel" and "I'm sure" weaken the essay because they signal opinion rather than argument. Try replacing these with more analytical framing: "This suggests that..." or "Evidence indicates that..."

Structure

Your essay follows a logical sequence (introduction → prejudice → fear/behavior change → conclusion), but transitions between paragraphs could be smoother. The jump from discussing Ryan White to "undetectable strands of AIDS" feels abrupt. Consider adding a transition sentence that connects the theme of paranoia from paragraph 2 to the new medical development in paragraph 3.

The conclusion is quite brief. Consider expanding it to synthesize your two main points and perhaps suggest what might happen going forward (more education, more research, etc) rather than just asserting optimism.

Grammar and Mechanics (Important for Revision)

You have a number of recurring errors worth addressing systematically:

Apostrophes and contractions/possessives:
   - "peoples lives" → *people's lives*
   - "it's presence," "it's consequences" → *its presence, its consequences* (possessive "its" has no apostrophe; "it's" = "it is")

Spelling errors:
   - "awhile" → *a while*
   - "untill" → *until*
   - "devistating" → *devastating*
   - "definately" → *definitely*
   - "strands" → likely you mean *strains* (a "strand" is a thread; a "strain" is a variant of a virus)

Word choice/idiom:
   - "there is more of stigma" → *there is more stigma* or *there is a greater stigma*
   - "casual sex... rarely occurs" — reconsider phrasing; perhaps *is less common* or *happens less frequently*

Run-on and comma issues:
   - The first sentence is a bit convoluted: "A discovery that has significantly changed many people's lives and their ways of thinking is the epidemic, known as AIDS." Consider simplifying: "AIDS is an epidemic that has significantly changed the way people live and think."
   - Watch for comma splices, eg, "Though it has probably been around for a while, its presence hasn't really been known until fairly recently, and its consequences have been devastating" — this is actually fine, but check similar sentences for clarity.

Style Suggestions

Avoid absolute words like "always" ("it always leads to death") since medical treatments have evolved; even in the historical period you're discussing, exceptions might complicate this claim. Consider "it was, at the time, considered a fatal diagnosis with no known cure."
Be cautious with words like "definitely," "probably," and "I'm sure"—used together, they create an inconsistent tone between confidence and uncertainty. Choose your certainty level deliberately for each claim.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Proofread carefully for spelling and apostrophe errors (this will have an immediate, high-impact improvement).
State your thesis more explicitly early in the essay.
Add specific evidence/sources for your claims about behavioral change in the 1980s.
Expand the Ryan White example with more concrete detail.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs.
Replace subjective phrases ("I feel," "I'm sure") with more analytical language.

You have a good foundation and a clear point of view—these revisions will help sharpen your argument and improve clarity for your reader. Keep up the good work!

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0014.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay on Nuclear Weapons and Historical Change

Thank you for sharing this thoughtful and personal essay. You clearly have a strong opinion and some vivid memories to draw on, which is a good foundation for argumentative writing. Below are some suggestions to help you strengthen the essay's clarity, organization, and argumentation.

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis—that nuclear weapons have been the most significant factor of change in recent history—is a bold and debatable claim, which is exactly what a strong argumentative thesis should be. However, the essay doesn't fully deliver on this claim. After the first paragraph, you shift focus several times: to the psychological effects of Cold War fear, to government spending, and finally to the optimistic dismantling of Cold War structures. Each of these is interesting, but none of them directly proves that nuclear weapons caused more change than, say, the internet, decolonization, or civil rights movements. To strengthen your argument, consider:

Explicitly returning to your central claim at the start of each paragraph. Ask yourself: "How does this paragraph prove that nuclear weapons were *the most significant* factor of change?"
Acknowledging (and then rebutting) a counterargument. For example, you could briefly address why someone might argue that another factor (technology, decolonization) was more transformative, then explain why you still find nuclear weapons more significant.

Organization

The essay follows a loose chronological structure: the buildup of nuclear fear, the cost and cultural impact of the Cold War, and then its recent thaw. This is a reasonable arc, but the transitions between paragraphs need work. The move from paragraph 2 (fear and spending) to paragraph 3 (the end of the Cold War) feels abrupt—you jump from criticizing nuclear culture to celebrating its decline without a clear transitional sentence. Consider adding a sentence that signals the shift, such as: "Yet even as this fear defined decades of policy, the final years of the century revealed how quickly such barriers could fall."

Also, the concluding paragraph reads more like a hopeful wish than a conclusion that ties back to your thesis. Try ending by explicitly restating *why* this evidence supports your original claim about nuclear weapons being the most significant force of change—for better and worse.

Development and Evidence

Right now, your strongest piece of evidence is personal experience (associating communism with nuclear threat, remembering bomb shelters). This is effective for establishing emotional resonance, but an argumentative essay benefits from concrete, verifiable evidence as well. Consider incorporating:

Specific statistics (eg, estimated Cold War military spending, number of nuclear warheads at their peak vs. now)
Historical dates and events (eg, the Cuban Missile Crisis, START treaties, Reagan-Gorbachev summits)
A specific example of "9 presidential administrations" and how their policies changed

This will make your claims more persuasive to readers who don't share your personal history.

Grammar and Word Choice

There are several grammatical issues to address in revision:

"Though advances in communication..." should be "Through advances..."
"the discovery and harness of atom and its energy" should be "the discovery and harnessing of the atom and its energy"
"the production of nuclear warheads has virtually arrived a standstill" should be "has virtually reached a standstill"
"cheif Executive commandpost" — check spelling ("chief") and consider whether "Americana" is the right word here; it usually refers to cultural artifacts/nostalgia, not political power.
"Although the old habits die hard, they are indeed dying" — this sentence is a nice turn of phrase, but check subject-verb agreement patterns throughout the essay; several sentences have comma splices (independent clauses joined only by a comma), such as the first paragraph's second sentence.

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, which often helps catch these grammatical slips, or asking a native-speaking peer to review a draft with you.

Style

Your voice is engaged and sincere, which is a strength. Phrases like "our frigid planet and cold neighbors may once again be a warm and friendly people" show a nice use of metaphor consistent with your Cold War theme. Keep developing this kind of figurative language, but make sure metaphors are grammatically consistent (a "planet" cannot itself be "a people").

Summary

Your essay has a promising central idea and personal insight, but needs a tighter argumentative structure, smoother transitions, more concrete evidence, and careful proofreading. I encourage you to revise with your thesis sentence taped above your desk, checking each paragraph against it as you go.

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0015.1.txt =====
Feedback Report: Penicillin and the Antibiotic Industry

Overall Impression

Your essay demonstrates a clear grasp of the historical narrative surrounding penicillin's discovery and its impact on the pharmaceutical industry. Your English is generally clear and grammatically sound, which is impressive. However, the piece currently reads as an informative/narrative summary rather than an argumentative essay. To meet the requirements of the assignment, you'll need to significantly rework the structure and purpose of your writing.

Establishing an Argument

The most important issue to address is that your essay lacks a thesis or claim that requires defense. Right now, you are describing a historical sequence of events ("penicillin was discovered → other antibiotics were found → synthesis increased → industry grew"). This is useful background, but an argumentative essay needs a position that a reasonable reader could disagree with.

Ask yourself: What is your claim about this history? Some possibilities:
Was the shift from natural to synthetic antibiotics *necessary* for medical progress, or did it introduce new problems (eg, resistance)?
Did the commercialization of antibiotics (companies like Upjohn) *help or harm* public health outcomes?
Was penicillin's discovery an *inevitable* scientific development, or was it primarily due to chance and individual genius?

Choose one arguable position and make it explicit in your introduction (eg, "While penicillin's discovery is often celebrated as a triumph of chance, its true significance lies in the systematic industrialization of antibiotic synthesis that followed"). Then organize your body paragraphs around evidence supporting *that specific claim*, rather than simply narrating chronology.

Evidence and Specificity

Your essay makes several broad claims without supporting evidence:
"one of the greatest advances in medicinal chemistry" — compared to what? Says who?
"improved the chances of a patient's survival" — by how much? Any data or named source?
"millions of people" — this is a strong claim; can you cite a source or example?

Academic argumentative writing depends on specific, verifiable evidence: dates, names, statistics, or citations from scholarly sources. For example, mentioning Alexander Fleming's 1928 discovery, the wartime mass-production efforts of the 1940s, or specific data on mortality rates before/after antibiotics would strengthen your credibility considerably. Currently, "Upjohn" is the only concrete example in the essay, and it appears without context—what did Upjohn actually do, and why does it matter to your argument?

Structure and Development

Your three paragraphs move through the ideas quickly, but each idea deserves more room to breathe:
Consider dedicating one paragraph exclusively to the significance of penicillin's discovery (with evidence).
Dedicate another to the shift toward synthetic antibiotics (with evidence and explanation of *why* this happened, not just *that* it happened).
Reserve the final paragraph for the industrial/commercial consequences, tied back to your thesis.

Right now, paragraph two tries to cover too much (synthesis, industry growth, commercial products, university research) in a compressed way. Slowing down and providing an example for each point will make your argument more persuasive.

Language and Style Notes

A few specific language issues to address:

Spelling: "attemped" should be "attempted."
Quotation marks vs. italics: "in vitro" is a Latin phrase and should be italicized (*in vitro*), not placed in quotation marks. Similarly, "wonder-drug" in quotes is fine if you are signaling it's a colloquial term, but be consistent in how you flag such terms throughout.
Overuse of passive voice: Phrases like "was discovered," "It was also attempted," "It was discovered that" appear repeatedly. This makes your writing feel impersonal and vague about *who* did what. Try more active constructions: "Chemists discovered that older drugs could be synthesized efficiently" instead of "It was discovered that older drugs could be synthesized efficiently."
Repetition of key terms: "discovery/discovered" appears many times in close proximity. Consider synonyms (eg, "identified," "isolated," "found," "developed") to add variety.
Tense consistency: You shift between past simple and past passive constructions somewhat inconsistently; review each paragraph to ensure a consistent narrative tense.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Develop a clear, arguable thesis and revise your introduction to state it explicitly.
Add specific evidence (dates, names, data, or citations) to support each major claim.
Restructure body paragraphs so each one builds a piece of your argument, rather than narrating a timeline.
Revise for active voice and reduce repetition of key terms.
Fix minor errors (spelling, italics for Latin terms).

You have a solid factual foundation here—now the task is to transform description into argument. I encourage you to revise with a specific claim in mind and to bring in outside sources to substantiate your points. Feel free to bring a draft of your revised thesis to office hours for feedback before you continue with the full revision.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Invention of Television

Thank you for sharing this essay on the impact of television. You've chosen an engaging topic with genuine debate potential, and I can see you have real ideas to contribute. Below is feedback organized by area, along with suggestions for revision.

Content and Argumentation

Your essay identifies three effects of television (entertainment, communication, family life), which gives it a clear structure. However, the essay reads more as a balanced overview than an argumentative essay. You present "both sides" for each point (television is good/bad for entertainment, family, etc) but never commit to your own position. 

Suggestion: Since this is an *argumentative* essay, decide what you actually believe. Is television's impact primarily positive or negative? Revise your thesis to state this clearly, and then use the body paragraphs to support that claim—while still acknowledging counterarguments, which you can then rebut. For example: "While television has undeniably improved access to information, its overall effect on family life and cultural values has been more harmful than beneficial." This gives your essay a clear argumentative thread.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your three-paragraph structure (entertainment, communication, family) is a reasonable start, but each paragraph tries to cover a lot of ground quickly. Consider:
Giving each main point (entertainment, communication, family) more room to breathe, possibly in separate, more developed paragraphs.
Adding a topic sentence to each paragraph that states its main claim clearly, rather than transitioning abruptly between ideas.
Strengthening your conclusion—right now it summarizes without adding insight. Consider ending with a stronger final judgment or a thought-provoking implication (eg, what this means for future generations).

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors worth addressing:

"Their" vs. "there": You write "Of course their have been..." and "There is..." — "their" is possessive; "there" indicates existence/location. Review this distinction carefully, as it appears multiple times.

Verb agreement: "The types of programs is another issue" should be "are another issue" (subject "types" is plural). Also, "they become desensitized" should be "they become" (this one's correct) but check "programing improves their is" — should be "there is."

Word choice/spelling: 
   - "way of live" → "way of life"
   - "yonkies" → likely "junkies" (informal; consider "addicted to" for more academic tone)
   - "20 century" → "20th century" (missing ordinal suffix, appears twice)

Punctuation and symbols: Avoid using "&" in formal academic writing—write out "and."

Redundant word: "and and diminished" — remove the duplicate "and."

Comma splices/run-ons: Some sentences string ideas together without clear breaks (eg, "Everybody comes together at night to watch a favorite program. However, others say..."). Check that each sentence has a clear main clause.

Article usage: "in to our homes" should be "into our homes" (one word, as a preposition here). Also watch for missing articles: "is a major source for fast & accurate communicating" reads awkwardly—consider "a major source of fast and accurate communication."

Style and Tone

The phrase "with a push of a button" is a nice idiomatic touch—well done incorporating natural expressions.
Try to avoid overly casual phrases like "it is anyone's guess" in formal academic writing; replace with more formal alternatives like "it remains uncertain" or "this debate is unlikely to be resolved soon."
The exclamation point in your final sentence ("...invention of the 20 century!") is too informal for an academic essay. End with a calm, confident statement instead.

Priority Revisions

If you focus on just three things for your next draft, I'd suggest:
Sharpen your thesis to take a clear argumentative stance.
Proofread for "their/there," subject-verb agreement, and spelling (especially "20th century").
Revise the conclusion to avoid simply repeating the introduction—add a final insight or judgment.

You have good ideas and a logical structure to build from. With attention to these grammar patterns and a clearer argumentative stance, this essay will be much stronger. Keep up the good work!

===== ICLE-US-MICH-0017.1.txt =====
Feedback on "The Washing Machine Essay"

Overall Impression

You have an interesting and even provocative idea buried in this essay: that labor-saving devices, while convenient, may be contributing to a more sedentary, less physically active society. This is a thoughtful angle, but right now the essay reads more like an informal, humorous reflection than an academic argument. Below are some specific areas to strengthen for revision.

Thesis and Argument

Your central claim is unclear until the final paragraph. For most of the essay, you seem to be arguing that washing machines are indispensable and beloved (paragraphs 1–3), but then you pivot in paragraph 4 to argue that such conveniences are actually harmful because they reduce physical activity. This creates a confusing reading experience—readers don't know what you're building toward until the very end.

Suggestion: State your real argument—that modern conveniences, despite their benefits, have contributed to reduced physical activity and health issues—in your introduction. Then use the body paragraphs to build toward that point deliberately, rather than surprising the reader with it at the end.

Structure and Organization

The essay lacks clear topic sentences that connect each paragraph to your thesis. Paragraphs 2 and 3 both describe how attached people are to washing machines through exaggerated, humorous scenarios (running from a fire while clutching the machine; panicking at the sight of a washboard). While vivid, these examples are repetitive—they make the same point twice without advancing your argument. Consider condensing them into one paragraph, or make sure the second one adds something new.

Suggestion: Try outlining your essay before revising:
Introduction: modern conveniences have transformed how we live → thesis
Body 1: how dependent we've become on these devices (with evidence, not just anecdote)
Body 2: the health/behavioral cost of that dependence
Conclusion: restate significance, perhaps suggest implications or a call to reflection

Evidence and Support

Right now, your support consists mainly of hypothetical, exaggerated scenarios (people clutching washing machines during a fire; adults panicking at a washboard). These are entertaining but are not persuasive as *evidence*—they are speculative and impossible to verify. Academic argumentative writing benefits from more concrete support: statistics on obesity trends, studies linking sedentary technology use to health outcomes, historical comparisons of physical labor before/after industrialization, or even quotes from credible sources discussing convenience culture.

Suggestion: Look for at least two or three sources (articles, studies, or expert opinions) that support your claim about the link between convenience technology and declining physical activity. This will make your final paragraph's argument—which is actually your most original and interesting point—much more convincing.

Tone and Style

The tone throughout is very informal and conversational, which works well for a blog post but not for academic argumentative writing. Some specific issues:

Rhetorical questions ("What ever would a college student do with out a washing machine?") are used often; in academic writing, use them sparingly, if at all.
The ellipsis ("But is that all that bad?....") and scare quotes around "wonders" and "modern" feel informal.
Humor and hyperbole (the fire scenario) can undercut the seriousness of your later, more substantive point about health.

Suggestion: Aim for a more measured, analytical tone. You can still be engaging without relying on jokes or exaggeration—let your ideas and evidence create interest instead.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are some recurring errors to watch for in revision:

Spelling: "continously" → continuously; "necesity" → necessity
Spacing/word choice: "with out" → without (one word); "hand clothes washing" → "washing clothes by hand"
Ampersands (&): Avoid using "&" in formal writing; write out "and."
Pronoun consistency: You shift between "man" (generic), "we," "I," and "his/her." Choose a consistent approach—ideally more inclusive and consistent, such as "people" or "we" throughout.
Capitalization: "Should there be a fire" is fine, but check consistency in how you introduce hypothetical scenarios.

I recommend reading your essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller issues after you've revised the content.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Decide on your one central argument and state it clearly in your introduction.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one builds toward that argument.
Replace some hypothetical/humorous examples with real evidence (statistics, sources).
Adopt a more formal, analytical tone; reduce rhetorical questions and casual punctuation.
Proofread carefully for spelling and mechanical errors.

You have a genuinely interesting idea here—now it just needs a clearer, more academic structure to shine through. I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Zip-Lock Bag

Overall Impression

This is an engaging, well-organized essay with a clear personal voice and a nice sense of humor. Your enthusiasm for the topic comes through, and you support your central claim with concrete, relatable examples. Below are some suggestions to help strengthen the argument and polish the writing.

Structure and Argument

Strengths: Your essay follows a logical progression—freshness, odor control, and versatility—and each paragraph builds on a clear topic. The personal anecdotes (the smelly locker, the "dry-clean only's") make abstract claims concrete and memorable.

Areas to improve:

Thesis clarity: Your thesis appears in the first paragraph ("that is an invention I cannot live without"), but consider stating more explicitly *why* this matters as an argument. Is your claim that zip-lock bags are the *best* invention, or simply *a great* invention? Right now the essay reads more like an appreciation than a persuasive argument. If this is meant to be argumentative, you may want to acknowledge a counterpoint (eg, "Some might argue plastic bags harm the environment...") and respond to it, which would strengthen your position.

The "solutions" paragraph is confusing. In paragraph 4, you present three "solutions" to the smell-transfer problem, but the third ("bag everything!") feels like a joke that undercuts the logical structure. Consider either developing this humorously and clearly signaling that it's a joke, or cutting it to keep the paragraph focused.

Conclusion could be stronger. Your final paragraph restates examples rather than synthesizing your argument. Consider ending with a broader reflection—why does an invention like this matter in daily life? This would give your essay a more persuasive close rather than just a summary.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

Since English is not your first language, here are some specific points to revise:

Comma splices and punctuation: Watch for extra or misplaced commas, eg, "bring to school to lunch, and by the time lunch came around, I could smell the sandwich before I even opened my locker. ." There's a stray period after "locker." Also: "Gallon size zip-lock bags, solve this problem" — remove the comma before "solve."

Word choice/parentheses: "(onlies???)" is a fun aside, but in formal writing you should resolve this uncertainty rather than leaving it as a question to the reader. You could write "dry-clean-only items" instead.

Consistency in hyphenation: You alternate between "zip-lock" and "ziplock" — pick one form and use it consistently throughout (note: the brand name is "Ziploc," so if you're referring to the general product category, "zip-top bag" or "resealable bag" may be more accurate and avoid trademark issues).

Verb tense consistency: In "I cannot even count the number of times that my father would pack..." you shift into past habitual tense, which is fine, but make sure surrounding sentences match this time frame clearly.

Redundancy: "Anything can be put in zip-lock bag, and not get freezer burn" — revise to "Almost anything can be stored in a zip-lock bag without getting freezer burn" for smoother phrasing and correct article usage ("a zip-lock bag").

Rhetorical questions: You use two rhetorical questions ("Where else can you put ice...?" and "What more could you ask for?"). These are effective for engaging the reader, but using them at both the body and conclusion feels repetitive. Consider varying your rhetorical strategy.

Style Suggestions

Your informal, conversational tone suits a personal essay, but if this is meant to be more formally argumentative, consider tightening some casual phrases (eg, "There are other uses" → "Beyond these functions, zip-lock bags also serve...").
Some sentences are quite long and list-heavy (the paragraph about earrings, Q-tips, pantyhose). Consider breaking these into two sentences for clarity and impact.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to clarify whether this is a personal appreciation or a persuasive argument—consider addressing a counterargument.
Clean up punctuation errors and stray marks.
Choose one consistent term/spelling for the product.
Strengthen the conclusion with a broader insight rather than repetition.
Vary sentence length in list-heavy sections for better flow.

You have a strong foundation with vivid, personal examples—these revisions will help sharpen the argument and polish the prose. Great work overall!

Feedback Report: The Computer's Impact on Modern Life

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and important topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. However, the essay currently reads more as a description or prediction than an argument. To strengthen this into a compelling piece of academic writing, you'll need to sharpen your thesis, provide concrete evidence, and address the complexity of your claims more critically.

Argument and Evidence

The central weakness of this essay is the lack of a clear, arguable thesis. Statements like "the computer has changed the way people live" and "the personal computer will continue to play a major role in our lives" are broadly true but not really debatable—few readers would disagree. A stronger argumentative essay needs a claim that requires defending, such as: *"The personal computer has been the single most transformative invention of the 20th century because it fundamentally altered [specific area]—more than X or Y invention."* This gives you something to prove rather than simply describe.

Additionally, several claims need evidence:

"Productivity of people has increased ten-fold" — Where does this statistic come from? Without a source, this looks like an exaggeration rather than a fact. Consider citing a specific study, report, or concrete example (eg, a particular industry or task that has been transformed).
"Every human being in the developed world will have their own PC linked by cellular modem..." — This is a prediction, not an argument. Predictions about the future are risky in academic writing unless grounded in trends, expert opinion, or data you can cite.

I'd recommend choosing 2-3 specific, well-supported examples (eg, how computers changed medical research, global communication, or manufacturing) rather than sweeping generalizations. Specificity will make your argument much more persuasive.

Counterargument

Right now, the essay presents only one side of the story—the positive impact of computers. A stronger essay would acknowledge complexity: Are there downsides (job displacement, digital divide, privacy concerns)? Even briefly addressing a counterpoint and then explaining why your argument still stands would make your position more credible and sophisticated.

Structure

Your essay follows a logical basic structure (introduction → historical development → impact → future implications), but each section is underdeveloped. Consider:

Introduction: State your specific, arguable thesis clearly at the end of the first paragraph.
Body Paragraph 1 (History): This paragraph is descriptive, which is fine as background, but connect it more explicitly back to your thesis—why does this history matter to your argument?
Body Paragraph 2 (Impact): This is where your argument should be most developed. Right now it moves quickly from present impact to speculative future impact within one paragraph. Consider splitting these into two paragraphs: one on documented current impact (with evidence), and one on future implications (framed carefully as speculation grounded in current trends).
Conclusion: Currently missing. The essay ends on a forward-looking note but doesn't return to reinforce your main argument. Add a concluding paragraph that restates your thesis in light of the evidence presented and leaves the reader with a clear final impression.

Language and Style

A few issues to address in revision:

Typos/spelling: "occured" → "occurred"; "2st century" → "21st century."
Word choice: Phrases like "expensive monsters" and "20th century miracle" are colorful but overly informal/cliché for academic writing. Consider more precise, formal language (eg, "large, costly machines").
Vague quantifiers: "ten-fold," "much has occurred," "everything their predecessors did and much more" — these are imprecise. Replace with specific, supportable claims.
Grammar: 
   - "the way data and information *is* dealt with" → should be "are dealt with" (subject-verb agreement, since "data and information" is plural/compound).
   - "small digital assistants that will replace the printed page" — clarify what you mean; this is vague.
Punctuation: Check paragraph indentation and spacing consistency (some paragraphs have extra leading spaces).
Quotation marks around "bright" — unclear why this word is in quotes; if it's not a direct quote or ironic usage, remove the quotation marks.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to make a specific, debatable claim.
Add concrete evidence (statistics with sources, specific examples, expert opinions) to support each major claim.
Acknowledge at least one counterargument or limitation.
Separate "current impact" from "future speculation" into distinct, clearly signaled sections.
Write a proper concluding paragraph.
Proofread carefully for typos, subject-verb agreement, and word choice.

With these revisions, your essay will move from being a general reflection on computers to a focused, evidence-based argument. You have good instincts about the topic's importance—now it's a matter of making your case more rigorously to convince a skeptical reader.

Feedback on Your Essay: AIDS and Its Impact on Society

Thank you for tackling such an important and sensitive topic. Your essay shows genuine engagement with the social history of AIDS, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. Below is feedback organized by key areas for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction claims AIDS is "one of the most important discoveries" and says it "changed how people think, live, and view mortality." This is a strong opening idea, but the essay doesn't consistently argue this claim—it drifts between several related but distinct arguments: (1) society's discriminatory response to AIDS, (2) the debate over sex education vs. abstinence, and (3) the need for acceptance of AIDS victims. Consider narrowing your thesis to one central argument. For example, you might argue specifically that *society's response to AIDS reveals a pattern of selective compassion based on who is perceived as a "worthy" victim*. This would give you a clear thread to follow through Ryan White, children, and Magic Johnson.

Factual Precision

There's an important scientific/historical distinction you should clarify: AIDS is a syndrome caused by HIV; it wasn't "discovered" in the way a single event—it was identified and understood gradually through research in the early 1980s. Referring to it as "the discovery of AIDS" throughout is imprecise. Also, verify your dates and details about Ryan White (he was diagnosed in 1984 and became a national figure through school segregation cases in Indiana)—adding specific, accurate details here would strengthen your credibility and give the essay more scholarly weight.

Logical Gaps and Unsupported Claims

Several statements need evidence or more careful qualification:
"No one really cared that gays would die" — this is a strong generalization. Some historical evidence (eg, government inaction, media coverage patterns, quotes from LGBTQ+ activists of the era) would substantiate this rather than leaving it as an assertion.
"Children are becoming sexually active at the age of eleven" — this needs a citation. Without a source, this reads as an unsupported claim that weakens your argument about sex education.
The jump from discrimination against Ryan White to discussing condom distribution in schools feels abrupt. Add a transition sentence explaining how public fear of AIDS *led to* the educational campaigns you describe.

Paragraph Organization

Your third paragraph tries to cover too much ground: Ryan White, public panic, educational campaigns, the abstinence debate, and the assertion that AIDS "has become less of a taboo subject." Consider splitting this into two paragraphs—one focused on Ryan White and the resulting public education campaigns, and a second focused specifically on the ongoing debate about what that education should include (abstinence vs. comprehensive sex education).

Conclusion Strength

Your concluding paragraph raises a powerful point—that celebrity status (Magic Johnson) shapes public attention to a disease—but this idea deserves more development rather than being introduced in the final paragraph. Consider moving this observation earlier and building your conclusion around it, using it to reinforce your central thesis about selective empathy in the AIDS crisis.

Style and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to address:
Ampersands (&): Avoid using "&" in formal academic writing; always spell out "and."
Sentence fragments: "Even if a cure is never found." is a fragment—it needs to be attached to the following sentence or revised: "Even if a cure is never found, AIDS victims should be accepted and given the respect they deserve."
Informal phrasing: Phrases like "How wrong they were" and "It's too bad that" are conversational and better suited to spoken argument than academic prose. Consider more formal alternatives: "This assumption proved tragically mistaken" or "It is unfortunate that..."
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past tense (describing the 1980s) and present tense (describing ongoing debates). This is appropriate when discussing history versus current events, but make sure the shifts are intentional and clearly marked (eg, "Today, parents are still fighting...").

Citations

This essay would benefit significantly from at least a few outside sources—historical accounts of the AIDS crisis, CDC statistics, or scholarship on stigma and public health. Even brief citations (eg, referencing a specific study or historical document about the Ryan White CARE Act) would transform this from a personal reflection into a more persuasive, evidence-based argument.

Summary of Priority Revisions
Narrow and clarify your thesis.
Verify historical/scientific facts.
Add evidence for generalizations.
Reorganize paragraph 3 into two focused paragraphs.
Revise fragments and informal language.
Incorporate at least 2–3 outside sources.

You have a compelling argument here about societal hypocrisy and the uneven distribution of compassion—sharpening the focus and adding evidence will make this essay much more persuasive. Good work, and I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an ambitious and philosophically interesting angle—linking technology to human fear of death and insecurity. This is a compelling thesis with real intellectual substance. However, the essay in its current form is too brief and underdeveloped to fully support such a bold claim. Below are suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim—that only medical inventions of the 20th century have improved life—is provocative and could work well as a strong argumentative essay. However, this claim needs much more scaffolding before readers will find it convincing. Currently, you assert the connection between technology, fear of death, and domination without demonstrating *why* this connection exists. Consider adding a paragraph early on that walks the reader through this reasoning with a concrete example (eg, how the automobile or nuclear technology reflects a desire for control over nature or other people).

Also, be careful with absolute language like "only." This is a very strong claim. If a reader thinks of a counterexample (eg, the internet, vaccines that aren't purely "medical," sanitation systems), your argument could collapse. You might consider softening slightly to "predominantly" or explicitly address and refute potential counterexamples—this would actually strengthen your argument by showing you've considered alternatives.

Development and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Each paragraph currently makes a claim but doesn't support it with specific evidence, examples, or reasoning. For instance:

Paragraph 2 claims inventions lead us "away from a lifestyle peacefully coexistent with nature," but offers no specific inventions or data. Which 20th-century inventions do you mean? The automobile? Plastics? Industrial agriculture? Naming 2-3 concrete examples and briefly explaining their environmental impact would make this paragraph far more persuasive.

Paragraph 3 on weapons and economic systems is only two sentences. This is a rich area—you could discuss specific examples like nuclear weapons, or economic systems like colonial extraction—but as written, it feels more like a placeholder than a developed argument.

Paragraph 4 on medical inventions also lacks specifics. Which medical inventions do you have in mind? Penicillin? Vaccines? Insulin? Naming them and briefly explaining their humanitarian impact (with no downside of domination) would make your contrast far more vivid and convincing.

Suggestion: Aim to develop each paragraph with a clear topic sentence, 2-3 sentences of specific evidence or examples, and a sentence of analysis explaining how that evidence supports your thesis.

Addressing Counterarguments

A stronger version of this essay would anticipate objections. For example: What about the invention of vaccines that also serve public health systems tied to political power? What about agricultural technology that reduced famine deaths—doesn't this also "dominate nature" while saving lives? Engaging with a counterargument, even briefly, and explaining why your thesis still holds, will make your argument much more sophisticated and credible to an academic reader.

Tone and Register

The opening line ("Interestingly enough, this topic is one that is very important to me") is conversational and works better in a personal reflection than in an academic argumentative essay. Consider revising your introduction to move more quickly into the analytical content—you can still convey personal investment in the topic, but frame it through the argument itself rather than a direct statement of personal interest.

Conclusion

Right now, the essay ends abruptly after the paragraph on medical inventions. You need a conclusion that restates your thesis in light of the evidence you've presented and perhaps gestures toward broader implications (eg, what does this mean for how we should evaluate future inventions?).

Language and Mechanics

A few specific corrections to watch for:
"0the century" → "20th century" (typo)
"occured" → "occurred"
"truely" → "truly"
"affect" → "effect" (as a noun, use "effect")
Watch comma placement, eg, "For these reasons , I would argue" (remove space before comma)

These are minor issues but worth proofreading carefully in your next draft.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Expand each paragraph with specific, named examples (inventions, technologies, historical moments).
Address at least one counterargument to strengthen your credibility.
Revise the introduction to be more analytical and less conversational.
Add a conclusion that ties your argument together.
Proofread for the mechanical errors noted above.

You have a genuinely interesting philosophical argument here—the next draft should focus on giving it the concrete support and complexity it deserves. I look forward to seeing the expanded version!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Satellite Dish and Global Communication

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've chosen an interesting and ambitious topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through. However, the essay needs significant revision in several key areas before it will be persuasive to an academic audience. Let me walk you through the main issues and offer suggestions.

Establishing a Clear Thesis and Argument

Your introduction opens with a vivid but confusing image ("a large, 1/2 egg... as large as a small FIAT"). While creative writing can be engaging, an argumentative essay needs a clear, debatable thesis statement early on. Right now, it's unclear what you are actually arguing. Is your claim that satellite technology has been beneficial? Harmful? Both, in complicated ways? Your conclusion suggests ambivalence ("for better and sometimes worse"), but an argumentative essay should take a position and defend it with evidence, not simply present a list of pros and cons and leave the judgment to the reader.

Suggestion: Revise your thesis to state a specific claim, eg, "While satellite technology has dramatically improved global access to information, its unregulated growth has produced troubling social and economic consequences." This gives your essay direction.

Organization and Paragraph Unity

Each paragraph currently touches on a different domain (news, business, entertainment, military) without clear transitions or a unifying argument connecting them back to your thesis. This makes the essay feel like a list of disconnected facts rather than a sustained argument.

Suggestion: Group your paragraphs around 2-3 major claims that support your thesis, and use topic sentences that explicitly state how each paragraph's evidence supports your argument. For example, if arguing that satellite technology has increased global interdependence, dedicate one paragraph to political effects (CNN and diplomacy), one to economic effects (business/stock markets), and one to cultural effects (entertainment).

Evidence and Verification

Several factual claims are unsupported or appear inaccurate:
"The satellite dish, created in the last 0 years" — this phrase is confusing (likely a typo, but also factually vague).
"40% of U.S. millionaires are entertainers" — this statistic needs a citation. As written, it's unverifiable and likely incorrect.
"The intelligence of an average teenager is lowering itself due to cable viewing" — this is a strong claim requiring research support (studies, statistics), not assertion.
The reference to "STARWARS" (likely the Strategic Defense Initiative) needs explanation for readers unfamiliar with this Cold War program.

Suggestion: For every claim of fact, ask yourself: "How do I know this, and can I cite a source?" Academic essays require evidence from credible sources (news archives, academic journals, government reports) rather than general impressions.

Language and Mechanics

As a non-native English speaker, you're clearly working hard on complex ideas, which is commendable. However, there are recurring language issues that obscure your meaning:

Spelling/typos: "recieve" (receive), "ctry" (century), "panasonic" (Panasonic — proper nouns need capitalization), "millionares" (millionaires), "affect" should be "effect" (noun form), "people lives" (people's lives), "pro's and con's" (pros and cons — no apostrophe needed for plurals).//
Word choice: "naval programs" — do you mean "national defense programs" or specifically "military/navy programs"? This is unclear and affects your argument's credibility.
Punctuation: "it's center" should be "its center" (possessive, not contraction).
Sentence fragments: "Not certain about the date of its invention, it must be recognized..." is grammatically awkward — the modifying phrase doesn't logically attach to "it" (the satellite dish object itself isn't "not certain").

Suggestion: After revising content, do a careful proofreading pass, or ask a writing center tutor to review mechanics with you. Reading your essay aloud can also help catch unclear phrasing.

Tone and Audience

The playful opening ("1/2 egg... small FIAT") is memorable but inconsistent with the more serious, analytical tone expected in academic argumentation. Consider whether this image serves your argument or distracts from it.

Overall Recommendation

Your topic has real potential — the global impact of satellite technology touches on politics, economics, and culture in fascinating ways. To strengthen this essay:

Write a clear, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around 2-3 main supporting claims.
Research and cite specific evidence for each claim.
Revise for grammar and word choice.
Ensure your conclusion reinforces your thesis rather than remaining neutral.

I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further or look at a revised draft. Keep working—the ideas here are worth developing carefully.

Feedback Report on "The Impact of Television"

Overall Impression

This essay tackles an ambitious and genuinely interesting topic: how television reshaped American culture through mass culturalization, commercialization, and the acceleration of information flow. Your three-part structure is clear and logical, and you support each point with concrete, well-chosen examples (The Cleavers, Green Acres, the Challenger explosion, Vietnam War coverage). This is a strong conceptual foundation. However, the essay needs significant revision in clarity, grammar, and argumentative development before it reaches its full potential. Below are the main areas to address.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is clear and your three supporting points (mass culturalization, commercialization, information speed) map logically onto your three body paragraphs. This is good essay architecture. To strengthen it:

Sharpen the thesis statement. Currently it reads as a simple list. Consider adding a claim about *why* this matters—what is the ultimate consequence of these three trends? Your conclusion hints at this (manipulation vs. stimulation), so consider foreshadowing that tension in your introduction.
The opening sentence needs work. "The 0th Century" is a typo (should be "20th Century"), and starting with "The [invention] that has had one of the most radical effects" is a common but weak formula. Try opening with a more vivid image or a provocative claim—something like a scene of a family gathered around a TV set—to draw the reader in immediately.

Paragraph Development

Each paragraph would benefit from deeper analysis, not just more examples. Right now, you tend to state a claim, give 2-3 examples, and move on. Ask "so what?" after each example:

In paragraph 2, you note that a farmer in Iowa understands NYC families and white people understand "ghetto life" through TV. Push further: What are the *dangers* of this kind of understanding? You call the portrayals "blown-up and exaggerated"—this is an important point, but you don't develop it. Does mass culturalization create false intimacy or stereotypes? This tension is worth exploring, since it complicates your claim rather than just supporting it.
In paragraph 3 (commercialization), you make a strong point but it's the shortest, least-developed paragraph. Consider adding an example of a specific ad campaign or discussing how commercialization has affected values (materialism, self-image, children's culture) rather than just noting that ads have become "an art form."
In paragraph 4, your Vietnam example is excellent—specific and emotionally resonant. Use this technique more consistently in other paragraphs.

Grammar, Spelling, and Mechanics

There are numerous errors that distract the reader and undermine credibility. Please proofread carefully, ideally reading the essay aloud. Key patterns to fix:

Spelling errors: "grupes" → groups; "breath" → breadth; "inhance" → enhance; "livingrooms" → living rooms; "peoples" → people's (needs apostrophe).
Inconsistent capitalization: "vietnam" should be "Vietnam"; "united States" should be "United States." Be consistent with proper nouns.
Abbreviations are inappropriate in formal essays. Avoid "&," "w/," "w/o," and numerals like "15 second" (spell out "fifteen-second") in academic writing. Spell out all words fully: "and," "with," "without."
Punctuation errors: Several sentences have comma splices or misplaced punctuation, eg, "We all saw the same families, The Cleavers & The Andersons, etc telling how we should behave." This sentence needs restructuring—perhaps: "We all saw the same families—the Cleavers, the Andersons—teaching us how to behave."
Fragment: "The television a powerful and dangerous tool" is missing a verb ("is").
Backslash used incorrectly in "experienced w/ the advent"—again, avoid abbreviations.

I recommend reading through the essay specifically hunting for these mechanical issues in a separate pass, since they are numerous enough to distract from your ideas.

Word Choice and Register

Some phrasing is imprecise or informal for academic writing:

"mass culturization of various grupes" — do you mean "the standardization of diverse cultural groups"? Clarify this key term early, since it's central to your argument.
"the picture of perfection was the same in every home" — strong phrase, but consider varying your sentence openings; several paragraphs begin with short, similar declarative statements.
"We must watch what we watch" is a clever, memorable closing line—well done. Consider building your conclusion around this insight a bit more, perhaps connecting it back to your opening image.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fix spelling, capitalization, and abbreviation issues throughout (highest priority—these are distracting and easy to correct).
Deepen analysis in each paragraph, especially the commercialization section.
Reconsider the introduction and thesis for a stronger opening hook and clearer framing.
Address comma splices and sentence fragments for smoother, more formal prose.

You have compelling ideas and good examples—this revision should focus on polishing expression so your argument comes through with full clarity and authority.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Fall of the Berlin Wall

Overall Impression

This is an evocative, personal piece with genuine emotional power. Your opening paragraph does an excellent job of recreating the atmosphere of that historic day, and the anecdote about your classmate's father adds a valuable personal dimension that many essays on this topic lack. However, as an argumentative essay, the piece needs a clearer thesis, more logical structure, and stronger evidence to support your central claim. Below are specific areas to address.

Identify and Sharpen Your Thesis

Your argument seems to be: *the fall of the Berlin Wall was the catalyst that ended the Cold War and inspired revolutions across Eastern Europe.* This is a solid, debatable claim—but it doesn't emerge clearly until the final paragraph. Readers should know your argument from the introduction. Consider adding a sentence early on that states this thesis explicitly, so the personal narrative that follows feels purposeful rather than purely descriptive.

Strengthen the Argumentative Structure

Right now, the essay reads more like a personal reflection/narrative than an argument. To strengthen it:

Add a clear roadmap: After your thesis, briefly indicate what points you'll cover (eg, the symbolic impact, the domino effect on neighboring countries, the human cost that makes the outcome meaningful).
Develop your counterargument more rigorously: You raise an interesting point—"Eventually, I think that things would have turned out as they did"—but then abandon it in one sentence. This concession deserves more attention. Why do you believe change was inevitable? What makes the Berlin Wall's fall the *specific catalyst* rather than just one event among many? Strengthening this concession-and-rebuttal will make your argument more persuasive.
Add evidence beyond personal testimony: Your classmate's father's story is compelling, but for an argumentative essay, you should also reference historical facts, dates, or figures (eg, specific events in Hungary, Czechoslovakia, Poland, Romania) to substantiate the claim that the Wall's fall directly triggered these revolutions. Right now the causal link is asserted but not demonstrated.

Clarify the "Happiness" Digression

The line "Whether the people are happier or not, I cannot say" introduces an interesting but underdeveloped idea. If this isn't central to your argument, consider cutting it or connecting it more clearly to your thesis (eg, "regardless of individual outcomes, the political transformation itself validates the struggle"). As it stands, it feels like a tangent that weakens your focus.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to address in revision:

Spelling: "mesmirized" → *mesmerized*; "Tchekoslovaquia" → *Czechoslovakia*; "Roumania" → *Romania*
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense in ways that sometimes confuse the timeline (eg, "Now, three years later, communism... no longer exists" is present tense, but surrounding sentences are past). Since you're writing in the early 1990s, look at each sentence and confirm whether you're describing the past event or your present reflection.
Pronoun agreement: "I had a classmate whose father had escaped from Hungary. He had left his family..." — but later you use "her father's words," creating confusion about whether the classmate is male or female. Please clarify.
Sentence fragment/ambiguous line: "Without the 'events of Berlin Wall' history probably would not have taken a very different course" — this sentence is confusing and seems to contradict your next sentence. Do you mean history *would* have taken a different course, or would *not* have? Revise for clarity, as this is a pivotal logical moment in your argument.
Punctuation: You use dashes (--) very frequently where periods or semicolons would be more appropriate. This makes some paragraphs feel like run-on sentences. Vary your punctuation to give readers clearer pauses.
Capitalization: "as-President Bush" should be "then-President Bush" (a common phrase meaning "who was President at that time").

Suggestions for Revision

Add a clear thesis statement near the end of your first paragraph or the start of your second.
Expand the counterargument about historical inevitability—engage with it seriously rather than dismissing it in one line.
Incorporate at least one or two concrete historical facts (dates, events, statistics) about the other Eastern Bloc revolutions to support your "domino effect" claim.
Proofread carefully for spelling, tense consistency, and pronoun clarity.
Revise punctuation, replacing many dashes with periods or semicolons for smoother, clearer prose.

Final Thought

You have a strong emotional core and a genuinely interesting argument buried within this essay. With a clearer thesis, more developed evidence, and tighter mechanics, this could become a compelling piece that combines personal narrative with historical argument effectively. Keep the personal voice—it's one of your strengths—but pair it with more explicit argumentative scaffolding.
