Feedback Report: Essay on the Television

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and debatable topic—identifying television as the most significant twentieth-century invention—and you clearly have ideas and enthusiasm for the subject. The comparison with radio is a smart rhetorical strategy, as it gives your reader a "before and after" picture. However, the essay would benefit from a clearer thesis, more developed argumentation, and careful proofreading. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis appears in the first paragraph, but it is somewhat buried and hedged: "Perhaps one does stand out a little more among its competition, however; that is the television." Since this is an *argumentative* essay, your thesis should be stated with more confidence and precision. Consider something like: "Among the countless inventions of the twentieth century, television stands out as the most transformative because it fundamentally changed how humanity accesses information, experiences entertainment, and connects across borders."

Additionally, your essay would be strengthened by explicitly stating your criteria for judging significance early on. Are you measuring significance by reach, by emotional impact, by economic influence, or by long-term societal change? Without stated criteria, your claim that television is "the most significant" invention feels asserted rather than argued.

Organization and Paragraph Development

Your paragraph structure is logical (radio era → television's improvement → unifying power → cautionary note), but each paragraph needs a clearer topic sentence that connects back to your central claim. For example, paragraph 3 opens with a strong point (television unites the world), but this idea needs one or two more sentences of analysis—*why* does bringing people together matter? How does this compare to what other inventions have done?

The final paragraph introduces a new and important idea—television's power to persuade and its commercial nature—but this feels like a separate essay's worth of content rather than a conclusion. Right now it functions as a caveat that slightly undermines your thesis instead of reinforcing it. If you want to argue that television is the *most significant* invention, you might reframe this paragraph to show that its very power (for better or worse) is *evidence* of its unmatched influence, rather than a warning that weakens your case. Alternatively, restructure the essay so this becomes a fourth body paragraph exploring television's dual nature (benefit and risk), followed by a distinct concluding paragraph that circles back to your thesis and closing thought.

Development and Evidence

Your examples (the sports broadcast, the president speaking to another country, the mother watching her son's Olympic race) are effective and relatable, but they remain hypothetical and generic. Since this is an academic argumentative essay, consider incorporating at least one or two concrete historical examples—for instance, the Kennedy-Nixon debates (often cited as a turning point where television changed politics), the moon landing broadcast, or the Vietnam War coverage that shifted public opinion. Real, specific evidence will make your argument far more persuasive than generalized scenarios.

You might also briefly acknowledge and rebut a counterargument (eg, someone might argue the internet or the computer is more transformative). Directly addressing an opposing view—even in one paragraph—would strengthen your essay's persuasive power.

Grammar, Spelling, and Word Choice

There are several recurring errors worth addressing in revision:

Spelling: "vertually" → virtually; "boarders" → borders (you mean the edges of a country, not people who board); "absorbtion" → absorption; "necesary" → necessary; "spening" → spending; "olympic" → Olympic (capitalize).
Word choice: "peak" should be "peek" (a quick look), since "peak" refers to a summit or high point.
Apostrophe/possessive errors: "one lives easier" should be "one's life easier"; "television strength" should be "television's strength."
Comma splices and run-ons: The sentence beginning "A system that seemed to work, many households..." is a fragment/run-on that needs restructuring—perhaps: "This system seemed to work well, as many households throughout the nation owned or had access to a radio and/or newspaper..."
Consistency: You alternate between formal and informal tone (eg, "peak," casual phrasing) versus more academic diction elsewhere. Aim for consistent academic register throughout.

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool for a final proofreading pass, as many of these are surface-level errors that a careful re-read will catch.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph currently functions more as a new point (television's commercial/persuasive power) than a true conclusion. A strong conclusion should briefly restate your thesis, synthesize your main points, and leave the reader with a final, memorable insight—perhaps returning to your opening idea about the "explosion of inventions" and explaining why television remains unmatched among them.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen and foreground your thesis with clear criteria for "significance."
Strengthen paragraphs with specific historical evidence.
Address a counterargument.
Reorganize the final paragraph to support (not undercut) your thesis, and add a proper concluding paragraph.
Proofread carefully for spelling, apostrophes, and sentence fragments.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a series of interesting observations to a tightly argued, well-supported case for television's significance. Keep developing this—you have a genuinely compelling angle here!

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on the Computer's Impact

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses an interesting and relevant topic, and you demonstrate a clear understanding of how the personal computer has transformed information processing over time. The writing shows good effort in tracing a historical trajectory from early computing to modern portable devices. However, the essay would benefit significantly from revisions in several key areas: thesis clarity, argumentative structure, factual accuracy, and language precision. Below are specific areas to address.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction states that the computer "has had a great impact," but this is a *descriptive* claim rather than an *argumentative* one. A strong argumentative essay needs a debatable thesis—something a reasonable person could disagree with. Consider revising your thesis to make a more specific, arguable claim. For example: "The personal computer has fundamentally transformed not just business practices, but the boundary between professional and personal life, creating both opportunities and challenges for how individuals manage information." This gives you something to *prove*, rather than simply *describe*.

Factual Accuracy

There is an important factual error that needs correction: IBM did not invent the personal computer in 1980. The IBM PC was released in 1981, and it was not the first personal computer—companies like Apple (1976), Commodore, and Tandy had already introduced PCs in the mid-to-late 1970s. IBM's contribution was significant because it legitimized the PC for business use and established industry standards, but claiming IBM "started" the revolution is historically inaccurate. Please research this section more carefully, as factual errors undermine your credibility as a writer. Consider citing sources to support your historical claims throughout the essay.

Structure and Organization

Your essay follows a loosely chronological structure, but it lacks clear signposting between paragraphs. Consider the following:

Paragraph 2 functions as background/context—this is useful but should more explicitly connect to your thesis.
Paragraph 3 is very long and tries to cover too much ground (the PC's invention, its capabilities, technological advances, modems, and portable computers all in one paragraph). Consider breaking this into 2-3 focused paragraphs, each with a clear topic sentence and a single main idea.
Paragraph 4 (your conclusion) currently just restates the introduction rather than synthesizing an argument or addressing counterpoints. A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge potential objections (eg, information overload, digital divide, job displacement) even briefly, and explain why your position still holds.

Development of Argument

Currently, the essay reads more like a historical summary than an argument. To strengthen it:
Add specific examples or evidence (statistics, expert opinions, or concrete case studies) to support your claims.
Consider addressing a counterargument—for instance, has the computer also created negative impacts (privacy concerns, over-reliance on technology, job losses)? Engaging with this would strengthen your credibility and demonstrate critical thinking.
Explain *why* the changes you describe matter, not just *what* changed. For example, when you say people can "do their taxes" or "look over databases" at home, explain the broader significance—does this democratize access to information? Does it increase productivity? Does it blur work-life boundaries?

Language and Grammar

There are several recurring language issues to address:

Subject-verb agreement: "There has been many inventions" should be "There have been many inventions."
Spelling: "recieve" should be "receive" (this appears multiple times).
Capitalization: "Modem" should not be capitalized mid-sentence ("with the use of Modem").
Pronoun consistency: You shift between third person ("society," "people") and second person ("you," "your") throughout the essay. Academic writing typically maintains a consistent voice—consider using third person throughout for a more formal, objective tone (eg, "individuals can write papers" rather than "you can write papers").
Run-on sentences: Several sentences are quite long and contain multiple ideas without clear connectors. Consider breaking these into shorter, clearer sentences.
Redundancy: Phrases like "add and receive information" and "sort it out and put that information into a way" could be tightened for clarity.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to present a clear, debatable argument.
Verify historical facts and add citations where appropriate.
Break up long paragraphs into focused units with clear topic sentences.
Add a counterargument and rebuttal to strengthen your position.
Proofread carefully for spelling, capitalization, and pronoun consistency.
Conclude with a synthesis of your argument rather than mere repetition.

Final Note

You have a solid foundation and clear enthusiasm for the topic. With attention to argumentative structure, factual verification, and language polish, this essay has strong potential. I encourage you to visit the writing center for additional support with grammar and sentence-level clarity. Keep up the good work!

Feedback on "The Century of Democracy"

Overall Impression
This essay has an engaging, personal voice and tackles an ambitious topic. Your enthusiasm for the political changes you witnessed is clear and appealing. However, the essay needs stronger argumentative structure, more consistent evidence, and closer attention to the logical connection between your thesis and your supporting paragraphs. Below are specific areas to revise.

Thesis and Scope
Your thesis claims the entire 20th century should be called "The Century of Democracy," but your evidence focuses almost exclusively on events from 1989–1992 (the fall of the Berlin Wall, German reunification, the Soviet collapse). This creates a mismatch between the scale of your claim and the scale of your evidence.

Consider one of two revisions:
Narrow the thesis to focus on the *end* of the century (eg, "the final decade of the 20th century marked a turning point for global democracy"), which matches your evidence much better; or
Broaden the evidence by briefly discussing earlier democratic milestones (decolonization movements, the fall of European fascism after WWII, civil rights movements) to justify the century-wide claim.

Right now, the opening paragraph's survey of "fads" per decade (flappers, the Depression, hippies) doesn't actually build toward your argument about democracy—it feels like a separate essay. Cut or heavily revise this list so that each detail you mention supports your thesis rather than simply describing "typical" decades.

A Timing Inconsistency to Fix
You write "as we embark on the year 2000," but you also reference the Expo Sevilla (1992) and describe the Soviet Union's *transition* to democracy as still happening. This places the essay's actual date around 1992, not 1999–2000. Readers unfamiliar with this history will be confused. Decide when your essay is "set" and make all references consistent—or better, add a clear date/context in your introduction so readers understand your vantage point (eg, "Writing in the fall of 1992...").

Structure and Paragraph Development
The essay reads more like a personal reflection or diary entry than an argumentative essay. Each body paragraph introduces a new example (Germany, the Soviet Union, the end of the Cold War) but doesn't clearly connect back to the thesis or build a cumulative argument. To strengthen this:
Add a topic sentence to each paragraph that explicitly ties the example to your claim about the "Century of Democracy."
Consider ordering your paragraphs chronologically or by scale (local → global) rather than as a loose sequence of "Perhaps... Perhaps... Perhaps."
The paragraph about arms sales and nuclear war feels underdeveloped—just two short sentences with no evidence. Either expand it with specific data/examples or cut it.

Evidence and Support
Right now, your evidence is largely anecdotal (buying a piece of the Berlin Wall, feeling "patriotic," personal impressions). This is effective for creating voice, but an argumentative essay needs more objective support. Consider adding:
Specific dates and facts (when did the Wall fall? When did the USSR dissolve?).
A statistic or authoritative source about the global increase in democratic governments during this period.
A brief counterexample or acknowledgment of countries that did *not* democratize, which would make your argument more nuanced and credible (right now it reads as one-sided).

Conclusion
Your conclusion offers a nice sentiment but repeats ideas ("as the turn of the century slowly approaches...") without adding new insight or fully resolving your argument. Try ending with a more specific, forward-looking claim: what specific challenges remain for global democracy? What would you want readers now (in 2000) to understand about the transition you witnessed?

Language and Style Notes
Ethnocentric idealism: strong phrase—make sure your following sentences clearly justify why you're *not* engaging in it, since the whole essay is written from a very U.S.-centered viewpoint (starting with "the U.S. elections").
Word choice: "capricious decade" is an interesting but slightly awkward description for the 1920s—consider "volatile" or "unpredictable."
Punctuation: Watch comma splices, eg, "Perhaps, it is because today is November 4th, the day after the U.S. elections." The comma after "Perhaps" is used inconsistently—decide on one punctuation style for this repeated structure.
Capitalization: "marxism" should be capitalized as "Marxism."
Some sentences are quite long and could be split for clarity, eg, the final sentence of the essay.

Summary of Priorities
Align your thesis with the scope of your evidence (or expand your evidence).
Fix the confusing timeline (1992 events vs. "year 2000").
Strengthen paragraph transitions and topic sentences to reinforce the argument.
Add more factual/historical support beyond personal anecdote.
Proofread for punctuation and word choice.

You have a compelling personal perspective—use it as the entry point into a more tightly argued essay, rather than as the argument itself. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Dangers of Nuclear Energy

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and important topic, and your passion for environmental protection comes through clearly. Your essay has an emotional force that engages readers. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of argumentative structure, factual accuracy, and clarity of purpose. Below are specific areas to address.

Factual Accuracy (Important)

There's a significant historical error that undermines your credibility: Oppenheimer did not "unveil" the atomic bomb in 1945 as a public invention he introduced to the world. He directed the scientific team that developed it (the Manhattan Project), but the bombs were used militarily by the U.S. government (Hiroshima and Nagasaki). Since this appears in your opening paragraph, it's crucial to correct this, as factual errors early in an essay can weaken reader trust in everything that follows. I'd recommend researching this historical context more carefully.

Argumentative Focus and Structure

Your essay conflates two distinct issues:
Nuclear weapons (bombs, destruction, warfare)
Nuclear energy (power plants, electricity generation)

These are related but different topics with different risks, benefits, and policy debates. Right now, your essay moves between them without clearly distinguishing them, which confuses your argument. Ask yourself: What is my essay actually arguing?
Is it that nuclear weapons should be abolished?
Is it that nuclear power plants should be shut down in favor of alternative energy?
Is it a broader statement about environmental apathy?

Choose ONE clear thesis and organize your paragraphs around supporting that specific claim.

Evidence and Support

Your essay relies heavily on emotional appeals ("the saddest part," "appalling," "tragedy") but lacks concrete evidence:
What specific environmental damage has nuclear energy caused? (Consider citing specific incidents like Chernobyl or Fukushima)
What are these "many other power source alternatives" you mention? Name them (solar, wind, hydroelectric) and explain why they're preferable.
What does "the world's need for this dangerous energy augments" mean in concrete terms? Provide statistics or examples.

Suggestion: For each major claim, ask "How do I know this? What evidence supports it?" Academic argumentation requires you to show your reasoning, not just assert it.

Logical Connections

Some of your reasoning needs stronger connections. For example:
You mention ozone layer holes and soil problems as evidence against nuclear energy, but ozone depletion is primarily caused by CFCs, not nuclear power. This logical leap needs correction or removal.
The transition from environmental damage to "leaders should confer" needs more development—what specific actions do you propose?

Language and Mechanics

A few technical issues to address in revision:
"The discovery of the Atomic energy, is undoubtedly the the most awesome" — remove the comma after "energy" and fix the duplicated "the"
"is routed in" should be "is rooted in"
"It is considered" — avoid starting sentences with capital "It" after a comma-separated clause; check capitalization consistency
"irreversable" → "irreversible"
Consider varying your sentence openings—you use "Never before..." twice in succession, which is stylistically repetitive (though intentional repetition can be effective if used purposefully)

Tone Shift at the End

Your conclusion shifts from formal academic argument to a direct call-to-action ("Write your local congressman"). This can be effective in persuasive writing, but it feels abrupt here. Consider:
Is this essay meant to be persuasive/activist in nature, or analytical?
If persuasive, build toward this call-to-action more deliberately throughout
If analytical, you may want a more traditional concluding synthesis instead

Suggestions for Revision

Narrow your thesis: Choose either nuclear weapons OR nuclear energy as your focus, or explicitly frame the essay as addressing both while keeping them analytically distinct.
Add research: Include specific facts, dates, statistics, or case studies to strengthen your claims.
Verify historical claims: Correct the Oppenheimer reference.
Strengthen paragraph transitions: Make sure each paragraph clearly builds on the previous one's argument.
Proofread for grammar: Review the essay for repeated words, subject-verb agreement, and spelling.

A Final Note

Your writing shows genuine conviction and a clear moral stance, which are strengths in persuasive writing. With more precise research, sharper focus, and tighter logical connections, this essay could become a much more powerful and convincing piece of argumentation. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to discuss any of this feedback further with me.

Feedback on Your Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay. You've chosen an interesting and debatable topic, and your writing shows a good grasp of English sentence structure. Below is feedback organized by key areas, with suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your essay's central claim seems to be that television is the most significant invention of the 20th century in communications, but this claim gets muddled by competing ideas. Notice how your introduction mentions:
Speed of information as "the invention"
Television as "the most influential invention"
Downsides of television

By the end of the introduction, readers aren't sure if you're arguing that television is beneficial, harmful, or both. Revise your thesis to state one clear, arguable position. For example: "While television has revolutionized global communication and awareness, its overall impact on society has been more transformative than harmful." Then structure your body paragraphs to support this specific claim.

Structure and Paragraph Focus

Your two body paragraphs each try to do too much:

Paragraph 2 discusses television's role in war coverage (Vietnam, Somalia) but ends with an oddly placed comparison to radio. This paragraph needs a clearer topic sentence connecting these examples to your main argument. Ask yourself: what is the *point* of these examples? Is it that television creates informed citizens? Provokes political action? Be explicit.

Paragraph 3 tries to cover three different ideas: (1) television "shrinking" the world, (2) television as a communication tool, and (3) television's potential harms (desensitization). This is too much for one paragraph. Consider separating these into distinct paragraphs, each with its own clear topic sentence, evidence, and analysis.

Addressing the Counterargument

You mention "downsides" in your introduction and briefly return to this in your conclusion ("there are many who feel..."), but you never fully develop this counterargument. For a strong argumentative essay, you should:
Dedicate a full paragraph to the opposing view (eg, television causes desensitization, misinformation, or passive consumption)
Explain *why* people hold this view with specific examples
Then rebut or qualify this view to strengthen your own argument

Right now, the counterargument feels like an afterthought rather than an integrated part of your reasoning.

Sentence-Level Issues to Review

Capitalization: "vietnam war" should be "Vietnam War" (proper nouns are always capitalized in English).

Awkward phrasing: 
   - "it has, and continues to shrink the planet" — try "it has shrunk, and continues to shrink, the planet"
   - "like all inventions can be used in a useless way" — this is unclear. Do you mean "misused" or "used carelessly"? "Useless way" doesn't quite convey your meaning.
   - "Not even truly dealing with the amount of television that is watched" — this opening is confusing. Perhaps you mean: "Setting aside the sheer amount of television people watch, there are other ways..."

Vague pronoun reference: "This was one of the factors" — This what, exactly? Be specific: "This graphic coverage was one of the factors..."

Passive voice overuse: Sentences like "information can be graphically displayed" and "news and information can be graphically displayed" rely heavily on passive constructions. Try more active phrasing: "Television displays news and information graphically as events unfold."

Development and Evidence

Your examples (Vietnam War, Somalia) are promising but underdeveloped. For each example, consider adding:
A specific detail or statistic (eg, how many households owned televisions during Vietnam?)
A brief explanation of *how* the coverage led to protests or humanitarian concern—what specifically did people see, and how did they react?

This will make your argument more persuasive and less general.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently repeats ideas from the introduction and body without adding new insight or a final, memorable statement. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement: What does television's legacy suggest about the future of communication technology (eg, internet, social media)?

Summary of Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis into one clear, arguable claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has a single focus.
Develop the counterargument more fully before addressing it.
Add specific evidence to support your examples.
Proofread for capitalization and awkward phrasing.

You have a solid foundation here—the ideas are compelling. With clearer organization and more precise language, this essay will be much stronger. I'd be happy to review your revision!

Vague word choice: "various things like" is informal; consider "factors such as"

Avoid absolute/informal language like "astronomical" and "basically"—academic writing benefits from more precise, measured language
"I can admit" is a curious phrase choice; simply "I admit" or "I realize now" would be more direct

Write a conclusion — this is currently missing and is essential for a complete argumentative essay

Add concrete evidence to your claims about international trade and business

Feedback on Your Essay on AIDS and Education

Thank you for tackling such an important and complex topic. Your essay shows genuine concern for public health, and you attempt to connect medical limitations with the power of education. However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of argument clarity, organization, development, and language conventions. Below is detailed feedback organized by area.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your introduction suggests two possible directions: (1) medicine has failed to cure AIDS, and (2) education might be a substitute solution. This is a reasonable pairing, but the essay never commits fully to a single, clear claim. Ask yourself: what is the *one* argument you want a reader to walk away believing? Right now, the essay drifts between "medicine isn't working" and "AIDS affects more people than we think" and "our culture is becoming more educated," without weaving these into one coherent thesis.

Suggestion: Try a more specific thesis, such as: "Because no medical cure for AIDS yet exists, public education about transmission and risk is the most effective tool we currently have for reducing new infections." This gives you a single argument to support in every paragraph.

Organization and Paragraph Unity

Each paragraph should develop *one* idea that clearly supports your thesis. Currently, several paragraphs introduce new topics without transitions connecting them to your main argument:

The paragraph about schools inviting speakers is only two sentences and isn't developed—why does this matter? What effect does it have?
The paragraph about "many people choose to ignore" AIDS shifts into a discussion of drug use as a "possible suicide," which is a separate and somewhat confusing claim. Consider: is this paragraph about denial, about misconceptions of who is at risk, or about the added danger of needle use? Choose one focus.
The short paragraph "Obviously, our culture has begun to educate its population or I would be unable to write about this particular topic" is a weak, almost circular argument. The fact that you personally know about AIDS doesn't prove that broad cultural education has succeeded. This point needs actual evidence (statistics, examples of programs, dates) rather than a self-referential claim.

Suggestion: Outline your essay before revising. List your thesis, then 3–4 supporting points, then find or generate concrete evidence (statistics on infection rates, examples of real school or hospital programs, expert quotations) for each point.

Development and Evidence

This is the essay's biggest weakness: it makes strong claims without support. For example:
"Due to the ineffectiveness of medicines" — Is this accurate? Antiretroviral therapy has made AIDS more manageable, even though it's not a cure. Overgeneralizing here weakens your credibility.
"Dentists who 'forget to put on their plastic gloves'" — this claim needs a source or should be removed if you cannot support it.
No statistics are cited about infection rates, transmission routes, or the effectiveness of education programs.

Suggestion: Find 2–3 credible sources (CDC, WHO, or peer-reviewed articles) and integrate specific facts and figures. This will transform your essay from opinion-based to evidence-based, which is essential for academic argumentation.

Language and Mechanics

Because this is an academic essay, avoid text-message abbreviations like "&," "w/," and "b/c." Spell out "and," "with," "because," etc This is a simple fix but crucial for formal writing.

Other issues to address:
Subject-verb agreement: "The AIDS viruses was discovered" → "The AIDS virus was discovered" (virus is singular).
Comma splices/run-ons: Several sentences join independent clauses with only a semicolon or comma where a period or conjunction is needed. Example: "People are afraid to come in contact w/ carriers of AIDS; however, they interact sexual w/ complete strangers" — "interact sexual" should be "interact sexually," and this sentence needs clearer logic (are these the same people or different groups?).
Word choice: "selling their lives short" is an unusual idiom choice here; perhaps "risking their lives" is clearer.
Sentence fragments: Some sentences beginning with "However" lack a complete independent clause structure. Review comma usage after introductory transitional words.

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates the introduction rather than synthesizing your argument or leaving the reader with a forward-looking insight. Try ending with a specific call to action or a prediction based on your evidence, rather than repeating "the AIDS virus was discovered" and "hopefully we can find a cure."

Overall Priority for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into one clear, arguable claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has a single, well-developed point tied to that thesis.
Add concrete evidence and citations.
Eliminate informal abbreviations and fix grammar (verb agreement, comma splices).
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize rather than repeat.

With these revisions, your essay will have a much stronger, more persuasive argument. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on "Contact Lenses" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay is engaging and personal—you clearly care about your topic, and your enthusiasm comes through. However, this assignment asks for an *argumentative* essay, and right now your piece reads more like a personal narrative or reflection. To strengthen it, you need to shift from "here is my experience" to "here is why contact lenses deserve to be considered one of the most significant inventions of the 20th century," supported by more than just your own life.

Content and Argument

Your central claim—that contact lenses are one of the most important 20th-century inventions—is a strong, debatable claim, which is good. But an argumentative essay needs to *convince* a skeptical reader, and currently your only evidence is anecdotal (your basketball games, your swimming, your morning routine). Ask yourself: why should a reader who has never worn contacts care?

Consider adding:
Broader impact: How have contact lenses changed life for millions of people, not just you? Think about safety (athletes, workers), self-esteem, employment opportunities, quality of life for people with severe vision problems.
Comparison: Since you're claiming contacts are *more* important than other 20th-century inventions, briefly acknowledge one or two competitors (eg, laser surgery, antibiotics, the internet) and explain why contacts still matter more, or reframe your claim to be more specific and defensible (eg, "one of the most important vision-related inventions").
A counterargument: Right now you briefly mention colored lenses being "a bit ridiculous" but dismiss the thought immediately. A stronger essay would take this seriously—address a real objection (cost, risk of infection, discomfort of hard lenses) and respond to it with evidence or reasoning, not just a shrug.

Organization

Your structure follows a chronological pattern (hard lenses → your own soft lenses → disposables → colored lenses), which works reasonably well as a narrative device, but each paragraph needs a clearer connection back to your thesis. Right now, paragraphs feel like separate anecdotes. Try adding a sentence at the end of each paragraph that ties the point back to *why this matters* for your overall argument.

Your conclusion is too short and simply restates your opening claim ("glasses are a thing of the past!") without adding anything new. Consider ending with a broader statement about the future of vision correction, or a final appeal to why this invention deserves recognition alongside other landmark 20th-century innovations.

Language and Style

A few things to revise for a more academic tone:

Ampersands (&): Replace all instances of "&" with "and." The ampersand is too informal for academic writing.
Exclamation points: You use several exclamation marks ("I'd be blind most of the time!", "who am I to say!"). These create a conversational, almost spoken tone. Academic essays generally avoid exclamation marks; let your word choice carry the emphasis instead.
Quotation marks: You use quotation marks around informal terms ("lense craze," "sting," "blind," "feel my way"). This is a common but distracting habit—use quotation marks only for direct quotes or to introduce a genuinely unusual term once, not to signal "casual phrase."
Spelling: "Lense" should be "lens" (plural: "lenses").
Subject-verb agreement: 
   - "a style called 'hard lenses' were the only kind made" → should be "was."
   - "another type of lenses were made accessible" → should be "was" (referring to "type," singular).
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences use commas where periods or semicolons are needed, eg, "Contact lenses are a part of my everyday life; something that I take for granted, yet on the other hand, something that I'd be lost without" is a fragment tacked onto a semicolon—revise to a complete sentence structure.

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis so it clearly states your argumentative claim and previews your reasoning (eg, "Because contact lenses have improved safety, independence, and quality of life for millions of people, they deserve recognition as one of the most transformative inventions of the 20th century.")
Add at least one body paragraph with evidence beyond personal experience—even a general statement about how many people worldwide wear contacts would help.
Address a counterargument directly.
Proofread for ampersands, exclamation points, and subject-verb agreement.
Strengthen your conclusion by looking outward, not just restating your opening.

You have a genuine, relatable voice—now you need to build the scaffolding of formal argument around it. Good luck with your revision!

Several claims need more precision and, in some cases, correction:

Factual issue: You mention women winning the right to vote "in the 1800s" as an exception to feminism being a 20th-century phenomenon, but in the U.S. (which seems to be your reference point given the ERA discussion), women gained the vote in 1920—squarely in the 20th century. Please verify this date, as it currently undermines your own timeline.
Vague statistics: "Something like 60 cents for every dollar" needs a source and a specific figure. Academic writing requires you to say *whose* data this is and *when* it was collected (the wage gap has changed over decades).
Vague time references: "The 70s" should be a specific year or range (the ERA was proposed in 1972 and failed to be ratified by 1982). Precision here will make your argument more credible.

Expand the Underdeveloped Third Paragraph

Your paragraph on the "image of women" raises an important point but only asserts it—it doesn't demonstrate it. You mention pornography and language as examples but don't explain *how* they shape women's image or give a concrete example (a specific word, phrase, or media example would help). This paragraph is currently the shortest and least convincing part of your essay; it deserves as much development as your historical paragraph, if not more, since it's your most contemporary and thought-provoking claim.

Reconsider Your Organization

Right now the essay moves: (1) introduce feminism as a discovery → (2) historical/economic overview → (3) image of women → (4) conclusion reframing feminism as "in progress." The jump between paragraphs 2 and 3 is abrupt—there's no transition connecting economic equality to representation/image. Consider adding a linking sentence, such as: "Equality in wages and rights is only one dimension of feminism; another concerns how women are *perceived*, not just how they are paid." This will make the structure feel more unified rather than like two separate lists of facts.

Language and Mechanics

A few specific corrections:

"0th century" should be "20th century" (likely a typo, but proofread carefully before submission).
"It was still the most typical as well as the ideal to have the woman in the home" is awkward. Consider: "It was still both the norm and the ideal for women to remain at home, keeping house and raising children."
"the woman is still only earning" — since you're speaking generally, consider "women still earn" (plural, to avoid sounding like you mean one specific woman).
Watch comma splices and run-on sentences, eg, in the final paragraph: "Only when women have achieved equality with men, will there cease to be a need for feminism" — the comma before "will" is not needed grammatically in English inversion structures; revise to "Only when women have achieved equality with men will there cease to be a need for feminism."

Conclusion Needs Earlier Groundwork

Your closing idea—that feminism is a "discovery in progress"—is compelling, but since it's introduced only in the final paragraph, it feels like a new argument rather than a natural conclusion. Consider signaling this idea in your introduction so the reader anticipates it by the end.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Tie the "discovery" metaphor more explicitly to each paragraph.
Verify/correct historical facts and dates.
Add specific sources for statistics.
Expand the paragraph on the image of women with concrete examples.
Improve transitions between paragraphs.
Proofread carefully for typos and awkward phrasing.

You have a strong conceptual idea here—now it needs fuller development and tighter organization to match its ambition. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay addresses a genuinely important topic—environmental awareness—and you've organized it with clear body paragraphs, each tackling a different aspect (nuclear waste, nature, recycling, energy, animal life). This topical organization is a strength. However, the essay needs significant development in terms of argumentation, evidence, and academic tone to meet university-level standards. Below are key areas for revision.

Strengthen Your Thesis and Argument

Your introduction states the idea (environmental awareness) but doesn't present an *argument*—a claim that needs to be defended. Currently, the essay reads more like a list of environmental concerns than a persuasive essay. Ask yourself: What is the specific claim I'm making? Consider revising your thesis to something like: "Growing environmental awareness has fundamentally reshaped policy, economics, and everyday behavior in ways no other 20th-century idea has." Then, each paragraph should work to prove this claim, not simply describe a topic.

Provide Evidence and Examples

This is the most critical area for improvement. Currently, your claims are broad and unsupported:
"Countries with many weapons have the power to destroy the world" — Which countries? What treaties or events illustrate this?
"Many groups have formed to plant more trees" — Name an organization (eg, the Rainforest Alliance, or a national reforestation campaign).
"Countries realize that recycling is beneficial" — Cite a specific country's recycling program or statistic.

Academic writing requires specific, verifiable evidence—names, dates, statistics, or documented events—rather than generalizations. Without this, your claims feel unsupported and anecdotal.

Avoid Informal Language and Abbreviations

This is an academic essay, so avoid text-message-style abbreviations:
"&" should be "and"
"w/o" should be "without"
"b/c" should be "because"

Also revise casual phrasing:
"become more considerable of" → "become more considerate of" (this is also a word-choice error—see below)
"we will have fixed everything we have destroyed!" → the exclamation point is too informal; end with a more measured, analytical statement.

Grammar and Word Choice Corrections

A few recurring errors to address:
"countries that which have" → "countries that have" (redundant relative pronouns)
"concerned with nuclear weapons" → consider "concerned about" (more natural collocation)
"We realize that conserving is not beneficial... but it is also saves money" → this sentence has a grammatical error and unclear logic. Likely you mean: "We realize that conserving not only benefits the environment but also saves money." Revise for clarity.
"hopeful by the 21st century" → "we are hopeful that by the 21st century..."
"One realizes that animals have rights too" — avoid shifting to the impersonal "one" when you've been using "we" throughout; maintain pronoun consistency.

Deepen Your Analysis

Many paragraphs state a fact and move on without analysis. For example, in the nuclear waste paragraph, you mention fines for companies but don't explain *why* this represents a meaningful shift in thinking, or what challenges remain (eg, enforcement, cost, political resistance). Push yourself to answer "so what?" after each point. This will transform description into argument.

Reconsider Paragraph Balance and Focus

You have five body paragraphs, each covering a different subtopic (weapons/waste, deforestation, recycling, energy, animals). This breadth is admirable, but it may prevent depth. Consider narrowing your focus to two or three subtopics and developing them more thoroughly with evidence and analysis, rather than covering five topics superficially. Quality of argument matters more than quantity of examples.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion currently restates that awareness is "new" and hopes we'll "fix everything," which feels vague and overly optimistic without acknowledging obstacles. A stronger conclusion might:
Briefly synthesize your strongest evidence
Acknowledge remaining challenges (political resistance, economic costs, global inequality in environmental policy)
End with a forward-looking but realistic statement, avoiding absolute claims like "fixed everything"

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add specific evidence (statistics, named organizations, historical events) to every paragraph.
Eliminate informal abbreviations and casual language.
Sharpen your thesis into an arguable claim.
Deepen analysis—explain the significance of each fact, not just state it.
Proofread for grammar issues, particularly redundant clauses and sentence structure.

With more concrete evidence and sustained analysis, your essay's good structural foundation will support a much more persuasive and academically rigorous argument. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to bring a revised draft to discuss further.

Feedback on Your Essay: "How Computers Have Affected My Life"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic you clearly know well from personal experience, and your examples (checkbook balancing, word processing, the study-abroad application) are relatable and concrete. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: developing a clearer argument, organizing your ideas more logically, and correcting language/mechanical errors. Below are specific suggestions.

Strengthen Your Argument

Your opening sentence—"Computers have definitely effected peoples lives"—is too vague to serve as a thesis. It doesn't tell the reader *what* you will argue or *how* you will support it. Consider revising your thesis to preview your specific points, for example: "Computers have transformed daily life by making information storage, calculation, and communication faster and more efficient."

More importantly, your conclusion claims something much larger than what you've actually argued: "Computers have had a most significant impact on the people in the latter half of the 20th century." This is a sweeping historical/societal claim, but your evidence is entirely personal and anecdotal (your desk, your checkbook, your application to a study-abroad program). Either:
Narrow your thesis to match your evidence (eg, "Computers have changed how I manage information and communicate"), or
Broaden your evidence to support the larger claim (eg, briefly discuss how computers have changed workplaces, education, or communication generally, not just your own experience).

Right now there's a mismatch between the modest, personal scope of your body paragraphs and the ambitious claim of your conclusion.

Improve Organization

Your two paragraphs feel like separate essays loosely connected by the word "computers." The transition "I'm now here in Madrid. How did I get here?" is abrupt and conversational—more suited to spoken storytelling than an academic essay. Consider:
Adding a topic sentence to paragraph two that explicitly connects it to your first paragraph's argument (eg, "Beyond personal tasks, computers have also streamlined institutional processes, as my own experience applying to study abroad illustrates.")
Within paragraph one, you list several benefits (storage, calculation, editing, communication) somewhat randomly. Consider organizing them by importance or by category (eg, grouping "storage and organization" together, then "processing tasks," then "communication").

Language and Mechanics

This is the area needing the most attention. A few patterns to fix:

a) Avoid abbreviations and symbols in formal writing. Words like "w/," "&," and "info" are appropriate for notes or texting, not academic essays. Always write out "with," "and," and "information."

b) Correct word confusion:
"effected" → should be "affected" (affect = to influence; effect = a result)
"peoples lives" → "people's lives" (possessive apostrophe needed)
"my life has change" → "my life has changed"
"flew here form the US" → "flew here from the US"
"the this program" → remove "the": "this program"

c) Fix run-on sentences and comma splices. For example:
> "I'm able to store almost all the information I need for school & for other things in my computer whereas before I had a computer my desk was cluttered w/ paper..."

This sentence tries to do too much at once. Break it into two sentences:
> "I am able to store almost all the information I need for school and other purposes on my computer. Before I owned one, my desk was cluttered with paper and records of all sorts."

d) Check subject-verb agreement and missing words:
> "the International Programs Office would inevitably inundated w/ paperwork"
should be:
> "the International Programs Office would inevitably be inundated with paperwork"

e) Reduce repetitive transitions. You use "Also," "Aside from," "furthermore," and "moreover" in quick succession, which feels mechanical. Vary your sentence openings and connect ideas more organically.

Suggested Revision Priorities

Rewrite your thesis to match the scope of your evidence.
Add a clear transition between paragraphs one and two.
Proofread carefully for word confusion (affect/effect) and missing words.
Eliminate all abbreviations (w/, &) and spell out full words.
Break up long run-on sentences into shorter, clearer ones.

Final Note

You have good instincts for using concrete, specific examples—this is a real strength. With careful editing for mechanics and a tighter, more consistent argument, this essay will be much stronger. I recommend reading your revised draft aloud; this often helps catch run-ons and missing words that are easy to overlook when writing quickly.

Feedback on Your Essay on Satellite Technology

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and thought-provoking topic—the transformative power of satellite technology—and your example of the Tiananmen Square protests is compelling and relevant. You clearly have a strong opinion and some good instincts for using historical events to support an argument. However, the essay needs significant revision in three main areas: structure, argument development, and language mechanics.

Structure and Organization

Currently, your entire essay is a single paragraph. For an argumentative essay, readers expect a clear structure:

Introduction: State your thesis clearly. Right now, "the geo-centric satellite is the invention of the century" appears as your opening claim, but it's very broad. Consider narrowing your focus—perhaps to the satellite's role in political transparency and human rights, which is where your evidence actually leads.
Body paragraphs: Break your ideas into distinct paragraphs. For instance, one paragraph could address the historical context (radio, TV, videotape), another could develop the Tiananmen Square example in depth, and another could address the long-term implications you mention (governments behaving better because they're "watched").
Conclusion: Your final sentence ("Plus when China is placed down the block...") feels abrupt and underdeveloped. A conclusion should synthesize your argument and leave the reader with a clear final thought, not introduce a new idea in casual language.

Argument Development

Your central claim—that satellites promote global accountability and democracy by providing "nowness"—is promising, but needs more support:

One example is not enough. You mention Tiananmen Square, but a strong argumentative essay typically needs 2-3 well-developed examples or a mix of evidence (historical events, statistics, expert opinions) to be persuasive.

Address counterarguments. You claim satellites will make "governments and people alike... less tempted to do wrong," but this is a strong prediction that needs qualification. Are there counterexamples where global visibility did *not* prevent injustice? Acknowledging this complexity would strengthen your credibility.

Clarify causal claims. You state "It is reasonable to conclude" that world demands for China's compliance came *because* of the satellite—but you don't explain the mechanism. How exactly did visibility translate into diplomatic pressure? A sentence or two of explanation would help.

Avoid absolute language. Phrases like "the invention of the century" and "it is certain" are difficult to defend and can weaken your credibility. Consider more measured language: "one of the most significant inventions" or "it seems likely that..."

Language and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address in your revision:

Spelling: "evention" → invention; "preceed" → precede; "openned" → opened; "litterally" → literally; "Teniman" → Tiananmen; "sensorship" → censorship; "mor" → more
Word choice/typos: "he young hero" → the young hero; "a like" → alike
Punctuation: "Freedom US. the tank" — this should likely be "Freedom vs. the tank," and needs to be integrated into a full sentence rather than standing alone as a fragment.
Register: Phrases like "Plus when China is placed down the block, who wouldn't want to help his neighbor?" are too colloquial for academic writing. Consider a more formal way to express this idea, such as: "As global neighbors become more visible to one another, the international community may feel a greater sense of responsibility to intervene."

Specific Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement for your introduction. Try something like: "The advent of satellite technology has fundamentally transformed global political accountability by making injustice instantly visible to the world."

Expand your Tiananmen Square paragraph. You have good material here—develop it with more specific details (dates, the "Tank Man" image's global impact, specific government reactions).

Add at least one more example — perhaps a more recent event, or a contrasting case where satellite coverage did *not* lead to change, which would show more nuanced thinking.

Rewrite the conclusion to summarize your argument and end with a forward-looking, formal statement rather than the current casual question.

Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar errors, or ask a native-speaking friend or tutor to help you catch these.

Final Note

Your ideas here are genuinely interesting, and the Tiananmen Square connection is a strong choice. With more structural organization, deeper development of your evidence, and careful editing for language errors, this essay has the potential to make a compelling argument. I encourage you to revise with these suggestions in mind and feel free to bring a draft to office hours for further discussion.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Impact of Computers"

Thank you for submitting this essay. You've chosen an interesting and relevant topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. Below is feedback organized by area, with specific suggestions for revision.

Argumentative Focus

This assignment calls for an argumentative essay, but your current draft reads more like a descriptive/informative piece. You list ways computers have been helpful, but you don't make a debatable claim or engage with any counterargument. To strengthen this:

Develop a clear thesis that takes a position someone could disagree with. For example: "While critics worry that computers make students overly dependent on technology, computers have overwhelmingly improved education by enabling more efficient research, writing, and personalized learning."
Consider addressing a counterargument — for instance, concerns about computers reducing critical thinking, students' typing skills, or increased distraction. Even briefly acknowledging and refuting an opposing view will make your essay far more persuasive.

Structure and Development

Your paragraphs are organized by topic (companies, students, teaching tools), which is a reasonable structure. However:

The introduction doesn't clearly state your argument — it currently just describes computers in general. Revise it to end with a strong thesis statement.
Each body paragraph would benefit from more specific, developed examples. Right now, claims like "computers have been a god send for large companies" are asserted rather than demonstrated. Consider adding a concrete example (a statistic, a named company, a specific software) to support your point.
The paragraph about learning tools ("Biology to understanding of the cosm graph in Mathematics") is unclear — I'm not sure what "cosm graph" refers to. Please clarify this example or replace it with one you're confident explaining fully.
The conclusion is underdeveloped. Ending with "who knows where these machines will take us" is vague and doesn't reinforce your argument. Try restating your thesis in new words and leaving the reader with a stronger final thought — perhaps a call to action or a forward-looking claim tied to your argument.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address in revision:

Spelling:
"definantly" → *definitely*
"accesible" → *accessible*
"previosly" → *previously*
"extend" → *extent* (in "The extend to which computers...")

Word choice/register:
"god send" should be one word: *godsend*. Also, this is a fairly informal expression — consider a more academic phrase, such as "invaluable resource."
"w/o" is a text-message abbreviation; write it out as *without* in formal writing.
"imp" appears to be an abbreviation for *important* — always spell words out fully in academic essays.

Grammar:
"computers enabled to students" → should be *enabled students* (remove "to")
"but they is clearly imp" → subject-verb agreement error; should be *they are*
Watch for run-on sentences and comma splices, eg, "Students no longer have to spend hours trying to type their paper w/o making spelling errors, now they can focus..." — this should either be split into two sentences or joined with a conjunction: "...spelling errors; now they can focus..."
The last paragraph ends with a comma instead of a period after "previosly used," — check punctuation at paragraph breaks.

Style Suggestions

Several sentences begin with "Computers..." — try varying your sentence openings to improve flow (eg, "Thanks to computers, students no longer..." or "This technology has also transformed...").
Avoid absolute language like "computers are revolutionary" without qualifying it — academic writing often benefits from measured claims (eg, "computers have significantly changed how students write").

Summary of Priority Revisions

Add a clear, arguable thesis statement and consider including a counterargument.
Expand your examples with specific, concrete details in each body paragraph.
Clarify the unclear reference to "cosm graph."
Strengthen your conclusion by restating your argument, not just trailing off.
Proofread carefully for the spelling and grammar issues noted above — reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.

You have good, real-world examples to work with (libraries, hospitals, law firms, student writing). With a clearer argumentative angle and more developed evidence, this essay has strong potential. I encourage you to revise with these points in mind and feel free to bring a draft to office hours if you'd like to discuss your thesis further.

Feedback on "The Impact of Television" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and debatable topic, and your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. You make some genuinely insightful points—particularly about TV enabling real-time, unmediated access to events versus the delayed, distorted news of the past. However, the essay would benefit from stronger organization, clearer argumentation, and attention to sentence-level grammar. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Content and Argumentation

Strengths: Your comparison between pre-television news (slow, filtered through interpretation) and television news (immediate, direct) is compelling and well-explained. The "global village" concept is a nice touch, though it needs a proper citation (who is this journalist? This appears to be Marshall McLuhan—you should name him).

Areas for improvement:

Thesis statement: Your essay lacks a clear, arguable thesis. Right now, your opening simply states television is important; it doesn't tell the reader *what argument* you will make about its impact. Consider revising your first paragraph to explicitly state your claim—for example: "Television has fundamentally transformed society by democratizing access to information and unifying disparate cultures, despite legitimate concerns about its distorting effects on our perception of reality."

Underdeveloped counterargument: Your final paragraph raises an important concern—that TV glorifies violence and presents an unrealistic view of consequences—but you dismiss it too quickly with an unsupported claim ("this sense of reality is not so distorted"). This is your weakest point argumentatively. *Why* isn't it distorted? What evidence or reasoning supports this? A stronger essay would either engage more seriously with this counterargument (acknowledging the real dangers, then explaining why the benefits outweigh them) or would need more evidence to support your dismissal.

Missing conclusion: The essay ends abruptly after your point about "a well-developed sense of reality." You need a concluding paragraph that ties your two main points (information access and entertainment) back to your central argument and leaves the reader with a final, memorable statement.

Lack of concrete examples: Your claims are general and abstract. Can you name a specific historical event that was significant precisely *because* it was televised in real time (eg, the Vietnam War, the moon landing, a major political scandal)? Concrete, specific examples will make your argument far more persuasive than hypothetical scenarios ("if something is happening...").

Organization

Your first paragraph is doing too much work—it introduces the topic, discusses the news function, discusses cultural exchange, *and* alludes to the global village concept, all in one very long paragraph. Consider breaking this into two paragraphs: one focused on news/information, and one on cultural connection.

Your overall structure should probably follow this shape:
Introduction with a clear thesis
Body paragraph 1: TV and information/news
Body paragraph 2: TV and cultural connection (the "global village")
Body paragraph 3: TV and entertainment (including the counterargument)
Conclusion

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

Several of your sentences are overloaded with clauses connected by dashes, semicolons, and commas, which makes them hard to follow. For example:

> "While previously people had to wait days, or even weeks for news (which consisted of second-or third-hand accounts, which always involved much interpretation & distortion) now they can see it happening, for themselves, as it is happening, this allows them to make their own, informed judgment about the event; and if a reaction is necessary..."

This sentence tries to do too much. Break it into two or three shorter sentences for clarity.

Other issues to address in revision:
Avoid using "&" in academic writing—write out "and."
Comma splices appear frequently (eg, "...as it is happening, this allows them..." should have a period or semicolon, not a comma).
"make life seem the good guy always wins" is grammatically unclear—consider: "suggest that the good guy always wins."
Check subject-verb agreement in longer sentences where the subject and verb are separated by many words.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Write a clear thesis statement in your introduction.
Break the first paragraph into two focused paragraphs.
Add a proper conclusion.
Strengthen your response to the counterargument about violence/unrealistic portrayals with more specific reasoning or evidence.
Add at least one or two concrete, real-world examples.
Revise long, comma-spliced sentences into shorter, clearer ones.
Cite the journalist you reference for "global village" (likely Marshall McLuhan).

You have good ideas here—the revision process should focus on organizing them more clearly and supporting them with stronger evidence.

Feedback on Your Essay: "The Microwave Revolution"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging topic and your extended metaphor comparing culinary history to "seas" and "waves" shows creativity. Your enthusiasm for the subject comes through clearly. However, the essay needs revision in several key areas: argumentative structure, evidence, and language accuracy. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Content and Argumentation

Strengths: Your historical overview (fire → spits → stoves → microwave) effectively sets up the "break from tradition" argument. The point about eliminating fire hazards for children is a nice, specific detail.

Areas for Improvement:

Thesis clarity: Your essay seems to argue two related but distinct claims: (1) the microwave broke from tradition, and (2) the microwave saves time. Consider explicitly stating both claims in your introduction so readers know what to expect.

Evidence and support: Your claims would be much stronger with concrete evidence. For example:
   - *How much* time does the microwave actually save? A percentage or specific comparison would help.
   - Can you cite when the microwave was invented and by whom? This would ground your historical claims.
   - The "working mother" example is a nice illustration, but consider whether this reflects a stereotype. You might broaden this to discuss time-pressed individuals more generally.

Counterarguments: A stronger essay would acknowledge some limitations of the microwave (eg, debates about nutrient loss, uneven heating, or the argument that some traditional cooking methods remain superior for flavor). Addressing a counterargument—even briefly—would make your argument more persuasive and sophisticated.

The "revolutionized food preparation" claim: Be careful with absolute statements like "never before has such an invention revolutionized a science like this." This is a strong claim—do you want to defend it against, say, refrigeration or pasteurization? Consider softening slightly or providing a comparative point.

Structure

Your essay has a clear introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion, which is good. However:

The conclusion currently restates ideas without adding new insight. Try ending with a forward-looking statement: What does the microwave's success suggest about future kitchen innovations? Or reflect on what "progress" in food preparation might mean next.
Consider adding a topic sentence to each body paragraph that clearly signals its focus (eg, "The microwave's most significant break from tradition lies in its cooking method" vs. "The microwave's practical benefit is time efficiency").

Language and Style

Since you're writing in a second language, here are some specific corrections to focus on:

Spelling/Word choice:
"culunary" → culinary
"consistant" → consistent
"twentyth" → twentieth
"rythm" → rhythm
"comodity" → commodity
"differents" → different
"dizzing" → dizzying
"fool of mankind" → likely meant "food of mankind"

Grammar:
"Progress has move slow" → "Progress has moved slowly"
"the constant rythm...have showed little changed" → "...has shown little change"
"Never before has such an invention revolutionized a science like this" — check subject-verb agreement and clarity
"so as to staple" → unclear; perhaps "as a staple" or "became the staple"

Punctuation and capitalization:
Avoid capitalizing "History" mid-sentence unless referring to a specific title
The use of "FIRE" in all caps for emphasis is informal; consider italics or restructuring the sentence for emphasis instead
"For As I have said before" — this seems to be a typo/leftover fragment; revise for clarity

Register: Some phrases are quite informal for academic writing ("whip up something," "slaving in the kitchen," "or bewitched, or under the influence of heavy drugs"). While vivid, these might be toned down slightly for a more academic register, or you could keep some for stylistic flair if this is meant to be a more creative/personal essay style.

Next Steps

Revise your thesis to clearly state both main claims.
Add at least one piece of concrete evidence (a date, statistic, or named source).
Address one counterargument.
Proofread carefully for the spelling and grammar issues listed above—reading your essay aloud can help catch errors.

You have good ideas and a clear voice—now let's sharpen the argument and polish the language to match your creativity!

Feedback Report: Draft on the Impact of Radio

Overall Impression
Your essay has an interesting starting point—the idea that the machine gun and radio enabled repressive governments—but the essay does not follow through on this idea. Instead, it shifts into a general discussion of how radio (and later television) has shaped everyday life through information and entertainment. This creates a disconnect between your introduction and the rest of the essay that needs to be resolved before the essay will feel unified and persuasive.

Argument and Focus
State a clear thesis. Right now, readers don't know what you are arguing. Are you arguing that radio enabled government control (as your teacher suggested), or that radio transformed daily life through information and entertainment? These are two different essays. Choose one focus and make it explicit in your introduction. For example: *"While radio can be used as a tool of political control, its greater impact has been on how ordinary people access information and entertainment."* This kind of sentence would let you use the "repressive government" idea as a hook, while signaling that your real argument is about everyday life.

Follow through on ideas you introduce. The comment that radio "gives people the option of becoming passive observers rather than active participants" is one of the most interesting and complex claims in your essay—it suggests a *negative* consequence of radio, which connects nicely back to your opening point about propaganda and control. But you drop this idea immediately after stating it. Develop it: What are the effects of passivity? Does this connect to how dictators use radio to manipulate a population that has stopped thinking critically? This could become the analytical core of your essay.

Distinguish radio from television. Your last two paragraphs are really about television, not radio. If your essay is about radio specifically, you need to either limit your discussion to radio or explicitly justify the shift to television (eg, "radio's greatest legacy is arguably the technology it made possible: television"). Right now the shift feels like a change of topic rather than a deliberate move in your argument.

Strengthen your evidence. Personal anecdotes (your morning weather routine, your great-grandmother's love of TV) are effective for illustrating a point, but they cannot carry the whole argument. Consider adding a historical example (eg, FDR's radio addresses, wartime propaganda broadcasts, or the moon landing broadcast) to support your claims about radio's power to inform and unify—or manipulate—large populations.

Organization
Your paragraphs are very short and often contain only one idea without development. Try to build each paragraph around a clear topic sentence, followed by explanation and evidence, and a concluding thought that connects back to your thesis.
The conclusion ("I don't know what life was like before radio...") is too general. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis and clarify what you have shown the reader—ideally circling back to the "repressive government" idea from your introduction, since that is how you began.

Language and Mechanics
A few recurring issues need attention in revision:
Apostrophes: "don' t," "it' s" — check spacing and usage. "It's" should only be used for "it is"; for possession, use "its" (eg, "for its killings").
Capitalization/terminology: "Marshall Law" should be "martial law."
Spelling: "forcast" → "forecast."
Word choice: "my señora" is unclear to an English-speaking reader—do you mean your grandmother or great-grandmother? Consider clarifying, since this is a key example.
Punctuation: Several sentences use commas where periods or semicolons are needed (comma splices), eg, "A typical day for a child in today's world, consists of going to school & coming home to watch T.V." Avoid using "&" in formal writing—write out "and."
Consistency: Use "TV" or "T.V." consistently, not both.

Suggestions for Revision
Rewrite your introduction to state a clear, specific thesis.
Decide whether your essay is about radio's role in political control, or its role in shaping daily life through information/entertainment—or explicitly connect both ideas.
Develop the "passive observer" idea further, since it's your most original insight.
Either limit your body paragraphs to radio, or justify your shift to television.

Feedback on Your Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay has a clear, relatable topic and includes an engaging personal anecdote. The writing shows genuine voice and personality, which is a strength. However, the essay needs significant revision in three main areas: thesis clarity and argumentative focus, structure/paragraphing, and grammar/mechanics. Right now, the essay reads more like a personal reflection than an argumentative essay, since it lacks a clear claim that you are trying to persuade readers to accept.

Content and Argument

The assignment asks for an argumentative essay, but your essay doesn't take a firm position. You present the microwave as useful, then mention your great-uncle's view that it's unnecessary, but you never clearly argue for one side or synthesize these viewpoints into a thesis. Ask yourself: What is your claim? Possible options include:

"Despite objections from those who value traditional cooking, the microwave has become an essential tool for modern families."
"Technology like the microwave doesn't eliminate the value of traditional cooking; it simply gives families more flexibility."

Once you choose a clear thesis, every paragraph should work to support it. Right now, the great-uncle story reads as a counterargument, but you don't analyze or rebut it—you simply state "a microwave isn't right for everyone" and move on. For a stronger essay, address *why* his view differs from yours, and explain why your position remains valid despite this counterpoint. This is called "counterargument and rebuttal," and it's essential to argumentative writing.

Suggestion: Add a rebuttal paragraph. For example, you could argue that appreciating homemade food and using a microwave aren't mutually exclusive—families can do both depending on the occasion.

Structure

Your essay currently has many very short paragraphs (some are just one sentence), which fragments your ideas rather than developing them fully. Combine related ideas into more developed paragraphs. For example:

Merge your introduction sentences with your microwave explanation.
Combine the anecdote about your great-uncle into one flowing paragraph rather than breaking it into three separate ones.

A clearer structure might look like this:
Introduction – Introduce technology's impact and state your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1 – Explain how microwaves help modern families (your example).
Body Paragraph 2 – Present the counterargument (great-uncle's view).
Body Paragraph 3 – Rebut the counterargument and reinforce your thesis.
Conclusion – Restate your position and its broader significance.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several grammar and punctuation issues to address:

Apostrophes for possession: "peoples lives" → "people's lives"
Capitalization: "great Uncle and Aunt" → "great-uncle and great-aunt" (lowercase when not part of a name, and use a hyphen)
Verb agreement/tense: "we each take turns cook" → "we each take turns cooking"
Spelling: "recieved" → "received" (remember: "i before e except after c")
Awkward phrasing: "But the point is, is that a microwave isn't right for everyone." → Remove the repeated "is": "But the point is that a microwave isn't right for everyone."
Comma splices/run-ons: Some sentences need periods or conjunctions instead of commas.
Hyphenation inconsistency: "microwave" vs. "micro-wave"—choose one spelling and stick with it (the standard is "microwave," no hyphen).

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, which often helps catch these small errors.

Style Suggestions

Avoid starting too many sentences with "In fact," "For instance," or similar transitional phrases without variety. Mix up your sentence openings for better flow.
The phrase "The long and short of the story" is a nice idiomatic expression, but make sure your conclusion also ties back explicitly to your thesis, not just to the anecdote.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Develop a clear thesis statement that takes a definite position.
Add a rebuttal to the counterargument to strengthen your argumentative stance.
Combine short paragraphs into fuller, more developed ones.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors.
Strengthen the conclusion to clearly restate your argument and its significance.

You have good material here—an authentic example and an interesting counterpoint—but the essay needs a stronger argumentative backbone to fulfill the assignment. Focus on clarifying your position and using your anecdotes as *evidence* for that position, rather than as standalone reflections.

Keep up the good work, and don't hesitate to reach out if you'd like to discuss revision strategies further!
