Feedback Report: ATM Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay on ATMs and their impact on modern life. You've chosen a relevant topic and have some good ideas, but the essay needs development in several key areas to become a stronger piece of argumentative writing. Below is detailed feedback organized by category.

Content and Argumentation

Your central claim—that ATMs have significantly improved people's lives—is clear, but the essay needs more substantial development to be persuasive. Currently, you provide only brief, general examples (forgetting your wallet, avoiding large amounts of cash at home). To strengthen your argument, consider:

Adding concrete evidence: Statistics about ATM usage, historical context about when ATMs were introduced, or specific examples of how banking changed before/after ATMs would add credibility.
Expanding your counterargument: You mention that "people may argue that ATM machines will take away employment in banks," but you dismiss this in one sentence without genuine engagement. A strong argumentative essay addresses opposing views seriously before refuting them. Why is the convenience worth the job losses? Are there other jobs created by ATM technology?
Developing the danger/robbery point: This is an interesting angle, but it's underdeveloped. How exactly do ATMs reduce robbery risk? This deserves more explanation.

Structure and Organization

Your essay follows a basic five-paragraph structure, which is a good foundation, but consider these improvements:

Introduction: Currently combines your thesis with your counterargument dismissal. Consider separating these ideas more clearly, and perhaps save the counterargument for its own paragraph in the body.
Body paragraphs: Your second paragraph mixes multiple ideas (locations of ATMs, personal example, robbery prevention) without clear transitions. Each paragraph should ideally focus on ONE main idea, developed with specific examples.
Conclusion: Your final paragraph introduces a new idea ("computerized technology has enhanced our environment") that feels disconnected from the ATM-specific argument. Conclusions should synthesize what you've already argued, not introduce new claims.

Language and Grammar

There are several recurring issues to address:

Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense. For example: "which has significantly changed" (present perfect) vs. "would be without" (conditional) vs. "of the late 1900's" (past reference) but "which is very important" (present). Since ATMs remain relevant today, consider maintaining present tense throughout when discussing their current impact.

Word choice/phrasing:
   - "I can not comprehend how difficult it would be" — consider "I cannot imagine how difficult it would be"
   - "convenience at access to money" — this phrasing is unclear; perhaps "convenient access to money"
   - "This machinery" — ATMs aren't typically called "machinery" in this context; "This technology" or "This system" flows better
   - "entices robbers" — reconsider "entices"; perhaps "attracts" or "makes you a target for"

Sentence-level errors:
   - "I can not" should be "cannot" (one word)
   - Missing comma before "which" in several instances (eg, after "money" in the first paragraph)
   - "of the late 1900's" — this apostrophe usage is incorrect for a decade; should be "1900s" (no apostrophe)

Paragraph transitions: Words like "however," "furthermore," "in addition," or "for instance" would help connect your ideas more smoothly between sentences and paragraphs.

Formatting

Remove the dash (--) before your final sentence; this creates an unclear connection to your conclusion paragraph. If this final idea belongs in your conclusion, integrate it smoothly with a transition.
Check paragraph indentation for consistency (you have tabs before "It is also," "ATM machines," and "All in all," but not before the first paragraph or the last sentence).

Priority Revisions

If you focus on three things for your next draft, I recommend:

Expand your counterargument into its own well-developed paragraph with a genuine concession and rebuttal.
Add specific, concrete details (examples, data, or personal anecdotes) to support each main point.
Revise your conclusion so it summarizes your specific argument about ATMs rather than shifting to a broader, disconnected claim about technology.

Final Thoughts

You have a solid foundation with a clear opinion and personal connection to the topic. With more developed evidence, stronger paragraph organization, and attention to grammar/word choice, this essay can become a much more convincing argumentative piece. I encourage you to revise with these suggestions in mind and feel free to bring a revised draft to office hours if you'd like additional feedback.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Cellular Telephone

Thank you for sharing this essay on an interesting and relevant topic. You have some good ideas and a nice personal anecdote, but the essay needs stronger organization and a clearer argumentative structure. Below is feedback to help you revise.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction states that the cell phone is "a complete luxury and not a necessity." This sounds like your thesis, but the essay doesn't consistently argue this point. Instead, the body paragraphs describe *various effects* of cell phones (availability, accidents, cost, amazing technology) without clearly connecting each point back to the luxury vs. necessity debate.

Suggestion: Decide what you actually want to argue. Do you want to:
Argue that cell phones are unnecessary luxuries (and use the accident/cost paragraphs as evidence)?
Or explore the mixed effects of cell phones on society (positive and negative)?

Right now the essay tries to do both, which weakens your argument. Choose one clear position and make sure every paragraph supports it.

Paragraph Organization

Each paragraph currently covers too many ideas:

Paragraph 2 discusses availability/convenience — this is good, but it seems to *contradict* your thesis (if it's so convenient, isn't it becoming a necessity for some people?). Address this tension directly.
Paragraph 3 combines two very different ideas — accidents caused by phone use, and the cost of phones. These deserve separate paragraphs since they are distinct arguments.
Paragraph 4 (the anecdote about your family) is your most vivid, specific writing, but it currently feels disconnected from the rest of the essay. What point does this story support? Is it evidence that phones are amazing and useful (supporting necessity), or just a fun fact? Make its purpose clear, and connect it explicitly to your thesis.

Suggestion: Use topic sentences that clearly state the main idea of each paragraph, and make sure each paragraph has ONE controlling idea.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new topic (robots) that isn't connected to the cell phone discussion. While the idea of future technology is interesting, it currently makes the essay feel unfinished rather than concluded. A strong conclusion should:
Restate your thesis in light of the evidence you presented
Remind the reader of your strongest points
End with a thought that grows naturally from your argument (not a completely new topic)

Suggestion: You could end by reflecting on whether, given the evidence you've presented (convenience vs. danger/cost), you still believe cell phones are a luxury — or whether your own anecdote makes you reconsider.

Language and Mechanics

A few points to polish:

Punctuation with lists: "cars & on the streets" and "friends houses" — use "and" instead of "&" in formal writing, and remember apostrophes for possessives ("friends' houses").
Dashes and formatting: In the anecdote paragraph, avoid using a dash after "Madrid" — use a comma or period instead. Also, "party line" doesn't need quotation marks unless you're specifically defining a technical term for the reader.
Wordiness/Redundancy: "Whether you are making a call from the cellular phone or receiving a call, it costs a lot of money" could be shortened to "Both making and receiving calls cost a lot of money."
Logical connector needed: The sentence "Although most people who can afford to buy a cellular phone, can afford to pay the bills!" is a sentence fragment and also seems to contradict your point about high cost. Clarify: are you saying the cost isn't really a problem after all? If so, this undercuts your earlier argument — revise for consistency.
Exclamation points: You use several (eg, "especially when one person is in Spain and another is in a car!!"). In academic writing, minimize exclamation points; let your word choice convey enthusiasm instead.

Overall Suggestions for Revision

Narrow your thesis to one clear, arguable claim.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has a single, clear purpose that supports your thesis.
Connect the anecdote explicitly to your argument.
Revise the conclusion so it grows out of your argument rather than introducing a new topic.
Proofread carefully for punctuation, sentence fragments, and informal symbols (&).

You have engaging content and a genuine personal voice — with tighter organization and a clearer argumentative thread, this essay will be much more persuasive. Keep up the good work!

Overall Impression

This makes the essay read as one-sided advocacy rather than genuine analysis. For a stronger argumentative essay, consider:

Acknowledging any real limitations or unanswered problems in the two-model position, even briefly, to build credibility
Revising the thesis to reflect a more nuanced claim, if that's truly your position, or making clear this is a persuasive essay by design

There are numerous placeholder marks () throughout the essay where quotations or specific claims should appear (eg, the Darwin quote, Carleton's quote, Bliss's conclusion, the anonymous creationist quote). As it stands, a reader cannot evaluate your evidence at all. Prioritize:

Including a full reference list/works cited page

The legal argument (Mississippi law, Supreme Court case) needs more explanation. Readers unfamiliar with U.S. education law will not understand the relevance of the objective-study-of-religion ruling or why an anti-evolution law in one state matters to your argument. Structure and Transitions

The essay is organized around discrete "arguments" (reasoning, authority, evidence, rebuttal), which is a reasonable structure. To improve coherence:

Add clearer topic sentences that preview each paragraph's specific contribution to the overall claim
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs (eg, "In addition to this reasoning..." or "Beyond citing authorities, advocates also...")
The conclusion currently restates your thesis but doesn't synthesize *what specifically* was proven; add a brief summary of your strongest 2-3 points before recommending "next steps"

"E=MC²" — check formatting/encoding issues in special characters
"learned fro ma two-model approach" → "learned from a two-model approach"
"where as evolutionists" → "whereas evolutionists"

Priority for Revision

These two positions contradict each other, and a reader finishes the essay unsure what you actually believe.

Your conclusion offers a third, more nuanced position — euthanasia is acceptable only when the illness is truly incurable, but not when recovery is possible. This is actually your most defensible and interesting claim. There are several bracketed placeholders () where quotations or specific details seem to be missing — for example, "Some say that in cases of euthanasia they " and "The person suffering should remember ." These gaps make key sentences unreadable and leave your argument incomplete. Before submitting, make sure every sentence is finished with actual content, ideally specific evidence or quotations from your sources with citations (author, page/paragraph, or article title).

However, this framework appears only in the middle paragraphs and then disappears; the Cruzan and "gift of life" sections don't return to it. Consider:
Introducing this values/consequences distinction in your thesis paragraph.
Explicitly labeling which of your own arguments are "value-based" (eg, God-given freedom) versus "consequence-based" (eg, fear of euthanasia becoming "a habit").
Explaining *why* you think a consequence-based debate would change public opinion — this claim is asserted but never developed.

Improve Paragraph Transitions and Unity

Right now the essay reads as a sequence of loosely connected topics: definition → values debate → Kevorkian case → media tone → Cruzan case → religious argument → personal conclusion. Each paragraph has useful material, but the connections between them need explicit signposting. For example:
Before the Kevorkian paragraph, add a sentence explaining *why* this case illustrates the values debate you just described.
Before the religious argument, connect it back to your thesis: is this argument for or against choice? Right now it seems to support restricting euthanasia, which needs to be reconciled with your opening claim.

Language and Style Notes

You have many strong vocabulary choices ("ignited a fiery debate," "precipitous"), but some recurring errors affect clarity:

Article errors: "an complex issue" → "a complex issue"; "en a painful life" appears to be a typo, likely for "end."
Pronoun consistency: You alternate awkwardly between "his or her," "him or her," and "he or she" — this is fine for gender neutrality, but be consistent and avoid repeating it excessively in the same sentence (eg, "the giving of an easy, painless death to one suffering... whether or not to end his or her pain with death or to try to deal with it" — consider simplifying to "whether to end the pain through death or continue to endure it").
"than" vs. "then": "if the person is suffering so much... than that should be the patient's personal decision" — this should be "then."
Spelling consistency: "Kevorkian" is spelled correctly once and "Kervokian" elsewhere — proofread names carefully.
Wordy/unclear phrasing: "Society always has interest in speaking up on the lack of societal values than to speak of the consequences" is grammatically unclear. Consider: "Society seems more interested in debating the erosion of moral values than in examining the practical consequences of euthanasia."
Citation format: When quoting the Center for Medical Ethics director, make sure you introduce his name (Siegler) before using it, and format quotations consistently with quotation marks and citation of source.

Suggested Revision Priorities

Decide on ONE clear, consistent thesis (I'd suggest the nuanced "curable vs. incurable" position from your conclusion).
Fill in all missing quotations/evidence where  appears.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one visibly supports your thesis, with a transition sentence at the start of each new paragraph.

You have real ethical insight and a good instinct for using case studies as evidence. With a clearer thesis and tighter organization, this essay will be much more persuasive. I'm happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay: Capital Punishment Argument

Your essay tackles a complex and controversial topic with a clear thesis and organized structure. You've done solid work identifying key arguments on both sides. Below are suggestions to strengthen your essay further.

Strengths
Your thesis is clear: you argue that the anti-death penalty position is stronger, while acknowledging the pro-death penalty side has some merit.
You organize arguments logically, addressing "eye for an eye," deterrence, and the value of human life systematically.
You use outside sources (Hoyler, Nygaard, Conway, Reiman) to support your claims, which shows research effort.

Areas for Revision

Missing Citations
The placeholders  throughout your essay indicate missing quotations that never made it into this draft. This is a critical issue—without these quotes, readers cannot evaluate your evidence, and your argument feels incomplete. Please insert the actual quotations before submitting, and make sure each one is properly integrated with quotation marks, page numbers, and correct citation format (MLA, APA, etc, depending on your assignment requirements).

Logical Consistency
There's a tension in your argument that needs addressing. You claim capital punishment "lowers the value of human life" and makes society "just as criminal" as the murderer—but you also argue elsewhere that a criminal's life is "generally considered to be of less value than the life of an innocent man." These two claims seem to contradict each other. If you believe in the sanctity of all human life (including criminals'), you cannot also suggest that criminals' lives are inherently worth less. Clarify your actual position: Is it that *all* life has value, or that criminal lives have *less* value but killing them is still wrong? This needs to be resolved for your argument to feel coherent.

Emotional Language vs. Argumentative Rigor
Phrases like "capital punishment does not benefit anyone" or "society simply craves violence" are strong claims stated as fact but lack supporting evidence in this draft. In academic argumentative writing, especially when arguing a controversial topic, unsupported assertions weaken your credibility. Consider:
Adding statistics on deterrence (or lack thereof)
Citing wrongful conviction data, which is one of the strongest arguments against capital punishment
Being more measured in phrasing: "some argue" or "evidence suggests" rather than absolute statements

Underdeveloped Pro-Death Penalty Arguments
You state you'll show both sides have "strengths and weaknesses," but the pro-death penalty section is notably shorter and less developed than the opposition section. To make your comparative argument more persuasive, spend more time genuinely engaging with the strongest version of the opposing argument (this is called a "steel man" approach) before refuting it. Right now, readers may feel you're presenting a weak version of the pro-death penalty argument to make your side look stronger by comparison.

Conclusion Needs Strengthening
Your final paragraph introduces some new and somewhat unsupported claims ("society simply craves violence," "effective destruction of civilization") that feel more like rhetorical flourishes than logical conclusions. A strong conclusion should:
Briefly restate your main argument
Synthesize your key points (not introduce brand new claims)
End with a clear, evidence-based call to action or final thought

Consider revising this paragraph to summarize the deterrence and moral arguments you've already made, rather than introducing the new idea of societal "violence cravings," which isn't substantiated elsewhere in your essay.

Sentence-Level and Word Choice Issues
"The highest sentence that can be enforced" — consider "imposed" instead of "enforced" for more precise legal terminology.
Watch for repetitive phrasing like "This presents a strong point" and "These concepts...prove to act as relative strengths"—vary your transitions.
"It is a known fact that for most people, the biggest fear in life is the fear of death" — this is an assumption, not a fact. Rephrase as "Many assume..." or provide a source.

Next Steps
Insert all missing quotations and citations
Resolve the contradiction about the value of criminals' lives
Strengthen the pro-death penalty section for better balance
Add concrete evidence (statistics, case studies) to support deterrence claims
Revise the conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new claims

Your argument has a solid foundation—focus on tightening the logic and filling in the evidentiary gaps to make it truly persuasive.

Overall Impression

However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of structure, argumentation, and clarity before it fully succeeds. Below are the main areas to focus on.

Clarify this link, or consider trimming the opening anecdote so it more directly sets up your argument.

Missing Citations

This is the most urgent technical issue: your essay contains numerous  placeholders where quotations should be. Executive Order 10925, Shaw's definition, Gillespie's arguments, Beer's quotation—these are the evidentiary backbone of your essay, and without them, readers cannot evaluate your claims. Please insert the actual quotations (with proper citations) before this essay is considered complete. You assert that opponents "admit racism exists" by criticizing affirmative action's failures—this is a clever move, but it needs more unpacking. For example: "Beside the fact that the opponents make unfair generalizations, they ignore the fact that blacks probably got into these situations as a result of discrimination." A few claims need more support:
You state affirmative action "is not unconstitutional if carried out correctly," but don't explain what "correctly" means or cite the legal standard (eg, strict scrutiny, narrow tailoring).
Your point about racial separation increasing tension is important but circular as written: "the races will remain separate until affirmative action gives blacks a chance to inhabit the same positions." Word confusion: "tents" should be "tenets"; "stope" should be "stop"; "morals" likely should be "morale" (check your intended meaning); "achie ved" has a stray space.
Article usage: "achieve of their own merit" should be "achieve on their own merit" or simply "achieve through their own merit."
Subject-verb agreement: "writer's need" should be "writers need" (also, avoid the apostrophe—it's not possessive here).
Consider having a native-speaking peer or writing center tutor proofread your final draft for these smaller issues.

Keep this, but make sure your final paragraph doesn't introduce new claims (like the "expose both cultures" point) without having developed them earlier in the body.

Strengthen the constitutional/legal analysis with more of your own reasoning.
Deepen counterargument engagement—don't just dismiss opposing claims; refute them with evidence.

Feedback on Your Essay: "New Age" Argument Analysis

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've clearly invested time in researching this topic, and your enthusiasm comes through. However, there are several significant issues with argumentation, structure, and objectivity that need attention before this essay can be considered academically strong. Let me walk through the key areas for revision.

Clarify Your Purpose and Thesis

Your thesis states you'll show that the case *against* teaching New Age ideas is stronger than the case *for* it. However, the essay's actual approach undermines this goal. A rigorous argumentative essay should evaluate *both* sides using the same critical standards. Instead, you apply intense skepticism to pro-New Age sources (questioning author credibility, "made-up" facts) while accepting anti-New Age sources largely at face value. This asymmetry weakens your argument rather than strengthening it—readers will notice the double standard.

Revision suggestion: Apply the same critical lens to both sides. If you critique one source for relying on personal opinion, check whether your "against" sources also contain unsupported opinions dressed as fact.

Avoid Conflating Popularity with Validity

You argue that because *Christianity Today* reaches more readers, the anti-New Age argument is "more powerful." This is a logical fallacy (argumentum ad populum)—popularity does not equal truth or argumentative strength. A widely-read magazine can still contain weak or biased reasoning, and a niche publication can contain rigorous, well-supported claims.

Revision suggestion: Instead of citing circulation numbers as evidence of strength, focus on the *quality of reasoning and evidence* within each source.

Distinguish Personal Belief from Academic Argument

The paragraph in which you state "I feel that the Bible is very real and true" is a significant issue. In an academic essay, you're evaluating the *rhetorical strength* of arguments—not declaring your personal faith commitments. This passage reveals that your assessment may be driven by your own religious convictions rather than objective analysis of argumentative technique. This undermines your credibility as an impartial evaluator.

Revision suggestion: Frame your discussion of the Bible in terms of its *rhetorical function as a shared authority for the target audience*, not its objective truth. For example: "The Bible functions as an authoritative text for many readers of Christian publications, which lends persuasive weight to arguments that invoke it for that specific audience—though this appeal may not persuade readers outside that faith tradition."

Strengthen Definitions and Structure

Your definition of "New Age" is helpful, but the essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Consider adding a brief paragraph after your introduction outlining the criteria you'll use to judge argument strength (eg, use of evidence, logical consistency, audience reach, use of testimony). This will help readers follow your analysis and make your evaluative criteria more transparent and defensible.

Watch for Overgeneralization and Unsupported Claims

Several statements need more support or softening:
"The Bible... is accepted all over the world" — this is not accurate; the Bible is not universally accepted, and this claim will hurt your credibility.
"the most destructive was most likely alcohol" — this is presented as fact based on an informal conversation with neighbors, not research. If you want to challenge the New Age author's claim about tobacco, cite actual statistics.
Broad claims like "none of the authors had any books or hard facts" should be tempered—did you review *every* pro-New Age source available, or just those you found? Qualify claims like "the sources I reviewed."

Revise for Tone and Register

The opening (Yoda joke) is engaging but very informal for academic writing. Similarly, phrases like "I honestly ran out of my room" are conversational rather than analytical. Consider keeping some personal voice, but frame your reactions as *evidence of the source's persuasive failure* rather than simply narrating your surprise.

Editing and Mechanics

There are several typos and grammatical issues to address in your next draft:
"New< Age" (stray symbol)
"that is was not" → "that it was not"
Missing capitalization after some periods
Run-on sentences, especially in paragraphs discussing consequence and value

Summary of Priority Revisions

Apply equal scrutiny to both sides of the argument.
Remove the ad populum reasoning about magazine popularity.
Reframe the Bible discussion as rhetorical analysis, not personal testimony.
Add a clear evaluative framework early in the essay.
Support broad claims with cited evidence, not personal anecdotes.
Proofread carefully for typos and sentence-level clarity.

Your topic is genuinely interesting, and with more balanced, evidence-based analysis, this essay could become a much stronger piece of argumentative writing. I encourage you to revisit your sources with a more even-handed critical eye, and to consciously separate your personal beliefs from your rhetorical analysis. I'm happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Nuclear Power Debate

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, controversial topic and organized your essay around a clear rhetorical framework: opponents use values/emotion, proponents use facts/logic. This is a sophisticated analytical lens, and your essay is well-structured with clear topic sentences and logical paragraph progression. However, the essay has some significant issues with factual accuracy, argumentative balance, and language precision that need attention before this reaches its full potential.

Strengths

Clear thesis and structure: Your organizing principle (values vs. facts) gives the essay a strong analytical backbone, and each body paragraph clearly supports this framework.
Use of specific evidence: You attempt to ground your argument in concrete details (percentages, comparisons to aspirin-sized waste, rail cars of ash), which shows good rhetorical awareness—specific numbers and comparisons are more persuasive than vague claims.
Logical paragraph transitions: Phrases like "Once the topics are viewed on the same level" and "Now that the people are aware..." show you're thinking about how ideas connect.

Major Areas for Revision

Factual Accuracy
Your opening sentence states a nuclear bomb has "the explosive power of three hundred tons of TNT"—this is far too low; even small atomic bombs (like Hiroshima) were equivalent to roughly 15,000 tons of TNT. Since this is your very first sentence, an error here undermines credibility immediately. Please verify all numerical claims throughout the essay (the cost percentages, the "20-100 lives saved" statistic, etc) and cite your sources. Academic essays require verifiable evidence, not just numbers that sound authoritative.

Argumentative Balance and Fairness
You promised in the thesis to analyze "both arguments," but the essay becomes increasingly one-sided as it progresses. By the third and fourth body paragraphs, you're presenting the proponents' claims uncritically, and your conclusion abandons analysis altogether in favor of open advocacy ("nuclear power will truly be an asset to our revolutionary country"). This shifts the essay from *analysis* of a rhetorical debate into *personal argument*—which may not match your assignment's goals. Ask yourself: is this essay supposed to analyze how each side argues, or advocate for a side? If it's the former, you need to maintain more analytical distance, perhaps by also noting weaknesses or oversimplifications in the proponents' arguments (eg, do they ever address the waste problem's *long-term* risk, or just its *volume*?).

Unsupported Generalizations
Phrases like "the one-sided way Americans have been brought up" and describing the public as "uninformed" or under the media's spell are strong claims that need evidence or softening. Consider revising to avoid characterizing entire populations without support—this can come across as condescending and weakens your credibility as an objective analyst.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues
There are several recurring errors worth focused attention:
"do to" should be "due to"
"react or explosion" → check word choice; "reactor" is correct but review surrounding phrasing
Subject-verb agreement: "the reason...is because" is redundant—use "the reason...is that"
Comparative "then" vs. "than": You consistently write "more...then," "cheaper then," "less...then"—this should be "than" in all comparative structures. This is a frequent error throughout and worth reviewing carefully.
Incomplete sentence: "Though simple, easy-to-understand facts such as:  the average American can easily relate to..." — this sentence is broken; there seems to be a missing example or a formatting error. Please revise this sentence fully.
"meggawatts" → "megawatts"

Conclusion Needs Revision
Your conclusion introduces new claims ("It has capabilities of being unsafe and expensive in the beginning") without much preparation, and ends with a patriotic flourish that feels disconnected from the analytical tone established earlier. Consider returning to your original thesis about *how* each side argues, and reflect on what this rhetorical strategy reveals about public discourse on science and risk.

Suggested Next Steps

Fact-check every statistic and correct the nuclear bomb comparison immediately.
Rebalance the essay by adding at least one paragraph examining weaknesses in the proponents' rhetoric, not just describing their strategy sympathetically.
Proofread specifically for "then/than" errors—search your document and fix each instance.
Rewrite the conclusion to circle back to your rhetorical analysis (values vs. facts) rather than shifting into personal advocacy.
Consider citing your sources for statistics—this is essential in academic writing and will strengthen your credibility.

This essay shows real potential; the analytical framework is smart and the writing is engaged and confident. With more precision in facts, more balance in analysis, and careful proofreading, this could be a genuinely strong piece of academic writing.

Use of Sources

I notice several placeholder markers () where citations or specific information should appear. Connecting it explicitly to your research-based claims. Make this connection explicit rather than leaving it for the reader to notice.

Being cautious with generalizations from personal experience. "As can seen" should be "As can be seen."

Suggestions for Revision

Fill in all bracketed citations with complete, specific information.
Add a clear thesis statement that takes a position, not just describes the debate.
Create explicit topic sentences for each paragraph that connect back to your thesis.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize your argument rather than restate the introduction.
Proofread carefully for the grammar issues noted above, and consider having a native-speaking peer or writing tutor review a final draft.

Your essay has real potential—your personal experience adds authenticity, and your research shows genuine engagement with the topic. Focus your revision on sharpening your argument and connecting your evidence more explicitly to your central claims. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further before your next draft.

Overall Impression

The specific findings of the Penn State study (what percentage, what experience?)
A direct quotation from Steele's essay, properly cited
Any statistics on crime/poverty you reference in paragraph 3

Sentence fragments and awkward phrasing: "As a problem of not only the students who accept the organizations, but the administrators who allow them, the problem is not dealt with..." — this sentence is doing too much at once; break it into two clearer sentences.

Suggested Next Steps

Do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on pronouns, articles, and sentence boundaries (run-ons/fragments).

Feedback Report: Surrogate Motherhood Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult ethical question and you clearly have a position—that surrogate motherhood is unethical—which you maintain consistently. However, the draft as it stands is not ready for submission, primarily because of the numerous placeholder gaps (marked ) where definitions, quotations, or paraphrases of sources appear to be missing. Beyond this structural problem, there are several issues with argument development, use of evidence, and clarity that need attention before this becomes a strong academic essay.

Complete the Missing Content First

This is the most urgent issue. There are at least a dozen places where you've left blanks instead of writing out the definition, quotation, or opposing argument you intend to discuss (eg, the very first sentence: "Surrogate Motherhood is when a."). Right now, a reader cannot follow your reasoning because key pieces of information—especially the counterarguments you say you are "opposing"—are simply absent. Before revising anything else, fill in every blank with a complete, coherent sentence.

Sharpen and Simplify Your Thesis

Your core claim seems to be: *surrogate motherhood is unethical because it causes emotional harm to the surrogate mother and creates unequal treatment among the three parties involved.* This is a reasonable, arguable thesis, but it gets diluted because you also introduce a separate line of reasoning about biological parenthood ("biologically there are only two parents") that is not clearly connected to the emotional-harm argument. Decide which claim is your main thesis and make the other a supporting point, rather than presenting them as equally weighted, separate reasons. State this thesis clearly in your introduction and repeat it, in refined form, in your conclusion.

Strengthen Your Engagement with Sources and Counterarguments

You refer to "Health services" and "Annas" as if readers already know who these are and what they argue. Academic writing requires you to:
Introduce sources properly (eg, "According to bioethicist George Annas...")
Use direct quotations or clear paraphrases, not vague gestures ("Annas argues ")
Actually explain the opposing view *before* you rebut it. Right now, several paragraphs jump straight to your rebuttal without first stating what you're rebutting, which weakens your argument's credibility.

A pattern like "Many people feel X. Others feel Y." repeats throughout the essay without ever fully developing X or Y. Choose 2-3 strong counterarguments, explain them fully and fairly, and then respond to each with specific reasoning or evidence—not just assertions like "This is not significant enough."

Support Claims with Evidence, Not Just Assertion

Many of your strongest claims are stated but not proven. For example:
"No human being should be looked upon as a reproductive structure who has no feeling" — this is a strong moral claim, but you need to show *how* surrogates are treated this way (a specific case, statistic, or quotation).
"There are many people in the world who[se] lives have not been altered because they are infertile" — this needs a source or example to be persuasive.
The Baby M case is mentioned in your introduction but never developed. This is a perfect opportunity for concrete evidence—explain what happened, what the court decided, and how it illustrates the emotional/legal complications you're arguing about.

Improve Organization

Several paragraphs cover similar ground (the "walking womb" idea and the "renting a womb" idea are essentially the same point, made twice). Consider organizing your body paragraphs around distinct arguments, such as:
Emotional harm to the surrogate (supported by the Baby M case)
Unequal treatment of the parents (father's genetics vs. mother's exclusion)
The "baby selling" / commodification argument

This will reduce repetition and make your essay easier to follow.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Watch subject-verb agreement: "Others feels that..." should be "Others feel that..."
"who's" should be "whose" when used possessively ("people who's lives" → "people whose lives")
"substancial" should be "substantial"
"surrogate mother hood" should be one word: "surrogate motherhood"
Some sentences are vague or circular (eg, "Surrogacy has been looked upon as " needs completion, but also needs sharper phrasing throughout)

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all missing content with complete information.
Write a one-sentence thesis statement and check that every paragraph supports it.
Choose your 3 strongest arguments and cut repetitive ones.
Add at least one concrete piece of evidence (case detail, statistic, or direct quotation) per body paragraph.
Proofread for the grammar issues noted above.

You have real ethical reasoning here—your job now is to make sure every claim is fully stated, supported, and organized so your reader can follow your logic from beginning to end.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, important topic with genuine passion, and your personal connection as a nursing student adds credibility to your argument. You've also done solid research, incorporating specific statistics and case studies (Depo-Provera, Coumadin, Clozapril) that make your argument concrete rather than abstract. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: structure, evidence integration, clarity, and language mechanics. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Structure and Organization

Your essay would benefit from a clearer roadmap. Currently, readers must infer your organizational logic. Consider:

Strengthen your thesis statement in the introduction. Your final sentence ("federal intervention is necessary...") is good, but preview your main supporting points (eg, "This paper will demonstrate this through evidence from international price comparisons, case studies of specific drugs, and the human cost of unregulated pricing.")

Missing text markers: I notice two  placeholders in your draft (paragraphs 2 and 4) where content appears to be missing. Please fill these in before final submission—they currently break the logical flow of your argument.

Transition sentences: Each paragraph jumps to a new example without clearly connecting back to your thesis. After each case study (Depo-Provera, Coumadin, Clozapril), add a sentence explicitly linking the example back to your argument for price regulation boards.

Evidence and Argumentation

Your use of specific statistics and named companies is a real strength—this is far more persuasive than vague claims. To strengthen this further:

Cite your sources: Where do these statistics come from (Senate Committee report, industry publications)? Add in-text citations so readers can verify your claims and so you avoid any appearance of plagiarism.

Address the counterargument more fully: You mention the pharmaceutical industry's argument about competition, but your rebuttal ("actually the opposite seems to be true") needs more development. Explain *why* price regulation doesn't eliminate competition—perhaps regulated markets shift competition toward efficiency or innovation rather than pure profit margins.

Logical gap in Clozapril example: You write "if the monitors are of better quality, this should not be a problem," but this needs elaboration. Whose monitors are better—Sandoz's or the VA's? Clarify this point, as it's currently confusing.

Reconsider the Coumadin example: You argue that eliminating unit-dose packaging caused a 2400% price increase because hospitals absorbed labor costs—but is this actually the pharmaceutical company "overpricing," or is it a shift of costs to hospitals? Consider clarifying this distinction, as it affects your argument's precision.

Language and Mechanics

Since you're writing in a second language, focus on these recurring issues:

Spelling/typos: "agrues" (argues), "recup" (recoup), "recieve" (receive), "recieveproper" (receive proper), "senile dementia" (Clozapril is actually used for schizophrenia, not dementia—please verify this fact)

Verb tense consistency: "Health care statistics... are mind boggling" (present) shifts oddly with surrounding past-tense statistics. Keep tense consistent within paragraphs discussing historical data.

Sentence fragments and run-ons: "This price-gouging shows in high hospital bills, which ." — this sentence is incomplete. Also, watch for comma splices, eg, "As a nursing student, the indications of this are ominous, patients I treat..." should likely be two sentences.

Word choice: "drug buyers" is vague—do you mean pharmacists, insurers, or consumers? "Luxury drug" should be in quotation marks consistently and perhaps explained further for readers unfamiliar with the term.

Percentage clarity: "European subsidiaries, where rates are anywhere from 74-45 percent lower" — the order (74 to 45) is confusing; consider "45-74 percent lower" for clarity.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively personalizes the issue, which is compelling. However, ending with two rhetorical questions ("Where is the limit? When does drug overpricing stop?") without proposing next steps feels slightly unresolved. Consider adding one or two sentences suggesting what "the American people" should concretely do—contact representatives, support specific legislation, etc This would give your essay a stronger call to action.

Priority Revisions

If you have limited time, focus on: (1) filling in the missing  sections, (2) adding source citations, (3) proofreading for the spelling/grammar issues noted above, and (4) strengthening transitions between your case study paragraphs.

Your argument has real merit and emotional resonance—with revision, it will be even more persuasive. Well done on tackling such a substantive topic!

Feedback on "The Death Penalty" Essay

Overall Impression

You have tackled a complex, well-known debate with a clear structure and a confident thesis. Your organization—moving from statistical evidence, to constitutional issues, to ethical concerns—is logical and easy to follow. However, there is one critical issue that must be addressed before any other revision: your essay contains numerous placeholder markers () where actual evidence, statistics, and quotations should appear. As it stands, the essay makes claims about what studies "show" or what sources "report" without ever stating what they actually say. This is the single most urgent problem, because an argumentative essay about statistics and evidence cannot succeed if the evidence itself is missing. Please make sure your final draft includes complete, accurately cited data and quotations in every one of these spots.

Content and Use of Evidence

Beyond simply filling in the missing information, consider these points:

Specificity is your friend. Right now you tell readers *that* a drop in crime rates occurred, or *that* a study found something suggestive—but readers need actual numbers (percentages, rates, timeframes) to be persuaded. Vague claims like "many people believe" or "it is reported that" weaken your credibility. Replace them with concrete, attributed data.

Evaluate your sources' credibility and recency. Several studies you mention (Dann 1935, Archer/Gartner/Beittel) are quite old. If more recent research exists, use it, or at least explain why these older studies remain relevant today. This will strengthen your claim that the opposition offers "more accurate statistics."

Support your evaluative claims. You assert early on that the anti-death-penalty side has "more accurate statistics" and "more valid reasons" than the pro-death-penalty side—but you never actually analyze *why* the opposing statistics are more accurate. Simply presenting more evidence for one side is not the same as proving it is more valid. Consider directly comparing a specific pro-death-penalty statistic with a specific anti-death-penalty statistic and explaining why one is more reliable (eg, sample size, methodology, date, source bias).

Argument Structure and Balance

Your essay claims to examine "both sides," but the treatment of the pro-death-penalty argument is quite thin—summarized in only a few sentences per section before being dismissed. To make your argument more persuasive to a skeptical reader:

Give the opposing view a fuller, fairer hearing before rebutting it. For instance, in the ethics section, the "eye for an eye" and "protection of society" arguments are stated in one sentence each and quickly dismissed. Strengthening these counterarguments (with their own evidence) and then refuting them in detail will make your rebuttal more convincing, not less.

Watch for unsupported assertions used as transitions, such as "This would support the theory..." or "This usually occurs because courts are so eager to find a scapegoat." These read as your own speculation rather than conclusions drawn from cited evidence. Make clear whether these are your inferences or findings from a source.

The Roger Coleman example is inconsistent. You state he was "convicted... in 1982" and then "executed on May 20, 1982" in the same year—this is almost certainly a factual/typographical error, since his execution occurred in 1992. Double-check all case details for accuracy, as factual errors undermine your credibility on a topic where precision matters.

Paragraph and Sentence-Level Issues

The final paragraph of the ethics section (beginning "Furthermore, life imprisonment...") repeats a point already made about incarceration effects; consider tightening this for concision.
Some sentences are quite long and would benefit from being split for clarity, eg, the sentence about Cheatwood's study and its implications.
Minor language notes: "all together" should be "altogether"; watch subject-verb agreement in a few spots (eg, "his appeal was not only dealt with hastily, but..." needs a parallel completion); capitalize "Both" at the start of a sentence in your first paragraph.
Your conclusion largely repeats the introduction almost verbatim. Consider using the conclusion to synthesize the argument in a new way—perhaps by acknowledging the strongest opposing point one final time and explaining why it still fails to outweigh your evidence, giving the essay a stronger sense of closure.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Fill in all missing evidence () with accurate, specific, properly cited data.
Strengthen your comparative claim that opposing evidence is "more accurate" by directly analyzing source quality.
Give more depth and fairness to the pro-death-penalty arguments before rebutting them.
Verify factual details (dates, case outcomes) for accuracy.
Revise the conclusion so it adds new insight rather than repeating the introduction.

You have a solid foundation and a clear line of reasoning—the essay mainly needs the evidentiary "bones" filled in and a more balanced, rigorous treatment of the opposing side. I look forward to seeing your revised draft.

Feedback Report

Overall, this is a solid draft with a clear position and a logical structure. You make good use of case studies and expert testimony, and you anticipate counterarguments, which shows sophisticated argumentative thinking. However, there are several areas that need attention before this essay is ready for submission.

Double-check that the quotes you choose directly support the point being made in that paragraph—don't just insert them as decoration.

Thesis Clarity and Argument Structure

Your thesis is clear: *Prozac should continue to be prescribed because of its positive effects.* This is a good, defensible claim. However, the essay would benefit from a more explicit roadmap in the introduction. Tell your reader, in one or two sentences, what specific points you will cover (eg, medical safety, social benefits, addressing suicide concerns, addressing side-effect complaints). This will make the essay's organization easier to follow.

Development of Counterarguments

You do a good job acknowledging that critics exist, but your rebuttals sometimes feel rushed or underdeveloped. For example:
In the suicide paragraph, you present statistics (900,000–950,000 prescriptions vs. "a few" deaths), but "a few" is vague. Use a specific number if you have one—vague language weakens statistical arguments.
In the paragraph about patients feeling "numbed," you present Sam's loss of "quirkiness and sarcasm" as a counterpoint, but then move on without directly refuting it. Consider adding a sentence explaining *why* this trade-off might still be worth it (eg, quality of life improvements outweigh the loss of certain personality quirks), rather than simply asserting it "should be tolerable."

Word Choice and Precision

There are several places where word choice needs refinement:
"Banishment" (paragraph 1) is a strong, slightly odd word choice for a drug being taken off the market; consider "banning" or "prohibition."
"Devalue the rumors" (paragraph 1) is awkward phrasing—consider "dispel the rumors" or "counter misconceptions."
"Causes the serotonin to slow in spaces between the nerve cells" (paragraph 2) is scientifically imprecise. Prozac slows the *reabsorption* (reuptake) of serotonin, not the serotonin itself. Revise for accuracy: "The drug slows the reabsorption of serotonin in the spaces between nerve cells, allowing communication between neurons to become more efficient."
"Pas social lives" (end of paragraph 4) appears to be a typo for "past social lives"—please proofread carefully.

Grammar and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to address in revision:
Subject-verb agreement and tense consistency: Watch for shifts between present and past tense (eg, "Prozac was first invented" vs. "It also has worked to stop bulimia"—consider "has also worked").
Article usage: "an architect name Sam" should be "an architect named Sam."
Comma splices: Some sentences run two independent clauses together without proper punctuation (eg, "It also has worked to stop bulimia, panic, obsessive-compulsive disorder and other conditions" is fine, but check similar sentences elsewhere for clarity).
Proofread for missing or extra words, as in "the past depression many have suffered through" (consider rephrasing to "the depression many have suffered through in the past").

Conclusion

Your conclusion ends with a rhetorical question ("what is wrong with making yourself better?"), which is a nice persuasive touch, but the paragraph as a whole feels a bit thin without the quotations. Once you add Dr. Auerbach's and Dr. Cass's comments, consider following them with a sentence that synthesizes their views rather than just placing them side by side—show your reader how these two authorities agree and why that consensus matters.

Your essay contains multiple  placeholders where quotations should be. This is a serious problem: without the actual quotes, your argument has no evidence. Then make sure each quote is followed by your own explanation of *why* it supports your point—don't let the quote speak for itself.

Bias and Lack of Balance
An argumentative essay should acknowledge the strongest version of the opposing view before refuting it. Currently, Wall's position is dismissed rather quickly and somewhat unfairly (eg, calling his tone "lighthearted" without fully engaging his reasoning). Ask yourself: what is the *strongest* argument Wall makes? Even if you disagree, present it fairly first, then explain specifically why Pattullo's counter-argument is more convincing. This will make your essay feel more credible and less one-sided.

Logical Gaps
Several claims need stronger reasoning to connect them:
You claim homosexuals in the barracks "would destroy" cohesion, but this assumes discomfort is inevitable and irreversible. Consider addressing counter-evidence (eg, historical parallels with racial integration of the military, which faced similar "unit cohesion" arguments in the 1940s-50s).
The line "This would offend many heterosexuals" needs support—offense alone is a weak basis for policy; you should explain *why* this offense translates into operational harm.

Conclusion: You state that reading Pattullo changed your mind. This is honest and interesting, but for an academic essay, it may be more persuasive to frame this as the *reader's* likely response, or to focus the conclusion on synthesizing your reasons rather than narrating your personal journey.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Notes
"Pattullo's use of sound reasoning and his pointing out of the realistic consequences of gays in the military will make on change their mind" — this sentence has a grammatical error ("will make on change"). Revise to something like: "will make readers change their minds."
"James M. Wall's articles does not give" — subject-verb agreement issue ("article...does," not "articles does").
"there was not reason why homosexuals should not be prohibited" — this double negative is confusing. Do you mean *should be prohibited* or *should not be prohibited*? Clarify your intended meaning here, as it currently contradicts your thesis.

Suggested Next Steps

First priority: Insert all missing quotations and explain each one.
Revise the thesis into a single, clear sentence near the end of your introduction.
Add at least one paragraph that fairly represents Wall's strongest point before countering it.
Proofread for subject-verb agreement and article usage (a/an/the).

Overall, you have a clear position and a reasonable structure; the essay will be significantly strengthened by restoring the evidence, deepening the counter-argument treatment, and tightening the language. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft once you've filled in the quotations.

Feedback on Your Essay: Prayer in Public Schools

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex, multi-source synthesis assignment and clearly did the reading closely enough to identify each side's main arguments. Your organization by group (Conservative Religious Group, then Civil Liberties Group, then your own proposed solution) is a sensible structure for this kind of comparative essay. However, the essay currently reads more like a summary of three articles than an argument, and it needs revision in several areas: thesis clarity, paragraph development, integration of evidence, and grammar/mechanics.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis, buried at the end of a very long opening paragraph, tries to do too much at once: it summarizes both groups' positions *and* stakes your claim in a single overloaded sentence. Readers need to know your argument before you walk them through the details. Consider breaking the introduction into two parts: (1) a brief, neutral overview of the debate and the two sides, and (2) a separate, clearly stated thesis sentence that previews your evaluation (Article A is weak because X; Article B is weak because Y; Article C offers the best solution because Z).

Also reconsider your claim that Applebome/Klinghoffer's article is "biased" while Shriver's is not. This is a strong claim that needs to be argued, not just asserted. What specifically makes one biased and the other not? Is it the absence of evidence, the tone, the source selection? Right now you assert bias but your actual complaint is that the Conservative articles lack sufficient *evidence*—that's a different and more precise criticism than "bias," and you should use the more accurate term.

Argument Development and Use of Evidence

This is the area needing the most work. Several of your paragraphs follow the same thin pattern: introduce a claim, drop in a quotation, and then say some version of "this is a good/weak argument." You need to *unpack* the quotations—explain in your own words what they say, why they matter, and exactly how they support or fail to support the group's position. Right now the analysis sentences ("This is a good use by the author...") are repeated almost verbatim throughout the essay and don't tell the reader anything specific.

For example, in your discussion of the freedom-of-speech argument, you make a genuinely sharp point—that a ruling on student speech (the armband case) doesn't necessarily apply to prayer under the Establishment Clause—but you state it in one sentence and move on. This is your best piece of original analysis in the essay; it deserves a full paragraph developing the legal and logical distinction between free speech rights and government establishment of religion.

Similarly, your critique of the "violent crime, divorce, teenage pregnancy" argument (para. 3) is good—you correctly identify it as a claim without causal evidence. Push further: what would count as evidence here? Are there studies, statistics, or counter-examples you could point to (even hypothetically, as in "the author would need to show a causal link, not just a correlation, between the absence of prayer and rising social problems")?

Your treatment of the Civil Liberties Group is stronger and more detailed, but it also stays close to paraphrase. Ask yourself after each quotation: *So what?* What does this mean for a student, a teacher, or a minority-religion family? You gesture toward this with the minority-religion point near the end of that section — that's your best original idea in the whole essay. Develop it earlier and more fully.

Finally, your conclusion largely repeats what you already said about the moment of silence rather than synthesizing the essay's overall argument or addressing potential objections (eg, could a moment of silence still function as de facto prayer time and pressure minority students, undermining your own point?). Anticipating this objection would strengthen your position considerably.

Language and Mechanics

There are frequent spelling and word-choice errors that a careful proofreading pass would catch: "arrise" (arise), "know as" (known as), "torwads/torward" (towards), "conatin" (contain), "mionorities" (minorities), "ow" (how), "stengthen" (strengthen), "sustain from" (refrain from, or prevent), "titles" (titled). Read your essay aloud or use a spell-checker before submitting.

Watch for run-on sentences, especially in the introduction, and vague pronoun references ("This is a good argument" — which part, specifically?). Also, be careful with verb agreement ("has caused conflict to arrise") and article usage ("the constitutional amendment of church and state" should be "the constitutional principle" or "the First Amendment separation of church and state," since it's not literally an amendment about church and state).

Priority Revisions

Write a clear, single-sentence thesis and move it to the start.
Replace repeated evaluative phrases with specific analysis of each quotation.
Expand your two strongest original insights (free speech vs. establishment; minority students and moment of silence).
Proofread carefully for spelling and sentence structure.
Strengthen the conclusion by addressing a counterargument to your proposed solution.

Feedback on Your Death Penalty Essay

Your essay tackles an important and genuinely contested issue, and you've done solid work organizing your argument around three clear reasons (ineffectiveness, immorality, and systemic flaws). You've also made a good effort to bring in outside sources to support your claims. Below are suggestions to help strengthen the essay in a revision.

Strengths to Build On

Your three-pronged structure works well: each body paragraph tackles a distinct reason, which helps readers follow your logic. You also anticipate counterarguments (the "eye for an eye" reasoning, the deterrence argument) before refuting them, which is a sophisticated argumentative move.

Major Areas for Revision

Fill in the missing citations. Your essay has several bracketed placeholders  where quotations or statistics should appear. These are currently empty, which means readers cannot evaluate your evidence. Before you can call this essay complete, you need the actual quotes from Forst, Solomon, and Gittings, along with proper citations (author, publication, page number). Without this evidence, your claims about what "has been proven" remain unsupported assertions.

Address source credibility and dates. You cite studies from 1975 and 1983—these are now quite dated. Consider whether more recent research exists on deterrence and wrongful convictions; if you must use older sources, briefly acknowledge their age and explain why they remain relevant.

Strengthen paragraph-to-thesis connections. Your thesis mentions three reasons, but the transition into your second reason (immorality) feels abrupt—you shift from "many others see the death penalty as immoral" without clearly linking back to your first paragraph's argument. Add a transitional sentence that shows how morality relates to ineffectiveness.

Revise for grammatical accuracy. Several sentences need editing:
"the increases crime we face" → "the increased crime we face"
"how much money they have" → clarify: do you mean wealthy defendants avoid death sentences through better legal representation? Make this causal chain explicit.
"full-proof" → "foolproof" (common error, but worth fixing)
"If somebody makes a horrible mistake of murdering someone else, then is it moral for the government to turn right around and murder them" — this needs a question mark, and would read more smoothly as: "If somebody makes the horrible mistake of murdering someone else, is it moral for the government to respond by killing them in turn?"
"so ciety" should be "society" (likely a typo from line-wrapping)

Reconsider the "Liberal Democrats" claim. Historically, support for capital punishment in the U.S. has been more associated with conservative/Republican positions, not liberal Democrats. This factual claim needs verification—inaccurate political framing could undermine your credibility with informed readers. Double-check your sources here.

Clarify the deterrence-backfire argument. Your reasoning that fear of the death penalty might cause criminals to "murder anyone who could get him caught" is an interesting psychological claim, but it needs more explanation. Why would fear of a *harsher* penalty increase pre-emptive violence rather than deter the original crime? Walk your reader through this logic more carefully, perhaps with a concrete example.

Develop the "hindering society" claim from your thesis. You promise in your introduction that the death penalty "may even hinder the development of our society," but this idea doesn't get its own clear treatment. Is this the same as your "not foolproof" paragraph, or is it a separate point about resources, international reputation, or social values? Consider either integrating this claim explicitly into an existing paragraph or giving it more distinct treatment.

Improve your conclusion. Your ending paragraph introduces a new idea (education as an alternative solution) without developing it. If education is a viable alternative to the death penalty, this deserves at least a sentence or two of support earlier in the essay, or should be removed from the conclusion to avoid introducing unsupported new claims at the end.

Style Note

Your rhetorical question ("what does a serious murder case do besides bring up a moral debate...") is effective, but be careful not to overuse casual phrasing like "Well" to open sentences—this appears three times in the essay and reads as informal for academic writing. Consider replacing with transitional phrases like "However," "In fact," or "Moreover."

Next Steps

Prioritize finding and inserting your missing citations first—this is essential for the essay's credibility. Then work on smoothing transitions between paragraphs and tightening your grammar. Your ideas are strong; now the essay needs the evidentiary support and polish to match.

Feedback on "Prayer in Public Schools" Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen a genuinely debatable topic and have done real research—citing the New York Times, the Wall Street Journal, and a book on the subject. This shows initiative. However, the essay's persuasive power is currently weakened by an unclear thesis, over-reliance on quotations rather than your own analysis, some internal contradictions, and language issues that obscure your reasoning. Below are the main areas to revise.

Sharpen Your Thesis

Your thesis statement—"Time for prayer in public schools should not be instituted... because religion is imposed on the children who will recite the prayer with little interest to actually pray"—is difficult to follow. Are you objecting because (a) the government has no authority over religion, (b) mandated prayer is meaningless/insincere, or (c) it violates religious freedom? You actually make all three arguments in the essay, but the thesis only signals one, and imprecisely. Revise it into a single clear sentence that previews your strongest 2-3 reasons, eg: "Public schools should not institute mandatory prayer time because it oversteps the government's role in religious life, produces insincere and meaningless recitation, and threatens the very freedom of religious expression it claims to protect."

Resolve the Contradiction in Your Counterargument

Your rebuttal paragraph concedes that "the government should have public schools institute religious activities after class periods are finished." This is a reasonable compromise, but it sits awkwardly next to your broader claim that "the role of government is not to bring religion into public schools, because they have no right to do this." If public schools can host after-school prayer groups, the government is still involved in facilitating religious activity on school property—so your absolute claim in the conclusion needs qualification. Either narrow your thesis to focus specifically on *mandatory, curriculum-time* prayer (which would make your after-school proposal a consistent alternative), or explain more carefully why after-school voluntary activities differ in kind, not just degree, from your objection.

Balance Quotation with Your Own Analysis

Nearly every paragraph relies on a lengthy quotation, and in several places you quote first and only briefly restate what it means afterward ("She is stating that..."). This makes the essay feel like a chain of citations rather than a sustained argument in your own voice. For revision:
Try paraphrasing more sources instead of quoting them directly, reserving direct quotation for especially powerful or precise language.
After each quotation, don't just restate it—analyze *why* it supports your point and connect it explicitly back to your thesis.
Vary your quote-introduction phrases; "states" is used repeatedly ("states ... states ... states "). Consider "argues," "contends," "observes," "warns," etc

Strengthen Logical Connections

Some reasoning steps need to be made explicit. For example, in the paragraph on crime and academic stress, the logic is: students are stressed → they won't pray thoughtfully → therefore lack of prayer isn't really linked to crime. But the sentence "there is a little effect on the thoughts of most students" is unclear (likely a typo for "little effect," but the causal claim itself needs more support—*why* would academic stress make the prayer/crime link weak?). Walk the reader through each inferential step rather than asserting conclusions.

Address Language and Grammar

Because clarity of argument depends on clarity of language, please proofread carefully for:
Article and preposition errors: "promote moral values i children" (in), "Even is there is a silent time" (Even if).
Awkward phrasing: "The majority would simply receive a prayer that would pertain to them only" — clarify what "majority" and "them" refer to.
Redundant transitions like "The previous statement is a contradiction, because..." could be tightened.
Consider reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool, then verify suggested corrections make sense in context.

Revise the Conclusion

Your final paragraph largely repeats the introduction's claims rather than synthesizing the essay's strongest points into a memorable closing statement. Try ending with a forward-looking or values-based statement—what should readers *do* with this argument, or why does it matter beyond the classroom?

Priority Action List for Revision
Rewrite the thesis to clearly state 2-3 specific reasons.
Decide how the after-school prayer concession fits your overall claim; adjust either the thesis or the concession for consistency.
Reduce direct quotations by about a third; replace with paraphrase + your own analysis.
Proofread line-by-line for grammar (especially articles, verb forms, and unclear pronoun references).
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize rather than repeat.

You have strong source material and a clear stance—now the task is to make your own voice and logic drive the essay, with sources supporting *you* rather than the reverse. Good luck with the revision!

Your argument currently rests on claims you assert but never actually substantiate
Academic writing requires you to show your sources, not just reference that they exist

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

This creates a structural problem: you spend the rest of the essay explaining counterarguments (animal rights activists' views) only to dismiss them, rather than genuinely engaging with them.

Suggestion: Decide whether this is a persuasive essay or an essay exploring multiple ethical perspectives. If persuasive, state your thesis clearly and organize paragraphs to build toward it. If exploratory, don't tip your hand so early—let the tension between viewpoints drive the essay.

The verse itself is missing (another )

The line "who's life is more sacred, the life of a three year old boy, or a pig?" is a rhetorical device but risks being seen as a false dilemma—it implies these are the only two options, when the actual debate is about testing conditions, alternatives, and degrees, not a simple binary.

Some reasoning needs tightening:
You argue depression research on dogs "does not really classify as human pain and discomfort" and thus isn't justified—but this contradicts your broader claim that all human benefit outweighs animal suffering. The comparison to hunting/bullfighting near the end feels like whataboutism—it doesn't strengthen your argument, it deflects from it. Consider removing or reframing.

Right now the essay reads as a list of separate points (define ethics, present activist view, present researcher view, discuss thalidomide, discuss living conditions, discuss slippery slope) without clear transitions showing how each builds on the last. 

Suggestion: Try an outline with clear sections:
Introduce the ethical dilemma and your thesis
Present the animal rights perspective fully and fairly
Present the research perspective with evidence
Directly weigh them against each other (this is where nuance—like the dog/depression example—belongs)
Conclusion that doesn't just restate your opening claim, but shows how your evidence supports it

Language and Mechanics
A few recurring issues to proofread for:
"friends side" → friend's side
"some are not" → needs completion ("some are not [easily justified]")
"off" used for "of" (eg, "healing off birth defects")
"activity involved" → actively involved
"may have killed" vs. "kill"—check verb tense consistency
"who's" → whose (in your final line)

Summary
Your essay has a clear passion and a real ethical question at its core. To strengthen it: (1) fill in all missing quotations, (2) clarify whether you're arguing a position or exploring perspectives, (3) tighten your logic around competing standards for "benefit," and (4) proofread carefully. The hypothetical opening scenario is a strong rhetorical strategy that immediately engages the reader and sets up your thesis effectively. However, the essay needs revision in three main areas: evidence and support, logical consistency, and language precision.

This is the most significant weakness in your draft. There are numerous placeholders () throughout the essay where statistics, quotations, and specific examples should appear. Right now, key claims are left unsupported:

The statistic about last year's championship viewership is missing.
Linnea Smith's actual comparison/quote about the swimsuit issue is missing.
Pat Summitt's quote is missing.
The specific "argument against women athletes" you're refuting is never stated.

An argumentative essay lives or dies by its evidence. Right now, a reader cannot evaluate whether your rebuttals are convincing because the claims you're rebutting, and the evidence you're using to rebut them, are not actually present. Before doing anything else, fill in these gaps with your sourced material and make sure each quote/statistic is properly cited.

This seems to contradict your overall argument that women deserve *equal* treatment and *equal* pressure/prestige. If your point is that unequal treatment is unjust, be careful not to suggest in another paragraph that inequality has hidden benefits—readers will notice the inconsistency. Consider revising this section to argue that women can learn from men's mistakes *while still deserving equal support*, rather than implying lower pressure is a fair trade-off for lower funding.

Similarly, the transition from "the game was so well noticed... because it's rare to see women's games on TV" needs a clearer logical connection to your Title IX point. Spell out explicitly: does rarity increase interest, or does it suppress it? Right now the sentence structure is ambiguous.

Structure and Transitions
Your paragraphs are organized around themes (respect, facilities, money, pressure), which is a reasonable structure. However, transitions between paragraphs are abrupt. For example, the jump from discussing Linnea Smith's swimsuit-issue comparison to "Another argument against women athletes..." lacks a bridge. Consider adding transitional sentences that explicitly connect the *idea* of the previous paragraph to the next (eg, "Beyond issues of respect, critics also argue that women lack the same *interest* in sports as men do").

Also, your concluding paragraph is strong rhetorically ("sit in a boat, or pick up a bat"), but it could more directly loop back to your opening scenario about Passion Thompson. Consider a brief callback—something like referencing whether we'll ever *see* that final shot—to bring the essay full circle.

Language and Grammar
As a non-native speaker, you're doing well conveying complex ideas, but a careful proofreading pass will elevate the essay significantly. Some specific issues:

"the final half" — basketball uses "half" as a unit, so "final half" is confusing (do you mean "second half" or "final minutes"?). Clarify.
"supposeively" → "supposedly"
"Their lucky to hold a game" → "They're lucky..."
"coordinators fell that" → "coordinators feel that"
"being women does not seem to mix" → "being a woman does not seem to mix"
"Pat Summit" — double-check the correct spelling of proper names (Summitt).
Subject-verb agreement: "There is 30 seconds left" → "There are 30 seconds left."

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool for a final pass, focusing especially on subject-verb agreement and article usage (a/an/the), which are common trouble spots for many language learners.

Strengthening Your Rebuttals
When you introduce opposing views (eg, "Colleges may argue..."), you do a good job acknowledging them, but your rebuttals sometimes rely on assertion ("this all seems to be a big excuse") rather than evidence. Strengthen these moments by pairing your rebuttal with a concrete fact or statistic (once you fill in the missing citations) to make the counter-argument more persuasive.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Fill in all missing quotes/statistics and cite sources.
Resolve the logical tension in the "added pressures" section.
Smooth transitions between paragraphs.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice.
Strengthen rebuttals with concrete evidence rather than assertions.

You have a compelling argument and a strong rhetorical instinct—now it's a matter of shoring up the evidentiary foundation and polishing the language to match your ideas.

Your essay tackles an important and genuinely controversial topic, and you've done solid work organizing a classic argumentative structure: presenting opposing views and then countering them with evidence. Below are my observations to help you strengthen the draft.

However, I'd encourage you to make your transitions between sections more explicit. Phrases like "Another criticism..." and "The final argument..." work as signposts, but consider adding a sentence at the end of each section that explicitly states what you've proven, so the reader feels the argument building cumulatively rather than just listing separate rebuttals.

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay currently has numerous placeholders marked with —these represent missing statistics, quotations, and study results that are central to your argument. Right now, the essay reads as a skeleton of evidence rather than a fully realized argumentative essay. Before this can be evaluated as a finished piece, you need to:

Fill in the actual CDC statistics in your introduction
Provide Dr. Imellizzeri's specific objection and Fernandez's specific response
