Quote Epstein's article directly
Include the abstinence-effectiveness evidence you reference
Provide the Time magazine statistic
Cite the 1988 survey findings
Quote the Time statement on "safe sex" skepticism
Include the Epstein quotation on condom reliability
Provide Rygor's closing quotation

Without this content, readers cannot assess whether your evidence actually supports your claims—which is the heart of persuasive writing. I suspect these are placeholders you intended to fill in before submission, so prioritize this above all other revisions.

Argumentation and Logic

A few logical moves need refinement:

The abstinence section is a bit contradictory. You write that "there is evidence to prove that the goal of abstinence is realistic" and then immediately undercut this by calling it "a mere fantasy." Right now this whooshes past too quickly, and without the  evidence filled in, it's hard to tell if you're fairly representing the opposing view before dismantling it. Make sure you clearly explain *why* abstinence-only education fails in practice (behavior data) rather than simply asserting it does.

Dr. Imellizzeri's objection appears without development. You introduce her as a psychologist but never explain what her argument actually is or why it matters. Right now she's a name without content. Develop underdeveloped claims (especially Dr. Imellizzeri's argument and the abstinence debate) so each rebuttal feels fully earned.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay: Prayer in Public Schools

Overall Impression

This is a well-organized essay with a clear thesis and a logical structure that systematically addresses opposing arguments before countering them. Your command of persuasive techniques—analogy, counterargument, and appeals to fairness—demonstrates strong rhetorical instincts. However, the essay would benefit from revisions in three main areas: evidentiary support, sentence-level clarity, and idiomatic precision. Below are specific areas to address.

Structural Strengths

Your essay follows a clear pattern: you present a pro-prayer argument, then systematically dismantle it. This "concede and refute" structure is effective for argumentative writing. Your introduction also does good work establishing historical context and stating your thesis clearly.

Areas for Revision

Missing Evidence (the  placeholders)

I notice several bracketed placeholders  throughout the essay where quotations or specific claims should appear (eg, the Mike Gunn quote, the "prayer allows..." claim). Before final submission, these must be filled with actual, cited sources. Currently, your rebuttals respond to vague paraphrases rather than concrete claims, which weakens your credibility. Readers need to see the *exact* argument you're refuting to trust that you're representing it fairly (avoiding the strawman fallacy you're trying to avoid committing).

Ironic Use of Unsupported Claims

You critique opponents for relying on "shaky speculation" and lack of data, which is a strong critique—but your own essay sometimes falls into the same trap. For example, in paragraph 2, you assert that "no data shows that prayer actually causes a better educational or social environment," but you don't cite any actual studies or data yourself. Similarly, the claim about moral improvement since segregation-era America is asserted without support. Strengthen your argument by citing at least 2-3 concrete sources (studies, statistics, or scholarly articles) to match the rigor you're demanding from the other side.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

A few recurring errors to address:

Subject-verb agreement: "The concept of God is not discourage" → should be "discouraged"
Article usage: "the ban of school prayer" → "the ban *on* school prayer"; "a large part of the religious population evaporates" is a bit awkward—consider "excludes" or "ignores" rather than "evaporates," which suggests disappearance rather than exclusion
Word choice precision: "detriment" not "determent" (paragraph 6); "discourage" should likely be "discouraged" in the same sentence
Preposition issues: "influence by prayer" should likely be "influence *from* prayer"

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller errors, as they can distract careful readers from your argument.

Logical Clarity Needed

In paragraph 4, the phrase "moral tarnishes such as segregation, lynchings, and the oppression of women blemished society" during the era when prayer was allowed is a compelling point, but it needs more careful framing. As written, it risks implying correlation proves causation—the same fallacy you accuse your opponents of committing when they link the *absence* of prayer to rising social problems. Consider explicitly stating that you're using this example to show correlation is not causation in *either* direction, which would make your argument more intellectually consistent.

The Thimble/Burning House Analogy

This is a vivid and effective analogy—well done. Consider using 1-2 more analogies like this throughout the essay, as they make abstract arguments about "fairness" and "constitutional rights" more concrete and memorable for readers.

Conclusion Polish

Your final paragraph contains a slightly garbled sentence: "Because America's vast religious diversity and the lack of apparent good resulting from discouraging religion diversity, public schools need to remain free of public prayer." This sentence has a grammatical issue (the "because" clause needs a verb, eg, "Because America has vast religious diversity...") and the phrase "discouraging religion diversity" is unclear—do you mean "the lack of good resulting from *promoting* religious uniformity"? Revise this sentence for clarity, as it's your final point and deserves precision.

Priority Revisions for Next Draft

Fill in all  placeholders with specific, cited claims and quotations
Add 2-3 concrete sources (statistics, court cases, or scholarly research) to support your own claims about the lack of causal evidence
Proofread for grammar, especially subject-verb agreement and article usage
Revise the final sentence of the conclusion for grammatical clarity
Consider adding a second analogy to reinforce your rhetorical strength

Final Note

Your argument is intellectually sound and your rhetorical strategy—refuting opposing points systematically—is sophisticated. With stronger evidentiary support and careful proofreading, this essay could be very persuasive. Keep up the strong critical thinking!

Feedback Report: AIDS Discrimination Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and clearly relevant social issue, and your passion for the topic comes through strongly. You've done solid research and gathered several compelling real-world examples (Woodley, Lentz, the Valhalla pharmacist) that could make for a persuasive essay. However, the essay currently has some significant problems with logical consistency, source integration, and precision of language that need to be addressed before this argument will be fully convincing to an academic reader.

Critical Issue: A Self-Contradiction

The most serious problem is in the paragraph responding to the "health-risk" argument. After presenting quotations meant to prove AIDS *cannot* be transmitted through casual contact, you write: "Therefore, co-workers, patients, friends, family, or anyone else in close contact with the victim is in danger of contracting the disease from everyday occurrences, or contact."

This sentence directly contradicts your thesis and undermines your entire argument. I assume this is an error (perhaps you meant "is *not* in danger"), but as written, it hands your opposition their strongest point. Please revise this immediately — it's the kind of mistake that can make a reader doubt everything else in the essay.

Argument and Logic

Your thesis is clear: discrimination against people with AIDS in employment and insurance is both morally and legally wrong. This is a strong, arguable claim.
However, some of your reasoning needs tightening. For example, you assert the opposition's argument is "weak" and a "fallacy" very early on, before you've actually presented evidence. Save these judgments for after you've made your case — right now it feels like you're declaring victory before the debate has started.
Your rebuttal of the "insurance risk" argument is underdeveloped. You concede that AIDS patients are a "bad investment" in business terms, then pivot to a moral claim ("they are still human beings"). This is a good instinct, but you should also directly engage with *why* treating people as mere financial risks conflicts with legal principles (eg, the ADA) rather than relying solely on emotional appeal.
The comparison to cancer patients is effective and could be developed further — it's one of your strongest rhetorical moves.

Use of Sources

The placeholder brackets () suggest missing quotations that need to be filled in — make sure every one of these is completed with the actual quoted material, a citation, and (crucially) analysis. Right now, many quotes seem to be simply *inserted* rather than *integrated*. For each quotation, consider:

Introducing it with context (who said it, when, why it matters)
Following it with your own analysis of what it proves

For example, after David Allen's quote about firing Ron Lentz, you ask a good rhetorical question, but you don't fully explain *why* the hospital's justification fails legally or ethically. Push your analysis further.

Also, double-check your opening claim that AIDS is "the most common disease in the world today." This is a significant factual claim and needs a citation — as it stands, it may not be accurate (compare with cardiovascular disease, respiratory infections, etc), and using an unsupported/inaccurate claim in your first sentence can undermine your credibility from the start.

Structure and Paragraphing

Your case-study paragraphs (Woodley, Lentz, the pharmacist) work well as evidence, but each would benefit from a clear topic sentence stating *what point this example proves* before you tell the story. Right now the narrative sometimes overtakes the argument. Consider a structure like:

> "The case of Mark Woodley illustrates how even sympathetic employers succumb to unfounded fears about AIDS transmission." → *then* the story → *then* explicit analysis connecting back to your thesis.

The conclusion introduces a new and somewhat confusing point about the ADA classifying AIDS as a "disability," which you then say "is not true." This needs clarification — are you arguing the law's *classification* is inaccurate, but *strategically useful*? This is an interesting point but needs to be explained, not just asserted, since it currently sounds contradictory.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to check in revision:
Subject-verb agreement: "The instances of people acquiring AIDS... has been" → should be "have been"
Typos: "Wookley" (Woodley), "A AIDS," "hospice car" (care), "fays" (days)
The phrase "one can conclude" is repeated several times — vary your transitions
Watch comma splices and run-on sentences, especially in longer sentences with multiple clauses

Suggested Next Steps

Fix the contradictory sentence about transmission risk immediately.
Fill in and properly cite all placeholder quotations.
Add explicit topic sentences to each case-study paragraph.
Verify/cite your opening factual claim.
Proofread carefully for the mechanical errors listed above.
Strengthen your engagement with the opposing argument rather than dismissing it outright.

With these revisions, you'll have a much more rigorous and persuasive essay. The evidence you've gathered is genuinely strong — now let the logic and language do it justice.

Thank you for tackling such a complex and historically loaded topic. You have a clear stance and you clearly did research, but the essay needs work in several areas before it is ready to submit. Below are the main issues, organized by category.

Before revising anything else, fill these in with the actual quotations, properly cited (author, source, page if available).

Once the quotes are in place, be sure to:
Introduce speakers with full context the first time only, then use last names afterward (eg, "Kramer" instead of repeating full credentials each time).
Follow every quotation with your own analysis explaining why it supports your point. Right now some quotes seem to be dropped in without connecting sentences (eg, "Stories such as these certainly put a hold on the idea of 'family preservation'" — this needs more explanation of *why* the quote proves this).

The cost argument is dropped after being mentioned in the introduction and only reappears briefly in the paragraph about reducing welfare payments. Each new paragraph often starts with a new topic without linking back to the previous one. Add transitional sentences like "Beyond the historical stigma, opponents also raise a more practical objection: the breakup of families" to guide readers.

The conclusion also ends on a quotation () with no closing commentary from you. Essays should end with the writer's own voice — restate your thesis in new words and leave the reader with a final, memorable statement, not someone else's words.

"publics attention" → public's attention
"no where near" → nowhere near
"difficult to content with" → contend with
"he use of an orphanage" → the use
"provides and alternative" → provides an alternative
"it's own" → its own (when possessive)

Watch subject-verb agreement: "orphanages have been around for hundreds of years" is fine, but check sentences like "This 'social control' theory was seen..." for smoother phrasing.

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud after revising — this will help you catch missing words and awkward phrasing.

Summary

Your argument has real potential: the historical reframing of orphanages, the comparison to foster care instability, and the practical funding solution are all strong angles. Your top priorities for revision should be (1) filling in all missing quotations with proper citation, (2) strengthening the cost counterargument, (3) adding transitions between paragraphs, and (4) proofreading for the grammar issues listed above. Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this draft. Your essay tackles an important and complex topic—the relationship between business and ethics—and you clearly have a genuine argument you want to make. Below is feedback organized by key areas to help you strengthen the essay.

Overall Argument and Thesis

Your central claim seems to be that successful business depends on ethical relationships, not just profit. This is a solid, arguable thesis. However, it needs to be stated more clearly and placed prominently, likely at the end of your first paragraph. Right now, your introduction moves through several ideas (definitions of business, definitions of ethics, the claim that "business and ethics don't mix") before arriving at your point. Consider revising so a reader knows your specific argument within the first few sentences.

Structure and Paragraph Focus

Each paragraph introduces a good example (Sam Walton, advertising, B.F. Goodrich), but the connections between paragraphs are sometimes unclear, and topic sentences don't always signal what the paragraph will argue. For instance, the transition from discussing advertising to discussing "Norton's article" is abrupt. Try adding a sentence at the start of each paragraph that explicitly states how this new example supports your thesis, and a sentence at the end that ties back to your central claim.

Use of Sources

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay has numerous placeholder brackets  where quotations or evidence should be. In the final draft, every one of these needs to be filled in with:
The actual quotation or paraphrase
Proper in-text citation (author, page number, or appropriate format)
Your own analysis explaining why this evidence matters

Right now, sentences like "According to Fortune, . This supports my claim because..." show good structure (evidence + analysis), but without the actual quotation, a reader cannot evaluate whether the evidence truly supports your claim. Make sure quotations are integrated smoothly into your sentences rather than just dropped in.

The B.F. Goodrich Counter-Example

I want to highlight a specific issue: you introduce this case by saying "To contrast my claim," but then you explain how Goodrich's patience in building a relationship with the Air Force led to *long-term profit*—which actually supports your thesis rather than contradicting it. Either:
Revise your framing so this is presented as supporting evidence, or
Find a genuine counterargument (eg, a case where ethical behavior led to failure, or where unethical behavior succeeded) and explain why your thesis still holds despite this challenge.

A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge real counterarguments and respond to them—this strengthens your credibility rather than weakening your position.

Clarity and Word Choice

A few specific issues to address:

"Ethics are" should be "Ethics is" (ethics is typically treated as singular when referring to the field of study).
Repetition: The phrase "self satisfaction and profit" and the idea that people are "greedy" appears multiple times. Vary your language or trim repeated points.
Informal tone: Phrases like "This is what irritates business" and "everyone is happy" feel conversational. Academic writing benefits from more formal phrasing, eg, "This approach frustrates ethical business practices" or similar.
Vague pronouns: In sentences like "This explains why something has to change," make sure "this" clearly refers to a specific prior idea.

Grammar and Mechanics

Watch subject-verb agreement (eg, "Ethics are the goals" → "Ethics is the goal" or "Ethical principles are the goals").
Check comma usage before "which" and "because" clauses—some are missing, others are unnecessary.
Proofread for extra spaces (eg, "happiness, but" has an extra space before "but" earlier in the essay).

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis into one clear sentence and place it at the end of paragraph one.
Fill in all bracketed placeholders with real, properly cited evidence.
Reconsider the Goodrich example—either reframe it as supporting evidence or find a true counterargument.
Add transitional sentences at the start and end of each paragraph explicitly linking back to your thesis.
Proofread carefully for grammar, especially subject-verb agreement and informal phrasing.

Final Thoughts

You have chosen relevant, interesting examples (Walton, Goodrich, advertising) that could make a compelling case. The main work now is filling in the missing evidence, tightening the logical connections between your examples and your thesis, and reconsidering whether your "counterargument" actually contradicts your point. With revision, this essay can become a much stronger, more persuasive piece.

Good luck with your revisions!

Feedback on Your Essay: Comparing Shriver and Methvin

Overall Impression

You've taken on a genuinely challenging task—comparing two arguments on their rhetorical merits rather than just summarizing them—and your essay shows real analytical ambition. You correctly identify key concepts like claims, supporting evidence, and logical fallacies, and you apply them somewhat consistently to both authors. However, the essay needs work in several areas: sharpening your criteria for evaluation, deepening your analysis (rather than just asserting weakness/strength), fixing citation gaps, and cleaning up grammar issues that occasionally obscure your meaning.

Content and Argument

Establish clearer criteria upfront. You state that a strong argument needs "an arguable claim" and evidence that supports it—good, but this criteria list is thin. Consider adding what counts as strong *evidence* (facts, statistics, expert testimony from qualified sources) versus weak evidence (unsupported opinion, appeals to unqualified authority). This will make your later evaluations feel less like assertions and more like applications of a consistent standard.

Be careful with the fact/opinion distinction. Your thesis claims Shriver is stronger because he uses "facts" while Methvin relies on "opinions." But looking at your own analysis, Shriver also relies heavily on quotations and religious/theological reasoning—which are arguably interpretive claims, not empirical facts. Similarly, you fault Methvin for quoting an "unqualified authority," but you don't explain why Shriver's use of Biblical quotation is *not* also an appeal to authority. This is an important tension you should address directly rather than let it undermine your thesis. Perhaps the real distinction is not "facts vs. opinions" but "supported claims vs. unsupported claims" or "qualified vs. unqualified sources."

Deepen the analysis instead of repeating the complaint. Several times you note that Methvin "does not elaborate" on a good piece of evidence, then move to the next point without explaining *why* this matters or what elaboration would have looked like. Push yourself to explain the consequence: What conclusion is the reader left unable to draw? What connection remains implicit that should be explicit?

Revisit the introduction. The opening paragraph makes several large historical claims ("Religion essentially was the foundation of society of the past," "people... learned through war and consequence, that church and state do not mix") that are broad generalizations unsupported by evidence. Since your essay's real strength is close analysis of two texts, consider trimming this historical framing and moving more quickly to your actual analytical claim.

Organization

Your paragraph-by-paragraph structure (claim → evidence → fallacy) is clear and easy to follow, and using parallel structure to discuss both authors helps readability. One suggestion: your conclusion largely restates the thesis without adding new insight. Consider using the conclusion to reflect on the broader implication—what does this comparison teach us about what makes religious/political arguments persuasive in public discourse?

Evidence and Citation

This is the most urgent technical issue: every direct quotation is missing (marked with ). Since your entire argument depends on close reading of specific language, you must restore these quotes with proper in-text citation (author's name, page number if available). Without them, a reader cannot verify or evaluate your claims at all—this alone would need to be fixed before submission.

Language and Grammar

You have a number of errors that a careful proofreading pass should catch:

"it continues to be an influential factor" (missing article)
"people have learned through war and consequence" (verb form)
"This ruling has become a subject of controversy" (typo: "ha")
"A researcher for his argument..." or rephrase: "As research for his argument, he conducted a survey..."
"he defines the first reason as a theological reason for why praying has should not be allowed" (redundant verb—remove "has")
"he has only gathered momen tum" (spacing typo)
Watch subject-verb agreement and article usage throughout (a/an/the)—this is a recurring pattern worth reviewing systematically.

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller issues after you've revised the content.

Summary of Priorities

Restore all missing quotations with citations (top priority).
Clarify and complicate your fact/opinion distinction so it doesn't work against your own examples.
Push your analysis further—explain the "so what" after each observation.
Trim the overly broad introduction.
Proofread carefully for grammar and typos.

You have a solid analytical instinct; with revision, this essay's argument will be much more persuasive and precise.

Feedback Report on "Media Access to Executions" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, debatable topic and anchored your argument in a real, well-documented case (the Robert Alton Harris execution). This is a strong foundation. However, the essay currently reads more like a case summary than an argumentative essay, and there are some structural, logical, and language issues that need attention before this fully succeeds as persuasive writing.

Strengths

Concrete evidence: Using a specific legal case with named individuals (Vasquez, Schnacke, Brelsford, Sherwood, Morain) gives your argument real-world grounding, which is far more persuasive than abstract claims.
Counterargument acknowledged: You address the "media wants ratings" objection, which shows awareness of the debate's complexity.
Clear position: Your final paragraph states your stance unambiguously.

Areas Needing Revision

Missing Content (Critical)
You have several  placeholders where quotations or paraphrased content should be. These are essential—your reasoning currently *depends* on what Vasquez said, what the First Amendment states, what Brelsford argued, etc Please fill these in before this can be evaluated as a complete argument. Also, since you're using specific facts and quotes from named sources, you'll need to cite them properly (in-text citations and a references list, depending on your citation style).

Thesis and Logical Structure
Your opening sentence tries to do too much at once and creates a shaky syllogism:
> "the public has a right to be informed about anything and everything... people want to be informed about the death penalty; therefore, media should have access..."

This logic has a gap: wanting information isn't the same as having a *right* to it, and "media access" isn't automatically justified just because the public is curious. Consider revising to something like:

> "Because the First Amendment guarantees freedom of the press, and because executions are a matter of significant public concern, media should be permitted to witness and report on executions—though their access should reasonably be limited to prevent security risks."

This gives you a clearer, more defensible claim to build the rest of the essay around.

Balance Between Narrative and Argument
Right now, roughly 60% of your essay narrates the Harris case chronologically (arrest, appeals, lawsuit, ruling) before you pivot to argumentation. Consider:
Trimming the biographical/legal narrative to only the details that directly serve your argument (eg, you don't need his full criminal history).
Front-loading your argument, then using the case as *evidence* for specific claims, rather than as the main structure of the essay.

A useful technique: after your thesis, state a claim ("Media presence does not compromise security"), then use the Harris case as a supporting example, rather than letting the case dictate your paragraph order.

Conclusion
Your ending—"If you find the event grisly and gruesome, turn it off; it is that simple"—is catchy but slightly dismissive in tone for an academic essay. It also oversimplifies your own argument, since you've spent the essay discussing *print/eyewitness* access, not televised broadcast (which you actually argue should remain restricted). Consider a conclusion that reaffirms the distinction you built: informed witnessing is a democratic necessity, while filming raises legitimate safety concerns that justify narrower limits.

Language and Grammar
As you continue improving your English, here are some specific corrections to watch for:

"there access" → "their access"
"receive national attention" → "received national attention"
"made to effort to justify" → "made no effort to justify" (this changes the meaning significantly—please check this sentence carefully, as it currently seems garbled)
"inmate are more tense" → "inmates are more tense"
"This argument is debated often around times when..." → smoother as "This debate resurfaces whenever the death penalty is carried out."

I'd also recommend reading complex sentences aloud—several run slightly long and could be split for clarity, especially in the paragraph explaining Schnacke's ruling.

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all  sections with properly cited material.
Rewrite your thesis to more precisely connect your evidence to your claim.
Restructure so your argument (not the chronological case narrative) drives the paragraph order.
Revise the conclusion to reflect the nuanced distinction between *witnessing* and *filming* that you develop in the body.
Proofread for the grammar issues noted above—consider reading each sentence in isolation to catch subject-verb agreement and word choice errors.

You have excellent raw material here. With tighter argumentative structure and completed citations, this essay could be quite persuasive. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing such a personal and courageous piece of writing. Your willingness to use your own experience to support your argument is a real strength—it gives the essay emotional weight and authenticity that many academic essays lack. Below are some observations and suggestions to help you strengthen this draft.

Strengths

Your personal narrative is compelling and serves your thesis well. The opening story about your father's failed attempt to discuss sex, followed by your own experience with unplanned pregnancy and divorce, creates a strong emotional foundation for your argument that schools need to fill the gap parents leave. The essay also comes full circle nicely, ending with your son's experience—this structure (personal problem → argument → personal resolution) is effective.

Areas for Revision

Missing Evidence
This is the most urgent issue. Throughout the essay, you have placeholders like  where statistics and evidence should be. These need to be filled in with actual, cited data before this essay can be evaluated on its argumentative merits. For example:
What was the actual 1989 statistic about unwanted pregnancies?
What did the studies on sex education in schools actually conclude?
What evidence do you have about churches "turning their backs" on youth?

Without this evidence, your claims—however reasonable they may sound—remain unsupported assertions. Please locate your sources and integrate the actual data with proper citations (check whether your instructor requires MLA, APA, or another format).

Clarify Your Central Argument
Your thesis seems to be: *schools should teach sexual morals and values because parents and churches are failing to do so.* This is a debatable, arguable claim—good. However, the essay sometimes blurs into a stance that seems more like *"casual sex is wrong and teens should wait for marriage,"* which is a different (and more contested) claim. Consider whether your thesis is about:
(a) the importance of sex education generally, or
(b) the importance of teaching a specific set of values (abstinence-until-marriage) through schools.

These are related but distinct arguments requiring different kinds of evidence. Choose one primary focus and make sure every paragraph supports it.

Address Counterarguments More Fully
You raise the objection that some parents find school-based sex education "unethical," but your response doesn't fully engage with *why* they object (likely because they want to control what values are taught, not just whether sex ed happens at all). A stronger rebuttal would acknowledge this concern specifically and then explain why schools are nonetheless a necessary or appropriate venue.

Sentence-Level Revisions
Since you're writing in a second language, a few grammar patterns appear repeatedly and are worth reviewing:
Possessive apostrophes with plurals: "bird's and the bee's" should be "birds and the bees"; "parental guidance...for youths'" should be "for youth" or "for youths" (no apostrophe needed here).
Comma splices: eg, "Casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading, animalistic, and runs the risk of disease" — this needs "it's" (contraction) and could be restructured as: "Casual sex is unacceptable; it is degrading, animalistic, and risks disease."
Sentence fragments used as complete sentences: eg, "The loving compassion two people should give each other." This is a noun phrase, not a full sentence. Consider integrating it into the previous sentence with a colon or semicolon, or rewriting as a complete clause: "They also learn the loving compassion two people should give each other."
Word choice: "Aids is a perfectly good example" — "perfectly good" sounds oddly casual for a disease with serious health consequences; consider "a clear example" or "one significant example."

Paragraph Focus
Some paragraphs try to do too much. For instance, the paragraph beginning "Education of sexual activity for youth is no longer taught by parents" moves from parental failure, to STDs, to marriage statistics, to the "sacredness of marriage" — four different ideas. Consider breaking this into two focused paragraphs: one on parental failure and its consequences, another on your marriage-related evidence.

Conclusion
Your ending is touching, but consider adding one or two sentences that explicitly restate your argument's broader implication—not just for your son, but for society or policy. This will help tie your personal story back to the academic argument you're making.

Priority for Revision
Fill in all missing evidence with real, cited sources.
Sharpen and unify your thesis.
Strengthen the counterargument section.
Proofread for grammar patterns noted above.

You have a genuine, persuasive voice here—the personal narrative gives your argument real credibility. With solid evidence and tighter organization, this essay will be much stronger. Good work, and I look forward to seeing your revision.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Right to Refuse Life-Support

Thank you for sharing this thoughtful and personal essay. You've chosen a genuinely difficult topic and engaged with it sincerely. Below is feedback organized by key areas, along with suggestions for revision.

Strengths

Your essay tackles a complex ethical issue with genuine reflection. The personal paragraph near the end (beginning "I personally consider...") is your strongest writing—it's concrete, specific, and emotionally honest. You also do well to acknowledge multiple perspectives (those who want to prolong life at any cost vs. those who don't).

Structure and Organization

Your essay follows a logical progression: define life → define death → explore different attitudes → consider the medical perspective → state your own view. This is a sound structure. However, the essay would benefit from:

A clearer thesis statement. Your opening paragraph ends with a strong claim ("The right to refuse life-support is an individual one"), but the essay sometimes drifts into general reflection on life and death rather than building a sustained argument for this specific right. Make sure each paragraph clearly connects back to *why* this is an individual right, not just *that* the topic is complicated.

Missing content marked by . Several sentences appear to be missing (indicated by ), especially in key transitional moments—defining life, defining death, and the conclusion. These gaps make it hard to fully evaluate your argument's development. Please ensure the final version has no missing text, especially in the conclusion, which currently ends abruptly.

The physician paragraph feels underdeveloped. You raise an interesting question ("How do they face it?") but don't answer it. Consider either developing this paragraph with a real example, a hypothetical scenario, or research/testimony, or trimming it if it's not central to your argument.

Paragraph Development

The paragraph on defining "life" (paragraph 2) relies heavily on short, similar sentences ("Our memories...," "Our personality...," "We each want...") that list factors without synthesizing them. Try combining some of these into more complex sentences that show *relationships* between ideas:

> *Weak:* "Our memories of childhood define how we look upon events. Our personality plays a part in our perception of instances in our life."
>
> *Stronger:* "Because our childhood memories and personalities shape how we interpret events, no two people can define 'a meaningful life' in exactly the same way."

This kind of combining will also help with sentence variety, discussed below.

Sentence-Level Issues

Sentence fragments and run-ons: Some sentences lack clear grammatical completeness, eg, "I've heard of people o want to be put on life support" (likely a typo for "who want"). Please proofread carefully for typos.

Repetitive sentence structure: Many sentences begin with "We" or "Our" and follow a simple subject-verb-object pattern. Varying sentence openings and length will make your prose more dynamic. Compare:

   - Original: "We must consider what the boundaries are..."
   - Revised: "How far are we willing to go to cheat death? This question forces us to consider..."

Awkward phrasing: Some phrases need refinement:
   - "used to use" → likely meant "us" ("determined... to us")
   - "the answer must be our own decision" → consider "the answer must come from within each of us"

Rhetorical questions: You use these effectively in places ("Where do you draw the line...?"), but overuse can weaken argumentative force. Reserve them for key moments, and follow them with your own reasoned response rather than leaving them open.

Content Suggestions

Strengthen the definition of "death" section. You trace a brief history (breathing → heartbeat → brain activity) but don't fully explain *why* this history matters to your argument. Connect it explicitly: how does the *evolution* of this definition support the idea that death (and thus life-support decisions) should be personally, not universally, defined?

Address counterarguments more directly. You mention people who value life "at any cost" fairly sympathetically, but you might strengthen your argument by acknowledging *why* someone might disagree with your final position—and then explain why individual choice should still prevail even so.

Revise your conclusion. Right now it restates points already made without adding new insight or a memorable closing thought. Consider ending with a specific call to action (eg, encouraging readers to document their wishes through legal means, like a living will) to give the essay a practical, forward-looking close.

Summary

Your essay shows real thought and care about a serious topic. Focus your revision on (1) filling in the missing content, (2) tightening the connection between each section and your central argument, (3) varying sentence structure, and (4) strengthening your conclusion. The personal paragraph shows you *can* write vividly and specifically—try to bring that same voice to the rest of the essay.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a complex and important topic, and your personal experience adds a powerful, authentic dimension that many academic essays lack. You clearly have strong convictions and relevant firsthand knowledge. Below are suggestions to help strengthen the essay's structure, argumentation, and clarity.

Strengths to Build On

Your personal anecdote about being encouraged to quit your job is the most compelling part of the essay—it grounds your argument in lived experience and gives you credibility. The specific example of Spring Meadows Children's Home also works well because it shows a concrete alternative to the policies you criticize, moving your argument from complaint to constructive proposal.

Areas for Revision

Missing Content
You have two bracketed placeholders  where quotations should appear (Hoover's statement and the social worker's reasoning). These are important pieces of evidence—please fill them in before submitting. Without them, key claims feel unsupported.

Historical Accuracy
Please verify your claim that Hoover *began* the welfare system. Historically, Hoover resisted federal relief programs and favored local/private charity; the federal welfare system is more commonly attributed to FDR's New Deal (particularly the Social Security Act of 1935). If you have a source for the Hoover claim, cite it; otherwise, revise this section to avoid a factual error that could undermine your credibility with readers.

Thesis Clarity
Your central claim—that welfare reform must restore family values to succeed—is worth stating explicitly and early, ideally by the end of your introduction. Currently, the thesis emerges gradually across several paragraphs. Consider adding a clear thesis sentence at the end of paragraph one.

Logical Gaps
Some causal claims need more support:
You assert that welfare dependency "eroded" family values and "led to" violence and crime. This is a strong claim that many social scientists debate. Consider acknowledging alternative explanations (structural poverty, systemic discrimination, deindustrialization) or citing evidence for your causal chain, even briefly.
The jump from "families lost pride" to "this caused crime and violence" needs an intermediate step or supporting evidence.

Characterization of Opponents
Your critique of the "Contract with America" would be more persuasive with balance. Right now, phrases like "obvious that Mr. Gingrich does not understand" and "coldness toward the poor" may alienate readers who don't already agree with you. Try steelmanning the opposing view first (eg, "Proponents argue that immediate work requirements will restore accountability") before explaining why you find it insufficient. This strengthens your ethos as a fair-minded arguer.

Organization
Your essay covers a lot of ground: history, personal experience, critique of Republican policy, and your own proposal. Consider tightening the structure:
Para 1: Introduction + clear thesis
Para 2: Historical context (brief, verified)
Para 3: Personal experience as evidence
Para 4: Critique of "Contract with America" (with acknowledgment of opposing view)
Para 5: Your proposed solution (job training, day care, group homes, Spring Meadows example)
Para 6: Conclusion

This would create a clearer throughline from problem → evidence → critique → solution.

Language and Mechanics
A few recurring issues to address in editing:
"It's" vs. "its": You use "it's" for the possessive multiple times (eg, "it's purpose," "it's values"). Remember: *its* = possessive; *it's* = "it is."
Capitalization: Avoid capitalizing common nouns unless they're part of an official title (eg, "welfare system" not "Welfare System," unless referring to a specific named program).
"Air programs": This appears to be a typo—did you mean "aid programs"?
Comma splices: Several sentences run two independent clauses together with only a comma (eg, "Hoover's efforts were put forth somewhat reluctantly. He stated that..."). Consider using a period, semicolon, or conjunction.
Word choice: "Indolent" is a strong, judgmental word choice; consider softer language like "discouraged from working" to maintain a persuasive rather than accusatory tone.

Final Thoughts

Your essay has a genuine, persuasive voice and a solid structure of problem-critique-solution. The main tasks now are: (1) fill in missing quotations, (2) verify historical claims, (3) tighten the logical connections between dependency and social problems, and (4) polish grammar/mechanics. With these revisions, this will be a much stronger piece.

Feedback Report: Draft Essay on Marquette's "Avenue Commons" Project

Overall Impression

You've chosen a timely, locally relevant topic and clearly did research into costs, transit impacts, and alternative options (football, cutting the rifle team). Your passion comes through, and you have real evidence to work with. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of loosely connected complaints than a tightly argued position. Below are the main areas to strengthen in revision.

Clarify Your Central Claim

Your essay's title problem is "ethics," but you never clearly define what makes a university decision "ethical" or "unethical." You use the word repeatedly (comparing Avenue Commons to UCLA's curriculum changes, calling the football idea "completely ethical") without establishing criteria. Revise your thesis to state explicitly: What ethical principle is being violated? Is it that students bear costs without meaningful benefit? That the university is prioritizing image over education? That decisions are made without student input? Choose one clear ethical framework and apply it consistently—this will unify your paragraphs, which currently jump between financial, aesthetic, and identity-based objections.

Fix the Missing Citations

There are several  placeholders in your draft where quotations or references should appear (the motto, the multicultural center transition, the Todd Clausen quote, the final "tradition of the "). These gaps make it impossible for a reader to follow your argument, especially the paragraph about Marquette's "Urban" identity, which currently makes no sense without the missing quote. Before doing anything else, fill these in.

Strengthen the Use of Evidence

You have good raw material—the $10 million figure, the transit revenue loss, ammunition costs—but the numbers appear without clear sourcing. Where did the "$1 million and $600,000" transit estimate come from? Who reported the tuition figures? Even brief in-text attributions ("according to the Milwaukee Journal Sentinel...") will make your argument far more credible to an academic reader. Also double-check your math and consistency: you say ten million from students and a matching ten million from "locals," but never reconcile how both amounts are being paid or over what timeline.

Reconsider the Comparisons

The opening comparison to UCLA's "so-called physcological differences" [sic] courses is confusing and risks alienating readers—it's unclear what point you're making, and the phrasing sounds dismissive of diversity-related coursework in a way that may distract from your actual argument about spending priorities. Consider cutting this comparison or replacing it with something more directly relevant (eg, another university's failed beautification project).

Similarly, the rifle team paragraph is vivid and well-detailed, but it needs a clearer transition explaining *why* it belongs in this essay. Right now it feels like a separate essay about wasteful athletic programs. Make the connective logic explicit: "Just as Avenue Commons prioritizes appearance over student needs, so does funding a low-visibility sport like rifle over broad-based investments like football." Otherwise, readers may feel you've changed topics.

Organize Around a Clear Structure

Right now the essay moves: enrollment → cost of commons → transit → rent/property tax → "grass won't help learning" → existing green space → football alternative → rifle team → tuition/Todd Clausen → conclusion. This is a lot of ground covered somewhat unevenly. Consider reorganizing into clear sections:

Introduction – state the ethical claim clearly.
Financial costs to students (tuition, rent/property tax, transit losses) – combine your related financial points here.
Questioning the stated benefit (existing green space argument, "grass won't help you learn").
Alternative solutions (football, cutting low-enrollment programs) – frame this as "here's what ethical spending would look like instead."
Conclusion – return explicitly to your ethical framework.

Tone and Word Choice

Some phrasing undermines your credibility: "none the least" (likely meant "nonetheless" or similar), "hoopla," "many a person's parade," "physcological" (spelling), "off" for "of." A proofreading pass focused on word choice and spelling will help. Also, phrases like "Think about it" (used three times) and rhetorical questions work occasionally but become repetitive—vary your rhetorical strategies.

Address the Counterargument More Fully

You briefly acknowledge that some students want the green space for appearance's sake, which is good. Push this further: acknowledge the strongest version of the university's argument (eg, that aesthetic improvements can meaningfully affect recruitment and thus long-term institutional health) before rebutting it. This will make your own position more persuasive rather than dismissive.

Next Steps

Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Write one sentence defining your ethical standard, and revise topic sentences to connect back to it.
Reorganize paragraphs by theme (financial/practical/alternative).
Proofread for spelling and word choice.
Add a paragraph steelmanning the opposing view before your rebuttal.

You have strong instincts for using concrete detail (ammunition costs, specific dollar figures)—the revision task now is mainly architectural: giving those details a clear framework to live in.

Feedback on "Baby Richard" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, emotionally resonant topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. Your central argument—that removing Richard from his adoptive family would cause lasting psychological harm—is well-conceived and supported by a genuine attempt to use developmental psychology (Erikson) as evidence. This is a strong foundation. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: organization, source integration, argumentative balance, and language precision.

Structure and Argumentation

Missing thesis roadmap: Your introduction sets up the story well, but it doesn't clearly preview your three main arguments (psychological harm to Richard, emotional harm to the Does, and damage to the institution of adoption). Consider adding a sentence near the end of your introduction that maps out these points for the reader.

Incomplete evidence: Several places show  where quotations, timelines, or citations should appear. These are clearly placeholders, but remember: a strong argumentative essay depends on *fully integrated* evidence, not just gestures toward sources. When you insert Mark Hansen's quote or Erikson's theory, make sure you (1) introduce the source with context, (2) quote or paraphrase precisely, and (3) explain *how* this evidence supports your specific claim, rather than simply reporting information.

Counterargument is underdeveloped: You mention Kirchner's perspective only briefly ("Perhaps Kirchner's intentions are good...") near the end. A stronger essay would engage seriously with the biological father's legal and emotional claims *earlier*, and then explain why your position still outweighs his. Right now, the essay risks feeling one-sided rather than persuasive to a skeptical reader.

Emotional appeals need logical support: Rhetorical questions like "Think back to when you were four years old..." and "Nobody likes to be hurt, so why go through with it?" are engaging, but in academic argumentation they should be paired with more explicit reasoning or evidence, not stand alone as persuasive strategies. Consider revising these into transitions that lead into your psychological or legal evidence.

Paragraph on adoption more broadly: The paragraph on adoption's societal benefits (paragraph 7) feels slightly disconnected from your main argument about Richard specifically. Consider tightening the transition—explain explicitly how the *Baby Richard* case damages the broader system, using this as a bridge to the Uniform Adoption Act discussion.

Use of Sources

You reference Newsweek, the ABA Journal, and Erikson, which shows good research effort. However:
Make sure every source is properly introduced (author, publication, date) and cited according to your required format (MLA/APA).
Avoid inserting long block quotes without analysis—follow each quotation with 2-3 sentences of your own interpretation.
The comparison to "Baby Jessica" is a good analytical move, but you should clarify *why* age-based developmental differences matter using more specific research beyond Erikson's single theory, if possible.

Language and Style Notes

Since English is your second language, here are some specific patterns to watch for:

Capitalization after periods: "when Daniela Janikova gave birth" → "When Daniela Janikova gave birth" (sentence should begin with capital letter).
Preposition errors: "she lied to Kirchner in telling him" → "by telling him"; "ripping him away form the only family" → "from."
Relative pronouns: "whom are referred to as Jane and John Doe" → "who are referred to" (use "who" for subject position).
Verb agreement/spelling: "will no longer very from state to state" → "vary."
Awkward phrasing: "the baby therefore gets a much deserved chance at life" and "won't have to kill it" (referring to abortion) — this phrasing is emotionally loaded and imprecise; consider more neutral, academic language such as "avoid terminating the pregnancy" or simply focus on the adoption benefit without the abortion comparison, which introduces a separate controversial topic that distracts from your main argument.
Informal tone: Phrases like "the tug of war battle for Richard begins" are vivid but journalistic. For an academic essay, aim for a slightly more formal register throughout, saving vivid language for particular emphasis rather than general narration.

Suggestions for Revision

Add a clear thesis statement with a roadmap of your three main arguments.
Fill in all  placeholders with fully cited, well-integrated evidence.
Add a paragraph engaging seriously with Kirchner's rights/perspective, then rebut it.
Proofread carefully for capitalization, prepositions, and pronoun errors (a grammar checker or writing center consultation would help).
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs, especially between the adoption-institution argument and the Uniform Adoption Act discussion.
Reconsider the abortion comparison—it may weaken your focus rather than strengthen it.

You have a genuine argumentative instinct and clear organizational logic underlying this draft. With attention to source integration, counterargument, and language precision, this essay could become a much more polished and persuasive piece. Good work so far—keep revising!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles a genuinely difficult ethical issue, and I can see you've put effort into structuring your argument around three clear reasons. Below is feedback organized by area, with suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is identifiable, but it needs tightening. You state that euthanasia "should not be allowed to be practiced as freely as it is" — this is a more moderate claim than some of your later language suggests (eg, "euthanasia should not be allowed to exist as it does" in the conclusion). Decide clearly: are you arguing for *more restrictions* or for *abolition*? Right now the essay drifts between these two positions, which weakens your argument. Pick one stance and apply it consistently throughout.

Your three-reason structure (public opposition, comparison to abortion, vague law) is a reasonable organizational strategy, and your topic sentences do a good job signaling each new section. Keep this framework, but strengthen the connections between reasons and thesis.

Evidence and Logic

This is the area that needs the most attention.

The monkey example: This anecdote is vague — no names, dates, or sources are given, which makes it hard for readers to verify or take seriously. Also, the logic is questionable: protesters wanting to *prevent* euthanasia (letting the monkeys die of illness rather than be euthanized) doesn't clearly support your argument that "America is not ready" for euthanasia. If anything, it shows resistance to *ending* suffering, which cuts against your point. Clarify what actually happened and how it supports your claim.

Veterinarian example: The connection between euthanizing stray dogs and *human* euthanasia is asserted but not explained. Why should we assume public attitudes toward animal euthanasia predict attitudes toward human euthanasia? Spell out this connection or drop the example.

Abortion comparison: This is your strongest section, but the logical leap needs more support. You claim euthanasia and abortion "involve similar ethical and moral questions," but you should explain *specifically* which questions overlap (bodily autonomy? definitions of personhood? state interest in life?). Simply asserting similarity isn't enough — a skeptical reader could argue the cases are quite different (one involves a person's own death by choice; the other involves ending a potential life). Address this counterargument.

Missing citations: You have several  placeholders where quotations should appear (from McKown, Americans Against Human Suffering, and the sponsors of the "act"). These are essential — right now, your key evidence for the "vague law" argument is literally missing. Fill these in with actual quotations and full citations.

Unsupported claims: Statements like "there must be some sort of consensus" (based on politicians not supporting the act) are shaky. Lack of political support could stem from many factors unrelated to ethical concerns (lobbying, funding, unrelated provisions in the bill). Avoid over-reaching conclusions from limited evidence.

Counterarguments

You mention the pro-euthanasia view briefly in your introduction (citing the Declaration of Independence), but you don't return to seriously address it. A strong argumentative essay engages with the opposing view's *strongest* points and explains why your position still holds. Consider dedicating a paragraph to this, perhaps addressing the autonomy argument directly: even if people generally have the right to make decisions about their own bodies, why should this right not extend to end-of-life decisions? Your answer (mental competency concerns) is good — expand on it more rigorously.

Language and Style

Since English is not your first language, here are some specific issues to address:

Article and pronoun errors: "there own lives" → "their own lives"; "a live is worth living" → "a life is worth living"; "the existing safety standards... are not adequate enough" (avoid double comparatives like "not adequate enough" — just "not adequate").
Sentence fragments/run-ons: "Simply because that there are few people..." is not a complete sentence attached properly to the previous one. Revise to: "This is because there are few people who consider taking their own lives while of sound mind."
Word choice: "full of wholes" should be "full of holes." "Snuffed out" is a bit informal for academic writing — consider "ended" instead.
Wordy transitions: Phrases like "By now the question that may arise is..." are conversational. Academic writing typically uses more direct transitions: "This raises an important question:..."

Suggestions for Revision

Clarify your thesis: restriction vs. abolition — choose one and apply it consistently.
Fill in all missing quotations and cite sources properly (author, title, date, page if possible).
Strengthen the logical connections between your evidence and claims, especially in the monkey and dog examples.
Add a dedicated counterargument paragraph addressing the autonomy/rights argument directly.
Proofread carefully for grammar, especially articles, pronouns, and sentence boundaries.

Overall: Your essay shows real engagement with a complex issue and a logical three-part structure. Focus your revision on strengthening evidence, filling in missing citations, and tightening the connection between each piece of evidence and your central claim. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: Televised Executions

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and complex issue, and the opening anecdote about Amy is a strong, memorable hook. You clearly did research and are engaging with real arguments (KQED, Sherman, Clark, Smolowe). However, the essay currently reads more like a set of separate notes than a unified argument. Below are specific areas to revise.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your thesis appears at the end of paragraph one, but it is difficult to follow because of its length and repeated qualifiers ("is established as an issue of consequence and is not a strong argument because..."). Try to state your claim in one clear, direct sentence: *eg, "Although KQED argues that televising executions is protected by the First Amendment, this argument is weaker than the opposition's because it treats the issue as a matter of value rather than confronting its real-world consequences."* A crisp thesis will help you and the reader track the argument through the rest of the essay.

You introduce a useful distinction—framing this as a debate between "value" and "consequence"—but you never fully explain what these terms mean before using them. Define them early (perhaps in the introduction) so readers understand the analytical framework you're applying throughout.

Structure and Organization

The essay loses momentum in the second half. Paragraphs 5 and 6 mostly consist of quotations without analysis (". According to Jill Smolowe, ."). Right now these read as a list of citations rather than an argument. Each quotation needs:
A signal phrase introducing who is speaking and why they're relevant
Analysis afterward explaining how the quotation supports (or complicates) your thesis

Consider merging paragraphs 5 and 6, since they cover related territory (public reaction, pro/con views), and make sure each new source clearly advances your argument rather than simply adding another voice.

Also, reconsider the placement of the Pat Clark and Sherman counterpoint late in the essay. Since your central claim is that the opposition's argument is stronger, you may want to present the strongest opposing evidence (Sherman, the Bethea hanging) earlier and closer together, then address counterarguments before your conclusion, so the essay builds toward its verdict rather than scattering evidence.

Use of Sources

There are several unfilled citation placeholders () — remember to insert the actual quotations and provide proper in-text citations (author, page/paragraph number) before submission. Also be consistent in how you refer to authors: you call the same person "Sherman," "Jonathan Sherman," and "his" — decide whether you'll use full name on first mention and last name afterward, and stick to that convention throughout.

Repetition and Word Choice

Certain phrases are overused: "not a valid argument," "consequence(s)," and "concrete" appear many times in close proximity. Vary your vocabulary — try words like *unsubstantiated, one-dimensional, speculative,* or *well-supported, empirically grounded* as alternatives. Similarly, avoid saying "This is not a valid argument because... is therefore not a valid argument" — this circular phrasing weakens rather than strengthens your critique. Explain *specifically* why the reasoning fails (eg, it doesn't address potential harms, it relies on a single principle without addressing counter-evidence).

Grammar and Mechanics

"usual routing" → "usual routine"
"her mother was in the be drooms" → "bedrooms" (likely a formatting/typo error—check for stray line breaks)
"affect" vs. "effect": "how televising this execution will effect their ratings" → should be "affect"
"pat Clark" → capitalize: "Pat Clark"
"their argument is more thoroughly defined" → capitalize "Their"
Watch subject-verb agreement and comma splices, especially in longer sentences (eg, paragraph 2, sentence 3).

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, which often catches these small errors and awkward phrasings naturally.

Conclusion

Your return to Amy's story is a nice rhetorical move, tying the essay together. However, the final paragraph could more explicitly restate *why* the opposition's argument is stronger — remind the reader of the key comparative point (values vs. consequences) rather than simply restating that the argument is "not concrete."

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Sharpen and simplify your thesis statement.
Define your "value vs. consequence" framework early.
Fill in all citation placeholders and cite consistently.
Turn quotation-lists into analyzed evidence with clear signal phrases.
Reduce repetitive phrasing; vary vocabulary.
Proofread carefully for typos and word confusion (affect/effect, capitalization).

You have a compelling case here — with tighter organization and clearer analysis, this essay could be very persuasive. Nice work overall, and I look forward to seeing the revision!
