Feedback on Your Essay: "The Morality of Abortion"

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've chosen a genuinely difficult and important topic, and your writing shows real conviction and effort to build a logical case. Below is feedback organized around the areas that will most improve your next revision.

Strengths

Your essay has a clear thesis (abortion is morally wrong because it kills an innocent human life) and you attempt a logical, step-by-step structure: first proving the fetus is alive, then that it is a separate entity, then addressing the doctor's moral responsibility. This is a sound organizational strategy for an argumentative essay, and your use of expert testimony (Callahan, Grisez, Liley, Dr. Hanley) shows you did real research.

Areas for Revision

Thesis and Balance
Right now your essay reads more as advocacy than academic argumentation. A strong argumentative essay should demonstrate that you understand the *strongest* version of the opposing view before refuting it. Currently, the pro-choice position is summarized in a single sentence and never seriously engaged with again. Consider: What is the pro-choice response to the "separate entity" argument? What about cases of rape, incest, or medical necessity? Addressing these would make your rebuttal more persuasive rather than one-sided.

Unsupported Leaps in Logic
Several key moves in your argument happen too quickly. For example, you move from "the fetus has biological attributes X, Y, Z" directly to "abortion is murder" without addressing the philosophical question of *personhood*—which is really the crux of the debate. Simply establishing biological life does not, by itself, settle the moral question of whether ending that life is equivalent to murder (a legal term with specific criteria). You should explicitly explain *why* biological life = full moral status, since this is precisely what pro-choice thinkers dispute.

Missing Citations
Because several quotations are represented only by placeholder marks (), I cannot evaluate whether your evidence actually supports your claims as strongly as you assert it does. In your revision, make sure every quotation is fully integrated, properly cited (author, source, page), and followed by *your own analysis* of why it proves your point. A quotation should never be left to speak for itself—show the reader how it connects to your argument.

Loaded and Emotional Language
Phrases like "senseless killing," "innocent child," and "turn their backs" are rhetorically powerful but can undermine academic credibility because they presume the conclusion rather than argue for it. Academic argument is more persuasive when language is precise and measured, letting the evidence carry the emotional weight rather than the adjectives.

Overuse of Rhetorical Questions
You rely heavily on rhetorical questions ("Can one really justify...?", "Is it not then morally wrong...?"). Used occasionally, these are effective; used repeatedly, they can feel like a substitute for argument rather than a tool within it. Try converting at least half of these into direct claims followed by evidence.

The Conclusion Undercuts Your Thesis
This is the most important issue. Your final line—"You can't legislate morality"—actually contradicts your entire essay's purpose, since you have spent five paragraphs arguing that abortion *should* be treated as immoral and, by extension, restricted. If you want to end on the idea that this is an intractable value conflict, you need to reconcile that with your strong opening thesis. Otherwise, the essay seems to argue itself into uncertainty at the very end. Reconsider whether your conclusion should restate and reinforce your position, or whether your thesis itself needs to be reframed as more exploratory if you want to end on this note of moral pluralism.

Grammar and Mechanics
There are several sentence-level errors to correct in revision:
"for more ninety-eight percent" → "for more *than* ninety-eight percent"
"A direct quote taken form the oath" → "taken *from*"
Watch subject-verb agreement in sentences with delayed subjects (eg, "and, therefore, be subject to whatever decision")
Some sentences are quite long; consider breaking them for clarity (eg, the sentence beginning "This evidence from reputable people...").

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Strengthen engagement with the opposing view.
Make the philosophical link between "biological life" and "moral status" explicit.
Fully integrate and analyze all quotations.
Revise the conclusion so it aligns with (or intentionally complicates) your thesis.
Proofread for grammar and reduce rhetorical questions in favor of direct claims.

You have a clear voice and a well-organized skeleton here—the next draft should focus on deepening the *reasoning* between your claims and evidence, and ensuring your ending matches the confidence of your introduction.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0036.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely provocative and thought-provoking question: whether prolonging life through medical technology has hidden costs to genetic health, resource allocation, and even our understanding of what makes life valuable. This is an ambitious topic, and your writing shows real engagement with it. However, the essay currently suffers from three interrelated problems: (1) a thesis that shifts and becomes conflated with a claim that risks sounding like an argument for withholding treatment from people with genetic or chronic illness; (2) reasoning that misapplies scientific concepts (particularly evolution and antibiotic resistance); and (3) missing citations and abrupt transitions that weaken your credibility as a writer. Below I address each area with suggestions for revision.

Clarify and Narrow Your Thesis

Your introduction raises many ideas—constitutional rights, medical technology, the "death and taxes" cliché, the Hippocratic oath, healthcare costs—before arriving at your real claim in the last sentence: that we should scrutinize our assumption that life must be preserved "at any cost." This is a defensible philosophical position (many bioethicists debate resource allocation and quality-of-life questions), but as written, your essay drifts into a much more troubling claim: that keeping people with genetic diseases alive is bad because it "weakens the gene pool." This conflates two very different arguments—(a) *we should reconsider how we allocate medical resources and define the value of prolonged life*, and (b) *it would be evolutionarily better if people with genetic diseases died earlier*. The second argument has serious ethical implications (echoes of eugenics) that you likely don't intend, but as currently phrased, readers will hear it. I'd strongly recommend narrowing your thesis to the resource-allocation and philosophical question, and removing or heavily qualifying the evolutionary/genetic argument, or at least explicitly acknowledging its ethical dangers and addressing them.

Check Your Science

Evolution: "Survival of the fittest" is a popular shorthand, not a precise scientific principle. Natural selection acts on reproductive success within a specific environment, not on some abstract, universal standard of "fitness." Modern medicine is now part of the human environment; arguing that medicine is "artificially" interfering with a "natural" evolutionary process assumes there's a normative baseline evolution should follow. Most biologists would reject that framing. You'll need a stronger source than a general appeal to Darwin here—consider consulting current work in evolutionary medicine.
Antibiotics and viruses: You spend a paragraph explaining antibiotic resistance (misuse of antibiotics allowing resistant bacteria to survive), but antibiotics don't treat viral infections at all—this is actually your own point in the final sentence of that paragraph, but it arrives too late and muddles the logic. Similarly, your Hantavirus example is introduced but never explained (the placeholder  suggests you meant to insert supporting information here). Mixing bacterial resistance with viral mutation as if they are the same phenomenon will confuse readers who know the biology.

Missing Sources and Placeholders

Several  symbols appear where quotations or supporting data should be inserted. These need to be filled in with properly cited material before submission. Right now, key claims—such as what Heimer "observes" or what the parent said in Bosk's book—are left blank, which breaks the reader's trust in your argument at crucial moments. Also, double-check that all sources (Heimer, Bosk, PKD incidence rates, AZT, Hantavirus) are cited according to your assigned citation style (author, year, page).

Tone: Avoid Moralizing Language

Phrases like "I see no honor to life in this parent's selfish act" and "genetic counselors are failing... this is disgraceful" insert strong moral judgments about real people's private medical decisions without sufficient context or evidence about their actual circumstances. Academic argument is more persuasive when it presents reasoning and evidence and lets the reader draw conclusions, rather than condemning individuals. Consider revising these passages to focus on systemic or policy-level critique (eg, "genetic counseling practices could better support informed decision-making") rather than personal blame.

Structure and Transitions

The essay moves from constitutional rights → medical costs → PKD → genetic counseling → antibiotics → viruses → healthcare costs, but the connections between sections aren't always explicit. Each paragraph should open with a sentence that ties back to your thesis (eg, "This raises the question of whether prolonging life through treatment is worth its long-term costs") rather than launching directly into a new topic. Consider adding a roadmap sentence in your introduction that tells readers which cases (PKD, antibiotics, AIDS) you will examine and why.

Style

The conversational, second-person tone ("like your mother told you," "I'm sure you've heard of") is engaging but inconsistent with the analytical register of the rest of the essay. Decide on one voice and keep it consistent throughout.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Narrow and clarify your thesis; separate the resource-allocation argument from the genetics/evolution argument.
Fix scientific reasoning about evolution and antibiotic resistance.
Fill in missing citations and quotations.
Revise judgmental language about individuals' choices.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs.

You have an interesting and ambitious argument here—with more precision and a tighter focus, it could be very compelling.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0037.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: Assisted Suicide and Dr. Kevorkian

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely difficult and emotionally charged topic, and your essay shows real engagement with multiple perspectives. You clearly understand the structure of a rhetorical analysis (identifying strengths and weaknesses in each side's argument), which is a sophisticated approach. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: clarity of purpose, organization, evidence integration, and sentence-level accuracy. Below are specific areas to address.

Clarify Your Purpose and Thesis

It's unclear whether this essay is meant to be a rhetorical analysis (evaluating how well each side argues its case) or an argumentative essay (taking your own position on assisted suicide). Right now it reads as a hybrid, and this creates confusion. Your introduction promises to evaluate "strengths and weaknesses" in each argument, and you do this somewhat mechanically throughout ("This is a strength because..." / "This is a weakness because..."). But your conclusion suddenly shifts to calling for society to "find a median"—which sounds like a policy recommendation, not a rhetorical evaluation.

Revision suggestion: Decide which essay you are writing. If it's rhetorical analysis, your conclusion should synthesize *how* the arguments succeed or fail rhetorically, not propose solutions. If it's argumentative, your thesis needs to state your own position clearly, and the strength/weakness analysis should serve that argument rather than remaining neutral throughout.

Missing Quotations

Throughout the essay, quotations are marked with  placeholders rather than actual text. This is a critical gap—your entire analytical method depends on quoting sources (Hendin, Saunders, Kevorkian, Garcia, Reich) and then explaining their significance. Without the actual words, readers cannot evaluate your interpretation, and the essay currently cannot be assessed for how well you handle evidence. This must be fixed before further revision is meaningful.

Repetitive Structure Undermines Analysis

Nearly every paragraph follows the same template: introduce a claim → quote a source → state "this is significant because..." → identify one strength → identify one weakness. While consistency is good, this becomes formulaic and predictable. Readers can predict each paragraph's structure by the third paragraph, which reduces engagement.

Suggestion: Vary how you introduce evidence and analysis. Sometimes lead with the weakness before the strength; sometimes integrate two sources in conversation with each other; sometimes let the analysis emerge from a specific example (like the AIDS diagnosis scenario) rather than stating it explicitly as "a disadvantage is..."

The Martin Luther King Jr. Comparison Needs Development

Comparing Kevorkian to MLK is a bold and interesting move, but it's asserted rather than argued. Simply pointing out that both were arrested for their beliefs is a weak analogy—many people are arrested for beliefs that history does *not* vindicate. You need to either:
Justify why this comparison is *apt* (what specific moral reasoning connects civil disobedience for civil rights to physician-assisted suicide?), or
Acknowledge this as a *weakness* in the pro-Kevorkian argument (an appeal to emotion/false analogy) rather than presenting it uncritically as a strength.

Right now it reads as if you're endorsing the comparison, which may not be fully thought through.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address:
Sentence fragments: "While opponents believe that Kevorkian is trying to play God..." is a fragment (should be joined to the previous sentence or revised).
Typos: "faith i doctors," "af raid," "improving i the care," "neglection" (should be "neglect" or "failure to name")
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences need periods or semicolons instead of commas (eg, "This is important because this would let people be presented with other options, which could allow them to lead their normal lives...")
Word choice: "the reasoning seems a bit steep" — consider "extreme" or "exaggerated." "Revolutionize" in the doctors paragraph seems to mean the opposite of what you intend (you likely mean "undermine" or "destabilize," not "revolutionize," which suggests positive change).

Strengthen the Conclusion

The conclusion currently repeats claims made in the introduction without adding new insight. The final call to "find a median" is intriguing but underdeveloped—what would a median actually look like? Improved hospice care? Restricted eligibility criteria? Give your reader something concrete to take away.

Priority Revisions

Insert actual quotations and make sure your analysis directly engages with the specific language used.
Clarify your essay's purpose—analysis vs. argument—and align your conclusion accordingly.
Vary paragraph structure to avoid formulaic repetition.
Revisit the MLK analogy critically.
Proofread carefully for fragments, typos, and unclear phrasing.

You have strong instincts for locating tension in an argument—keep pushing yourself to explain *why* something is a strength or weakness with more precision and specific reasoning, rather than stating it as a general observation.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0038.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Corporal Punishment in Schools

Overall Impression

You have a genuinely interesting and debatable thesis—that opponents of corporal punishment weaken their own cause through imprecise terminology and inconsistent argumentation. This is a sophisticated, meta-level argument (critiquing *how* an argument is made rather than the issue itself), and your narrative opening about Tommy is a vivid, effective hook. However, the essay currently has some structural and evidentiary gaps that need attention before it will fully succeed.

Missing Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)

The placeholder marks () where quotations should appear are the biggest problem in this draft. Your entire argument depends on showing *specific* inconsistencies and contradictions in the opponents' definitions and claims. Right now, you *assert* that Jambor's definition differs from Johns and MacNaughton's, and that Diamantes contradicts himself, but you never show the reader the actual words. Without the quotations, your argument is just a claim about claims—unsupported. This needs to be your first priority in revision. Insert the actual quotations, and make sure to briefly analyze *how* each one is ambiguous or contradictory rather than just juxtaposing them.

Your Own Position

You spend the whole essay critiquing opponents of corporal punishment but never clarify where you personally stand on the issue. Are you a covert supporter of corporal punishment, or simply a neutral critic of weak argumentation? Readers may wonder why you're motivated to write this critique at all. A sentence or two clarifying your purpose (eg, "This essay does not argue for or against corporal punishment itself, but demonstrates that opponents have not yet made a rhetorically effective case") would sharpen your ethos and prevent readers from assuming bias.

Structure and Transitions

Your organization mostly follows your thesis (terminology, then wavering position), which is good. But some transitions are abrupt. For example, after the strong opening anecdote, the shift into rhetorical questions and then into the thesis statement happens very quickly—consider a smoother bridge sentence. Also, the paragraph about "what opponents might do to strengthen their argument" feels underdeveloped; since you promise concrete suggestions, give more than two brief sentences—perhaps a proposed shared definition, or an example of how one might resolve a contradiction.

Use of Evidence and Currency

Your 1988 statistic about states banning corporal punishment is outdated for a contemporary argument. If your essay is meant to address a current audience, note explicitly that this is historical context, or find more recent statistics to strengthen credibility. Otherwise, readers may question the currency of your other sources as well.

Repetition

Several phrases repeat almost verbatim ("the argument...is rendered ineffective," "were you a tommy?"). While repetition can be a rhetorical device, here it reads more as a drafting habit than an intentional strategy. Vary your phrasing to maintain reader engagement, especially in the conclusion, which largely restates the introduction's questions without adding new insight. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement or call to action instead.

Language and Grammar

A few specific corrections:
"took he seat" → "took his seat"
"unteeteringly" is not a standard English word; consider "unwaveringly" or "consistently"
"subjected the paddle" → "subjected to the paddle"
The sentence "The authors who define the term do not apply the same definition, several of the authors fail to make any attempt at defining" is a comma splice—split into two sentences or use a semicolon, and clarify "fail to make any attempt at defining *it*."
Watch article usage ("the focal term," "a firm decision to heed... the pleas") — some phrasing here is slightly formal/awkward; simplifying will help clarity (eg, "to accept or reject the opponents' claims").

These are minor but frequent enough that a careful proofreading pass (perhaps reading the essay aloud) will help you catch them.

Citation Format

Right now, sources are introduced narratively ("In his article... Tom Jambor defines...") but there's no indication of proper academic citation (page numbers, a Works Cited list). Make sure your final draft includes complete in-text citations and a reference list in whatever style your course requires (MLA seems likely, given your phrasing).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Insert actual quotations where  appears, and analyze them explicitly.
Clarify your own stance/purpose to avoid reader confusion.
Expand the "solutions" paragraph with concrete suggestions.
Update or contextualize your statistical evidence.
Reduce repetition, especially in the conclusion.
Proofread carefully for grammar and word choice.
Add complete citations.

You have a strong, original angle here—once the evidence gaps are filled in and the prose is tightened, this essay could be quite compelling. Good luck with your revision!

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0039.1.txt =====
Feedback Report: Censorship Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done research on this topic and gathered compelling evidence (the Huston study, the Berns article, the Groller survey). However, the essay's biggest challenge is not language, but stance and structure. Let's work through this.

Clarify Your Argumentative Position

This is your most important revision priority. Throughout the essay, you write about what "the advocates for censorship" believe and prove—almost as if you are summarizing someone else's argument rather than making your own. Look at how often variations of "the advocates for censorship realize/prove/show" appear (I count over a dozen times). This creates distance between you and the claim.

Ask yourself: Is this *your* argument, or a report on other people's argument? If you support increased censorship, say so directly and argue it in your own voice: "Television needs greater censorship because..." rather than "The advocates for censorship believe that..." The repeated third-person framing makes the essay read like a rhetorical analysis of *other people's* persuasive techniques, not a persuasive essay itself. Decide which essay you're writing, and revise consistently.

Strengthen the Counterargument Section

Your rebuttal paragraph is the shortest and least developed part of the essay, yet it's crucial for a strong argumentative essay. You raise good points (parental negligence, children watching secretly) but present them only as questions, not fully reasoned responses. For each objection:
State the opposing view clearly
Explain why it has some validity
Then explain why your position still stands

Right now this section feels rushed compared to the extensive evidence-gathering elsewhere.

Fix the Missing Source Material

There are multiple  placeholders throughout the essay where quotations or examples seem to be missing (the TV show names, direct quotes from Berns, the Groller survey question, etc). Before submitting, make sure every placeholder is filled with the actual content—right now the essay is literally incomplete, and readers can't evaluate your evidence without it.

Improve Paragraph Focus and Transitions

Several paragraphs try to do too much at once (eg, the paragraph on Grace Under Fire and NYPD Blue jumps between two very different shows and arguments). Consider:
One controlling idea per paragraph
Clear topic sentences that state the point *before* the example
Explicit transitions connecting paragraphs (eg, "Just as children's cartoons desensitize young viewers to violence, sitcoms and dramas desensitize them to sexual content")

Citation and Attribution

You reference several real sources (Huston's study, Berns's article, the Groller survey)—good use of research! But integrate citations more consistently: introduce the source, quote or paraphrase it, then explain its significance to your argument (the classic "quote sandwich"). Also double check whether your instructor requires formal citations (MLA/APA), since currently sources are named informally in-text only.

Language and Mechanics

Because English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for in revision:

Effect vs. affect: You use "effect" as a verb multiple times ("what they view effects their behavior," "how these programs effect the minds"). Affect = verb (to influence); effect = noun (a result). Revise these instances.
Its vs. it's: "if censorship doesn't increase networks will continue..." and "is censorship does not increase" — check for typos here; likely "if censorship does not increase."
Run-on sentences: Many sentences chain multiple clauses with commas where periods or semicolons are needed. Example: "Children are very impressionable, the information that is instilled into them, whether it be good or bad becomes a part of them." Break into two sentences.
Verb agreement/tense: "the proponents can see how powerful the networks are... By how the networks manipulated" — this creates a sentence fragment. Combine or rephrase.
Spelling: "caniving" (conniving), "deminish" (diminish), "peak" should be "pique," "bout" should be "about," "suppose to" should be "supposed to," "fore shadowing" should be one word "foreshadowing."

I recommend reading your essay aloud—this often catches run-ons and missing words naturally.

Conclusion

Your conclusion currently summarizes "the advocates" argument rather than restating *your* thesis and its implications. Revise this to speak in your own voice, reinforcing your stance and perhaps ending with a forward-looking call to action.

Priority Revision Steps:
Rewrite in your own persuasive voice (remove "advocates for censorship" framing)
Fill in all  placeholders
Expand the counterargument section
Proofread for effect/affect, run-ons, and spelling
Rewrite conclusion to reflect your own claim

You have strong research and real conviction here—now let the essay speak with your voice, making a direct case rather than reporting someone else's.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0040.1.txt =====
Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've clearly done research on this topic and have identified strong potential evidence (Sinsheimer, Maddox, Annas, Aldhous). However, the essay has one fundamental structural problem that needs to be addressed before smaller issues matter: you have written an essay *about* how to construct an argument, rather than actually *making* the argument.

The Core Issue: Meta-Argument vs. Argument

Notice how often you use conditional, hypothetical language: "One should present the history..." "This reference to the Nazis would be effective..." "Citing his example... would be excellent..." "This consequence could sway a reader..." You are describing what a *hypothetical* essay would do and why it would be persuasive, rather than actually persuading your reader yourself.

This creates real distance between you and your claims. Compare:
What you wrote: "Arguing this point of eliminating the beneficial along with the malevolent would be effective, for genetic research is leading to this."
What an argumentative essay needs: "Eliminating the sickle-cell gene would also eliminate its resistance to malaria—a trade-off that shows how genetic research risks discarding beneficial traits along with harmful ones."

Revise every "would be" and "could" statement into a direct claim. Commit to the argument rather than narrating it from the outside. Your thesis and conclusion should state your actual position on genetic research, not describe a strategy for arguing a position.

Missing Evidence

The essay is built around quotations that never appear—every citation is marked with a placeholder (). Without the actual words from Sinsheimer, Maddox, and Annas, readers cannot evaluate your interpretations or your use of evidence. This is the most urgent revision task: insert the real quotations, properly cited, and then analyze them directly rather than summarizing what they "deal with." Right now you often paraphrase around a missing quote rather than engaging with its content.

Genuine Engagement with Counterarguments

You promise in your introduction to "take the arguments for continuing genetic research and refute them one by one." This is a good structural plan, but the execution is thin. For instance, with Maddox's point about screening for disease genes, you acknowledge the sickle-cell/malaria trade-off, but you don't fully refute the pro-research position—you mostly restate it as a concern. A real refutation would concede what is legitimate in the opposing view (eg, that screening does prevent suffering) and then explain specifically why the risks outweigh those benefits. Right now the "refutation" sections read more like extensions of the counterargument than rebuttals.

Similarly, the nuclear technology analogy (knowledge inevitably leads to application) is a useful comparison, but it needs more development—why should we assume genetic knowledge will follow the same trajectory as nuclear knowledge? A skeptical reader will ask this, and the essay needs to anticipate that question.

Logical Development

The Nazi/eugenics history is powerful but risks being a slippery-slope argument if not carefully qualified. You do gesture toward this ("one cannot predict the future events"), which is good, but you should make explicit *why* the historical analogy is relevant to a genome project focused on disease research, rather than population-level manipulation—otherwise readers may find the comparison overstated.

Language and Mechanics

A few recurring issues to fix in revision:
"Mendal" → Mendel; "Gregorian monk" should be Augustinian monk (Gregorian refers to the calendar, not Mendel's order).
"by doing three doings" → awkward; try "in three steps" or "by taking three approaches."
"One consequences deals with privacy" → subject-verb agreement: "One consequence deals with..."
"The question is if genetic information be kept private" → needs a full conditional clause: "The question is whether genetic information can be kept private..."
Watch article usage throughout ("a Gregorian monk," "the field," etc)—generally strong, but double-check singular/plural nouns like "arguments," "consequences."
The conclusion restates the essay's plan again ("It would show how...") rather than synthesizing an actual conclusion about genetic research. Revise this to state your final judgment plainly.

Suggested Revision Priorities

Rewrite the introduction and thesis to state your actual position on genetic research (not a plan for arguing about it).
Insert and analyze real quotations in place of every .
Convert conditional/hypothetical sentences into direct claims and analysis.
Strengthen your refutations by conceding valid points in the opposing view before explaining why your position still holds.
Proofread for the grammar issues noted above.

You have good source material and a logical three-part structure in mind—the next draft should focus on turning your outline into an actual argument.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0041.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This is a solid rhetorical analysis with a clear organizing principle: comparing how audience shapes the presentation of the same topic (binge drinking) in two different publications (JAMA vs. Newsweek). You successfully identify categories of comparison—context, audience assumptions, physical/social context, and attitude—and apply them consistently. The essay shows genuine analytical thinking. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen the argument, the evidence, and the clarity of expression.

---

Thesis and Argument

Your thesis (last sentence of paragraph 1) is functional but buried in a long, complex sentence. Consider isolating it and making it sharper: state directly *what* difference you will prove and *why it matters* (eg, does one approach mislead readers? Is one more ethical or effective?). Right now the essay describes differences well but doesn't take a strong evaluative stance. You gesture toward one in paragraph 2 ("exploit a topic to increase readership") but then largely abandon this critical edge in favor of neutral description. Decide whether your purpose is purely descriptive (how audience shapes rhetoric) or evaluative (which approach serves readers better), and make that consistent throughout.

Evidence and Citation

This is the most significant issue. Several places have unfilled placeholders ()—for example, where you promise a startling statistic in bold type, or specific emotional language from the third line of the Newsweek article. These gaps make it impossible for a reader to judge your claims. Before submitting, you must:
Insert actual quotations with quotation marks.
Cite page numbers or paragraph locations for both articles.
Introduce quotations with signal phrases (eg, "The authors write, '...'" ) rather than just describing them.

Without this direct textual evidence, your claims about "shocking" or "emotional" language remain assertions rather than demonstrated analysis.

Structure and Cohesion

The essay is organized logically by category (context → audience assumptions → physical context → social context → attitude), which is a strength. However:
Some paragraphs restate the same point from the previous one before adding new information (eg, paragraph 3 repeats ideas from paragraph 2 almost verbatim). Trim these repetitions.
Transitional sentences like "we will look at that" or "as we have seen" are conversational filler; replace them with transitions that preview the *content* of the next section (eg, "This assumption about audience becomes even clearer when we examine each article's use of physical context").
The conclusion mostly repeats earlier points rather than synthesizing them. Try ending with a broader implication: What should readers of media do with this awareness? What responsibility do writers have when adapting facts to an audience?

Analytical Depth

You do good work identifying *what* each article does, but you could go deeper on *why* this matters rhetorically. For instance, when discussing Newsweek's omission of non-drinking statistics, you could analyze this as a rhetorical strategy (ethos, pathos) rather than simply noting the omission. Similarly, your claim that "presumptions are what make a good writer" needs more support or reconsideration—it's an interesting claim, but it currently reads as an unexamined assertion rather than an argued point.

Language and Grammar

Since English is not your first language, here are patterns to watch for in revision:
Possessives: "todays college campuses" → "today's"; "students look" is fine, but "the authors assumptions" → "the authors' assumptions" or "the author's assumptions" depending on number.
Preposition errors: "56% or men" → "56% of men."
Subject-verb agreement: "this make for differences" → "this makes for differences."
Article use: "It uses its physical context... to hook its audience" — check "it audience" → "its audience."
Word choice/register: Phrases like "as any college student can tell you" are informal for academic writing; consider replacing with more analytical phrasing (eg, "however, this framing arguably oversimplifies a more complex reality").

I'd recommend having a native-speaking peer or writing center tutor read a revised draft aloud with you, focusing especially on prepositions and possessives, which are the most frequent issues here.

Style

Your sentences are often long and coordinate multiple ideas with "and" or "but," which can obscure your main point. Try breaking some of these into two sentences for clarity. Also, avoid absolute claims ("few people would argue") unless you can support them—consider softening to "most researchers agree."

---

Priority Revisions
Fill in all  placeholders with actual, cited evidence.
Sharpen your thesis to include an evaluative claim, not just a descriptive one.
Reduce repetition between paragraphs; make transitions substantive.
Proofread for possessives, prepositions, and article usage.
Strengthen the conclusion with a broader insight about media literacy.

With these revisions, this essay has strong potential—the comparative framework is smart, and your instinct to look at both physical and social context shows sophisticated rhetorical awareness. Focus your energy on evidence and precision first; that will yield the most improvement in your final draft.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0042.txt =====
Feedback on Your Essay: Comprehensive vs. Abstinence-Only Sex Education

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've clearly done substantial research and are engaging with a genuinely controversial topic. Below are my main observations, organized by priority, to help you revise.

Missing Evidence (Most Urgent Issue)

Your essay contains numerous placeholders marked with  where quotations or specific data should appear. As it stands, the essay cannot be properly evaluated because the actual evidence supporting your claims is absent. For example, in your opening sentence, we never learn what Xavier Flores actually claims, nor what his opponents say in response. Before revising anything else, you must fill in all of these gaps with the actual quotes, statistics, or paraphrased claims you intend to use. Until this is done, readers cannot assess whether your evidence supports your points.

Clarify Your Terminology: "Proponents" vs. "Opponents"

This is a significant source of confusion throughout the essay. You use "proponents" and "opponents" inconsistently—sometimes it seems "proponents" refers to abstinence-only advocates, but the relationship shifts depending on the paragraph, and readers must constantly re-orient themselves. Consider replacing these vague terms entirely with concrete labels like "abstinence-only advocates" and "comprehensive sex education advocates" throughout the essay. This will make your argument far easier to follow and will prevent you from confusing yourself as you write.

Establish and Maintain a Clear Thesis

Your introduction promises an examination of a "controversy," but it isn't clear from the outset what *your* position is. Are you siding with comprehensive sex education advocates? Are you critiquing the rhetorical tactics used on both sides? Your body paragraphs consistently present the comprehensive-education side's critiques of abstinence-only programs favorably, which suggests you *do* have a position—but this isn't stated as a clear thesis in your introduction. Consider adding a sentence near the end of your introduction such as: "This essay argues that abstinence-only advocates rely on flawed evidence and misleading rhetoric, making comprehensive sex education the more defensible approach." Then make sure every paragraph explicitly supports that claim, rather than simply summarizing both sides.

Strengthen Paragraph Structure and Topic Sentences

Each paragraph currently opens with a fairly generic topic sentence (eg, "Many of the suggestions that supporters of 'abstinence-only education' make can be easily refuted with common sense"). This works reasonably well, but the paragraphs sometimes drift—introducing new studies or examples without clearly connecting them back to the paragraph's stated purpose. When you revise, check that every example directly demonstrates the specific claim in your topic sentence. For instance, in your third paragraph, you move from the Dawson study, to Sullivan, to the San Diego Union report, to a claim about a principal's methodology—these are all useful examples, but you need transitional sentences explaining *how each one builds on the last* to strengthen your argument about unreliable research.

Address the Counterargument More Directly

Your essay does a good job cataloguing criticisms of abstinence-only education, but it would be more persuasive—and more balanced—if you acknowledged what abstinence-only advocates might say in response to these criticisms, even briefly, before showing why their response is insufficient. Right now, the essay risks feeling one-sided rather than genuinely "argumentative," since you rarely grant any legitimacy to the opposing view before dismantling it.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring grammar patterns to review:
"the rates...would decrease" — check subject-verb agreement and tense consistency throughout, especially when reporting claims (eg, "supporters *claim* that rates *would* decrease" vs. stating it as fact).
"teens will not being informed" → "teens will not be informed"
"birthcontrol" → "birth control" (two words)
"opponents question whether any would" — this sentence is incomplete; make sure to finish this thought.
"The proponents case" → "The proponents' case" (missing possessive apostrophe)
Watch for missing articles ("a" and "the") in several places, which is common when translating from languages that don't use articles the same way English does.

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud after revising—this often helps catch missing words or awkward phrasing that's easy to miss when reading silently.

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion raises a good question ("Should we teach prevention methods in addition to abstinence?") but then says "that is a question each school will decide," which undercuts the argumentative stance you've built throughout the essay. If your essay has been arguing that abstinence-only programs use flawed evidence, your conclusion should affirm that comprehensive education is the better-supported choice, not defer the question entirely to individual schools.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Fill in all  placeholders with actual evidence.
Standardize terminology (avoid ambiguous "proponents/opponents").
State a clear thesis and align every paragraph with it.
Add transitions connecting examples within paragraphs.
Proofread for grammar (articles, verb forms, possessives).
Revise the conclusion to affirm your argument, not neutralize it.

Once these issues are addressed, this essay has strong potential—you've clearly gathered compelling material. Good luck with the revision!

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This essay demonstrates a genuinely sophisticated analytical framework—you're evaluating *how well each side argues* (values, consequences, evidence quality) rather than simply taking a position on adoption rights. This is an ambitious rhetorical approach, and your analysis often shows real critical insight (eg, noting that citing rare/extreme cases weakens an argument, or that the "one year later" follow-up on Baby Jessica is methodologically premature). However, the essay needs revision in several key areas: source integration, structural clarity, and precision of argumentation.

Major Issues to Address

Missing Source Material (Critical)
Several paragraphs contain bracketed placeholders  where quotations or statistics should appear (eg, "the Schmidt's were portrayed as ," "According to the National Council for Adoption, "). These gaps make it impossible for a reader to evaluate your claims. Before resubmitting, you must fill these in with actual cited material, or the essay's evidentiary basis collapses. This is the most urgent fix.

Clarify Your Central Thesis
Your thesis—that neither side uses strong values, consequences, or attempts to shift public opinion—is analytically interesting but gets somewhat lost. Consider stating more precisely *what* would count as a "strong" value or consequence, so readers understand your evaluative criteria from the start. Right now, you critique both sides for relying on "hypothetical consequences" or "rare examples," but you could be more explicit early on about why these are argumentative weaknesses (eg, rare cases don't generalize; hypothetical consequences aren't verifiable).

Paragraph Focus and Transitions
Some paragraphs shift between summarizing an argument and critiquing it without clear signposting. For example, in the paragraph on "burden of guilt," you move from the biological parents' reasoning into the Baby Jessica case, then back to a general critique—the reader can lose the thread. Consider using clearer transitional phrases like "This argument is weak because..." or "In contrast, the opposing view holds that..." to mark when you shift from description to evaluation.

The "False Dilemma" Point Deserves More Development
Your strongest and most original point appears in the penultimate paragraph: that media coverage created a false dilemma by generalizing from extreme cases. This insight is excellent, but it arrives very late. Consider foregrounding this claim earlier (perhaps in your introduction) and returning to it throughout as a unifying thread, rather than saving it for near the end.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues
"give another couple that same chance" — reword for clarity; consider "give another couple the chance to raise the child"
"these proponents' claim of the domino effect" — you introduce "domino effect" without earlier defining it; make sure this term is established before you rely on it
"This is a somewhat strong argument" / "somewhat reasonable" — try to avoid hedging phrases like "somewhat" repeatedly; be more precise (eg, "this argument is compelling because..." or "this argument is limited because...")
"one could argue" — check spelling: "on could argue" should be "one could argue"
Watch pronoun-antecedent agreement: "raise him" in the first paragraph assumes gender; consider "raise the child" for consistency
Some sentences run long with multiple clauses (eg, the second sentence of your introduction). Consider breaking these into shorter, clearer statements.

Conclusion Could Be Stronger
Your conclusion effectively restates your thesis but ends on a slightly speculative note ("what if all this struggle could have been avoided by one simple signature..."). This is emotionally resonant, but for an academic argumentative essay, consider ending with a more concrete recommendation: what *specifically* should proponents on either side do differently? You gesture toward this throughout the essay (eg, "find other examples," "state why this is a bad court decision")—pulling these specific suggestions together in your conclusion would give the essay a stronger, more actionable close.

Strengths to Preserve

Your meta-level critique (analyzing argumentative strategy rather than just the adoption debate itself) is intellectually ambitious and well-suited to a course on rhetoric/argumentation.
You consistently push back against both sides, avoiding the trap of simply endorsing one position—this even-handedness strengthens your credibility as an analyst.
Specific examples (Baby Jessica, Baby Richard, the "divorced" child case) are used to good effect when fully developed.

Priority Revision Checklist

Fill in all  placeholders with real quotations/data.
State your evaluative criteria (what makes a value/consequence "strong") in the introduction.
Move your "false dilemma" insight earlier and reference it throughout.
Tighten transitions between summary and critique within paragraphs.
Proofread for hedging language and minor grammar errors.
Strengthen the conclusion with concrete recommendations.

With these revisions, this essay could move from a solid draft to a genuinely compelling piece of analytical writing. Well done on tackling such a nuanced rhetorical task.

Overall Impression

You have tackled a relevant and debatable topic, and your essay shows real engagement—especially through your personal experience with cheating and honor codes. However, the essay currently reads more like a set of notes than a fully developed argument. Below are the main areas to focus on as you revise.

Clarify and Maintain a Consistent Thesis

Your introduction states that "revamping the academic code and establishing strict honor codes are the two strongest methods," implying they are roughly equal. But later, your body paragraph on academic codes ends by saying "the loopholes...are too strong," and your conclusion firmly favors honor codes alone. This creates a contradiction: is your essay evaluating *two* strong solutions, or arguing that *one* is superior? Decide on your central claim now and revise your thesis statement so it explicitly says something like: "While both revamped academic codes and honor codes have been proposed as solutions, honor codes are the more effective deterrent to cheating because..." This will help every paragraph work toward the same goal instead of seeming to compare two equally strong options and then dismissing one.

Strengthen Paragraph Structure and Topic Sentences

Each body paragraph needs a clear topic sentence that states its main point before diving into evidence. For example, your paragraph on the "XF" grade jumps straight into definition and statistics without first telling the reader *why* this matters to your argument. Consider revising the opening sentence to something like: "Revamping academic codes through stricter, more visible penalties—such as the 'XF' grade—has proven effective in reducing cheating, though this method still has significant weaknesses." This helps the reader anticipate both the strength and the critique that follows.

Fill in the Gaps () with Real Evidence

The placeholders in your draft () are a serious problem—they leave key claims unsupported. For instance:
"Mr Levine of Harvard believes that " — the reader has no idea what Levine actually argues.
"It is hard for students to " — this sentence is incomplete and confusing.
"Trying to curtail cheaters through the court system is weak because of the " — again, the reasoning is missing.

Before resubmitting, make sure every claim is backed by a specific, cited source (author, institution, or study) and that every sentence is grammatically complete. Right now, the essay relies heavily on assertion ("this shows," "this proves") without enough concrete evidence connecting each claim to your conclusion.

Balance Personal Experience with Academic Evidence

Your personal anecdotes ("I have seen others cheat and get a better grade," "If we had an honor code at Marquette...") are effective for engaging the reader, but in an academic argumentative essay they should support—not replace—your researched evidence. Consider integrating your personal experience *after* presenting the university data (eg, University of Virginia's honor code process), so the anecdote illustrates the broader point rather than standing alone.

Watch Language and Grammar

As a non-native speaker, your writing is generally clear, but a few recurring issues should be corrected in revision:
Article/preposition errors: "ensure and honest academic environment" → "ensure an honest academic environment"; "when taken in account" → "when taking into account."
Word form errors: "to reluctant to report" → "too reluctant to report"; "eliminated this problem" (in conclusion) → "eliminate this problem."
Sentence fragments: Several sentences (especially near  marks) are incomplete. Read each sentence aloud to check it has a subject and a complete verb phrase.
Wordy phrasing: "several college officials have become wary of students' unethical attitude toward cheating" could be more concise: "college officials are increasingly concerned about student cheating."

Strengthen the Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively restates your preference for honor codes, but it repeats earlier points rather than synthesizing them into a final, forward-looking statement. Consider ending with a recommendation: what should universities like Marquette specifically do next? This will give your essay a stronger sense of closure and purpose.

Summary of Priority Revisions
Fix the thesis so it clearly commits to one central argument.
Complete all sentences and replace  with actual cited evidence.
Add clear topic sentences to each paragraph.
Correct grammar issues (articles, verb forms, fragments).
Sharpen the conclusion with a concrete recommendation.

With these revisions, your essay will move from a promising outline to a fully persuasive academic argument. Good luck with your next draft!

Feedback Report on Fourth Amendment Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

This is an ambitious and well-researched essay that shows genuine engagement with a complex legal and rhetorical issue. You clearly understand the details of the Simpson case and have done solid research into how both sides constructed their arguments. However, the essay currently reads more like a rhetorical analysis (how each side argues) than an argumentative essay (what you believe and why). This is the most important issue to address in revision.

Clarify Your Purpose and Thesis

Your introduction poses a good question ("How should the Fourth Amendment be interpreted?"), but the body of the essay spends almost all of its space describing *how* the prosecution and defense make their cases rhetorically, rather than evaluating *which* argument is more persuasive or legally sound. Your conclusion explicitly states that "whether one side's reasons seem more credible... is not the issue"—but for an argumentative essay, this *is* the issue. Readers will finish your essay unsure of your actual position on Fourth Amendment interpretation.

Suggestion: Decide on a clear stance (eg, "exigent circumstances should be narrowly interpreted" or "the judge's ruling was justified because..."). Revise your thesis to state this position explicitly, and use your analysis of both sides' rhetoric as *evidence* for your own evaluation, not as the end goal itself.

Missing Quotations

Several key moments use placeholders () where quotations should appear—for example, the "emotional statements from the detectives" and Judge Ito's quote about the detectives' behavior. These are crucial pieces of evidence for your analysis, and their absence weakens your argument significantly. Before final submission, make sure every quotation is filled in, properly introduced, and cited (author, publication, date).

Citation Practices

You refer to articles by title ("Detectives in Simpson Case Defend Search," "Judge Rejects Barrage of Objections by Simpson's Lawyers") but never give full publication information (newspaper name, date). Academic writing requires consistent citation so readers can verify your sources. Consider adopting a standard format (MLA or APA) throughout, and cite specific page or paragraph locations when quoting.

Factual and Proofreading Errors

The victim's name is Ronald Goldman, not "Goldwin"—please correct this throughout.
"Los Angelas" appears many times; it should be "Los Angeles."
Capitalization is inconsistent (eg, "one article quotes Judge Ito" should begin with a capital "O").
Watch verb tense consistency, especially in your narrative paragraph about the night of the murders (mixing past simple and past perfect where needed for sequence, eg, "detective Mark Fuhrman discovered" should logically follow "had climbed").

A careful proofreading pass focused on names, capitalization, and tense will significantly improve the polish of this essay.

Strengthen Logical Connections

Some inferential leaps need more support:
"If the detectives lied about something minor like this, why would they be opposed to lying about other things concerning that night?" This is a rhetorical question standing in for an argument. Rather than implying the conclusion, explain *why* this specific lie undermines their overall credibility (eg, discuss the legal principle of witness credibility, "false in one, false in all").
The claim that Fuhrman "carelessly gathered" evidence is asserted without full support—tie this more explicitly to the specific facts you provide (10 unaccounted minutes, prior tampering accusation).

Structural Suggestions

Your body paragraphs are logically organized (narrative → proposition's strategy → opposition's strategy), which is a strength. However, each side's section could benefit from a mini-conclusion where you, as the writer, briefly weigh in on the strength of that particular argument before moving to the next section. This will help you build toward a real argumentative conclusion rather than a neutral summary.

Conclusion

Rewrite your conclusion so that it does not merely restate that "both sides argue effectively," but instead delivers your judgment: Which argument is more convincing, and why? Your final sentence about obtaining warrants in the future is a good practical point—consider expanding this into a policy recommendation that flows from your thesis.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add a clear, arguable thesis stating your own position.
Fill in missing quotations and cite all sources properly.
Correct factual errors (names, city spelling) and proofread for capitalization/tense.
Strengthen logical transitions between evidence and claims.
Revise the conclusion to take and defend a definitive stance.

You have strong research and organizational instincts—this revision should focus on transforming description into argumentation.

Feedback Report on Suicide Argumentative Essay

Thank you for tackling such a difficult and sensitive topic. Your essay shows real analytical ambition—you are not simply summarizing positions but evaluating the *quality of argumentation* itself, which is a sophisticated rhetorical move. However, several issues need attention before this essay is ready, ranging from thesis clarity to sentence-level problems.

Clarify Your Actual Argument

Your introduction promises to analyze "opponents of suicide," but your real argument is different and more interesting: you are claiming that opponents' arguments, while emotionally persuasive, lack empirical evidence. This is a meta-argument about argumentation quality, not really an argument about suicide itself. Right now, this distinction is muddy. Consider revising your thesis to explicitly state something like: "While opponents of suicide raise compelling points about selfishness, inner struggle, family grief, and religion, their arguments remain rhetorically persuasive but empirically weak because they consistently fail to cite supporting evidence or data." This would give your essay a clear analytical spine.

Relatedly, your final paragraph shifts tone dramatically, suggesting we should be "objective" and that "suicide will never cease to exist." This conclusion feels disconnected from your body paragraphs, which focused on evidentiary weakness, not on whether suicide is right or wrong. Make sure your conclusion returns to and reinforces your actual thesis rather than introducing a new philosophical stance.

Address a Troubling Early Contradiction

In your first paragraph, you write: "Suicide means the end of all problems (Though those problems are usually solved easily.)" This sentence is confusing and potentially undermines the seriousness of your topic—it reads as though you're suggesting most problems that lead to suicide are trivial or easily fixed. Given the gravity of the subject, this needs to be rewritten with much more care and precision, or removed. Similarly, "They feel no pity for the person who committed suicide either" is unclear—whose feelings are being described here? Revise for clarity and sensitivity throughout.

Missing Citations Are a Major Problem

Your essay repeatedly uses the placeholder "" instead of actual quotations from Rauscher, Forbes, Ingram and Ellis, and others. Since your central argument is *that these authors don't provide evidence*, it is essential that your own paper models rigorous citation practice. Readers cannot evaluate your analysis of these authors' claims without seeing what they actually said. This must be corrected in your next draft—include full, accurate quotations with proper citations (page numbers, publication dates).

Formulaic, Repetitive Structure

Nearly every body paragraph follows the same pattern: "[Author] states that <quote>. [Author] wants to show X. However, there is no evidence." This repetition becomes predictable and reduces the essay's persuasive power. Consider varying your sentence structures and paragraph organization. You might also strengthen your analysis by comparing *how* different authors fail to provide evidence—do some fail more egregiously than others? Is there a hierarchy of argumentative weakness you could establish?

Unsupported Claims of Your Own

Ironically, given your critique of others' lack of evidence, you occasionally make unsupported assertions yourself. For example: "someone dying of lung cancer may make his or her family more upset if he or she were alive" is presented without support, and the claim itself is speculative and could be seen as insensitive without careful framing. Similarly, your assertion that psychologists "can offer their experiences with different sessions" needs concrete examples or citations to be persuasive—right now it's exactly the kind of unsubstantiated claim you're criticizing others for making.

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

"There is little safety from diseases such as these" — check subject-verb agreement issues throughout (eg, "There when little safety").
"For all the reader's know" should be "For all the readers know" (no apostrophe).
Several sentences are quite long and could be split for clarity, particularly in paragraphs discussing Ingram and Ellis.
The AIDS paragraph feels disconnected—it's introduced but not fully integrated into your broader argument about evidence. Consider either expanding this connection or trimming this section.

Priority for Revision

First priority: Restore all missing quotations and citations—this is essential for the essay to function at all.
Second priority: Sharpen your thesis to clearly signal that this is an argument about *evidentiary weakness*, not a judgment on suicide's morality.
Third priority: Revise the confusing/insensitive statements (especially in paragraph 1) and eliminate unsupported claims of your own.
Fourth priority: Vary paragraph structure and improve transitions between sections.

Your instinct to interrogate the *rigor* of moral arguments—rather than simply accepting emotionally resonant claims—is a valuable critical skill. With revision, this essay could become a genuinely compelling piece of rhetorical analysis. I look forward to seeing your next draft.

Feedback on Your Essay: Adolescent Suicide and Theoretical Perspectives

Overall Impression

You've tackled a difficult and important topic, and your essay shows real engagement with the material—particularly your effort to connect sociological theory (Durkheim, Marx) to a contemporary social problem. The statistical evidence in your introduction is compelling. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: grammar and sentence-level clarity, structural organization, development of theoretical connections, and citation practices. Below are specific areas to focus on.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

This is the area needing the most attention. Some recurring patterns:

Subject-verb agreement: "the worst problem concerning are young people" should be "concerning our young people is." Check every sentence for agreement.
Article/possessive errors: "its a very sensitive" → "it's" (contraction) vs. "its" (possessive)—these are consistently confused throughout. Also "contraversal" → "controversial."
Verb tense and form: "has looked" should be "have looked" (subject is plural "works and studies"); "developed there classifications" → "three classifications."
Comma splices and run-ons: "Some researchers have just looked at statistical data ;while, others..." — the semicolon usage here is incorrect. Revise to: "Some researchers have looked at statistical data, while others have focused on..."
Repeated phrases: In the anomic suicide paragraph, you write "adolescents who have experienced the death of a parent" twice in the same sentence—this appears to be a drafting error that needs removal.

Suggestion: After revising content, do a dedicated grammar pass, or ask a writing center tutor to help you identify patterns in these errors so you can watch for them in future writing.

Citation and Source Integration

You have numerous `<R>` and  placeholders throughout the text that appear to be unfilled citation markers. These must be completed before submission. Additionally:

When you mention specific sources (Rabkin's book, Marx, Durkheim), make sure each claim is properly cited with page numbers where required by your citation style (APA, MLA, etc).
The quote "she'd look at everyone around her and feel different and crazy" needs quotation marks and a proper citation.
Consider paraphrasing more and quoting less—right now some sentences feel like they're waiting for a citation rather than making an argument in your own voice.

Structural and Organizational Suggestions

Your essay follows a logical progression (data → theories → Durkheim → Marx → conclusion), which is good. However:

The thesis needs sharpening. Your first paragraph presents adolescent suicide as understudied/under-publicized, but then your second paragraph immediately says there have been "many works and studies." This creates a slight contradiction. Clarify: Is the problem *lack of attention*, or is it that existing attention hasn't used a *theoretical* lens? Your real argument seems to be the latter—make that clearer early on.

The conclusion is too brief. Your final paragraph is only two sentences. Given the essay's length and complexity, you should more explicitly synthesize what Durkheim's and Marx's theories reveal that other approaches (case studies, statistics) don't. Restate why applying theory matters for understanding or preventing adolescent suicide.

Depth of Theoretical Analysis

This is the most important area for substantive revision:

Your explanations of egoistic and anomic suicide are solid, but the connection to adolescents feels asserted rather than argued. For example, you state that isolation "goes right along with" adolescent experience—but *why*? Elaborate on the mechanism: What specifically about adolescence (identity formation, peer dependence, family structure) makes Durkheim's categories applicable?

Your Marx section is weaker than your Durkheim section. The connection between dialectical materialism and adolescent suicide is not fully explained. You mention "suicidal families" and self-interest, but you don't clearly show *how* conflict theory (which is fundamentally about power, class, and social structures) explains suicide specifically. This section would benefit from a clearer, more explicit causal argument: Are you arguing that family conflict, generational conflict, or socioeconomic conflict drives adolescent suicide? Pick one thread and develop it.

The claim that depression is the "common ground" between these theories is interesting but needs more support. Right now it reads as an assertion. Explain *how* Durkheim's and Marx's frameworks each lead to or explain depression as a mechanism.

Word Choice and Precision

"Phenomenal" is used to describe suicide rates in a way that sounds like "amazing" (positive connotation)—consider "striking," "alarming," or "significant" instead.
"Bondness" is not standard English; use "a sense of belonging" or "connectedness."

Priority Revisions

Fill in all citation placeholders and verify sources.
Fix subject-verb agreement and its/it's errors throughout.
Strengthen the Marx/conflict theory section with clearer causal reasoning.
Expand your conclusion to synthesize your argument.
Clarify your thesis in the introduction.

You have a strong foundation here—the core idea of using classical sociological theory to illuminate a modern crisis is a genuinely good approach. Focus your revision on clarity, evidence integration, and deepening the "why" behind each theoretical connection.

Feedback on "Water Pollution and Sociological Theory" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen an interesting and timely topic, and you demonstrate genuine effort to connect environmental science with sociological theory. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas before it will be fully effective. Below are the main issues to address, organized by priority.

Missing Citations — Most Urgent Issue

Throughout the essay, I see placeholder markers like  and `<R>` where citations or source material should appear (eg, "The Rational Choice Theory is  `<R>`"). These gaps are critical because:
They leave key sentences grammatically and logically incomplete
The reader cannot evaluate your argument without knowing what evidence supports it
Your definitions of both theories are never actually stated in your own words

Action: Before revising anything else, fill in these placeholders with proper definitions and cited evidence. This is not optional—the essay cannot be assessed in its current form.

Thesis and Structure

Your introduction promises to examine water pollution through two theories, but the essay reads more like three separate mini-essays (background on pollution types/sources, then Rational Choice Theory, then Exchange Theory) rather than one integrated argument. Consider:

Sharpen your thesis in the introduction to state clearly *what claim* you are making about the relationship between these theories and pollution behavior (not just "this paper examines...").
The long section on pollution categories (infectious agents, organic/inorganic chemicals, radioactive materials) and point/non-point sources reads as encyclopedic background with no analytical connection to your theories. Either integrate this material directly into your theoretical discussion or trim it significantly.

Application of Theory Needs Deepening

You explain what each theory says in general terms, but the application to water pollution is often asserted rather than argued. For example:

> "If the water does not taste good, people will realize it, form their own opinion and stop drinking it. This is highly personalized and fits into the Exchange Theory."

This is a good start, but you need to explicitly connect *why* this exemplifies the theory's core mechanism (reward/cost, reinforcement) rather than just asserting the fit. Ask yourself for each example: *What specific concept from the theory does this illustrate, and how?*

Similarly, for Rational Choice Theory, distinguish more clearly between individual rational choices (a homeowner overusing fertilizer) and corporate rational choices (a company dumping waste)—you shift between these without acknowledging that they may operate differently under the theory.

Evidence Integration

Your statistics (CDC disease counts, beach closures, Milwaukee outbreak) are compelling, but they often appear as isolated facts rather than being woven into your argument. For each statistic, add a sentence explaining: "This matters for [theory] because..."

Conclusion

The conclusion introduces a new example (Milwaukee outbreak) rather than synthesizing your argument. Save new evidence for the body paragraphs. Your conclusion should instead:
Briefly restate how both theories illuminate the problem differently/similarly
Suggest what this theoretical understanding implies for policy or individual behavior
Avoid exclamation points in academic writing ("we all must take responsibility for!")

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

A few recurring patterns to watch for:
Apostrophe placement: "its'" should be "its" (its is already possessive; "it's" = "it is")
Subject-verb agreement: "The main source of these agents are" → "is"
Word confusion: "war pollution" should likely be "water pollution"; "then divide" should be "them"
Pronoun consistency: You shift between "they/she/one" when discussing hypothetical individuals—choose one pattern (eg, "they") for consistency
Run-on sentences: Several sentences (especially in the Exchange Theory paragraph) chain multiple ideas with commas where periods or semicolons would improve clarity

Suggested Next Steps

Fill in all citation placeholders with actual source material
Revise your thesis to state a clear, arguable claim
Trim the general background section on pollution types
For each theoretical application, explicitly explain the *mechanism* connecting theory to behavior
Rewrite the conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new evidence
Proofread for the grammar patterns noted above

This essay has a solid foundation of interesting content—the revision work now is about *tightening the connective tissue* between your evidence and your theoretical claims. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Marijuana Legalization Essay

Your essay tackles a complex, multifaceted topic with impressive research depth and a clear organizational strategy. You've gathered compelling evidence and attempted to apply sociological theory throughout. Below are areas to strengthen for your revision.

Strengths to Build On

You've done substantial research, incorporating historical context, medical evidence, economic data, and criminal justice concerns. Your attempt to consistently apply three sociological theories (Structural Functionalism, Exchange Theory, and Conflict Theory) shows theoretical ambition beyond a typical argumentative essay.

Major Areas for Revision

Thesis and Argument Structure
Your third paragraph reads as a list of topics ("This paper will focus on...") repeated three times. Consolidate this into one clear, concise thesis statement that identifies your main argument and previews your key points without redundancy. A strong thesis might state your position in one or two sentences, then briefly outline your supporting pillars (medical, economic, criminal justice).

Source Citations
The `<R>` markers throughout suggest incomplete citation formatting. You must replace these with proper citations (author, date, page number) according to your required style (APA, MLA, etc). Uncited claims—especially strong statistical assertions like "$4 billion to $24 billion"—will undermine your credibility without proper sourcing.

Theoretical Application Needs Depth
Your application of Exchange Theory tends to be repetitive ("rewards outweigh costs") without deeper analysis. For each theory:
Define the theory briefly the first time you use it
Explain *why* specific costs/rewards fit that theoretical lens, not just state that they do
Consider dedicating more sustained analysis to fewer applications rather than mentioning all three theories in nearly every section

Logical Consistency
Some claims need more careful framing:
You argue marijuana has "no well-defined fatal dose" as evidence of safety, then later say "not a single death... directly attributed" to marijuana—this is a strong claim requiring careful sourcing, as it may overstate certainty.
The claim that legalizing industrial hemp will make "people more pleased to buy American products" and reduce unemployment needs stronger causal support—these are significant economic claims requiring data, not assumption.
Consider addressing counterarguments more substantively. You mention "opposition" briefly but don't seriously engage with why the DEA, medical associations, or law enforcement oppose legalization. A stronger essay anticipates and rebuts these positions directly.

Language and Mechanics
Several errors need correction for a polished final draft:
"cannavis sativa" should be "*Cannabis sativa*" (and italicized as a scientific name)
"ganga" should be "ganja"
"heroine" should be "heroin"

Watch for inconsistent capitalization (eg, "Pot" vs. "pot")
Several sentence fragments and run-ons need revision, particularly in the historical timeline paragraph

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller errors systematically.
