Feedback on Your Essay on Homelessness

Thank you for this ambitious essay. You clearly did substantial research and have real command of the historical background and sociological theories involved. Below is feedback organized by the major areas that need attention.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your introduction raises several distinct ideas—the right to shelter, the growing homeless population, disagreement about *how* to address homelessness—but never resolves into a single, clear claim. The sentence "Advocates for people without homes tend to get so wrapped up in the size of the homeless population that they ignore the fact that it is not right that this discrepancy exists" is confusing and seems to contradict your final paragraphs, where you argue that focusing on numbers *distracts* from the moral problem. Decide on your central argument: Is it that homelessness is a moral crisis requiring immediate action? That conflict theory best explains its causes? That labeling perpetuates it? Your essay currently tries to do all three (historical survey + causal analysis + theoretical application) without a thesis statement that unifies them. Revise your introduction to state explicitly what you will argue and how the historical, causal, and theoretical sections serve that argument.

Structure and Transitions

The essay reads as three separate papers stitched together: (1) a history of homelessness in America, (2) causes of homelessness, and (3) application of conflict theory and symbolic interactionism. Each section is informative on its own, but you need explicit transitions showing *why* the history matters for your argument and *how* the theories help explain the causes you listed earlier (mental illness, housing costs, racial discrimination, technology). Right now these sections don't talk to each other. Consider adding a sentence at the end of each section that links forward to the next (eg, "These historical patterns of blame set the stage for the theoretical debate over who is responsible for homelessness today").

Use of Evidence

You cite Fantasia and Isserman but leave a placeholder () instead of the actual quotation—remember to fill this in before submitting. More importantly, this is your *only* citation in a nine-page essay full of specific historical and statistical claims ("the housing for low income families has dropped," "drug use has increased dramatically," "an uneven number of African-Americans are forced to take the jobs available to them"). These claims need sources. As it stands, the essay reads as assertion rather than argument. Go back through and add citations for any factual claim that isn't common knowledge, especially in the causes section and the discussion of race and education.

Depth of Theoretical Application

Your explanations of conflict theory and symbolic interactionism are generally accurate, but they stay at the level of *summary* rather than *analysis*. For example, you write "the results of these inequities are poverty, homelessness, discrimination, and oppression" without showing the mechanism—how, specifically, does the ownership of property translate into a family losing housing? Strengthen this by returning to concrete cases from your causes section (deinstitutionalization, housing costs, racial discrimination) and showing step-by-step how each theory would explain them. Right now the theory sections and the causes section exist in parallel rather than integrating with each other.

Also, your final paragraph asserts that "it is better to examine [homelessness] from a micro-level point of view" via symbolic interactionism, but earlier you say conflict theory is valuable for seeing "the broad picture." These two claims are in tension and need to be reconciled—are you arguing for one theory over the other, or for combining them? Clarify this in your conclusion.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

There are several recurring errors worth reviewing:
Word confusion: "Contact theorists" should be "Conflict theorists"; "lobs" should be "jobs"; "mayor cause" should be "major cause"; "socked endures" is unclear (perhaps "society endures").
Awkward phrasing: "This trend has continued" and similar vague transitional sentences could be more precise about *what specifically* continued.
Run-on or unclear sentences: "These negative feelings of the powerful to the powerless and vice versa contributes greatly to the consent unrest socked endures" is difficult to parse and should be rewritten for clarity.
Subject-verb agreement: Watch for singular/plural mismatches (eg, "feelings... contributes" should be "contribute").

I recommend reading your essay aloud, section by section, to catch these issues, or asking a writing center tutor to review sentence-level grammar with you.

Overall Recommendation

Your strongest material is the historical overview and your grasp of the two sociological theories. To improve this essay, I suggest: (1) writing a clear thesis that states your specific claim; (2) explicitly connecting the history, causes, and theory sections; (3) adding citations throughout; and (4) revising the conclusion so it doesn't contradict earlier claims about which theoretical lens is most useful. With revision, this could be a strong, well-integrated piece.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

This is an ambitious, well-researched essay that tackles a complex policy debate through two competing sociological lenses (Spencer's Social Darwinism and Merton's Matthew Effect). You clearly did substantial research, and the comparative structure—using two theorists to represent conservative and liberal positions—is a sophisticated approach. However, the essay needs revision in several key areas: citation completion, argumentative focus, balance of evidence, and language accuracy.

Missing Citations and Quotations (Priority Issue)

Throughout the draft, there are unresolved placeholders marked `<R>` and  (eg, "says Ann Clark... " or "she quit school because "). These appear to be spots where you intended to insert quotations or citations but never completed them. As it stands, several sentences are incomplete or nonsensical (eg, "a says Ann Clark"). Before anything else, you must fill in these missing quotes and add proper in-text citations (author, year, page) for every fact and statistic. Right now, a reader cannot verify any of your data, which undermines your credibility, especially since so much of your argument rests on statistics.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your actual thesis doesn't fully emerge until the final two paragraphs, where you stake out a synthesized position: neither full abolition nor a laissez-faire "survival of the fittest" model is acceptable, but reform must be realistic and account for recipients' actual barriers. This is a reasonable and interesting position—but it needs to be stated clearly in your introduction so readers know where you're heading. Currently, the essay reads as a historical survey followed by two extended "book reports" on Spencer and Merton, with your own voice appearing only at the very end. Consider adding a clear thesis statement in paragraph one (eg, "This essay argues that while Social Darwinism oversimplifies welfare dependency as mere laziness, purely structural explanations also fail to account for individual agency; effective reform must combine opportunity-based support with realistic accountability").

Structural Balance

The historical background (paragraphs 2–8) is quite long and could be condensed. You don't need a full legislative history of AFDC to make your argument; a few sentences establishing context would suffice, freeing up space for deeper analysis of the Spencer/Merton debate and your own conclusions.

Additionally, the Spencer and Merton sections are structurally similar (theory explained, then evidence marshaled to "support" the theory), which works well as a rhetorical device, but you should make sure the evidence you use for each is not applied inconsistently. For example, you use the Project Match statistics first to support Spencer's skepticism about welfare-to-work programs (high dropout rates), but later use the same program's struggles to argue for more realistic job training. Clarify explicitly that you are reinterpreting the same evidence to reach a different conclusion—right now it reads as if the evidence contradicts itself.

Use of Evidence

You have a huge amount of statistical evidence, which is a strength, but many statistics are simply listed rather than analyzed. For instance, in the paragraph beginning "Twenty-five to forty percent of welfare recipients have learning disabilities..." you present five separate statistics in three sentences without explaining their significance. Choose your strongest 2–3 statistics per point and explain *why* they matter, rather than trying to include every number you found.

Language and Mechanics

There are several errors to correct in revision:
"Me United States" → "the United States" (typo, para. 1)
"in to a large" → "into a large" (para. 6)
"deferent men" → "different men" (Aletha Townsend paragraph)
"he no useful place" → "has no useful place" (Spencer paragraph)
Comma splice: "In Spencer's theory of Social Darwinism, Spencer viewed..." — avoid repeating "Spencer" awkwardly; revise to "In this theory of Social Darwinism, he viewed..."
"third of American children are born out of wedlock, and those children are four times as likely" — check this statistic against your source; "one-third" seems very high for the historical period being discussed and should be verified.
Watch for inconsistent verb tense when moving between historical narrative (past tense) and theoretical discussion (present tense).

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement for your introduction.
Trim the historical background section by half.
Complete all missing citations and quotations.
Add brief transitional sentences explaining how each theorist's evidence supports *your* argument, not just theirs.
Proofread carefully for typos and grammar, ideally reading the essay aloud.
Strengthen the conclusion by explicitly returning to your thesis and summarizing your specific reform proposals.

This essay has strong bones and impressive research—your final revision should focus on making your own argumentative voice clearer and more consistent throughout, rather than only at the end.

Feedback on Your Essay: Divorce and Sociological Theory

Overall Impression

You've done impressive work organizing a large amount of sociological material into a coherent framework. Your use of multiple theoretical perspectives (symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, exchange theory) to analyze divorce shows genuine engagement with course concepts, and your citations (marked with `<R>`) suggest careful research. However, the essay currently reads more like an informative report than an argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to strengthen for revision.

Clarify Your Thesis and Argument

Right now, it's unclear what you are *arguing*. Is your claim that:
Divorce results from multiple, compounding social causes rather than any single one?
No single theory adequately explains divorce, but together they provide a fuller picture?
Society can meaningfully reduce divorce rates through the specific interventions you propose?

Choose one central claim and state it explicitly in your introduction. Currently, the essay reads as "here are four theories about divorce" followed by "here are some things we could do." Consider restructuring so each theoretical section explicitly supports a larger argument (for example: "each theory reveals a controllable social factor, which suggests that targeted interventions could meaningfully reduce divorce").

Strengthen the Transitions Between Theories

Each theoretical section (symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, exchange theory) is presented almost as a self-contained mini-essay. To make this feel like one unified argument rather than four separate summaries:
Add sentences that compare/contrast the theories explicitly (eg, "Unlike symbolic interactionists, who focus on changing meanings, conflict theorists locate the source of divorce in structural inequality.")
At the end of each section, briefly indicate how that theory connects to your overall thesis.

Integrate Evidence More Analytically

You cite good statistics and studies (the divorce rate trends, Ogburn, Burgess and Locke, Spanier and Thompson), but much of this evidence is simply *reported* rather than *analyzed*. For each citation, ask: "What does this evidence prove, and how does it support my argument?" For example, after citing Spanier and Thompson's findings on economic conflict, explain *why* this supports the conflict theory framework you're using, rather than just listing the findings.

Reconsider the "Solutions" Section

The final third of the essay pivots to proposed solutions, but these are quite general ("couples need to try to have an equal partnership," "people should not stay in an unhappy marriage"). These read as common-sense advice rather than conclusions that follow logically from the theories discussed. Strengthen this section by:
Tying each solution explicitly back to the theoretical section it addresses
Being more specific: What would a policy or program based on exchange theory actually look like? What would strengthening "family units" from a functionalist perspective require in practice (eg, policy, community structures)?
Acknowledging tensions between theories' implications (eg, if conflict theory says marriage reflects gender inequality, would a "solution" require broader social change, not just individual effort?)

Language and Style Issues to Fix

Word choice/typos: "coning" → "coming"; "fob" → "job"; "wilt" → "will"; "Piety" → likely "Society" or another word; "then own job" → "their own job"; "moves forward divorce" → "moves toward divorce"; "involved through history" → "evolved through history"
Redundant phrasing: "The next step is to consider..." followed immediately by a paragraph doing exactly that — this signals structure but feels mechanical. Consider integrating this transition more smoothly into your conclusion of the analysis section.
Repetition in conclusion: The final paragraph re-summarizes almost every section already summarized in-text. Trim this to focus on your strongest, most original point rather than restating everything.
Passive/vague constructions: "It is predicted that..." — specify by whom, to strengthen credibility and specificity.

Suggested Structural Revision

Consider this framework for your revision:
Introduction: State the trend (rising divorce) and your specific claim about *why* multiple theories together explain this trend, or what unifies them.
Body: For each theory, add a concluding sentence linking back to thesis; add explicit comparisons between theories.
Solutions: Make each solution specific and directly derived from the theory preceding it.
Conclusion: Avoid pure summary; instead, synthesize what the multi-theoretical approach reveals that a single theory could not, and end with a forward-looking claim (not just a hope).

Final Note

Your grasp of the sociological content is strong — the challenge now is argumentative shape: transforming a well-researched survey into a persuasive essay with a clear, sustained claim. Focus your revision on sharpening the thesis, tightening transitions, and making your evidence work explicitly in service of that claim.

Feedback on "Running the Score Up in College Football"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an engaging, specific topic and clearly have real knowledge of college football. Your voice is conversational and confident, which makes the essay enjoyable to read. However, the essay currently reads more like a passionate defense of these coaches than a tightly organized argumentative essay. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the argument, structure, and language.

Thesis and Argument Focus

Your central claim seems to be: "coaches are not really guilty of running up the score; it happens for legitimate reasons." This is a solid, arguable thesis, but it needs to be stated clearly and early—ideally at the end of your introduction. Right now, readers must wait until the final paragraph to understand your actual position. Consider revising your introduction so that after the vivid opening scene, you state directly: "Although the NCAA criticizes coaches for running up the score, this practice is often unavoidable and even beneficial for both teams and programs."

You offer several *different* justifications (backup players proving themselves, Heisman campaigns, job security, fan entertainment). These are all interesting, but right now they feel like separate mini-essays rather than parts of one unified argument. Consider explicitly organizing them as "reasons" in a clear sequence, and use topic sentences that connect back to your thesis (eg, "A second reason coaches are not truly at fault is...").

Structure and Paragraphing

Your paragraphs are organized by topic, which is good, but transitions between them are weak or missing. For example, the jump from the Heisman Trophy paragraph to the "job security" paragraph could be smoothed with a transitional sentence like "Beyond individual awards, there is also a financial incentive at the institutional level."

Also, the essay would benefit from a counterargument section placed before your rebuttal. You do address the NCAA's position, but you weave your rebuttal in immediately rather than fully explaining the opposing view first. Try structuring it as: (1) explain why critics say running up the score is unfair/unsportsmanlike, (2) then systematically dismantle that view with your reasons.

Evidence and Examples

The Florida-South Carolina and Florida State ranking example is excellent—concrete, detailed, and relevant. Use more examples like this throughout. Right now, many of your later claims (about Heisman money, bowl money, fan ticket sales) are presented as general assertions without specific evidence. Adding even one concrete number (eg, "Bowl payouts can range from $X to $Y million") or a specific player/team example would make these paragraphs far more persuasive.

Also, be careful with logic: in the Florida/Florida State ranking anecdote, you seem to argue *against* your own point—Florida was penalized for restraint (starting the second-string QB) while Ohio State (which ran up the score) was rewarded. This actually shows the *system* encourages running up the score, which supports your argument that coaches aren't "guilty" since they're responding rationally to incentives. Make this connection explicit for your reader; right now it's left as a rhetorical question ("No one knows").

Language and Clarity

There are several grammar and word-choice issues to address in revision:

"will they go for it?" / "Yes they are going for it" — fragments; consider integrating more smoothly into a rhetorical question-answer format.
"does it" vs. "do they" — check subject-verb agreement (eg, "the NCAA discourages it, or do they" should be "or does it").
"unsurmountable" should be "insurmountable."
"destructive" (used to describe passing plays) is an odd word choice — you likely mean something like "efficient" or "high-scoring."
"filter" the football program should probably be "fund" or "support."
"tables up" should likely be "adds up" or "comes down to."
"Bobby Bowden,'" has a stray punctuation mark.
The placeholder "" appears to be an incomplete sentence — you need to finish this thought (it seems you meant to describe deliberately not trying, eg, "just take a knee" or "don't even try to score").

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, to catch these issues, and/or visiting your school's writing center for a line-edit pass focused on articles, subject-verb agreement, and word choice.

Tone

Your tone is engaging and appropriately informal for a sports-focused argument, but a few phrases ("getting their butts beat bad," "I know I made Steve Spurrier sound like a jerk") are too casual for academic argumentative writing. Consider replacing these with more formal equivalents while keeping your energetic voice.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

State your thesis clearly and early.
Add clear topic sentences linking each paragraph back to that thesis.
Strengthen the counterargument section before your rebuttal.
Add concrete evidence/numbers where you currently generalize.
Fix grammar, word choice, and the incomplete sentence.
Adjust a few overly casual phrases.

You have a strong, original argument here—the revision work is mainly about *organization* and *precision*, not about generating new ideas. Good luck!

Feedback on "The Confederate Battle Flag" Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely difficult, emotionally charged topic, and you write with personal investment that gives your essay authenticity. Your willingness to complicate the "heritage vs. hate" debate by distinguishing between two different flags shows real critical thinking. However, the essay's central argument needs more logical consistency, and several claims require stronger historical support. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Clarify Your Central Argument

Right now, your position shifts in ways that confuse readers. In paragraph 2, you say the South's heritage should be honored, "but not by keeping the Confederate battle flag flying." This implies no flag should fly. But by the end, you argue a *different* flag (the "real" Confederate flag) *should* fly with an explanatory plaque. These are two different policy positions. Decide which argument you're making:

Option A: No Confederate-associated flag should fly over a government building, because all such flags are tied to a slaveholding nation.
Option B: The battle flag should be replaced with a different Confederate flag that represents "heritage" rather than "hatred."

Right now you seem to argue both, which weakens your credibility. I'd recommend committing to one thesis and restating it clearly in your introduction and conclusion.

Re-examine the Historical Claims

Your distinction between the "battle flag" (which you say represents slavery/hatred) and the "real" Confederate flag (which you say represents heritage/way of life) is the most vulnerable part of your argument. Historically, the Confederacy had multiple official flags, and *all* of them represented a government founded explicitly to preserve slavery — including the national flags, not just the battle flag. A reader familiar with this history may find your distinction arbitrary rather than persuasive. Consider:

Researching the actual history of Confederate flags (First National, Second National, Third National, and the battle flag) using credible sources.
Asking yourself: is the distinction really about *design*, or is it about how each flag has been *used* historically and in the present? If it's about use, say so explicitly — that's actually a stronger argument than one based on which bars and stars appear on the cloth.

Support Your Strongest Point with Evidence

Your paragraph about the flag being raised in 1964 (in connection with the Civil Rights Movement) is your most compelling piece of evidence — but you present it without a citation. Since this is a factual historical claim, and since your credibility depends on it, please verify the exact date and context and cite a source (a book, newspaper archive, or reputable website). Right now, a skeptical reader might just discount this claim as unsupported.

Strengthen the Counterargument Section

You write: "One should not argue that the battle flag stands for heritage not hatred." This dismisses the opposing view by simply restating your own conclusion rather than engaging with the reasons people *do* associate the flag with heritage. To make your argument stronger, briefly acknowledge *why* people feel this way (family history, regional identity, etc) before explaining why you believe that view is mistaken or incomplete. Right now the paragraph feels like an assertion rather than a rebuttal.

Sentence-Level and Word Choice Issues

"succeeded" should be "seceded" (South Carolina *seceded* from the Union).
Watch comma splices, eg, "I agree because I am one of those people who think that the Confederate flag reflects on my history. I was brought up..." — consider combining or restructuring for smoother flow.
Some sentences repeat the same idea in slightly different words (eg, multiple sentences insisting the battle flag = hatred). Trim repetition and use that space for evidence instead.
Be careful with flag descriptions — reread your visual descriptions (eg, "red with blue bars, with stars in them, crossing through the center") to ensure grammatical clarity; a reader unfamiliar with the flags may struggle to picture what you mean.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph introduces a new, somewhat complex policy proposal (raising a different flag *plus* an explanatory plaque) that hasn't been set up earlier in the essay. If this is truly your main argument, foreshadow it in your introduction so the reader isn't surprised by a shift in the final paragraph.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Pick one clear, consistent thesis.
Verify and cite your historical claims (especially the 1964 date).
Reconsider whether the "battle flag vs. real flag" distinction is historically accurate or persuasive — if not, rework this section.
Address counterarguments more substantively.
Proofread for word choice ("seceded") and sentence structure.

You have a strong personal voice and a real stake in this topic — with tighter logic and stronger sourcing, this essay could be very persuasive. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for sharing this thought-provoking and clearly passionate piece of writing. You tackle a complex social issue with confidence, and you clearly did research to support your position. Below are suggestions to help strengthen the essay for a revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Stance

Your thesis—that premarital sex is "overall destructive" because of three effects (loss of respect for women, illegitimate families, STDs)—is clear and gives the essay a roadmap. However, the claim is very absolute. Academic argumentation is usually more persuasive when it acknowledges complexity and counterarguments. For example, you note that the Sexual Revolution also brought positive outcomes (better health education, family planning, gender equality), but you quickly dismiss these as merely providing an excuse for immorality. Consider engaging more seriously with the benefits before pivoting to your critique—this will make your argument seem more balanced and credible to skeptical readers.

Causation vs. Correlation

This is the most important logical issue to address. Throughout the essay, you attribute social problems (STDs, single parenthood, disrespectful language, crime, poor education) directly to premarital sex, but many of these could have multiple causes (poverty, lack of education, media culture, etc). For instance, the connection between "illegitimate families" and higher crime rates is presented as self-evident, but no causal mechanism is explained—only correlation is implied. Strengthen this section by explicitly explaining *why* premarital sex, specifically, causes these outcomes, or acknowledge that other factors may contribute.

Use of Evidence and Citations

You use a good range of sources (Reiss, Gardner, Janus, Schur), which shows solid research. However:
Several statistics are unclear or seem to contain typographical errors (e.ts., "45% if single women," "Janus 13 5"). Please double-check all numbers and citation page numbers against your original sources.
Some statistics don't clearly support your point. For example, the fact that "90% of people believe family is the most important institution" doesn't obviously prove that "the idea of family has changed"—the logical link needs to be spelled out.
Be consistent with in-text citation format (author, page number) throughout.

Tone and Word Choice

The essay uses several informal or emotionally loaded expressions ("din-finishing," "BOOM," "Mr. Rockwell's," ".5 being the dog of course"). These add personality, but in formal academic writing they can undercut your credibility, especially when discussing sensitive topics like women being called derogatory names. Consider adopting a more measured, analytical tone—let the facts and logic carry the emotional weight rather than sarcasm.

Additionally, the conclusion's call for a "return to Christian values" is a significant claim that appears without earlier development. If you want to argue for a religious/moral solution, you should build this idea gradually throughout the essay (eg, referencing religious or philosophical perspectives on sexual ethics) rather than introducing it only in the final lines.

Structure and Transitions

Your paragraphs are organized by theme (history of the Sexual Revolution, STDs, pregnancy/family structure, disrespect between genders), which is a logical structure. However, transitions between these sections could be smoother—right now each paragraph reads somewhat like a separate essay. Consider adding topic sentences that explicitly connect each section back to your three-part thesis (eg, "The second consequence of premarital sex, disrespect for women, is closely tied to...").

Language and Grammar (as this is likely most useful for a non-native writer)

There are recurring grammar patterns worth reviewing:
"lost"/"lose"/"loss" – you often use "lost" where "loss" is needed ("lost of respect" → "loss of respect").
"there" vs. "their" – used interchangeably in several places ("all the good things... had there bad side affects" → "their").
"affect" vs. "effect" – "affects" is often used where "effects" (the noun) is meant.
Subject-verb agreement and verb forms – eg, "cut down on have sex" → "cut down on having sex"; "contacted" → "contracted" (regarding disease).
Article and preposition usage – eg, "far to many" → "far too many"; "at lest" → "at least."

I recommend reading your essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool, then manually verifying each correction, since automated tools sometimes miss context-specific issues.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Soften the absolutist thesis and acknowledge counterarguments.
Clarify causal reasoning—explain *why* premarital sex leads to each negative outcome, not just correlations.
Verify and correctly format all statistics and citations.
Adjust tone to be more analytical and less sarcastic/informal.
Develop the "Christian/moral values" solution earlier if you intend to end with it.
Proofread carefully for the grammar patterns noted above.

You have a strong foundation of ideas and research here—the next draft should focus on tightening logic, improving academic tone, and polishing language mechanics. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report on "Women in Combat" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay presents a clear, personal position on a genuinely debated issue, and your passion for the topic is evident throughout. However, the essay currently reads more like a personal reflection than an academic argument. To strengthen it for an academic context, you'll need to add evidence, tighten your reasoning, and revise some language choices that may undermine your credibility with readers. Below are specific areas to focus on in revision.

Argument and Evidence

The most significant weakness is the lack of supporting evidence. You make several strong factual claims—that standards are "lowered" for women, that this leads to "distortion" of military readiness, that women's "emotional makeup" is fundamentally different—but none of these claims are supported by data, research, studies, or even specific examples. In academic writing, especially on a contested social issue, unsupported assertions will not persuade a skeptical reader.

Revision suggestion: For each major claim, ask "How do I know this?" Then find a source—a military policy report, a study on physical standards, or expert testimony—to back it up. Even one or two well-chosen sources per paragraph would substantially strengthen your argument.

Addressing Counterarguments

Right now, the essay presents only one side. A strong argumentative essay engages with the opposing view seriously before refuting it. For instance, you could address:
Women who have successfully served in combat roles (there is now documented history of this)
Arguments that physical standards can be role-specific rather than sex-specific
The argument that "emotional makeup" claims about women are stereotypes not supported by psychological research

Engaging honestly with these counterpoints—rather than dismissing "feminists" broadly—will make your own argument seem more carefully reasoned rather than one-sided.

Loaded Language and Tone

Some word choices will alienate readers rather than persuade them:
"the weaker sex" — this phrase is widely seen as demeaning and will cause many readers to disengage from your argument regardless of its merits. Consider more neutral, specific language (eg, "average differences in upper-body strength").
"the feminists that support this equality" — this frames an entire group as a monolith with negative connotation. Academic writing generally avoids characterizing opposing groups this way.
Highly personal statements like "I love having feminine characteristics" or "I have a difficult time trying to understand a woman that wants to be treated like a man" shift the essay from persuasive argument to personal preference. These may be true for you, but they don't function as argumentative support.

Revision suggestion: Reframe these as claims about policy and evidence rather than personal identity statements, eg, "Physical standards research suggests..." rather than "I love..."

Structure and Repetition

There is a near-verbatim repeated passage in paragraph 2 (the sentence about "intellectual capabilities" and "physical weakness" appears twice). This should be edited out—likely a draft/editing error.

The essay would also benefit from a clearer structure:
Paragraph 1: Introduce the issue and thesis (currently good, but could be sharper)
Paragraph 2: One clear claim with evidence (currently mixes multiple ideas)
Paragraph 3: Address the "difference vs. deficiency" argument with support
Paragraph 4: Counterargument and rebuttal
Conclusion: Synthesize, don't just restate personal feelings

Right now, similar ideas (physical differences, emotional differences, philosophical questions about "why change women") appear scattered across paragraphs rather than being developed in a logical sequence.

Language and Grammar Notes

A few specific items to review:
"a females" should be "a female's" (possessive)
The long sentence in paragraph 2 needs to be broken into shorter sentences for clarity
Watch for comma splices (eg, "...capacity as a male, the standards..." — check punctuation rules for joining independent clauses)
"Why is it so important..." and other rhetorical questions are effective rhetorically but should not replace argument—follow each question with a developed answer, not just another question

Suggested Next Steps

Find 3–4 credible sources (military reports, physiological studies, policy analyses) to support your key claims.
Revise loaded language into neutral, evidence-based phrasing.
Add a paragraph that seriously considers and responds to the counterargument.
Remove the duplicated passage and tighten sentence structure throughout.
Reframe personal statements as reasoned claims rather than expressions of preference.

Your core question—whether pursuing equality can sometimes overlook meaningful differences—is a substantive one worth arguing. With stronger evidence and more careful language, this essay could make a compelling academic case. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report

Your essay tackles a genuinely important and complex topic, and you've done solid research to support your points—citing Farrell, Masters, Friedan, and Pollitt shows real engagement with the source material. However, there are several areas where revision would strengthen your argument considerably.

Thesis and Argumentative Consistency

Re-read your own body paragraphs: nearly every piece of evidence you provide (Ralph's estrangement from his family, boys being ridiculed for sensitivity, women losing their identities, the housewife syndrome, the woman who refused treatment) demonstrates the *harm* of rigid gender roles, not their benefit. Your essay actually builds a strong case *against* maintaining traditional gender identities. I'd suggest revising your thesis to match the argument you're actually making, which seems to be that rigid gender stereotyping is harmful and American culture should move toward flexibility and individual choice. This would also resolve the tension with your strong concluding paragraph, which is your most persuasive writing in the piece.

Structure and Paragraph Balance

Your "advantages" of stereotyping are stated very briefly (one sentence each) before pivoting to disadvantages that receive much more space and evidence. This imbalance is actually appropriate if your real argument is anti-stereotyping—but then you shouldn't claim in your thesis that roles should be maintained. Consider restructuring: you could present the traditional argument for gender roles as a viewpoint you're addressing and then systematically dismantle it with your evidence, rather than presenting "advantages" as your own position.

Incomplete Citations and Missing Content

"they think" [] — what do psychiatrists think happens to sensitive boys?
The citation for women dropping out of college
The citation for Friedan's cancer story
The citation for the "housewife syndrome" study
Pollitt's actual argument about the Smurfette Principle is missing entirely (currently just "Pollitt feels that ")

These gaps make it hard to fully evaluate your argument, and they must be resolved—readers need the actual content, not brackets.

Clarity and Logical Connections

Some sentences need clearer causal logic. For example: "The very few women who went to college ended up dropping out to get married for fear of too much education being a bar to marriage" — this sentence has structural confusion. Try: "Few women attended college, and those who did often dropped out to marry, fearing that too much education would make them less desirable as wives."

Similarly, in your paragraph about male stereotypes, the transition from Farrell to Masters is abrupt. "Psychologist have" should be "Psychologists have"

"should be allowed to do so" — check consistent verb tense throughout body paragraphs (you shift between present and past)

Suggestions for Revision

Rewrite your thesis to align with the evidence you actually present.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Residence Hall Regulations

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic you clearly feel passionate about, and this passion comes through in your writing. Your use of personal experience (the USC visitation policy, roommate contracts) gives the essay authenticity and specificity that many student essays lack. However, the essay needs significant revision in three key areas: thesis clarity, organization, and argumentative consistency. Below, I'll walk through these issues and suggest concrete steps for improvement.

Thesis and Central Argument

Your introduction promises an essay about how college dorm rules prevent maturation, but the essay actually develops into several different (sometimes contradictory) arguments:
Rules prevent students from maturing
Rules are unfair/discriminatory (gender-based visitation policies)
Rules unfairly advantage LGBT students
Quiet hours are unreasonable

Suggestion: Choose ONE central claim and subordinate everything else to it. Right now, your strongest and most original argument is the discrimination angle (heterosexual vs. same-sex visitation policies)—this is a genuinely interesting point I haven't seen many students make. Consider making *this* your thesis, with the "maturity" argument as supporting context, rather than the reverse.

Organization

The essay currently reads as a list of grievances rather than a structured argument. Each paragraph raises a new complaint without clear logical connections to previous ones. For instance, the "maturity" theme from your introduction disappears once you start discussing visitation policies, then resurfaces only in the conclusion.

Suggestion: Try this structure:
Intro: State thesis clearly (eg, "Residence hall visitation policies claim to ensure safety, but their gendered enforcement reveals inconsistency and discrimination.")
Body 1: Explain the visitation policy and its stated purpose (safety)
Body 2: Show how the policy fails its own logic (the rape-warning inconsistency, the LGBT loophole)
Body 3: Address the "quiet hours" as a secondary, related example of paternalism
Conclusion: Return to the "maturity" theme, connecting specific problems to the larger claim

Logical Consistency

Some arguments undercut each other. For example:
You argue that rules should protect against sexual assault, then argue that "nine times out of ten" a male and female together aren't a danger—this weakens your own case for why visitation *should* exist in modified form.
You call the LGBT exemption "discrimination" against heterosexuals, but you should consider: is the solution to *remove* protections from same-sex couples, or to *rethink* the safety rationale entirely? Right now this section could be read as resentment toward LGBT students rather than a critique of policy logic. Clarify your reasoning here, as this is a sensitive point that needs careful framing to strengthen (not weaken) your credibility.

Evidence and Development

Your best moments are concrete: the USC policy times, the roommate contract process, the specific example of a guest waiting outside. These sections are much stronger than abstract complaints ("that's ridiculous!"). 

Suggestion: Cut some of the repetitive rhetorical questions ("What is too loud? When am I supposed to know?") and instead add more concrete evidence: Do you have statistics on dorm rule violations? Could you cite university policy documents directly? Even one or two outside sources (a news article about dorm policy debates, or your university's official policy rationale) would strengthen your credibility significantly.

Language and Grammar

As a non-native speaker, you're doing well conveying complex ideas, but there are recurring errors worth addressing:

Subject-verb agreement: "guest have to be out" → "guests have"; "Men are rape also" → "Men are raped too"
Word choice/spelling: "roll" should be "role" (parenting role, not roll); "there" vs. "their" (residents have "their" opinions, not "there")
Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense often within the same paragraph. Try to maintain present tense throughout, since you're describing ongoing policies.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: "That's ridiculous!" works as emphasis but appears too casually; consider integrating this reaction into a fuller sentence for a more academic tone.
Informal register: Phrases like "mom and dad from downstairs," "Heaven forbid," and direct address ("I know you think...") create a conversational tone. This can work rhetorically, but use it *deliberately* and sparingly—right now it appears throughout, making the essay feel more like a spoken argument than a formal essay. Choose a few key moments for this technique rather than using it constantly.

Next Steps

Rewrite your introduction with one clear thesis statement.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each supports that thesis directly.
Cut the weakest/most repetitive rhetorical questions; replace with evidence.
Do a full proofreading pass focused on subject-verb agreement and homophone errors (there/their, role/roll).
Reconsider the LGBT paragraph's framing—strengthen the *policy critique* without implying resentment toward a group of students.

You have real insight here, especially in noticing the visitation policy's internal inconsistency. With tighter focus and organization, this could become a genuinely compelling piece of persuasive writing.

Overall Impression

You've tackled an ambitious topic with strong passion and clear conviction. Your use of specific historical examples (1992-93, 1993-94, and 1994 seasons) provides excellent supporting evidence, and your knowledge of college football history is impressive. However, the essay needs significant revision in three key areas: structure and argumentation, grammar and sentence-level clarity, and source integration. Below are specific suggestions organized by priority.

Structural and Argumentative Issues

Thesis and Body Balance: Your thesis promises three benefits of a playoff system: (1) more revenue, (2) more excitement, and (3) determining a true champion. However, roughly 85% of your essay focuses on point #3 (the "true champion" argument through historical examples), while points #1 and #2 receive only brief treatment near the end. Consider either:
Expanding your revenue and excitement arguments with concrete data (actual TV ratings, attendance figures, revenue comparisons between bowl systems and other playoff sports)
Or narrowing your thesis to focus primarily on fairness/legitimacy, since that's where your strongest evidence lies

Redundant Conclusions: After each of your three case studies (1992-93, 1993-94, 1994), you end with nearly identical sentences: "the only way to answer this question fairly is to have a playoff system." While repetition can reinforce an argument, doing this three times in nearly the same words feels formulaic. Vary your language and, more importantly, use each case study to build toward increasingly sophisticated points rather than restating the same conclusion.

Counter-argument Treatment: Your treatment of opposing views (bowl tradition, season length, revenue concerns) appears only briefly near the end. This section feels like an afterthought rather than a serious engagement with opposition. Strengthen this by:
Providing more detail on *why* traditionalists value bowls
Directly refuting each counter-argument with evidence, not just assertion
Addressing Bobby Bowden's specific concern about season length—how would your 16-team, 4-week plan actually address this?

The Proposal Itself: Your specific playoff proposal (16 teams, bowls as neutral sites, 4 weeks) appears late and receives less development than your historical examples. Since this is your actual policy recommendation, consider moving it earlier and explaining the logistics more thoroughly—how are the 16 teams selected? What happens to conference tie-ins? This is the "so what" of your essay and deserves more weight.

Grammar and Sentence-Level Issues

I noticed recurring patterns worth addressing:

Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense unpredictably (eg, "Nebraska is in the Big Eight Conference" vs. "Nebraska was playing Miami"). Choose past tense for historical events and stay consistent.
Missing/incorrect words: Phrases like "there was always some debated" (debate), "couldn't really Florida State ahead" (missing verb, likely "rank"), and "going to going to the Cotton Bowl" (repeated words) need proofreading.
Comma splices and run-ons: Many sentences combine independent clauses without proper punctuation, eg, "But everything worked out after the bowl games were played on New Year's Day. Texas A&M was whipped..." Consider using semicolons or restructuring.
Subject-verb agreement: "There was no way that Nebraska and Penn State could have played" is correct, but watch for others like "their was some controversy" (should be "there").

Suggestion: After revising content, do a dedicated proofreading pass focused solely on grammar, ideally reading sentences aloud to catch awkward constructions.

Source Integration and Citation

Your quotations from Rick Reilly, Tom Osborne, and Joe Paterno are valuable, but the integration needs work:

The formatting is inconsistent and confusing (eg, "11" appears to be a typo for quotation marks in several places—check your original document for encoding errors)
Quotes are sometimes dropped in without context or follow-up analysis. For instance, after the Osborne/Paterno quotes, you move directly to your conclusion sentence without explaining *why* these coaches' opinions matter or how they support your specific argument
Ensure your citation style is consistent (you use (18), (19), (C3), (C6)—clarify what these refer to and follow a consistent citation format like MLA or APA)

Priority Revision Steps

First: Revise your thesis and ensure body paragraphs proportionally support all three claims
Second: Expand the "revenue and excitement" section with concrete evidence
Third: Strengthen counter-argument treatment with specific refutations
Fourth: Proofread line-by-line for grammar issues

Feedback on Your Go-Cart Safety Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay—your firsthand experience working at Frankie's Fun Park gives you real authority on this topic, and that authenticity comes through clearly. Readers can tell you know what you're talking about. Below are some observations to help you strengthen the piece.

Strengths

Your use of concrete, specific examples is your greatest asset. The detail about being hit by a go-cart going twenty-five miles per hour, and the description of thirty-eight cars on the track at once, make your argument vivid and credible. You're not just telling readers rules matter—you're showing them through real scenarios (the pit collisions, the child getting hurt, the bumper's actual purpose). This is exactly the kind of evidence that makes argumentative writing persuasive.

Areas for Revision

Thesis and Structure
Your introduction promises "many reasons why you should follow the rules," but the essay reads more like a list of individual rules (seatbelts, gas/brake, no bumping, pit procedures) rather than a structured argument building toward a clear claim. Consider revising your thesis to state explicitly *what* the consequences of rule-breaking are (injury, property damage, ruined experience for others), and then organize each paragraph to clearly support that central claim. Right now, each paragraph explains a different rule, but doesn't always tie back to why that specific rule matters to your overall argument.

Paragraph Focus
Some paragraphs contain multiple ideas that could be separated or better connected. For example, the paragraph on bumping covers group dynamics, small children, car damage, and the purpose of bumpers—all important points, but they'd have more impact if organized with a clearer internal logic (perhaps: risk to others → risk to equipment → misconception about bumpers).

Word Choice and Grammar
There are several recurring errors worth addressing in revision:
"go-carts" vs. "go-carts" — consistent, good.
"annoucements" → announcements
"being taking off" → should not be taken off
"there for" vs. "their for" — "The bumpers are not there for people to intentionally hit..."
Subject-verb agreement: "Having all these rules are for the safety" → "Having all these rules is for the safety" (or better: "These rules exist for the safety...")
Comma splices: Several sentences run two independent clauses together with only a comma (eg, "I was even lucky that when I got hit, I wasn't seriously hurt, I just bruised my arm real bad"). Consider breaking these into separate sentences or using semicolons.
"Where ever" → whenever (one word)
"real bad" → "very badly" (adverb form, more formal register)

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud slowly—this often helps catch places where sentences run together or where a word is missing (eg, "not one worker wants to get hit by a car" is correct, but check similar constructions throughout).

Tone and Audience
Your voice is warm and direct, which suits a workplace safety message. However, for an *academic* argumentative essay, consider slightly elevating the formality. Phrases like "I don't know how to express how important it is" or "you got to remember" work in conversation but could be replaced with clearer topic sentences that state your point directly. This doesn't mean losing your voice—just sharpening it.

Conclusion
Your ending has good instincts (returning to the "safe, fun, and enjoyable" theme), but it introduces a new idea—the workers' seven-hour shifts—that isn't developed elsewhere. Consider either expanding on worker welfare earlier in the essay or saving this ending for a tighter summary of your three or four core reasons (safety of self, safety of others, protection of equipment, smoother experience for workers).

Suggested Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis that names your 2–3 main reasons explicitly.
Reorganize paragraphs so each one clearly supports one of those reasons.
Proofread specifically for comma splices and subject-verb agreement—reading aloud will help.
Consider adding a short story or anecdote in your introduction (perhaps the moment you got hit) to hook readers immediately, then explain in the following paragraphs *why* this happened and how rule-following would have prevented it.

You have compelling material here—your job now is mainly to organize it and polish the mechanics so the strength of your evidence really shines through.

Feedback on Your Essay: The Susan Smith Case

Thank you for sharing this essay on a genuinely difficult topic. You clearly have strong feelings about this case, and your willingness to grapple with a real moral dilemma is commendable. Below are some suggestions to help strengthen your argument for revision.

Thesis and Focus

Your stated purpose is "to express my opinion on the justness of the sentence handed down to Susan Smith," and you do eventually argue that life in prison was the right decision. However, this thesis gets somewhat buried. Consider stating your central claim more directly and early—perhaps at the end of your first paragraph—so readers know exactly what you're arguing and why. Something like: "While many believe Smith deserved death, I argue that life imprisonment is actually the more fitting and severe punishment, because it forces her to confront her guilt every day of her life." This kind of clear thesis will help unify your paragraphs.

Logical Consistency

There's an important factual inconsistency to fix: you state that "the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty," but then a paragraph later say "the jury made the right decision." A jury, not the Supreme Court, determines sentencing in a criminal trial like this. Please verify this fact and use consistent terminology throughout—this is a credibility issue for readers who know the case.

Two Arguments, Not Fully Connected

Your essay essentially makes two different cases for why life imprisonment was correct:
A psychological/legal argument (her traumatic history and mental state diminished her culpability)
A theological argument (only God, not humans, should decide who lives or dies, and she deserves the chance at redemption)

Both are legitimate angles, but right now they sit somewhat separately rather than working together. Consider explicitly connecting them: does her troubled past *support* the religious argument for mercy? Or are these two independent reasons for the same conclusion? Making this connection explicit will make your essay feel more unified rather than like two essays stitched together.

Audience Awareness

Your religious argument is heartfelt, but remember that academic argumentative essays are typically written for a general audience that may not share your Christian faith. If you want this section to be persuasive to *all* readers (not just those who already agree), you might:
Explicitly acknowledge that not everyone shares this belief system
Frame the religious point as *one* reason among several, rather than the emotional climax of the essay
Consider whether a purely secular version of the "redemption/rehabilitation" argument (she can still contribute to society, help others, etc) might reach a wider audience

Missing Citations

You've inserted two placeholder brackets  for Bible quotations. Before submitting, make sure these are filled in with the actual verses (Exodus 20:13 for the sixth commandment, and likely something like Romans 10:9-13 or 1 John 1:9 for forgiveness). Also, since you reference specific facts about Smith's life (the psychiatrist's testimony, details of her past), consider citing your source—even informally, like "According to news reports at the time..."—to strengthen credibility.

Emotional Appeal vs. Evidence

Your description of what Smith will "have to live with"—hearing her sons' voices, seeing herself as a murderer—is vivid and effective. This is a strength! However, some claims are speculative ("she will always have to sit in the lonely jail cell and hear the two small voices"). Since we cannot know her actual internal experience, consider softening this to "she may relive" or "one can imagine she will hear," which is more intellectually honest while still being persuasive.

Sentence-Level Issues

There are several recurring errors worth fixing:
Spelling: "recieve" → receive; "prision" → prison (appears twice)
Comma splice: "Several months have passed... Some people are very relieved..." — the first sentence about several months passing needs a period, not to run into the next idea awkwardly; check punctuation between independent clauses throughout (eg, "the news media and many individuals have stated facts as well as opinions, I would like to express..." should be two sentences or joined with a semicolon/conjunction).
Redundancy: "Her boyfriend dumped her and gave her the reason..." repeats "dumped her" from the previous sentence—combine these into one sentence.
Watch subject-verb agreement and article usage (common areas of difficulty for non-native English writers)—reading your essay aloud can help catch these.

Next Steps

For your revision, I'd suggest: (1) sharpen and front-load your thesis, (2) fix the jury/Supreme Court error, (3) explicitly bridge your psychological and religious arguments, (4) fill in the Bible citations, and (5) do a careful proofreading pass for spelling and sentence boundaries. This is a thoughtful, passionate draft—these revisions will help your argument reach readers more persuasively and clearly.

Feedback on Your Essay: Lowering the Drinking Age

Thank you for tackling this controversial and complex topic. You've organized your essay around three clear reasons (crime reduction, responsible drinking, improved health), which gives your argument a logical skeleton. Below is feedback to help you strengthen the content, structure, and citation practices in your revision.

Strengths
Your thesis clearly previews three distinct supporting arguments, which helps orient the reader.
You attempt to use evidence from multiple sources rather than relying solely on opinion.
The topic is relevant and debatable—a good choice for an argumentative essay.

Areas for Revision

Citations Need Serious Attention
This is the most urgent issue. Several of your "sources" appear to be email addresses or informal websites (eg, "Rick Finley@rfinleygmail.vt.edu," "Netscape.Young Drinkers Homepage 2," "nttp.www.kfu.com/nsayer"). These are not credible academic sources and will undermine your credibility with an academic reader. Before revising further:
Replace these with peer-reviewed studies, government reports (properly formatted), or reputable news sources.
Use a consistent citation style (MLA or APA) throughout.
Double-check that every claim attributed to a source is accurately represented—right now it's hard to tell what these sources actually say.

Logical Gaps Need Filling
Several key claims are asserted without sufficient reasoning:
The "forbidden fruit" argument: You claim that legalizing drinking at 18 will make it "less glamorous" and reduce binge drinking. This is a significant psychological claim that needs evidence—does research support this? Countries with lower drinking ages don't automatically prove causation; you need to address *why* this would work in America specifically.
Crime reduction: You state that lowering the drinking age reduces crime because fewer people will be breaking the law to obtain alcohol. But this is somewhat circular—it reduces *this specific crime* by definition, not necessarily crime overall. Consider addressing violent crime, DUI, or campus incidents more directly with data.
The "military service" argument: This is a common argument in this debate, but you should preempt the counterargument: critics often argue that voting, signing contracts, and drinking involve different types of judgment and risk (long-term versus immediate impairment). Address this directly rather than stating the parallel and moving on.

Counterarguments Are Missing
A strong argumentative essay anticipates opposition. Currently, your essay reads as one-sided. Consider addressing:
Why was the drinking age raised to 21 in 1984? (You mention this fact but don't explain the reasoning behind the National Minimum Drinking Age Act—it was tied to reducing drunk-driving fatalities among young people, which is directly relevant to your health argument.)
What do opponents of lowering the drinking age argue, and why is your position still stronger?

Paragraph Organization
Your third paragraph (about crime) and second paragraph (also about crime) contain overlapping ideas—consider merging them into one clearer section. Similarly, the health paragraph shifts between binge drinking and general "alcoholism as a societal view" without a clear throughline. Try outlining each paragraph with one clear claim, followed by evidence, followed by explanation of *why* that evidence supports your claim.

Grammar and Mechanics
A few recurring issues to address in your revision:
Apostrophes with plurals: "eighteen-year old's" should be "eighteen-year-olds" (no apostrophe needed for simple plurals).
Word confusion: "effect" should be "affect" in your conclusion ("would effect the United States" → "would affect the United States").
Capitalization inconsistency: "Is" appears capitalized oddly in "Eighteen-year old Is would not feel a need..." — this looks like a typo (perhaps you meant "olds"?).
Missing commas: Several sentences need commas after introductory phrases (eg, "On most college campuses, alcohol is involved...").
Verb agreement: "Alcoholism is not a problem in other countries because of the alcohol is viewed" — this sentence needs to be restructured; likely you meant "because of how alcohol is viewed."

Suggested Next Steps
Replace all informal sources with academic, verifiable citations.
Add one paragraph addressing counterarguments (perhaps right after your thesis or before your conclusion).
Proofread carefully for the grammar patterns noted above—reading your essay aloud can help you catch these.
Strengthen transitions between paragraphs so the "crime" and "responsibility" sections don't feel repetitive.

Your essay has a solid argumentative structure at its core. With stronger, more credible evidence and attention to counterarguments, this could become a much more persuasive piece. Feel free to visit office hours if you'd like help locating academic sources on this topic.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling topic and your proposal—mandatory swimming instruction in P.E.—is practical and well-supported with logistical details. The narrative opening is a strong rhetorical choice that draws readers in emotionally before you pivot to your argument. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: structure, evidence, paragraph organization, and proofreading. Below are specific areas to address.

Structure and Argumentation

Strengths: Your solution-oriented approach (using existing pools, partnering with certified instructors, creating employment for lifeguards) shows genuine problem-solving and anticipates practical objections. This is exactly what strong argumentative writing needs—not just "what" but "how."

Areas for Improvement:

Evidence and support: Your essay relies heavily on the opening narrative and general claims ("this is a common story," "alarmingly, many people don't know how to swim"). For a stronger academic argument, you need concrete evidence: statistics on drowning deaths among children/teens, data on how many schools currently offer swim programs, or research on swimming instruction's effectiveness. Without this, your argument feels persuasive but not substantiated.

The rhetorical question about dodgeball ("How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone's life?") is a nice touch of wit, but it's an oversimplified comparison. P.E. serves multiple purposes (fitness, teamwork, motor skills), so this comparison could be seen as a false equivalence. Consider replacing it with a more substantive point about *prioritizing* life-saving skills within an already-existing curriculum requirement.

Paragraph 3 is doing too much work. It introduces the proposal, explains P.E. requirements, states your thesis, and criticizes the education community—all in one paragraph. Consider splitting this into two: one paragraph introducing your proposed solution, and a second developing your thesis with clearer support.

Conclusion is underdeveloped. Your final paragraph is only two sentences and doesn't synthesize your argument or leave the reader with a strong closing thought. Consider returning to your opening narrative—what would be different for that child if this policy existed? This creates a satisfying circular structure.

Paragraph-Level Issues

The transition from the emotional narrative to the analytical argument (paragraph 2) is abrupt. Consider adding a sentence that explicitly bridges the story to your thesis (eg, "Behind this scenario lies a solvable problem...").
Paragraph 4 makes a good point about the "four weeks" timeframe but doesn't cite where this figure comes from. Is this based on research, personal experience, or general assumption? Always indicate your source, even if informally ("according to swim instructors..." or "studies show...").

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for:

Spelling errors: "cericulum" (curriculum), "instuctor" (instructor), "wuold" (would), "drownding" (drowning), "simlpe" (simple), "remidied" (remedied), "employent" (employment), "thaech" (teach), "panicing" (panicking). *Suggestion: Run your essay through spell-check, then read it aloud slowly to catch remaining errors.*

Capitalization: "News" should be lowercase ("news"); sentence-initial words after periods need capitals (eg, "your only problem" → "Your only problem").

Run-on sentences and comma splices: Several sentences fuse independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, "one class that is common from school to school and state to state is a class in Physical Education.I think..." needs a space and possibly separate sentences).

Pronoun-antecedent agreement: "It would give evryone the chance to learn how to swim, how to recognize when somebody is in trouble and how to handle that situation" — smooth, but double-check "evryone" (everyone) and consider varying sentence openings, as many paragraphs begin with "I" or "It."

Verb tense consistency: Watch for shifts between present and past tense, especially in the narrative opening (mostly present tense—good) versus the analytical sections (should remain consistent, typically present tense for arguments).

Suggestions for Revision

Add 2-3 pieces of concrete evidence (statistics, expert opinions, or reputable sources) to strengthen your claims about drowning rates and swimming instruction effectiveness.
Revise the conclusion to be at least one full paragraph, synthesizing your argument and connecting back to the opening story.
Proofread carefully, perhaps using a spelling/grammar checker or asking a peer/tutor to review.
Reconsider the dodgeball comparison for a more precise, less flippant point.

Your instinct for using narrative to hook readers and for offering realistic implementation strategies shows strong argumentative thinking. With more evidence and careful editing, this essay will be much more persuasive and polished.

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and timely topic, and your central argument—that rigid gender identities are outdated and harmful—is clear and consistently maintained throughout. You've done solid work integrating outside sources to support your claims. However, the essay would benefit from revision in several key areas: sentence-level clarity, paragraph organization, evidence integration, and grammatical consistency. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Structure and Organization

Your essay follows a logical progression: definition → tradition vs. modern reality → psychological effects on women → psychological effects on men → single-parent families as evidence → conclusion. This is a sound structure. However, consider these improvements:

Thesis clarity: Your thesis appears in the first paragraph, but it could be sharper. Consider stating explicitly *why* distinct gender identities are harmful (psychologically damaging, practically unrealistic) before diving into examples.

Topic sentences: Some paragraphs (especially the second one) introduce multiple ideas—tradition, expert opinion, and examples—without a clear controlling idea. Try starting each paragraph with one focused claim that the rest of the paragraph develops.

Conclusion: Your final paragraph summarizes well, but it introduces new evidence (women in the workplace) not discussed earlier. Save new points for the body; use the conclusion to synthesize what you've already argued.

Use of Evidence

You cite three sources (Masters/Johnson/Kolodny, Friedan, and Farrell), which shows good research effort. However:

Missing content: Several placeholders (marked with ) are incomplete—we don't see what the "distinct" male/female qualities were, what the researchers concluded, what Friedan actually said, or what "Ralph" states. These quotations and paraphrases are essential to your argument's credibility. Fill these in with specific, well-integrated evidence.

Citation format: You'll need proper in-text citations (eg, APA or MLA) for these named sources, and a Works Cited/References page.

Integration: When you introduce a quote or paraphrase, briefly explain *how* it connects to your point. For example, after the Farrell reference, clarify precisely what price "Ralph" paid and how this illustrates the psychological burden you're describing.

Sentence-Level and Grammar Issues

Capitalization after periods: Several sentences begin with lowercase letters after a period (eg, "if these traits were abolished..." and "it would be ridiculous to think..."). Proofread carefully for this.

Comma splices and run-ons: Some sentences merge independent clauses without proper punctuation. Review for clarity.

Word choice/spelling: "dependant" should be "dependent." Also, reconsider "emanate" and "radiate" when describing qualities—more natural phrasing might be "exhibit" or "demonstrate."

Repetition: Phrases like "distinct gender identity/identities" and "psychological burden/impact" appear very frequently. Vary your language to maintain reader engagement (eg, "rigid gender roles," "traditional expectations," "emotional toll").

Parallel structure: In the paragraph about single parents, you list characteristics ("strong, courageous, dependent, and self-reliant") but later paraphrase this list slightly differently. Keep parallel lists consistent for clarity.

Content Suggestions

Counterargument: Your essay would be strengthened by acknowledging an opposing viewpoint—perhaps someone who argues that gender differences are biologically rooted—and then refuting it. This is a common expectation in argumentative essays and would demonstrate critical engagement with the topic.

Contemporary relevance: Your evidence (Friedan, Masters/Johnson/Kolodny) seems dated. Consider whether more current research or examples (eg, discussions of gender fluidity, changing workplace norms) might strengthen your argument for a modern audience.

Define terms early: You state that "distinct gender identity" needs explanation, which is smart, but the definition itself is incomplete due to the placeholder. Make sure this definition is crisp and appears early, ideally in your introduction.

Priority Revisions

For your next draft, prioritize:
Filling in all missing quotations/evidence (the  and `<R>` markers)
Proofreading for capitalization and comma errors
Adding a counterargument paragraph
Reducing repetitive phrasing
Adding proper citations

Final Note

Your argument is thoughtful and your organization is fundamentally sound. With attention to these details—especially completing your evidence and polishing sentence-level grammar—this essay will be significantly stronger. Keep up the good analytical thinking!

Your essay covers many topics—models' measurements, eating disorders, plastic surgery, discrimination against obese women, and the beauty industry's profit motives. This is a lot of ground to cover in one essay. Consider:

Grouping related ideas into clear paragraphs, each with one main point (eg, one paragraph solely on eating disorders, one on cosmetic procedures, one on economic discrimination).

Next Steps

Thank you for sharing this draft. You've chosen an engaging, debatable topic, and you clearly have real knowledge of how talk shows work. Below are suggestions to help you strengthen the essay's argument, organization, and language.

Right now, it's difficult to tell what you are arguing. Revision suggestion: End your introduction with a clear thesis statement that states your specific claim. For example: "While talk shows are often criticized as exploitative, they serve an important social function by giving ordinary people a platform to seek help and be heard—though both hosts and guests bear responsibility for the risks involved." This would unify your body paragraphs and prepare readers for your conclusion.

Improve Paragraph Organization

Each paragraph currently mixes multiple ideas without clear topic sentences. For instance, your second paragraph discusses (a) entertainment value, (b) helpful information for viewers, and (c) hosts helping guests—three separate ideas that could each become their own paragraph with a clear focus.

Revision suggestion: Try the classic structure: one main idea per paragraph, stated in a topic sentence, followed by evidence/examples, then a concluding link back to your thesis. For example:
Paragraph 1: Entertainment value and audience appeal
Paragraph 2: Educational/social benefits (health, finances, counseling)
Paragraph 3: Risks and controversies (your murder example)
Paragraph 4: Responsibility and conclusion

This appears to reference the real 1995 Jenny Jones Show case—if so, name the show and provide a source. Without documentation, this reads as an unverified anecdote, which weakens your credibility as a writer.

Also, explain *why* this example matters to your argument. Is it evidence that shows should screen guests more carefully? That hosts share blame? That viewers should reconsider what they're watching? Right now the example is described vividly but not analyzed.

This is a reasonable point, but it feels disconnected from the earlier discussion of talk shows as "helpful" and "entertaining." This will make your ending feel like a natural conclusion rather than an abrupt new argument.

There are frequent run-on sentences, comma splices, and stray periods that interrupt sentences (eg, "trash t.v.. is vulgar. and inappropriate"). These errors make it hard for readers to follow your ideas. A few examples to fix:

"guest panelist" → should be plural: "guest panelists."
"thier grievances" → spelling: "their."
"The host dies deep into the minds" → likely a typo for "delves deep."
"fist" → should be "fists."
"heath" → "health."

Suggestion: After revising content and organization, do a separate proofreading pass focused only on grammar. Reading your essay aloud, or using a grammar-checking tool, can help you catch comma splices and subject-verb agreement errors (eg, "differences in opinions leads to" should be "lead to").

Word Choice and Tone

These are creative, but in an argumentative essay, aim for a balance between vivid description and clear, direct claims. Ask yourself: does this phrase advance my argument, or is it decorative?

Reorganize paragraphs around single main ideas.
Cite your source for the murder example and analyze its significance.
Connect your conclusion explicitly to your thesis.
