Feedback on "Legal Drinking Age" Essay

Thank you for taking a clear position on this debated topic. You've chosen an argument that many students find engaging, and your personal investment comes through, especially in the conclusion. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: logical consistency, argument development, evidence, and grammar. Below is feedback organized by category.

Argument and Logic

Your central claim—that eighteen should be the legal drinking age—is stated clearly in your thesis, but you undermine it in your final paragraph. You write, "Drinking should be prohibited to kids eighteen or older," which actually contradicts your thesis. I believe this is a typo (perhaps you meant "should *not* be prohibited"), but as written, it reverses your entire argument at the moment when clarity matters most. Please proofread your conclusion carefully.

Your military service argument (paragraph 2) is your strongest point because it appeals to a widely-shared sense of fairness: if a person is trusted with life-and-death responsibility, why not with alcohol? To strengthen this, consider addressing the counterargument directly: opponents might say that combat readiness and alcohol tolerance are different kinds of "maturity." Explain *why* you think this distinction doesn't hold.

Your high school diploma argument (paragraph 3) needs more development. Right now, the logic is unclear: why would earning a diploma indicate someone is mature enough to drink responsibly? Many responsible adults don't finish high school, and many people who graduate are not automatically mature. Also, your proposal to deny non-graduates the right to drink raises legal and ethical complications you don't explore (for example, this could be seen as punishing people for their educational status rather than their age). This section would benefit from a clearer connection between the claim and your reasoning.

Your crime argument (paragraph 4) relies on a common-sense assumption—that hiding alcohol use from parents causes drunk driving—but you need to explain the causal link more carefully. Does lowering the drinking age actually reduce drunk driving? Some argue the opposite: more legal access could mean more young drivers on the road after drinking. Address this counterpoint, even briefly, to make your argument more convincing.

Evidence

This essay relies entirely on personal reasoning and general claims ("Most kids eighteen to twenty are out of high school," "Most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving"). For an argumentative essay, you need supporting evidence: statistics on drunk-driving arrests by age group, data comparing countries with different drinking ages, or research on decision-making and the adolescent brain. Even one or two solid statistics would significantly strengthen your credibility. Try searching for CDC data, MADD (Mothers Against Drunk Driving) statistics, or comparative studies on drinking ages in other countries.

Organization

Your paragraph structure is generally logical (military, education, crime, conclusion), but each paragraph would benefit from a clearer topic sentence that states the main point before diving into examples. For instance, paragraph 2 could open with something like: "One of the strongest arguments for lowering the drinking age is the inconsistency between military service and alcohol restrictions."

Also, consider adding a paragraph that acknowledges the opposing view (eg, concerns about teenage brain development or public safety) before refuting it. This is a hallmark of strong argumentative writing and would make your essay more persuasive.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address:

Spelling: "Independance" → Independence; "dieing" → dying; "gonvernment" → government; "condemned" (paragraph 2) may not be the word you want—perhaps "required" or "sent."
Word confusion: "too young" is correct, but you wrote "two young"—a homophone error. Also check "affects" vs. "effects" (you likely want "effects" in paragraph 2, since you mean the results of drinking, not the verb "to affect").
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences need revision for clarity, such as the opening sentence, which is a fragment: "Every since the wild west, the civil war, and the drafting of the Declaration of Independence." Consider combining this with the previous sentence or rewriting it as a complete thought.
Verb tense and subject-verb agreement: "if eighteen year old was allowed" should be "eighteen year olds *were* allowed."

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, which often helps catch these errors, and using a grammar-checking tool as a supplement (not a replacement) for careful proofreading.

Summary

Your essay has a promising argument, particularly the military service comparison. To strengthen it, please: (1) fix the contradiction in your conclusion, (2) add research-based evidence, (3) address counterarguments, and (4) proofread carefully for spelling and grammar. I'd be happy to discuss revisions further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay tackles an important and complex topic—how gender identity is shaped in childhood and how this affects adult life. You've done solid work synthesizing multiple sources (Pollitt, Ferrell, Friedan, Grey), and your central claim—that gender roles should have a stable "core" but remain flexible—is a thoughtful position. Below are suggestions to help strengthen the essay.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis is somewhat buried and inconsistently stated. In the introduction, you say children need "a core of behavior" that is fixed, alongside behaviors "open to change." By the conclusion, you emphasize that people should not be "limited by their sex" at all. These aren't necessarily contradictory, but you need to clarify the relationship between the fixed "core" and the flexible elements early on, and return to this framework explicitly in your conclusion. Right now, the essay reads as a summary of four sources rather than an argument that uses those sources to support a single, clear claim.

Suggestion: Consider a thesis statement like: "While children need a stable sense of gender identity, society's current methods for teaching that identity rely on harmful stereotypes rather than allowing for the natural individual variation within genders." Then organize each body paragraph to explicitly support this claim.

Use of Sources

You cite several relevant authors, which shows good research. However:

Citations are incomplete or unclear. "(548)" and "(29)" are page numbers, but without a Works Cited page or clearer in-text signals (author name + page), readers cannot trace these back to specific sources. Also, sentences like "she cites." (with a stray period) and "Pollit contends:that" suggest the essay needs careful proofreading for citation mechanics.

Summary dominates over analysis. Long stretches of the essay describe what Pollitt, Ferrell, and Friedan say without stating *why* this matters to your argument or how these examples connect to each other. For instance, the paragraph on Ralph's Story and Friedan's homemakers describes both but doesn't explicitly link them back to the "core vs. changeable" framework from your introduction.

John Grey's section shifts from problem-description to solution, which is a good structural move—but this transition should be flagged explicitly for the reader ("Having shown the problem, we now turn to a proposed solution...").

Suggestion: After each source summary, add 1-2 sentences of your own analysis explaining how this evidence proves your specific point.

Clarity and Coherence

Some sentences contain logical gaps or unclear connections:

"Now that our children are off to a wonderful start they are ready for adulthood" — this ironic transition is a nice rhetorical touch, but the paragraph that follows shifts abruptly to two unrelated case studies without explaining the causal link between childhood messaging and adult unhappiness.
The sentence "Their approach is that gender roles are needed, they just need to be amended" is a good synthesis of Friedan, Ferrell, and Pollitt—place a similar synthesizing sentence at the *start* of each paragraph, not just at the end.

Language and Mechanics

As a non-native English speaker, you're clearly working with ambitious vocabulary and complex sentence structures, which is commendable. However, there are frequent errors that affect clarity and professionalism:

Spelling errors: "relazed" (relaxed?), "ammend" (amend), "spouse" (sponse), "scarry" (scary), "metophor" (metaphor), "Freidan/Fredian/Friedon" (inconsistent spelling of Friedan), "Californian" (Califorian), "Venus" (Venue), "point" (piont), "role" (rold)
Punctuation: Random periods appear mid-sentence ("she cites. many painful examples"), and colons are misused ("Pollit contents:that").
Word choice: "exogenic pressures" is unclear—did you mean "external" pressures? "Semiotics" is used unusually; you may mean "signals" or "signs" more broadly, though semiotics can work if defined.
Article and preposition use: "gender relazed, adulthood" and "starting girls into life's" need revision for standard usage.

Suggestion: After revising for content, do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on spelling and punctuation. Reading your essay aloud can help catch missing words or unclear phrasing.

Overall Recommendation

Your ideas are engaging and your source selection is strong. Focus your revision on:
Clarifying your thesis and returning to it explicitly in each paragraph
Adding analytical sentences that connect evidence to your argument
Correcting citation format (include a Works Cited page)
Careful proofreading for spelling and punctuation

With these revisions, this essay could offer a genuinely compelling argument about gender identity and childhood development.

Feedback Report: Argumentative Essay on Drug Legalization

Overall Impression

You tackle a genuinely complex, controversial topic, and you clearly have opinions and some real-world observations to draw on (the anecdote about your high school's smoking policy, for instance, is a nice touch). However, the essay currently reads more like a stream of loosely connected thoughts than a structured argument. My feedback focuses on three main areas: organization/structure, argument development, and language/mechanics. Revising with these in mind will significantly strengthen your paper.

Structure and Organization

Right now, the essay lacks a clear roadmap. The introduction never states your main claim precisely — is it "legalize marijuana," "legalize all drugs," or "government should regulate drugs"? You seem to shift between these positions throughout (sometimes defending marijuana specifically, sometimes all drugs, sometimes even suggesting no drug should be restricted at all). Choose one central thesis and stick to it.

Suggested thesis focus: Based on your strongest content, I'd recommend narrowing to: *"Legalizing and regulating marijuana specifically would benefit society by improving health safety, reducing crime, and easing the burden on the criminal justice system."* This is more defensible than the broader claim that all drugs should be legal, which you make near the end but don't adequately support.

Each paragraph currently touches on multiple ideas without transitions. For example, the paragraph on health problems mentions brain damage, purity regulation, marijuana vs. cigarettes, and crack cocaine all in a few sentences. Consider dedicating one paragraph to one main idea, with a clear topic sentence, supporting evidence, and a concluding link back to your thesis.

Suggested reorganization:
Intro (clear thesis)
Body 1: Health/safety argument (regulation = purity control, comparison to legal substances like alcohol/tobacco)
Body 2: Criminal justice argument (overcrowded jails, violent crime tied to illegal drug trade)
Body 3: Economic argument (jobs, tax revenue)
Body 4: Civil liberties argument (your quotation fits well here)
Address counterargument: the age-limit paragraph is interesting but currently feels disconnected — it could become a "concession" section where you acknowledge a real challenge to your policy and propose a solution.
Conclusion (restate thesis, why change is needed)

Argument Development

Several claims need more support or clearer reasoning:

"Most violent crimes stimulate from selling illicit drugs" — this is a strong claim. Do you have any statistics or sources? Even one citation would strengthen credibility here, especially since you show you *can* cite sources (the Wibotsky quote).
The needle/AIDS argument feels underdeveloped — expand this with a sentence or two on how needle-exchange or purchase-with-needle programs have worked elsewhere.
The civil liberties argument (the Wisotsky quote) is your most sophisticated moment, but it appears abruptly near the end. Introduce it earlier and build your argument toward it, rather than tacking it on.
Self-contradiction: You state "I do not feel as strongly about legalizing crack cocaine as others," which suggests you support marijuana legalization specifically. But later you write "if people want to harm their bodies, why stop them?" and discuss legalizing "drugs" broadly. Decide which position you're actually defending and revise inconsistent statements.
The final paragraph raises interesting rhetorical questions (alcohol vs. marijuana comparison) — this could actually make a strong opening, hooking the reader, rather than being buried at the end.

Language and Mechanics

There are recurring grammar issues that a careful revision pass should address:

Subject-verb agreement: "The side effects of marijuana is" → are; "Jails are over crowded" (correct) vs. "the number of drug users would decrease" (check tense consistency throughout — you shift between present and conditional).
Word choice/spelling: "lease harmful" → *least*; "anatomy" (used oddly — perhaps you mean "body"); "hann" → *harm*; "injuries" → *injures*; "use" → *us* (in the quotation).
Sentence fragments: "Health problems seem to be a concern... Issues such as permanent damage..." — the second "sentence" is a fragment. Combine: *"Health problems, such as permanent damage to the brain, are a common concern regarding legalization."*
Run-on/comma splices: "The legalization of marijuana among other drugs, would keep some people out of the streets" — remove the unnecessary comma before "would."
Citation format: Check your citation style guide (MLA/APA) for how to properly format the Wisotsky quote — currently it lacks a proper introduction and the author's name is misspelled.
Pronoun consistency: You shift between "I," "you," and general statements ("people," "someone"). Academic essays typically maintain a more consistent, formal voice — minimize "you" and personal asides like "I do not know of many people..." unless using them deliberately as illustrative evidence.

Priority Revision Steps

Write one clear thesis sentence and place it at the end of your intro.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has a single focus.
Cut or clarify contradictory statements about which drugs you're defending.
Add at least one more source/statistic to support the crime and health claims.
Proofread for subject-verb agreement and sentence fragments.

You have good instincts for real-world evidence and a strong closing rhetorical question — the raw material for a compelling essay is here. The next draft should focus on tightening the logical structure so your strongest points aren't lost in the disorganization.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay

Overall Impression

This is a promising draft with a clear personal narrative, a debatable claim, and a logical structure that moves from anecdote to broader argument to proposed solution. Your English proficiency is strong enough to construct complex sentences and sustain an argument across multiple paragraphs, which is commendable. However, the essay needs revision in several areas: formatting/typographical cleanup, sharper organization, stronger evidence beyond anecdote, and more precise word choice. Below are specific areas to address.

Typographical and Formatting Issues (Priority Fix)

There are numerous distracting errors that suggest the essay was not carefully proofread after drafting—likely leftover artifacts from OCR scanning or editing software:
Random periods and line breaks mid-sentence ("here at. USC," "she began-to have difficulty," "began. To ask")
Stray characters and symbols ("b t U she," "who-,@ spoke," "large 1, university")
Inconsistent spacing and capitalization

Action: Do a careful line-by-line proofread pass focused *only* on mechanics before addressing content. Read the essay aloud slowly; this will help you catch broken sentences that don't read smoothly.

Word Choice and Idiom

Some words are used inaccurately and should be revised:
"squander through complicated material" → likely you mean "struggle through" or "muddle through." *Squander* means to waste (usually money or resources), not to struggle.
"invoke the student to ponder" → consider "encourage the student to consider" or "prompt students to explore."
"teacher's aid/aide" is inconsistently spelled; also "TA" is more natural than "teacher's aide" in American academic English.
"his students," "his discipline" — consider using gender-neutral language ("their") throughout, since your example professor is female. This inconsistency (switching between "she" for your friend's professor and "he" for hypothetical professors) is a good opportunity to revise for consistency and inclusivity.

Structural and Organizational Suggestions

Your essay follows a logical arc: anecdote → thesis → supporting reasons → policy solution → conclusion. This is a strong skeleton. To strengthen it:

Trim the anecdote. The opening story is effective but overly long (nearly two full paragraphs). Consider condensing it by 30-40%, focusing on the details that most directly support your thesis (the professor's inability to explain concepts, the lack of available alternative sections) and cutting minor narrative details (buying the book, the exact quote from your friend's internal thoughts).

State your thesis earlier and more prominently. Currently, your explicit thesis ("I completely agree that before a professor should be allowed domain...") doesn't appear until the end of the first body paragraph. Consider placing a clear thesis statement at the end of your *introduction*, so readers know your argument before the supporting paragraphs begin.

Paragraph 3 (tuition/consumer argument) needs tightening. The analogy of "buying an item from a store" is a reasonable rhetorical move, but you spend a lot of sentences restating the same point ("students pay tuition, they should receive teaching in return") in slightly different words. Consolidate this into 2-3 sentences and use the saved space to develop a counterargument or additional evidence.

Strengthen Your Evidence

Right now, your argument rests entirely on one personal anecdote. This makes the essay persuasive on an emotional level but vulnerable to the criticism that it's an isolated incident, not a systemic problem. To strengthen your case:

Consider citing or referencing (even generally) any data, university policy documents, or other students' experiences that suggest this is a recurring issue at USC, not a one-time event.
You mention that TAs must pass an English proficiency test but professors do not—this is your strongest piece of evidence. Develop this further: Where did you learn this? Can you cite the specific policy or requirement? This comparison is central to your argument and deserves more support and detail.

Address the Counterargument

Right now, the essay does not acknowledge any opposing view. A stronger essay would briefly address why the university might currently *not* require English proficiency tests for PhD-holding professors (eg, valuing research contributions, respecting international academic credentials, avoiding legal/discrimination concerns) before refuting or complicating that view. This will make your argument more persuasive and show critical engagement with the issue, which is expected in academic argumentative writing.

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively restates your solution, but it repeats ideas from the previous paragraph almost verbatim. Consider using the conclusion to briefly acknowledge the complexity of the issue (eg, balancing international faculty diversity with student needs) before reaffirming your recommendation.

Summary of Priority Revisions
Proofread for typographical errors (highest priority—these currently distract from your argument).
Fix pronoun consistency (he/she/they).
Condense the opening anecdote.
State thesis explicitly at the end of the introduction.
Add a brief counterargument and rebuttal.
Provide more specific evidence for the TA/professor policy comparison.

You have a clear, defensible argument and a real command of complex sentence structures. With careful editing for mechanics and tightening for concision, this essay will be significantly stronger.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Business is Business"

Overall Impression
You've chosen a compelling, emotionally resonant topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. The essay has real potential, but it needs work in several areas: thesis clarity, organization, evidence handling, and language accuracy. Below are specific suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Argument
Your essay raises important questions ("Is business just business or do fans, tradition, and loyalty count?") but never commits to a clear, arguable thesis statement. Readers finish the essay agreeing that Modell behaved badly, but they don't know *what larger claim* you want them to accept—for example: "NFL owners exploit publicly funded stadiums while treating fan loyalty as irrelevant, and this pattern reveals a need for policy reform in team relocation rules." State this thesis explicitly in your introduction, and let every paragraph work to support it.

Currently, your essay reads more like a chronicle of grievances than an argument. Consider: what do you want readers to *do* or *believe* differently after reading this? A stronger conclusion should return to this thesis and perhaps call for a specific remedy (eg, NFL relocation restrictions, fan ownership models, legal protections for taxpayer investment).

Organization
The essay jumps around chronologically and topically—between other team relocations, Cleveland's civic improvements, Modell's decision, and the specific financial deal. Consider reorganizing with clear paragraph functions:
Intro: hook + thesis
Context: brief mention of the broader trend (Colts, Raiders, Rams, Oilers) to show this is a pattern, not an isolated case
Cleveland's investment: the city's efforts and civic revival (Gateway Project, Rock and Roll Hall of Fame)
The betrayal: Modell's promises versus his actions, the financial deal
Counterargument and rebuttal: address the "Cleveland's own fault" argument more seriously before dismissing it
Conclusion: return to thesis, suggest broader implications

Right now the counterargument (that Cleveland brought this on itself) appears briefly and is dismissed without real engagement. Strengthening this section will make your argument more persuasive and show you've considered multiple sides.

Evidence and Sources
You use strong factual details (dollar figures, percentages, dates), which is good. However:
Cite your sources. Where do these figures and quotes come from (newspaper, book, interview)? Academic writing requires attribution—use in-text citations and a works cited page.
Double-check facts. The Rams moved from *Anaheim* to *St. Louis* (not "Rains"), and the Houston team became the Tennessee *Oilers*, then later the *Titans*—not the "Cherokees." Errors like these undermine your credibility.
The Bob Costas quote needs a citation (when/where did he say this?).

Tone and Word Choice
Some phrases are too informal or inflammatory for academic argument: "gave Cleveland the one finger salute," "suck it up and take it like a man," "Arthur B. Modell is a liar." Strong claims need strong evidence and measured language. Instead of calling him a liar outright, let the *contradiction* between his quotes speak for itself: "Modell's 1995 statement, 'I will never take the team out of Cleveland,' stands in direct contrast to his later explanation, 'Business is Business'—a shift that suggests..." This is more persuasive than name-calling.

Grammar and Mechanics
There are frequent errors that need correcting in revision:
Apostrophes: "it's" should be "its" (possessive) in multiple places; "in it's own" → "on its own."
Comma splices/run-ons: "The city of Cleveland has been known as a dump city for over two decades! Yet, they never really cared..." needs restructuring.
Spelling/typos: "inwhich" (should be "in which"), "rapid" (likely meant "rabid"), "consciously" (likely meant "unconsciously" or you mean something else), "novel" (unusual word choice for "new").
Quotation punctuation: "Bob Costas of NBC's sports called it," The Renaissance of Cleveland."—the comma and quotation marks are misplaced. It should read: Bob Costas of NBC Sports called it "The Renaissance of Cleveland."
Subject-verb agreement: "The city...will be consciously devastated" — check subject/verb clarity throughout.
Numbers: Be consistent—write out numbers or use numerals consistently (eg, "70,000" not "70 ,000" with a stray space).

Suggested Next Steps
Draft a one-sentence thesis and revise your intro around it.
Reorganize paragraphs using the structure suggested above.
Find and cite 3–4 real sources (newspaper archives from 1995–96 would work well).
Do a careful proofreading pass focusing on apostrophes, run-ons, and factual accuracy.
Revise emotionally charged phrases into more analytical language.

You have a genuinely interesting case study here—with revision, this could become a compelling piece of sports/business criticism. Keep developing it!

Overall Impression

Your central claim—that people suffer when they let others dictate life-defining decisions—doesn't fully crystallize until fairly late in the essay. Right now, the introduction raises the *problem* well but doesn't clearly forecast your *argument* (that a middle path chosen by one's own heart is preferable to either extreme).

Also watch for statements that undercut your own argument. For example, you write "most men helped around the house," which weakens the point you're making about unequal burdens. If this is not central to your argument, consider cutting it; if it is relevant, develop it rather than leaving it as an unsupported aside.

Evidence and Citation

There are two places marked with  where quotations are missing. Be sure to insert the actual quoted material from Friedan and from "Ralph's Story," with proper in-text citation (author, page number, or paragraph number depending on your citation style). More broadly, some claims need direct textual support rather than paraphrase or generalization—for instance, "today a woman is almost required to work outside the home in order to make ends meet." Organization

Several paragraphs run long and blend multiple ideas (eg, the paragraph beginning "The alternative, if a woman chooses not to follow the herd..."). "erected of them" → likely meant "expected of them"

Priority Revisions

Insert the missing quotations and citations.

Break up long paragraphs; ensure each has a single focus.

Feedback Report: "Enjoying the Differences"

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely interesting and provocative topic—how society treats gender differences—and you clearly have passion for this subject. Your voice comes through strongly, and you're not afraid to make bold claims. However, the essay needs significant revision in three areas: clarifying your central argument, strengthening the logic connecting your examples, and correcting language errors that currently distract from your ideas.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction promises an essay about "enjoying" differences between men and women, but the essay actually pursues several loosely related arguments: sports funding, entertainment/objectification, workplace discrimination (Hooters), and athletic competition. These are all interesting topics, but they don't clearly support one unified thesis. Ask yourself: What is the ONE claim I want readers to accept by the end? Right now it could be any of these:
Society should stop treating displays of gendered attraction as inherently negative
Legal equality doesn't require erasing physical/social differences
Not all unequal treatment is discrimination

You need to choose your strongest, most defensible claim and organize everything around it.

A significant logical problem: Your football/volleyball example actually seems to *undermine* your thesis rather than support it. You describe how sex-based fairness concerns prevented a "obvious" solution (giving money to the more profitable program) and call the compromise "communism" in a negative tone. This example illustrates a *tension* between fairness and difference—it doesn't show that "enjoying differences" leads to harmony. Reconsider whether this example belongs in this essay.

Development of Ideas

Several key comparisons need more support or more careful framing:

Barkley/Madonna: Your claim that Barkley "brings an aura of violence" is unsupported and risks alienating readers rather than persuading them. Explain what work ethic or "natural talent" you mean when comparing an athlete's skill to a model's nude photography—many readers will see these as fundamentally different (skill vs. appearance), and you need to address that difference directly.

Strip clubs: This section makes bold claims ("no actual intercourse," "little touching") without acknowledging common counterarguments about exploitation, power imbalances, or the different social risks men and women may face in these environments. A stronger essay would anticipate objections and respond to them, rather than simply asserting the positive view.

Hooters: You dismiss the discrimination lawsuits with "This is not discrimination" but don't explain the legal reasoning behind employment discrimination law, or why "customer preference" as a business rationale is (or isn't) legally sufficient. This is a complex legal question—engaging with it more carefully would strengthen your credibility.

Suggestion: For each example, ask "What would someone who disagrees with me say?" and address that objection directly. This will make your argument feel more persuasive and less like a list of assertions.

Organization

Consider restructuring with:
A clearer thesis in your introduction (one or two sentences)
Topic sentences that explicitly link each paragraph back to that thesis
A conclusion that synthesizes your argument rather than simply restating examples

Right now, paragraphs feel like separate essays stitched together. Transitions like "Another problem" or "Globally" tell us we're moving to a new topic, but not *why* this topic supports your argument.

Language and Mechanics

There are frequent spelling and word-choice errors that need correction through careful proofreading (reading aloud helps catch these):

"there" → "their" (multiple instances)
"underlining" → "underlying"
"resent" → "recent"
"tearn" → "team"
"returmed" → "returned"
"hear" → "here"
"since" → "sense"
"warring" → "wearing"
"sexes" → "sexist" (in "nothing nasty or sexes")
"trask" → "trash"
"Calvin Cline" → "Calvin Klein"
"vise versa" → "vice versa"

I'd recommend running your next draft through a grammar-checking tool (like Grammarly) and then reading it aloud slowly—many of these are the kind of errors your ear will catch even if your eye skips over them.

Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis and test each paragraph against it: does this prove my thesis, or is it a tangent?
For your strongest 2-3 examples, add a paragraph addressing the counterargument
Do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on spelling/word confusion

You have real insight here—the challenge now is discipline: choosing your best material and making every paragraph work in service of one clear claim.

Feedback on Your Welfare Reform Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a complex policy issue and done solid research, pulling in multiple sources to support your points. Your thesis is clear, and you follow through on each element you promise (time limits, training, spending cuts, and the AFDC incentive issue). This is a good foundation. However, the essay needs significant revision in three main areas: argumentation/analysis, source integration, and mechanics/citation format. Below are specific suggestions.

Strengthen Your Argument (Not Just Report Information)

Right now, much of the essay reads as a *summary* of what Democrats and Republicans propose, plus supporting facts, rather than *your own argument* for why your specific compromise is the best solution. For an argumentative essay, you need to:

Explain your reasoning, not just state a position and follow it with a quote. For example, after describing Princess Rowell's story, tell the reader explicitly *why* this example proves your point. What does her story demonstrate about training programs that a reader might not immediately see?
Address counterarguments. A stronger essay would acknowledge potential objections (eg, "Critics might argue that cutting AFDC punishes children for their parents' circumstances") and then respond to them. This shows deeper critical thinking and strengthens your credibility.
Explain your "abolishing extra payments for more children" point. You mention this in your thesis, but the essay never clearly returns to it as its own developed section the way the other three points are. Make sure every promise in your thesis gets a matching, well-developed paragraph.

Improve Integration of Quotations

This is the area needing the most attention. Many paragraphs are strings of quotations loosely connected by short transitional phrases. To improve:

Introduce quotes with context. Instead of dropping a quote in, tell the reader who is speaking and why this evidence matters. For example: "According to journalist Ken Silverstein, welfare has become entrenched across generations, noting that 'some welfare case workers are even dealing with third-generation recipients.'" This is more effective than presenting the fact and citation with no lead-in.
Follow quotes with your own analysis. Don't let a quotation be the last word in a paragraph. After each piece of evidence, add a sentence explaining its significance to your argument.
Avoid over-quoting. Several short factual statements (like statistics) could be paraphrased in your own words rather than quoted directly. Save direct quotation for language that is especially powerful, distinctive, or difficult to paraphrase accurately.
Vary sentence structure. Many paragraphs follow the same pattern: claim + quote + citation. Mix this up with paraphrase, synthesis of multiple sources in one sentence, and your own transitional commentary.

Fix Citation and Mechanical Errors

There are several recurring technical issues that need cleanup:

Spelling of source name: "Silverstein" is misspelled as "Sliverstein" in multiple places. Double-check all proper nouns.
Punctuation with quotation marks: You have inconsistent and sometimes incorrect punctuation, such as commas placed outside quotation marks (eg, "second chance or being able to receive welfare.") or stray characters (eg, "199911" should be "1999"). Also, curly/smart quotes are inconsistently rendered as commas in several places ('how,' 'good-bye,' 'thank you,'). Proofread carefully for these OCR-type or typing errors.
In-text citation format: Follow a consistent style (MLA, given your parenthetical citations). Page ranges and citation placement should be standardized — eg, "(Silverstein 13-15)" is correct, but ensure every quote has this pattern.
Capitalization and typos: "Princess Rowell" (check spelling against source), "highschool" should be "high school," "Project match" should likely be capitalized as "Project Match."
Run-on and comma-spliced sentences: Some sentences merge independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, "recipients, benefits" should be "recipients' benefits" — check for missing apostrophes throughout, as this is a recurring error).

Structural Suggestions

Your conclusion is strong in tone but could be more substantive. Rather than simply restating that compromise is possible, briefly synthesize *how* your four proposed solutions work together as a unified policy, and note one concrete outcome you expect if implemented.
Consider adding a topic sentence at the start of each body paragraph that states the paragraph's main claim clearly, before moving into evidence.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Add your own analysis after each piece of evidence — don't let quotes speak for themselves.
Fully develop the fourth part of your thesis (ending extra payments for more children).
Proofread carefully for spelling (especially "Silverstein"), apostrophes, and punctuation with quotations.
Consider addressing at least one counterargument to strengthen your credibility.

You have good source material and a clear structure — now it's time to make your own voice and reasoning more prominent. Good work so far!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: College Curfews and Overnight Visitation

Overall Impression

You've chosen a topic you clearly care about, and your personal experiences—especially the story about your cousin visiting before his deployment to Germany—give the essay emotional weight. This kind of concrete, lived experience is exactly what strong argumentative writing needs. However, the essay currently reads more like a series of frustrated observations than a structured argument. Below are suggestions to help you sharpen your claim and strengthen your case.

Clarify Your Central Argument

Right now, your essay argues for several different (and sometimes conflicting) things:
Overnight visitation for romantic partners
Overnight visitation for relatives
Elimination of curfew entirely
Curfew only on weekends

Pick one clear, focused claim and build your entire essay around it. For example: *"Universities should eliminate curfews in single-sex dormitories because students are legal adults capable of managing their own time and safety."* Every paragraph should then support this single thesis. Right now, when you say "not saying curfew should be lowered, but we shouldn't have one" and then later suggest "at least have overnight visitation on the weekends," you undermine your own position. Decide what you're truly asking for, and stay consistent.

Watch for Arguments That Backfire

Your story about trying to study late at night—when one girl was "talking so loud" and another was "playing the piano at 2 a.m."—actually seems to support the *need* for rules, since you describe this behavior as "rude" and disruptive to sleeping residents. If you use this anecdote, make sure you explain clearly how it connects to your argument (perhaps: the problem isn't curfew itself, but poor enforcement, or noise policies could exist separately from time restrictions). As it stands, this paragraph works against you rather than for you.

Strengthen the Logical Structure

Consider organizing your essay around 3–4 clear reasons, each in its own paragraph with a clear topic sentence:
Students are legal adults and should be trusted with responsibility (the "maturity" argument)
Family members deserve access, especially in special circumstances (your cousin's story)
The current system doesn't work anyway (your friend at Bates House signing in guests)
Curfews may create *more* danger, not less (students walking alone at 2 a.m.)

Right now, these ideas are present, but scattered across paragraphs without clear signaling. Add topic sentences that state the point of each paragraph before diving into your example.

Address the Counterargument

A more persuasive essay would acknowledge the university's likely reasoning (safety concerns, avoiding harassment complaints, community standards) and then explain why your solution still makes sense despite these concerns. For instance, you make a compelling point that curfews may force students to walk alone at night, which is *more* dangerous—this is a great counter to the "safety" justification for curfews. Develop this point further; it's one of your strongest arguments.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for in revision:

Pronoun confusion: "he" and "be" are confused several times (eg, "he treated," "he bad to go"). Proofread carefully for correct pronoun/verb spelling.
Sentence fragments: Several sentences are incomplete or unclear, such as "I guess if I were a resident of one of the 'special' dorms A could, co-ed." Try rewriting this sentence completely—the meaning is unclear even after several readings.
Comma splices and run-ons: Many sentences string together independent clauses without proper punctuation (eg, "We're not at home we don't have certain luxuries anymore like a car"). Use periods, semicolons, or coordinating conjunctions to separate complete thoughts.
Capitalization: Watch for inconsistent capitalization mid-sentence ("Since we are In college," "Is dangerous").
Word choice: "Chill out" and "he bad to go" are casual—make sure your tone matches an academic argumentative essay throughout.

Revision Priorities

For your next draft, I recommend:
Write one clear thesis sentence and place it at the end of your introduction.
Cut or revise the piano/noise anecdote so it clearly supports your argument.
Reorganize your body paragraphs around distinct, clearly-stated reasons.
Add a brief counterargument paragraph.
Proofread carefully for pronoun errors, run-ons, and capitalization.

You have real conviction and genuine examples—now it's about giving them a clear, logical frame. I'd be happy to look at your revised draft once you've reworked the structure.

Feedback Report

Thank you for sharing this heartfelt essay about the importance of recognizing teachers. Your passion for this topic is evident, especially through your personal experiences with your high school teacher. Below is feedback organized by key areas to help you strengthen this essay in revision.

Thesis and Organization

Your essay would benefit from a clearer, more focused thesis statement early on. Currently, you introduce multiple related but distinct claims: (1) teachers deserve verbal/emotional recognition, (2) teachers deserve better pay, and (3) society should respect teachers like doctors. Each of these could be a strong essay on its own, but combined without clear transitions, the argument feels scattered. Consider choosing one central claim and using the others as supporting points, or explicitly signal in your introduction that you will address multiple dimensions of "reward" (emotional, material, financial, social status).

Additionally, several paragraphs introduce new sub-topics without clear connections to what precedes them (eg, the shift to teacher pay in paragraph 6, then to the doctor comparison in paragraph 8). Adding transitional sentences at the start of each paragraph would help readers follow your logical progression.

Development of Ideas

Your personal anecdotes are the strongest part of this essay—they're vivid and persuasive. However, several claims need more support:

"Students in high school seem to be the hardest to deal with" is stated twice but never explained. Why are high school students harder to teach than younger children?
"Teachers seem to have a mother instinct" is a generalization that could be nuanced. Not all teachers are maternal, and this framing may inadvertently exclude male teachers or reduce teaching to an innate trait rather than a skill.
The claim that doctors and teachers deserve equal respect is compelling but needs more development. Right now, it's asserted rather than argued. What specific evidence supports this comparison beyond the parallel wording ("save our physical lives" / "academic lives")?

Consider adding at least one concrete example or statistic (eg, teacher salary data, turnover rates) to support your economic argument in paragraphs 6-7.

Paragraph Focus

Some paragraphs contain a rhetorical question that seems to conclude the paragraph but then isn't answered until much later. For example, "So why are teachers underpaid?" appears in paragraph 7, but you don't answer it clearly until the following paragraph. Try to keep the question and its answer within the same paragraph, or use the question purely as a transition device.

Language and Grammar

There are several recurring grammar issues to address:

Sentence fragments: "A material gift or a kind word of expression" is not a complete sentence. Consider: "The reward can be a material gift or simply a kind word of expression."

Pronoun-antecedent agreement: "help him with something" should likely be "help them" for gender neutrality, especially since you're discussing a female teacher.

Missing words/typos: "It teachers that have helped made these figures" is unclear—likely you mean "It is teachers who have helped make these figures [successful]."

The placeholder "" in paragraph 5 needs to be filled in with actual words you said to your teacher—this is a missed opportunity for a powerful, specific example.

Comma splices: Several sentences join two independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "These teachers really care and try to work with their students" — check spacing around "and."). Review comma splice rules.

Word choice: "Get their flowers while they live" is a nice idiomatic expression, but make sure your audience will understand it, or briefly explain the metaphor.

Conclusion

Your ending is engaging and returns nicely to the personal tone from your introduction. Consider strengthening it by explicitly restating your main argument(s) before the final personal reflection, so readers leave with a clear sense of your central claim.

Priority Revisions

For your next draft, focus on:
Narrowing or clearly organizing your thesis around 2-3 main claims
Adding transitions between paragraphs
Filling in the placeholder quote
Proofreading for sentence fragments and pronoun agreement
Developing your "teachers vs. doctors" comparison with more concrete evidence

Your genuine appreciation for teachers shines through this essay. With tighter organization and more developed evidence, this could be a very persuasive piece. Keep up the good work!

Feedback Report: Television Violence Essay

Overall Impression

You've gathered relevant research on a genuinely important topic, and your sources (Hepburn, Stern, Jensen) provide useful factual grounding. However, the essay's biggest challenge right now is argumentative clarity: it's not clear what position you are taking. Revising this will require rethinking your central claim before polishing sentences.

Establish a Clear, Consistent Thesis

Your introduction suggests television violence is a "serious concern" that may cause children to "become violent." This implies you're against unregulated TV violence. But later, you write:

> "Some television shows show violence because it appeals to adult viewers. If a certain plot involves a murder, then the murder must happen... that is up to whomever is writing the television show."

This paragraph seems to defend creative freedom and downplay the problem — which contradicts your opening concern. Similarly, your conclusion states the cause of violence is "to add texture to the show and not to poison children's minds," which again shifts toward defending TV violence, while your final sentence ("that child will see violence and they will have to deal with it") suggests a kind of resignation rather than a clear stance.

Revision strategy: Choose one position and defend it consistently:
Option A: TV violence harms children and *some* regulation (like the V-chip or Congressional bills) is justified.
Option B: TV violence is a normal part of storytelling for adult audiences, and *parental responsibility*—not government regulation—is the real solution.

Right now the essay tries to do both, which confuses readers. Decide on your claim, then make sure every paragraph supports it.

Improve Paragraph Organization

Currently, paragraphs read like separate summaries of different sources rather than building one argument. For example:
Paragraph 2 explains the V-chip and Senate bills.
Paragraph 3 lists proposals and the NAB's objection.
Paragraph 4 pivots to defending violent content for adults.
Paragraph 5 returns to describing violent cartoons.
Paragraph 6 offers a conclusion that introduces a new idea (violence as "texture").

Suggestion: Organize by argument, not by source. For instance:
Introduction with a clear thesis.
Evidence that TV violence is widespread and reaches children (cartoons, prime-time dramas).
Regulatory responses (V-chip, Senate bills) and their strengths/weaknesses.
Counterargument (creative freedom, NAB's objection, adult content) and your response to it.
Conclusion that restates your thesis and suggests a resolution (eg, parental oversight, stronger content warnings, etc)

Strengthen Use of Evidence

You cite good sources, but mostly through summary rather than analysis. For example, you mention the Senate Commerce Committee's bills and the NAB's criticism, but you never state *your own evaluation* of whether the bills are reasonable or the NAB's objection is valid. Ask yourself: Do you agree with NAB's claim about "unprecedented federal censorship"? Why or why not? Adding your own analysis after each piece of evidence will make the essay feel like an argument, not a report.

Also, double check factual details — you write "1995-1995 season," which should likely be "1994-1995" or "1995-1996."

Address the Counterargument Directly

You raise a strong counterargument in paragraph 4 (creative necessity, adult appeal) but don't respond to it. A strong argumentative essay acknowledges opposing views and then explains why your position still stands. For example: *"While some argue that violent content is necessary for authentic storytelling, this argument does not account for children's unsupervised access to adult programming."* This kind of sentence would connect your counterargument back to your main claim.

Language and Mechanics

A few specific issues to revise:
"some people disagree with its effects on children" — unclear phrasing. Do you mean people disagree *about* its effects, or disagree *that* it has effects?
"how many parents actually watch television with their teenager?" — this rhetorical question is effective, but it needs a follow-up sentence explaining its significance to your argument.
Punctuation: "sinister combat violence'" is missing an opening quotation mark.
Check subject-verb agreement and article usage throughout (eg, "Television programming in the United States is considered the most violent" — good, but nearby sentences shift between singular/plural pronouns for "child/children").
In-text citations like (Hepburn 1) should be checked against your assigned citation style (APA/MLA) for correct formatting, and a Works Cited/References page should be included.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Decide on one clear thesis and revise all paragraphs to support it.
Reorganize paragraphs around ideas, not sources.
Add analysis after each cited fact — tell readers what it means for your argument.
Directly respond to the counterargument about creative freedom.
Proofread for citation consistency, punctuation, and factual accuracy.

With a clearer argumentative throughline, your strong research will have much more persuasive power. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on "Government Funding for the Arts"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling and debatable topic, and your essay reflects genuine passion for the arts along with a solid framework of reasons (historical/anthropological, communal, economic, therapeutic). This is a strong foundation. However, the essay would benefit from tighter organization, stronger evidence, and more careful sentence-level editing. Below are specific suggestions organized by priority.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis appears in the first paragraph but is somewhat buried in the extended metaphor about "ominous storm clouds." Consider stating your claim more directly and early: *"Government funding for the arts should be maintained or increased because it fosters historical legacy, social cohesion, economic growth, and psychological well-being."* This kind of explicit "roadmap" sentence would help readers track your five main arguments (anthropological/historical, communicative, communal, financial, therapeutic).

Your body paragraphs generally follow a logical progression, but the transitions between them are weak. Phrases like "However, art also helps us..." or "Following from the idea of sharing..." don't clearly signal *why* the next point follows from the previous one. Try transitions that explicitly connect ideas, eg, "Beyond its historical value, art also serves a present-day social function..."

Evidence and Development

This is the area needing the most attention. Several claims are asserted without support:

"As psychologists and psychoanalysts have told us for years..." — *Which* psychologists? Cite at least one specific study, theorist, or example.
"As researchers have found, arts such as drawing and painting help develop clearer thinking." — Same issue. Vague appeals to unnamed "research" weaken your credibility rather than strengthen it.
The prison arts program is a great example, but you don't name a specific program, location, or outcome (eg, recidivism rates). A single concrete statistic here would be far more persuasive than the general claim.

For a stronger essay, choose 2-3 of your strongest points and develop them with specific, named evidence rather than covering many points superficially. Right now the essay reads as a list of assertions; select your best 3 arguments and dig deeper into each.

The Pornography Paragraph

This paragraph feels like a digression that raises more questions than it answers. Defining art as inherently "positive" and "constructive" is a contestable claim that could undermine your argument (much celebrated art is disturbing, ambiguous, or critical). Unless you plan to address the funding-of-controversial-art debate directly (a legitimate and interesting angle, given real debates over NEA funding), consider cutting this paragraph or integrating it more purposefully—perhaps as a counterargument you rebut with evidence about how public funding decisions are actually made.

Conclusion

Your conclusion restates general ideas ("alternative means of enrichment," "basic needs of the people") but doesn't clearly synthesize your specific arguments or issue a final, memorable call to action. Consider ending with a sentence that names your strongest 2-3 reasons and makes a direct appeal to policymakers or readers.

Language and Sentence-Level Issues

Run-on/comma-spliced sentences: eg, "In an age when government funding cuts loom on the horizon like an ominous storm cloud, it leaves many to think which area of government spending will be cut next." Consider simplifying: "As government funding cuts loom, many wonder which programs will be next."
Awkward phrasing: "Necessarily, it's interesting to note the success..." — "Necessarily" doesn't fit here; consider "Notably" or "For instance."
Pronoun/agreement issues: "man has seen a need for interpreting and depicting his world" — consider gender-neutral language ("humans," "people," "their").
Word choice: "psychosanalysts" should be "psychoanalysts"; "oftentiines" should be "oftentimes."
Repetition: The phrase "communicate" and its variants appear many times in a short span (paragraphs 3-5). Vary your vocabulary.
Informal insertion: "As somewhat of an artist, I myself have found..." — this personal anecdote is a nice touch but feels abrupt; consider setting it off more clearly or expanding it slightly to make it feel intentional rather than inserted.

Priority Revisions

Sharpen your thesis statement and add a roadmap sentence.
Replace vague appeals to "research" and "psychologists" with specific citations or examples.
Cut or reframe the pornography paragraph.
Strengthen the conclusion by synthesizing your best arguments.
Proofread for spelling, run-ons, and word choice.

You have real insight and enthusiasm here—focus your revision on specificity and economy: fewer claims, better supported, will make a more persuasive essay than many claims loosely asserted. Nice work overall; this has strong potential with focused revision.

However, the essay needs significant revision in terms of structure, argumentation, and focus. Below are the main areas to work on.

Thesis and Structural Organization

Your introduction lists three reasons for a salary cap:
Players earn more than college graduates
Teams should spend money on facilities
Salary caps would stop players from negotiating high salaries

However, the body paragraphs don't follow this structure clearly. Instead, you spend most of the essay comparing football players to doctors (a point not mentioned in your thesis), then discuss "greediness" and O.J. Simpson, then facilities, then salary negotiation. Revise your introduction to match your actual argument, or reorganize your body paragraphs to match your stated thesis. Each paragraph should have one clear focus that a reader can predict from your topic sentence.

Logical Consistency Between Comparison and Solution

This is the most important issue to address. Much of your essay argues that football players are *overpaid relative to their social value* (compared to doctors, charity work, etc). But your proposed solution—a salary cap—doesn't send that money to doctors or charities. A salary cap would likely keep the money with team owners, not redistribute it to worthier causes. You need to either:
Explain *how* a salary cap connects to your moral argument (eg, "the money saved could be redirected to..."), or
Separate this into two arguments: one about proportional fairness in society, and one about internal NFL economics (facilities, player negotiations).

Right now, readers may feel the moral argument and the economic argument don't fully support each other.

Supporting Evidence and Reasoning

Several claims need stronger support or more careful reasoning:

"A person could not be smart enough to pass the second grade, but as long as he can play ball he is going to be rich." This is an overgeneralization that weakens your credibility. Consider softening this or removing it.
The O.J. Simpson example is a legal case about a criminal trial, not really about salary or greed directly. This connection needs to be explained more carefully, or the example may confuse readers about your point.
The broken seat/no toilet paper anecdote is a vivid personal example, but one experience doesn't prove teams are systematically neglecting facilities. Consider adding a statistic or citing a news report about NFL revenue vs. stadium spending to strengthen this claim.
"Money and power didn't get involved until the 1950's" — this claim needs a source or should be removed if you cannot verify it.

Addressing Counterarguments

You do attempt a counterargument in the Deion Sanders paragraph ("Some people would think this was unfair because the most popular players should make more..."), which is good practice! Try to add at least one more counterargument earlier in the essay—for example, someone might argue that football players deserve high pay due to short careers, high injury risk, or the revenue they generate for the league. Address this directly to make your argument more persuasive and complete.

Language and Mechanics

As a non-native speaker, you're doing well conveying complex ideas, but here are common patterns to review:

Word choice errors: "wee" → "we"; "as ant other team" → "than any other team"; "affective" → "effective"
Comma splices: Many sentences join two independent clauses with only a comma. Example: *"With this attitude it changes their whole outlook on life."* Consider revising to: *"This attitude changes their whole outlook on life."*
Rhetorical questions: You use many rhetorical questions ("Is this really fair?" "Why do they do this?"). This can be an effective technique, but overuse weakens impact. Try cutting some and replacing with direct statements.
Repetition: Phrases like "In many ways" and "Some of the" appear very frequently. Vary your sentence openings.
Names: "Andre Risen" — please double check the correct spelling of this player's name for accuracy.

Priority Revisions for Next Draft

Rewrite the introduction so it accurately previews your argument's structure.
Strengthen the logical link between the doctor/social-value argument and the salary cap solution.
Add at least one counterargument with a direct response.
Proofread for grammar, focusing especially on comma splices and word choice.
Verify or remove unsupported factual claims (the 1950s claim, the "second grade" claim).

Overall, you have a genuine, values-driven argument here. With tighter organization and clearer connections between your evidence and your proposed solution, this essay will be much more persuasive. Keep developing your voice—it's clear you care about this topic!

Feedback Report on "Gun Control" Essay

Overall Impression

You have tackled a complex, debate-heavy topic and show a clear command of the *content*—constitutional background, statistics, legislative history, and counterarguments are all present. This is a real strength. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of researched facts than a tightly argued essay. My feedback focuses on organization, argumentation, citation mechanics, and language, since improving these will make your genuine knowledge much more persuasive.

Thesis and Organization

Your thesis is buried. The opening paragraph actually contains several claims (guns cause avoidable deaths; regulation reduces impulsive crime; the Constitution permits gun control) that could each anchor a separate essay. Right now they are stacked together, which makes it hard for the reader to know which is your central argument.

Suggestion: Decide whether your essay is primarily about (a) whether gun control is constitutional, or (b) whether gun control reduces violence, or (c) a policy recommendation combining both. Right now you argue all three, which is fine for a longer paper, but you need one sentence—your thesis—that ties them together explicitly. For example: *"Because gun regulation is both constitutionally permissible and empirically effective at reducing violence, Congress should adopt stricter federal licensing and background-check laws."* Then organize paragraphs to build toward that claim in order: (1) constitutionality, (2) evidence of harm, (3) evidence that regulation helps, (4) counterarguments, (5) policy recommendation.

Currently the essay jumps between these topics more than once (eg, statistics appear in paragraph 4, disappear, then reappear in paragraph 8). Consolidating each theme into one place will make the logic easier to follow.

Argumentation

Your strongest paragraphs are the ones addressing the counterargument (the "statistics" paragraph and the "slippery slope" paragraph about banning all guns). This is exactly the kind of move a persuasive essay needs—acknowledging the other side and explaining why your position still holds.

However, several claims are asserted rather than argued:
"Law-abiding citizens do not need handguns since very few use them to defend themselves" — this needs evidence or citation; as written it sounds like an opinion.
"Firearms are certainly needed for national defense and law enforcement, but they are not needed by individual citizens" — this is a key claim that deserves more support, since it is central to your constitutional argument.

Suggestion: For each major claim, ask "Where is my evidence?" and make sure a citation or explanation immediately follows. You do this well with the Cozic and Cole sources; extend that habit to every assertion, especially in the concluding paragraphs, which currently rely on rhetorical appeal ("Founding Fathers foresaw...") rather than argument.

Use of Sources and Citation

There are several mechanical problems here:
The name "Cozic" is misspelled multiple times as "Cosec" and "Cosec"—please proofread all citations carefully.
One sentence appears garbled: *"lnl981,JimBrady,W'hiteHousePresssecretarywasshotduring..."* This looks like a formatting/OCR error where spaces were lost. Please check your original draft; it should read something like "In 1981, Jim Brady, White House Press Secretary, was shot during the attempted assassination..."
Quotations are sometimes introduced without a clear signal of whose words they are (eg, "The NRA debates..." — do you mean "argues" or "claims"? "Debates" is not quite the right verb here).
You cite the same sources many times (Cozic, Cole) but rarely vary your reporting verbs. Try alternatives: "argues," "contends," "reports," "concludes," "maintains."

Suggestion: Do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on citations—check spelling of author names, page numbers, and that every quotation has a clear introductory clause.

Language and Mechanics

As a non-native speaker, your control of complex academic vocabulary is impressive, but there are recurring small errors worth fixing:
"backgrounds" should be "background's" or rephrased: "checking a prospective buyer's background."
"prevent identification" should likely be "present identification."
"riffles" → "rifles"; "definatly" → "definitely"; "ant-gun" → "anti-gun."
"The problem with this argument is that it lacks statistical verification" is a strong sentence—use this level of clarity as your model elsewhere.
Watch subject-verb agreement: "More commonly, Guns lead to violence" — capitalization error mid-sentence; should be lowercase "guns."

Suggestion: Read your essay aloud, or use a grammar-checking tool, to catch these small but distracting errors before submission.

Conclusion Paragraph

Your conclusion shifts into a more emotional, rhetorical register ("Founding Fathers foresaw a great nation of peace and tranquility") that doesn't match the analytical tone of the rest of the essay. This can work if used deliberately, but right now it feels like a new argument rather than a synthesis of what came before. Consider revising it to explicitly restate your thesis and briefly summarize your three main supporting points (constitutionality, evidence of harm, effectiveness of regulation) before moving to the call to action.

Summary of Priorities for Revision
Write one clear thesis statement and reorganize paragraphs around it.
Support every assertion with a citation or clear reasoning.
Proofread all citations for accuracy (especially "Cozic").
Fix small grammar/spelling errors throughout.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new rhetorical claims.

You have excellent raw material here—now it's a matter of shaping it into a tighter, more persuasive argument.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay

Thank you for tackling such a challenging and sensitive topic. Your essay shows genuine passion and conviction, which is a great foundation for persuasive writing. Below is feedback organized around key areas for revision.

Overall Structure and Argument

Your essay would benefit from a clearer thesis statement early on. Currently, your position shifts slightly throughout the essay—you argue abortion is generally wrong, then carve out exceptions (rape, health risks), then conclude with a call for government regulation. This is actually a *nuanced and defensible position*, but you need to state it clearly in your introduction so readers know where you're headed. Consider a thesis like: "While I believe abortion is morally wrong in most circumstances, I recognize that cases of rape or serious health risk warrant exceptions, and therefore abortion should be regulated by law rather than left entirely to individual choice."

Logical Consistency

There are some tensions in your reasoning worth addressing:

The "no excuse" argument vs. contraceptive failure rates: You spend a paragraph explaining that even combined contraceptives are not 100% effective, yet conclude that women who become pregnant "have no excuse" for abortion. This undermines your own data. If contraception can fail, then pregnancy can occur despite responsible behavior—which seems to contradict your argument that abortion after consensual sex reflects a failure of responsibility.

The "jail for murder" statement: This is your most extreme claim, and it's not sufficiently supported by argument. If you want to make this claim, you need to address counterarguments (legal precedent, medical necessity cases, etc) rather than stating it and moving on.

Adoption alternative: You raise adoption as a solution but don't develop it. What about the risks and physical/emotional toll of pregnancy itself? Anticipating this counterargument would strengthen your case.

Use of Personal Narrative

Your cousin's story is the most powerful part of the essay—it's vivid, emotionally resonant, and gives your argument real stakes. However, it comes very late (paragraph 6) after all the abstract arguments. Consider moving a shortened version earlier, perhaps in your introduction, to hook readers immediately, then use the abstract arguments to build your case, returning to the fuller story as your climactic example.

Also examine what this story actually proves. It shows the *danger of family coercion* and *unsafe abortion practices*, but you use it to argue for *government-regulated* abortion. Make sure the connection between the story and your policy conclusion is explicit—right now readers must infer it.

Tone and Word Choice

Some language choices undermine the seriousness of your topic:
"if a person decides to play the game" and "horseplay" to describe sex trivializes the subject in a way that feels inconsistent with the gravity of your argument.
"wild maniac" and "vicious sperm" and "fiend" are overly dramatic and reduce the analytical credibility of the rape discussion. Simpler, more restrained language ("the rapist," "the assault") will feel more persuasive to academic readers.
The metaphor "like a bottle ripped from a baby's mouth" is confusing—babies don't typically have bottles ripped from their mouths in a way that clarifies your point about lost life experiences. Consider a clearer comparison.

Grammar and Mechanics

I noticed several recurring errors worth reviewing:
Subject-verb agreement: "persons how are financially unable" (should be "who")
Verb tense consistency: "is she was raped" (should be "if she was raped")
Missing words: "I does think the death of two people is a justified reason to prevent..." (I think this sentence actually means the *opposite* of what you wrote—please re-read this sentence carefully, as it seems to contradict your point in that paragraph)
Run-on sentences: The sentence beginning "For example, is she was raped..." combines several ideas without clear punctuation breaks.
"govermnent" should be "government"

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, or asking someone to read it aloud to you—this often reveals awkward phrasing and missing words that are easy to miss when reading silently.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement outlining your nuanced position (mostly against, with specific exceptions).
Reorganize so your cousin's story appears earlier to hook the reader, and reconnect it explicitly to your final argument about regulation.
Address the counterargument about contraceptive failure more carefully—perhaps distinguish between failure despite correct use versus irresponsible use.
Tone down dramatic language in favor of more measured, academic phrasing.
Proofread carefully for subject-verb agreement and complete sentences.

You have compelling material here, especially your personal story—with revision, this essay can be both more persuasive and more polished. Keep developing your voice; your conviction comes through clearly.

Feedback on Your Essay: Sex in the Media

Thank you for sharing this essay on an engaging and relevant topic. You clearly have strong opinions and real-world examples to support your argument. Below is feedback organized by key areas to help you revise.

Strengths

You do a nice job providing concrete, specific examples (Beverly Hills 90210, R. Kelly, Boyz II Men, Paul Mitchell, Gravity cologne) rather than staying abstract. This makes your argument more persuasive and shows you're observing the media critically. Your passion about the topic also comes through clearly.

Major Areas for Revision

Thesis and Argument Clarity
Your title phrase "overrated" is a bit confusing—do you mean sex is *overused* or *overrepresented* in media, or that its importance is exaggerated? These are different claims. I think you actually mean overused/omnipresent, which then leads to real consequences (teen pregnancy, STDs). Consider revising your thesis to something like: "The media's excessive and unrealistic portrayal of sex—without consequences or responsibility—contributes to risky behavior among teenagers." This gives your essay a clearer throughline connecting your media examples to your conclusion about teen behavior.

Organization
Currently, your essay moves through television → radio → magazines → consequences, but the connection between the first three sections (media examples) and the final sections (teen pregnancy/STDs) needs to be made explicit *before* you get there. Right now, the shift feels sudden. Consider adding a sentence at the end of your introduction that previews this connection, so readers know where you're heading.

Also, the essay would benefit from clearer topic sentences at the start of each paragraph that link back to your thesis, rather than just announcing the next media type ("We also have radio").

Evidence and Reasoning
Your claim that media causes teen pregnancy and STDs is your most important point, but it needs more support. Right now, you assert this connection without evidence (statistics, studies, or logical development). Ask yourself:
Is there research linking media sex portrayals to teen behavior?
Could you address counterarguments (eg, other factors like lack of sex education)?

Even one or two pieces of evidence here would significantly strengthen your argument.

Repetition
Several ideas are repeated almost word-for-word:
"Television and radio are not the only places sex is overrated" / "If sex is in television, radio and magazines..."
"You can't wonder why so many teenagers..." (used twice with similar content)

Revise to avoid redundancy—each repetition should add new information or move the argument forward, not restate what's already been said.

Missing Content
You have a placeholder  where the Gravity cologne slogan should go—remember to fill this in with actual evidence.

Language and Mechanics

Since you're writing in a second language, here are some patterns to watch for:

Article usage: "Sex Is used to do everything" → check capitalization and article use throughout (eg, "the media" vs. "media")
Verb tense/agreement: "bluntly saids" → should be "says" (not saying the "s" twice)
Word choice: "soak and wet" → "soaked and wet" or simply "wet"; "kissing on a woman" → "kissing a woman" (no "on" needed in standard English)
Sentence fragments: Some sentences function more as fragments for emphasis ("Nothing but a sheet.") — this can work stylistically, but use sparingly and intentionally
Comma splices: "I understand that 'sex sells', but why can't we have more decent ads" — check punctuation between independent clauses
Consistency: You switch between "media is" and treating media as plural at times; "media" can be tricky (technically plural of "medium," but often treated as singular in casual English)

I'd recommend reading your essay aloud, which often helps catch grammar issues and awkward phrasing naturally.

Suggestions for Revision

Sharpen your thesis to explicitly connect media portrayal with real consequences.
Add a paragraph or transition that logically bridges your media examples with the teen pregnancy/STD discussion.
Find one or two outside sources (even general statistics) to support the causal claim.
Trim repetitive sentences and use that space for deeper analysis of your existing examples—why does showing a half-dressed model sell shampoo? What does this suggest about societal values?
Proofread for grammar using the patterns noted above.

Final Thought

You have a compelling argument and good instincts for supporting claims with real examples. With clearer organization, stronger evidence for your causal claims, and some polishing of language mechanics, this essay will be much more persuasive. Keep developing your critical voice—it's a real strength here!
