Feedback on "The Psychology of Fashion" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay explores an interesting topic—the psychological dimensions of clothing choices—and you've clearly done research, incorporating multiple sources (Ryan, Flugel, Davis, Herrick, Hollingworth). However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of loosely related facts and quotations than a unified argument. To strengthen this piece, you need a clearer thesis, better organization, and more synthesis of your sources into your own analytical voice.

Thesis and Argument

Your introduction suggests the essay will argue that clothing reveals character or mood ("What a person wears can be a good indicator of how that person feels"). However, the body paragraphs drift through many different, only loosely connected claims: fashion identity instability, self-concept, adolescent peer pressure, consumer motivation, elderly fashion needs, and modesty. 

Revision suggestion: Decide on one central argument. For example: "Clothing choices are shaped by psychological needs for social acceptance and self-expression, and these needs change across the lifespan." Then organize each paragraph to support *that* specific claim, cutting material that doesn't fit.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

Right now, the essay moves topic to topic (fashion designers → poetry → self-concept → adolescents → consumers → elderly → modesty) without clear transitions or a logical progression. Each paragraph needs:
A clear topic sentence stating its main point
Evidence/support
A concluding sentence that connects back to your thesis

Suggestion: After drafting, outline your paragraphs by writing just the topic sentence of each. Ask yourself: "Does this list of sentences read like a logical argument?" If not, reorder or revise.

Use of the Herrick Poem

The Herrick poem is included but never analyzed. Quoting a 17th-century poem without explaining its relevance leaves readers confused about why it's there. 

Revision suggestion: Either explain specifically how "Delight in Disorder" illustrates your point about fashion instability/expression (eg, discuss what "sweet disorder" suggests about calculated carelessness in dress), or cut it if it doesn't serve your argument directly.

Integration of Sources

You rely heavily on paraphrasing and quoting Ryan, but much of this material simply summarizes her findings rather than engaging with them critically. Ask yourself: Do you agree with Ryan? Does her research have limitations? How does it connect to your own observations?

Your personal example about conforming to fit in socially is a good instinct—you should include more original analysis and examples like this throughout, not just in one paragraph. This is where your own voice can emerge.

The Modesty Section (Flugel)

This paragraph feels disconnected from the rest of the essay. You even admit "a full description...would have to be devoted to an entire paper," which signals that this material may not belong here. Consider either integrating modesty more explicitly into your broader argument (eg, how modesty relates to self-concept or social approval, themes you raise earlier) or removing it.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring errors to address in revision:

Subject-verb agreement: "What a person wears can be a good indicator on how that person feel" → *feels*. "If she wears a mini-skirt, she probably feel sexy" → *feels*.
Word choice: "fashion designers feed" is unclear—do you mean "capitalize on this" or "exploit this"? "fell self-pity" should be *feel*.
Capitalization: "one doesn't know" should begin with a capital letter after the period.
Citation consistency: You cite page numbers alone for Ryan after the first mention (eg, "(84)," "(85)")—make sure this follows your required citation style consistently, and double-check you're not missing citations for paraphrased material (eg, the Hollingworth quote needs a full citation the first time).

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces new ideas ("the thrill of getting a new outfit") rather than synthesizing your argument. A strong conclusion should return to your thesis and show how the evidence you presented supports it, not add fresh material.

Priority Revisions

Write a clear, specific thesis statement and revise body paragraphs to support it directly.
Cut or better integrate the Herrick poem and the modesty section.
Add more analysis in your own voice—don't just summarize sources; interpret and connect them.
Proofread carefully for subject-verb agreement and word choice.
Revise the conclusion to synthesize rather than introduce new points.

With a tighter focus and more of your own critical voice, this essay has the potential to make a compelling argument about the psychology of fashion. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay on Abortion

Thank you for tackling such a challenging and sensitive topic. You clearly have a position and some good instincts about counterargument, which is essential in argumentative writing. Below is feedback organized by area, along with suggestions for revision.

Thesis and Focus

Your thesis—that the decision should belong entirely to the woman—is clear enough, but it appears somewhat late and is diluted by surrounding claims. Consider stating it explicitly and early, perhaps at the end of your introduction: "This essay argues that the decision to have an abortion should rest solely with the woman involved, because she alone must live with the consequences." Right now, the introduction spends time on background (how long the debate has existed, the two "camps") before arriving at your actual claim, which weakens its impact.

Also, double-check your facts: abortion has been debated for far longer than "over two decades"—this claim undermines your credibility. Consider removing specific time claims unless you can support them with a citation.

Structure and Paragraphing

The essay would benefit from clearer organization. Right now, ideas are somewhat mixed within paragraphs. Consider this structure:

Introduction – background + clear thesis
Body paragraph 1 – refute the "even in rape cases, abortion is wrong" argument
Body paragraph 2 – refute the "genocide/Nazi" argument
Body paragraph 3 – your positive argument for why the choice belongs to the woman (psychological harm to unwanted children, unsafe self-induced abortions)
Conclusion – restate thesis, summarize, perhaps end with a broader reflection

Currently, your conclusion repeats ideas from the introduction almost verbatim ("the decision to have an abortion should be left entirely up to the woman"). Instead, use the conclusion to synthesize your argument and leave the reader with a final thought, rather than restating the same sentence.

Argumentation and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention:

The rape argument paragraph is confusing and appears to have a missing sentence. You write: "some anti-abortionist, think that even woman women who have abortions. Rape is something that the woman has no control over..." This is grammatically broken and the logic doesn't connect. It seems you meant to say something like: "Even in cases of rape, some anti-abortionists believe a woman should not be allowed to terminate the pregnancy." Please revise this sentence carefully—right now the reader cannot follow your point.

The "Slippery Nazi Slope" section is your strongest because you define a key term (genocide) and use that definition to dismantle the opposing argument. This is good analytical work—keep doing this elsewhere. Consider naming this more accurately as "the genocide argument" or "reductio ad Hitlerum," since "Slippery Nazi Slope" is not a standard term (you may be conflating "slippery slope" with a Nazi comparison).

Strengthen with evidence. Right now, your claims (eg, "there would be higher death rates from self-induced abortions," "every unwanted child is psychologically damaged for life") are asserted without support. Even brief references to statistics or studies (properly cited) would make these claims far more persuasive. As it stands, these read as opinions rather than argued conclusions.

Acknowledge the opposing view more fairly. Right now, you characterize pro-life arguments as "not very substantial" without fully representing their strongest points. A stronger essay briefly presents the opposing argument in its best form before refuting it—this makes your rebuttal more convincing, since readers see you are not attacking a weak version of the argument (a "straw man").

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues to address in revision:

Subject-verb agreement and word choice: "this categories" → "these categories"; "there tendencies" → "their tendencies"; "who really obtains" → "who really has."
Comma splices: "These debates began over two decades ago, they have done nothing..." should be split into two sentences or joined with a semicolon/conjunction.
Spacing and punctuation errors: "pro-choiceor pro-life," "genocide, it is obvious" and "unwanted children,he" need correction—reread carefully for stray spaces and missing punctuation.
Pronoun consistency: You switch between "woman" (singular) and "they/their" (plural) when referring to the same person. Choose one consistently: "a woman... she" or "women... they."
Word confusion: "proffers" appears to be a typo—perhaps you meant "pro-lifers"?

I recommend reading your essay aloud, sentence by sentence, to catch these errors, or asking a writing center tutor to review the revised draft with you.

Overall

Your essay has the seeds of a solid argument, particularly in the genocide-argument paragraph, which shows real analytical skill. To strengthen the whole essay, focus on: (1) clarifying and tightening your thesis, (2) fixing the confusing rape-argument paragraph, (3) adding evidence to support your claims, and (4) careful proofreading for grammar. I'd be happy to look at a revised draft.

Feedback Report on Argumentative Essay: Children and Television Content

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an important and relevant topic—the exposure of young children to inappropriate television content—and you clearly care about this issue, drawing on personal experience as a parent to strengthen your argument. This personal investment gives your writing authenticity. However, the essay would benefit significantly from revision in the areas of organization, thesis clarity, evidence, and grammar/mechanics. Below are specific suggestions to help you strengthen the piece.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your essay currently reads more like a collection of related observations than a tightly argued essay. Ask yourself: what is the single claim you want readers to accept by the end? Is it that:
Parents need to supervise more closely, or
Television networks should air more age-appropriate programming during after-school hours?

Your conclusion suggests the second, but much of your essay's body focuses on the first (parental distraction, lack of education). Choose one primary claim and make it explicit in your introduction. Right now, the thesis is buried and the essay drifts between blaming parents, blaming networks, and describing the problem. A clear thesis statement early on—something like "Because young children lack the maturity to critically process adult content, television networks should provide more age-appropriate programming during after-school hours"—would give your essay a backbone.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

Your paragraphs are very long and often combine multiple ideas without clear transitions. For example, the second paragraph moves from statistics about programming percentages, to a schedule of shows, to psychological effects on children, to a specific example about pregnancy—all in one block. Break these into separate paragraphs, each with one controlling idea:
One paragraph on the scarcity of children's programming (with your statistics)
One paragraph on the specific example of the after-school schedule
One paragraph on the psychological/developmental argument (children as imitators)

This will make your argument easier to follow and each point easier to develop fully.

Evidence and Statistics

Your claim that "98% of programs are not for children" is compelling, but you need a source. Where did this number come from? Without a citation, readers may doubt its accuracy. The same applies to claims about when sexuality is taught in schools ("eighth grade")—cite your source or acknowledge it as a general observation rather than a fact.

Your personal anecdotes (the four-year-old and the kissing couple, not allowing "The Simpsons") are your strongest evidence because they are specific and vivid. Consider expanding these rather than relying so heavily on generalized claims about "children" as a whole. Academic essays are more persuasive when they balance personal observation with researched evidence.

Logical Development

Some causal claims need more support. For instance, you assert that watching Ricki Lake will lead an eight-year-old to think "it's all right to have sex and get pregnant at an early age." This is a significant leap—you might strengthen it by referencing child development research or expert opinion on media influence, rather than presenting it as self-evident.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues to address in revision:
Sentence fragments and run-ons: eg, "Only adults do not usually show sexual relations..." reads awkwardly; consider rephrasing as "However, adults do not usually display sexual relations..."
Subject-verb agreement: "make the children to want to experiment" should be "make children want to experiment" (remove "to").
Punctuation errors: Watch for stray periods and commas (eg, "sex,, violence," "the the children"). Careful proofreading will catch these.
Quotation marks: You use "11" instead of quotation marks in a few places (likely a formatting/typing error)—check your document for these artifacts.
Verb tense consistency: You shift between present and past tense; try to maintain present tense for general claims and past tense only for specific anecdotes.
Capitalization: "Opra" should be "Oprah"; check other proper nouns.

Tone and Register

Some phrases are conversational ("but yet," "that's not always a problem") and would benefit from more formal academic phrasing ("However," "This does not always present a problem"). Additionally, avoid absolute claims like "every child is different" without connecting it back to your argument—this sentence currently feels like a stray thought rather than a supporting point.

Suggested Next Steps

Draft a clear one-sentence thesis and revise your introduction around it.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each contains one main idea.
Find at least one credible source for your statistics.
Proofread carefully for grammar, especially subject-verb agreement and punctuation.
Strengthen the connection between your personal anecdotes and your broader claims.

You have a strong, personally meaningful argument here—with revision for structure and clarity, this essay can be much more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay demonstrates genuine enthusiasm for the topic and impressive research effort—you've gathered substantial evidence about recycling different materials. This breadth of information is a strength. However, the essay currently reads more like an informational report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need to sharpen your thesis, improve organization, and address citation/source issues. Below are specific areas for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction states that "recycling needs to become commonplace uniformly," which is a reasonable claim, but the essay body doesn't consistently argue *why* this must happen or address counterarguments. Instead, it catalogs facts about various materials (plastic, glass, batteries, oil, etc) without building a persistent argument.

Suggestion: Decide what you are actually arguing. Are you claiming that individuals must change behavior? That policy should mandate recycling? That specific materials are more urgent than others? A stronger thesis might be: "Because landfill space is disappearing rapidly and recyclable materials pose environmental hazards when discarded, individuals must adopt specific recycling practices for each type of waste." Each paragraph should then connect back to this claim, rather than simply presenting facts.

Organization and Paragraph Structure

The essay moves through categories of recyclables (plastic, paper, glass, refrigerators, batteries, oil, cartridges, toxics) in a list-like fashion. This creates a "catalog" structure rather than a persuasive one.

Suggestion: Consider grouping your points around 2-3 main arguments rather than by material type. For example:
Argument 1: Recycling prevents environmental/health hazards (use refrigerant, batteries, motor oil, toxics as evidence)
Argument 2: Recycling conserves resources and landfill space (use glass, paper, aluminum statistics)
Argument 3: Recycling is easy and should become habitual (use your bag/battery/cartridge examples)

This would transform your list of facts into supporting evidence for a real argument.

The Plastic vs. Paper Bag Section

This section is confusing and undermines your credibility. You cite the Superbag Corporation—a company that *sells* plastic bags—as objective proof that "plastic bags are much better for the environment than paper bags." This is a biased source making a self-interested claim, and using it uncritically weakens your argument.

Suggestion: Either find a neutral source to support this claim, or acknowledge the source's potential bias and explain why you still find the argument compelling. Better yet, since your essay's broader point is about recycling *all* materials, you might simplify this section rather than getting drawn into the plastic-vs-paper debate, which somewhat distracts from your main argument.

Citation and Source Integration

You cite "Recycler's Bill of Rights" and "One Earth" frequently, but:
The long block quote from "Superbag" is inserted without a clear introduction or explanation of who wrote it or why we should trust it.
Citations like "(Recycler's 1)" and "(One Earth 1)" are inconsistent in format—make sure you're following a consistent citation style (MLA, APA, etc) as required by your assignment.
Quotations are sometimes dropped in without being integrated into your own sentences. Try using signal phrases: "According to the *Recycler's Bill of Rights*, most plastic can be recycled..."

Suggestion: Review your citation format with your instructor's guidelines and ensure every source is properly introduced, cited, and explained in your own words rather than just quoted.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several errors that need correction:
"fun" should be "full" ("eighty percent of the existing landfills will be full")
"fight a one hundred watt fight bulb" should be "light a one hundred watt light bulb"
"@" appears to be a typo/formatting error—remove or replace with the intended word
"m" appears instead of "in" multiple times (eg, "used m portable telephones," "regarded m")
"win" should be "will" ("someone win suffer")
Missing periods and inconsistent capitalization in several places
"wildlife than plastic substitutes . . ." has an unclear ellipsis usage; make sure quotations are properly punctuated

Suggestion: Read through the essay slowly, sentence by sentence, or use a grammar-checking tool, then verify each correction makes sense in context. Some of these appear to be typing/OCR errors rather than knowledge gaps, but they still need fixing before submission.

Conclusion

Your conclusion introduces a new comparison (recycling vs. putting gas in the car) that is engaging but appears without earlier setup. Strong conclusions typically synthesize the essay's main arguments rather than introducing new points.

Suggestion: Use the conclusion to briefly restate your central argument and remind readers of the strongest evidence (eg, the landfill statistics), then end with the car analogy as a memorable final image—but make sure it connects to points made earlier in the essay.

Priority Revisions

If you have limited time, focus on these three things first:
Clarify your thesis and ensure each paragraph explicitly supports it.
Fix the Superbag citation issue—either contextualize the bias or remove the block quote.
Proofread carefully for the typos and grammar errors listed above.

You have excellent research here—now it needs a clearer argumentative spine to hold it together. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report: Draft on Pink and Blue as Gendered Colors

Overall Impression

You have chosen an engaging, relatable topic, and your observations about commercials and baby products show good attentiveness to real-world evidence. However, the essay currently reads more as a description of a stereotype than as an *argument* about it. To move this from a list of observations to a persuasive essay, you need a clearer central claim, tighter organization, and deeper analysis of why this pattern matters. Below are suggestions organized by priority.

Develop a Clear Thesis and Argument

Right now, your essay states *that* pink is coded feminine and blue masculine, but it never tells the reader *what you want them to think or do about this*. Argumentative essays need a claim that can be debated. Consider:

Is this stereotype harmful (eg, it limits how children express themselves, reinforces rigid gender roles)?
Is it arbitrary or historically constructed (worth noting: pink was actually associated with boys in the early 20th century)?
Is it so deeply embedded in marketing that companies exploit it for profit?

Choose one angle and state it explicitly in your introduction. For example: "Although pink and blue are treated as natural markers of femininity and masculinity, this color-coding is a culturally constructed stereotype that limits self-expression and is reinforced—rather than reflected—by commercial marketing." This gives every paragraph a clear purpose: to support that claim.

Reorganize Around Ideas, Not Just Examples

Currently the essay moves through examples (commercials → babies → baby products → boys) without strong transitions or a governing logic connecting them. I suggest restructuring by argument function:

Paragraph 1: Introduce the stereotype and your thesis.
Paragraph 2: Present evidence that adults reinforce this coding (commercials for adult products).
Paragraph 3: Present evidence that this coding begins in infancy (baby products, hospital dressing).
Paragraph 4: Discuss the asymmetry you noticed—girls can wear "masculine" colors more easily than boys can wear "feminine" ones. This is actually your most interesting and original point; it deserves its own developed paragraph with analysis, not just a passing mention in the conclusion.
Paragraph 5: Conclusion—explain the significance. Why should readers care about this asymmetry?

Strengthen Analysis, Not Just Description

Many paragraphs list examples but don't explain their significance. For instance, after describing the Secret deodorant commercial, ask: *What does this reveal?* Perhaps: "Even a product marketed on the basis of physical strength ('strong enough for a man') is packaged in pink and blue with flowers to reassure consumers that the user remains feminine." This kind of interpretive sentence turns an example into evidence for your argument.

Similarly, your claim that "females can wear masculine colors and get away with it, but males cannot wear pink" is a striking observation—but it needs support. Why might this be true? You could argue it reflects a hierarchy in which "masculine" traits are aspirational for women, while "feminine" traits are stigmatized for men. This idea deserves development, perhaps with a sentence explaining the social value placed on masculinity versus femininity.

Consider a Counterargument

A strong argumentative essay acknowledges an opposing view. For example, some might argue that color preferences are natural, not constructed. You could briefly mention that historically pink was associated with boys, which helps prove the stereotype is a social convention rather than an innate one.

Language and Mechanics

Several sentence-level issues need attention:

Capitalization after "Why?": "why? because it is..." should be "Why? Because it is..." or better, combined into one sentence.
Run-on sentences and comma splices: eg, "Pink is considered a feminine color. Why? because it is a very soft color, and women are usually pictured..." Combine related ideas: "Pink is considered feminine because it is associated with softness, a trait stereotypically linked to women."
Inconsistent capitalization: "on the Secret deodorant commercial," "of course, the Huggies," "once a baby has developed" — these mid-sentence lowercase letters after periods should be capitalized.
Typo: "(jam referring to babies)" — likely meant "I am referring to babies." Proofread carefully for typos like this.
Repetition: The point about baby girls being dressed in pink and assumptions people make is repeated almost identically for baby boys. Consider combining these into one comparative paragraph instead of two parallel ones.

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write an explicit, arguable thesis statement.
Reorganize paragraphs around ideas, not just chronological examples.
Add analytical sentences explaining why each example matters.
Develop your most original insight (the asymmetry between girls/boys) more fully.
Proofread for capitalization, run-ons, and typos.

With a clearer argument and tighter analysis, this essay has strong potential—your examples are vivid and well-chosen. The main task now is to tell your reader *why* they matter.

Feedback Report: Drug Legalization Essay

Overall Impression
You've chosen an ambitious and genuinely debatable topic, and your position is clear from the start. You also organize your body paragraphs around distinct benefits, which gives the essay a logical skeleton. However, the essay currently reads as a list of assertions rather than a developed argument. Below are the main areas to strengthen in revision.

Missing Evidence and Broken Logic
This is the most urgent issue. Several paragraphs contain gaps (marked with ) where supporting explanation or evidence should be. As a result, causal claims don't connect. For example, you assert that legalization would lower prices, which would make it harder for "pushers to sell," but the missing sentence that would explain *why* lower prices hurt dealers leaves the logic incomplete. Similarly, in the crime paragraph, you jump from "drug prices would decrease" to "when drug prices are high... people will commit a crime," without showing the reasoning in between. Before you revise anything else, go through each paragraph and make sure every causal claim has an explicit mechanism: *A leads to B because C*. Right now the reader has to guess the "because."

Unsupported Statistics
Claims like "one third of people" in prison for drug offenses, or drug use increasing "by a factor of two or three," are presented as fact but have no citation. For an argumentative essay, numbers are your strongest persuasive tool—but only if they're sourced. Find and cite data (eg, from the Bureau of Justice Statistics or a source like your Duke citation) for every statistic you use. Right now, only one in-text citation appears in the entire essay ("Duke 231"), which is far too little support for an essay built almost entirely on empirical claims about crime, health, and economics.

Paragraph Organization
Each paragraph introduces a benefit but doesn't always develop it fully before moving to the next idea. The paragraph on public health, for instance, tries to cover physical health risks, substitution effects between alcohol and drugs, and reduced smoking—all in one paragraph, with a confusing sentence ("Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers") that lost its subject. Consider splitting this into two paragraphs: one on direct health effects of legal, regulated drugs; one on substitution effects between drugs and alcohol/tobacco. Also, revise that broken sentence—I think you mean something like: "Similarly, when people cannot obtain heroin, some turn to alcohol abuse instead."

Repetitive Transitions
Your paragraphs open with nearly identical phrasing: "One of the most important benefits...", "Another benefit...", "The next benefit...", "Another good factor..." This list-like structure makes the essay feel mechanical. Try varying your transitions and, more importantly, showing *relationships* between benefits (eg, "This reduction in crime is closely tied to another consequence of legalization: lower incarceration rates.").

Missing Counterargument
A strong argumentative essay acknowledges opposing views and refutes them. Right now, your essay only presents your side, which risks sounding one-sided or naïve to a skeptical reader. Consider adding a paragraph addressing the strongest counterargument (eg, "Critics argue that legalization would increase overall drug use and normalize addiction") and then responding to it with evidence. This will make your ultimate position more persuasive.

Language and Style Issues
"Beneficiary" should be "beneficial" (paragraph on drug debts).
"Because of their socially and economically margin" is unclear—perhaps you mean "because of their social and economic marginalization"?
The phrase "have come to reality" should likely be "have come to realize" or "have come to see reality."
Avoid comma splices, such as: "They have come to reality and decided that, drug prohibition has not worked..." Remove the comma after "that" and consider whether this should be a direct quotation (if from Duke) with quotation marks and a citation.
Watch subject-verb agreement and article usage throughout (eg, "the fact that the drug users would be responsible" — check whether "the" is needed before "drug users").

Conclusion
Your conclusion restates your points well but doesn't leave the reader with a memorable closing thought. Consider ending with a forward-looking statement or a call to action that goes beyond simply repeating the list of benefits.

Priority for Revision
Fill in all missing evidence/logic gaps ().
Add citations for every statistic and factual claim.
Add at least one counterargument-and-rebuttal paragraph.
Fix grammatical errors, especially the broken sentence in the health paragraph.
Vary paragraph transitions and tighten conclusion.

You have a clear, organized structure and a confident voice—now the essay needs the evidentiary backbone to match that confidence. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on "Gun Control" Essay

Overall Impression

You have chosen a topic you clearly care about, and the personal stories about Mr. Glover and your Uncle Edward give the essay emotional power that many argumentative essays lack. This is a real strength—readers connect with human stories more than statistics. However, the essay needs significant revision in its argumentative structure, use of evidence, and clarity of reasoning to make it more persuasive and academically polished.

Strengths

Personal investment: Your use of local, personal, and family examples makes the issue feel urgent and real rather than abstract.
Clear position: You state your stance early and return to it at the end.
Attempt to use evidence: You clearly tried to support your claims with newspaper quotes and statistics rather than relying only on opinion.

Areas for Improvement

Thesis and Argument Development

Your thesis claims gun control will "reduce crime, save lives, and lessen fears," but the essay doesn't clearly organize evidence *around* these three claims. Instead, it moves through a list of anecdotes. Consider restructuring so each body paragraph explicitly ties back to one of your three claims. For example: "This incident demonstrates how stricter gun control could save lives because..." Right now, the connection between your stories and your thesis is implied rather than argued.

Logical Gaps

Several of your examples don't actually support the claim that *more gun control laws* would have prevented the tragedy. For instance:
The bank robbery example shows a need for better *security*, not necessarily stricter *purchase laws*—the citizens you quote ask for more police and security, not gun control.
The story of your uncle involves an argument that escalated to violence; it's unclear how a waiting period or background check would have stopped that specific acquaintance from acting.

To strengthen your argument, explicitly explain *how* the specific policy you support (eg, background checks, waiting periods) would have changed the outcome in each case. Otherwise, the reader may agree that gun violence is tragic without being convinced that your specific solution is the answer.

Addressing the Counterargument

You mention the NRA's opposition but dismiss it in one sentence ("This organization doesn't realize..."). This is a missed opportunity. A strong argumentative essay engages seriously with the opposing view—for example, the NRA's argument about the Second Amendment or self-defense—and then explains *why* your position is still stronger. Consider adding a paragraph that presents the counterargument fairly before refuting it with evidence.

Use and Integration of Sources

Your quotations are a bit long and are often dropped into the paragraph without enough introduction or follow-up analysis. For each quote, try this structure:
Introduce the source and context.
Quote the evidence.
Explain in your own words what it proves and how it connects to your argument.

Also, your citations need consistent, correct formatting. "The Times and Democrat, pg. 6A" and "Multimedia Resources-CD-Rom" are informal citations. Check with your instructor about which citation style (MLA, APA) is required, and apply it consistently, including in-text citations and a Works Cited/References page.

Statistics Need Clarification

The statistic about "hate crimes" (robbery 2.5%, murder 16.3%) is confusing as presented—readers can't tell what these percentages are *of* or how they connect specifically to *gun* violence rather than crime in general. Clarify the numbers and explicitly state why they matter for your argument.

Conclusion

Your proposal that only police and military should carry guns is a strong, specific policy stance—but it appears only in the conclusion. This is your most important idea and deserves to be introduced earlier, perhaps in the thesis, and developed with reasoning and evidence throughout the essay, not saved for the final paragraph.

Language and Mechanics

There are frequent issues with:
Verb tense/agreement: "needs to implemented" → "needs to be implemented"; "misue," "techers," "memebers" — proofread carefully for typos.
Sentence fragments and run-ons: Some sentences are very long and lose clarity (eg, the opening thesis sentence). Break these into shorter, clearer statements.
Punctuation with quotations: Watch for stray quotation marks and inconsistent spacing.

I recommend reading your essay aloud, which often helps catch grammar and flow issues that are easy to miss when reading silently.

Summary of Next Steps

Reorganize paragraphs so each one clearly supports one part of your thesis (reduce crime / save lives / lessen fear).
For each example, explicitly explain how your proposed policy (not just "gun control" in general) would have changed the outcome.
Add a paragraph that fairly presents and then rebuts the opposing view (NRA/Second Amendment).
Move your specific policy proposal (police/military only) into the introduction and develop it throughout.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and citation format.

You have compelling material here—the revision work now is about *organizing* and *tightening* the connection between your evidence and your argument. Well done on tackling such a serious and personal topic.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Capital Punishment

Thank you for sharing this essay. You've chosen a compelling topic and organized your argument in a clear, logical structure—addressing cost, retribution, and deterrence in turn. Below is feedback to help you strengthen both the content and the language of your essay.

Strengths

Your essay has a solid organizational skeleton: an introduction that previews your three counterarguments, three body paragraphs that each tackle one pro-capital-punishment argument, and a conclusion that summarizes your points. This structure makes your argument easy to follow. You also do a nice job anticipating counterarguments (eg, addressing the idea of eliminating the appeals process) before dismissing them—this shows critical thinking.

Areas for Improvement

Tone and Word Choice
Your tone is sometimes too informal or emotionally charged for academic writing. Phrases like "obscene form of punishment," "pull their plug," "throw a couple of statistics at you," and "Correct me if I'm wrong but..." read as conversational rather than analytical. Academic argumentation is most persuasive when it appears measured and evidence-driven, even when you feel strongly. Consider revising these phrases to sound more formal: for example, "obscene form of punishment" could become "a deeply flawed form of punishment."

Direct Address to the Reader
You frequently address the reader directly ("I will show you," "if the cost of punishment is at all a concern for you"). Academic essays typically avoid this second-person address. Try rephrasing in the third person: "The following analysis will demonstrate..." or "These costs should concern anyone invested in fiscal responsibility."

Evidence and Citations
This is your most significant area for improvement. You mention gathering statistics "doing a paper last year," and reference "studies in states" without naming them, citing sources, or providing specific numbers or dates. For an argumentative essay, especially one relying heavily on statistical claims (the "five times the cost" figure, the "eighteen years on death row" figure), you need actual citations. Without them, skeptical readers will doubt your claims, no matter how reasonable they sound. Look up your original sources and include proper in-text citations (eg, APA or MLA, depending on your course requirements).

Logical Development in the Retribution Paragraph
This paragraph is your weakest in terms of clarity. You assert that retribution amounts to "revenge" rather than "punishment," but you don't clearly define the distinction or explain why revenge is illegitimate while punishment is legitimate. Consider adding a sentence that defines both terms and explains the moral/legal difference. Additionally, the "hypocrisy" argument (society condemning killing while killing) is a good point, but it would benefit from acknowledging the counterargument that many people distinguish between "killing" and "lawful execution for a crime"—address why you find this distinction unpersuasive.

Language Errors
As a non-native English speaker, you have understandable errors that a native-speaking reader will notice and that undermine your credibility as a careful writer. I recommend running your essay through a grammar checker (like Grammarly) and doing a careful read-aloud pass. Some specific patterns to watch for:

Homophones/spelling: "untill" → "until"; "it's" → "its" (possessive, not contraction)—this appears multiple times; "Ounce" → "Once"; "inprison" → "imprison"; "recieves" → "receives"; "midevil" → "medieval"; "deference" → "deterrence"; "murdeer" → "murder"; "prol's and con's" → "pros and cons"; "statels" → "states"; "detramental" → "detrimental"; "el,,,actly" appears to be a typo for "exactly."
Subject-verb agreement: "reason's this has no effects" → "reasons this has no effect."
Word confusion: "defense" vs. "deterrence"—make sure you're using the term consistently; you switch between "deterent," "deference," and "deterrence."

I strongly recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or having someone else read it aloud to you, to catch these errors before submission.

Conclusion
Your conclusion effectively summarizes your three arguments, but it could be stronger if you added a final forward-looking statement—what should replace capital punishment, or what policy change do you advocate? Ending with a concrete call to action would leave a stronger final impression.

Priority Revisions

If you only have time for a few changes, prioritize:
Finding and citing real sources for your statistics
Revising informal/direct-address language into third-person academic tone
A careful proofreading pass for spelling and grammar

You have a genuinely strong argumentative structure here—with attention to sourcing and polish, this essay could be quite persuasive. Keep up the good critical thinking!

Feedback on Your Euthanasia Essay

Overall Impression

You've tackled a genuinely difficult and important topic, and your research into the Kevorkian case shows real effort. However, the essay currently reads more like an informative report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need a clearer thesis, more analysis (rather than summary), and stronger connections between your evidence and your claims.

Argument and Structure

Unclear thesis: Your introduction ends with a question ("Should it be legalized...?") rather than a thesis statement. An argumentative essay needs to take a clear position early on. Readers finish your introduction unsure of what you will argue.

Contradictory framing: Your opening paragraph describes euthanasia almost dismissively—as people looking for "an easy way out"—yet your final paragraphs seem sympathetic to patient autonomy. This creates confusion about your actual stance. Decide early what you believe, and make sure your language throughout reflects that position consistently.

Imbalanced structure: The middle of your essay (types of euthanasia, May's four forces, the Kevorkian narrative) is heavily descriptive. This is useful background, but it takes up the majority of your word count while your actual argument—found in the final two paragraphs—is compressed into just a few sentences. Consider restructuring so that background information is condensed and your analysis is expanded. As a rough guide, aim for no more than 1/3 of the essay as background/summary, with the rest devoted to argument.

Weak conclusion: The essay ends abruptly after introducing Fletcher's critique of the Hippocratic Oath. There is no concluding paragraph that ties your argument together, restates your position, or reflects on the implications. Right now, the essay just stops rather than concluding.

Development of Ideas

Several key moments in your essay need more analysis:

When you introduce May's four forces, you list them but don't explain *why* they matter to your argument or which ones you find most/least convincing.
The Kevorkian narrative is detailed and well-researched, but you don't tell the reader what conclusion to draw from it. Is Kevorkian a hero, a criminal, a symbol of legal ambiguity? Your own interpretation is missing.
Fletcher's critique of the Hippocratic Oath is introduced but never explained. What were the "two things" the oath promised, and why are they contradictory? Right now the paragraph ends before making its point—this is where your argument should have real teeth.
Your final paragraph makes a strong claim (doctors don't have moral authority to decide a patient's fate) but doesn't support it with reasoning or evidence. Why don't they have this authority? What ethical principle supports your claim? This is the heart of your argument, and it needs to be developed, not just asserted.

Citations

You have several bracketed placeholders () where quotations should be. These must be filled in with the actual quoted material and cited properly (author, page/paragraph number, and full source in a Works Cited page). Right now, a reader cannot verify or evaluate your evidence because it's missing. This is the most urgent fix needed before resubmission.

Language and Style Notes

Word choice: "leave in their will expressed wishes" should be revised to something like "state in their will that they wish..." for natural phrasing.
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense when discussing general practices vs. the Kevorkian case. Keep general/timeless statements in present tense ("Physicians are bound by...") and historical events in past tense ("Kevorkian developed...").
Apostrophe errors: "patient's" is used as a plural in "Those patient's who desire" — it should be "patients" (no apostrophe) since it's plural, not possessive.
Sentence fragments/run-ons: Some sentences are lengthy and could be split for clarity, eg, the sentence beginning "If medical conduct to end the patient's life is prohibited..." is difficult to parse. Consider breaking it into two sentences.
Vague pronouns: In "it is one's option as a physician to cooperate," clarify who "one" refers to for smoother, more direct prose.

Suggested Revision Priorities

Write a clear, one-sentence thesis stating your position and place it at the end of your introduction.
Fill in all missing quotations and add proper citations.
Condense the background/historical sections (types of euthanasia, Kevorkian case) to make room for more analysis.
Expand your discussion of Fletcher's argument and your own concluding argument—this is where your voice should be strongest.
Add a concluding paragraph that restates your thesis and explains its broader significance.
Proofread carefully for tense consistency and apostrophe usage.

With a clearer thesis and more developed analysis in the second half, this essay has strong potential. Good luck with your revisions!

Feedback on "The Wild Card" Essay

Overall Impression

You've written a detailed and informative essay that clearly explains a complex topic—the introduction of the wild card in baseball. Your command of the subject matter is impressive, and you support your claims with specific statistics and examples. However, the essay currently reads more like an explanatory report than a persuasive argumentative essay. Below are suggestions to strengthen both the structure and the argumentation.

Structure and Balance

The essay spends roughly the first third explaining *how* the wild card system works (the mechanics of divisions, home field advantage rules, etc) before turning to *why* it's good for baseball. For an argumentative essay, this balance should be reversed. Consider:

Trimming the explanatory sections significantly. Readers don't need the full technical breakdown of home-field-advantage rules to understand your argument. A brief paragraph summarizing "how the wild card works" would suffice, allowing you to spend more space developing your three main arguments (more teams, more races, more competition).
Moving the block quote and bulleted playoff scenarios into your own words, or condensing them substantially. Long quoted material can overwhelm your own voice and analysis. When you do quote, follow with a sentence or two explaining *why* this quote matters to your argument.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your thesis—"The wild card is good for baseball because it adds more teams to the playoffs, creates another race similar to a pennant race, and provides more competition"—is clear and appears early, which is excellent. However, the essay would benefit from:

Explicitly returning to this thesis in each topic sentence. For example, when you discuss star players, remind the reader early in the paragraph that this exemplifies your first point (more teams = more stars = more fan interest).
Addressing counterarguments. A stronger argumentative essay acknowledges opposing views. For instance, some traditionalists argue that the wild card dilutes the meaning of winning a division, or that it's "unfair" for a second-place team to make the playoffs. Briefly addressing and refuting such objections would make your argument more persuasive and show critical engagement with the topic, rather than a one-sided presentation.

Evidence and Support

Your use of statistics (runs scored, playoff scenarios, comparing the Rockies and Reds) is a strength—it grounds your argument in concrete evidence. A few suggestions:

Analyze the evidence more, rather than just listing it. For example, after listing all the star players who wouldn't have made the playoffs, explain *how* their presence benefited baseball beyond "people like stars in advertising." Did ticket sales increase? Did TV ratings improve? Even brief speculation tied to your argument would deepen the analysis.
The competition paragraph is strong, especially the comparison between the Rockies/Reds and the Yankees/Mariners series. This is a good model for how to use data as evidence rather than just narration.

Language and Style

As a non-native English speaker, your writing is generally clear, but there are several areas for polishing:

Word choice errors: "leagued" should be "League," "car" should be "card," "second-place record" should probably be "best non-division-winning record" for clarity.
Wordiness/redundancy: Phrases like "This year for the first time" and repeated "old system vs. new system" comparisons could be tightened. Aim for more concise sentences.
Repetitive sentence openers: The concluding paragraph repeats "Without the wild card..." five times. While this creates rhetorical rhythm, it risks feeling mechanical. Consider varying at least one or two of these for stylistic variety.
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past tense (describing 1995 events) and present tense (describing rules). Make sure this is intentional and consistent—present tense for ongoing rules ("the wild card team plays...") and past tense for specific 1995 events ("the Yankees lost...").
Comma splices and run-ons: Some sentences, like "The wild card is good for baseball because it adds more teams to the playoffs, creates another race similar to a pennant race, and provides more competition" are fine, but elsewhere you have long sentences that could be split for clarity (eg, the sentence beginning "The realignment produces three Division winners...").

Conclusion

Your conclusion effectively echoes your three main points, which is good practice. However, the final sentence ("losing popularity to football and baseball") contains an error—you likely meant "football and *basketball*." Also, consider ending with a slightly more forward-looking or persuasive final thought, rather than a repeated list of "without the wild card" statements.

Priority Revisions

Cut the technical explanation of home-field-advantage rules by half.
Add at least one paragraph addressing a counterargument.
Deepen analysis of evidence rather than simply presenting it.
Proofread for word confusion (car/card, leagued/League) and verb tense consistency.
Vary sentence structure in the conclusion.

With these revisions, your essay will shift from an informative summary to a more persuasive, analytically rich argument. You have excellent command of the facts—now let your own critical voice guide the reader through them.

Feedback Report: Media's "Right to Know" Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a timely and important issue—the tension between media freedom and personal privacy—with genuine passion and conviction. Your personal anecdote about refusing the newspaper subscription creates an engaging entry point, and you support your argument with a range of examples (Grenada, Desert Storm, the Surgeon General nomination, O.J. Simpson, Colin Powell). This shows you've been thinking critically about this issue over time. However, the essay needs significant revision in three key areas: thesis clarity and organization, argument development, and language mechanics.

Thesis and Focus

Your essay currently reads as a collection of grievances against the media rather than a unified argument. Ask yourself: what is the ONE central claim you want readers to accept?

Possible thesis directions:
The media's pursuit of profit has corrupted its constitutional "right to know," causing real harm to national security, personal privacy, and public discourse.
The media has redefined "newsworthy" in ways that prioritize sensationalism over genuine public interest.

Action step: Write a clear thesis statement in your introduction that previews your main line of argument. Currently, the essay drifts from military security concerns to celebrity gossip to personal privacy without a clear logical thread connecting them.

Organization

Your examples are strong, but they need better grouping and transitions. Consider organizing by *type of harm*:

National security harm (Grenada, Desert Storm, Bosnia)
Reputational harm to individuals (Surgeon General nominee, USC professor, O.J. trial witnesses)
Chilling effect on public service (Colin Powell example)
Erosion of personal privacy generally (hamburgers, sleep schedules, Reagan/Ford)

Right now, these move in sequence without signposting. Add transitional sentences like "Beyond endangering troops, media overreach also destroys civilian reputations" to guide readers between sections.

Argument Development

Some claims need more support or nuance:

The Surgeon General example (this appears to be Henry Foster, not a replacement for Elders—please verify this fact) states he "would have made an excellent Surgeon General," but you don't explain *why* beyond assertion. What were his qualifications?
The O.J. Simpson section raises an interesting question ("How did they know what jurors were doing...") but doesn't develop it—this could be a powerful point about media surveillance tactics if expanded.
Consider addressing the counterargument: media defenders would say the public *does* have a right to know about candidates' character or public figures' conduct. Engaging with this opposing view would strengthen your credibility.

Tone and Rhetorical Choices

Your indignant tone is effective in places ("Was this a rejection or a crucifixion?") but risks feeling repetitive when overused. The rhetorical questions are numerous—select your strongest ones and cut others for greater impact. The closing paragraphs (telephone tapping, cameras in homes) veer into slippery-slope territory that may weaken your credibility with skeptical readers. Consider grounding this speculation in a specific historical trend rather than presenting it as inevitable.

Language and Mechanics

This essay contains numerous errors that need correction in your revision:

Typos/spelling: "successfiil" → successful; "fives" → lives; "gwne" → game; "medi&s" → media's; "dodt" → don't; "immune" typo; "compunction"
Punctuation: Comma splices appear frequently (eg, "I refused to take a subscription to their paper. Several years ago..."—check comma usage throughout)
Verb tense consistency: Check past/present tense shifts, especially in the Grenada/Desert Storm paragraph
Quotation marks: Be consistent—you use both straight and curly quotes; standardize this
Word choice: "in lieu of money" should likely be "in exchange for money" or "for the sake of money" (in lieu of means "instead of," which reverses your intended meaning)

Action step: After revising content, do a dedicated proofreading pass focused only on mechanics. Reading your essay aloud can help catch these errors.

Specific Strengths to Preserve

Your personal connection to the Grenada example (as a parent) adds authentic emotional weight—keep this human element
The rhetorical question "Was this a rejection or a crucifixion?" is memorable and effective
Your closing line about "good news makes no money" is a strong, quotable conclusion—consider moving a version of this idea earlier as well, perhaps foreshadowed in your introduction

Next Steps

Draft a one-sentence thesis statement
Reorganize body paragraphs by theme (see suggestion above)
Add 1-2 sentences addressing a counterargument
Do a full proofreading pass for spelling/punctuation
Trim redundant rhetorical questions to keep your strongest ones

You have compelling material here—the revision work is about *sharpening* your argument's shape, not generating new content. Good luck!

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0013.2.txt =====
Feedback on Your Marijuana Legalization Essay

Thank you for sharing this essay. You clearly have strong personal convictions about this topic, and your willingness to draw on lived experience shows genuine engagement with the subject. However, to meet the expectations of academic argumentative writing, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: thesis clarity, evidence, organization, and tone. Below are detailed suggestions.

Thesis and Argument Structure

Your central claim—that marijuana should be legalized—doesn't emerge clearly until the very end of your introduction, and even then it's stated simply ("I think it should be legalized") without a preview of *why*. A strong argumentative essay needs a clear, specific thesis that forecasts your main reasons (eg, "Marijuana should be legalized because it has medical benefits, poses fewer risks than other regulated substances, and its criminalization disproportionately harms low-level offenders while ignoring larger systemic issues").

Currently, the essay reads more like a personal narrative or reflection than an argument. You move through memories (childhood, parents, "Black Mosses," friends) without clearly connecting each story back to a supporting reason for legalization. Revise by organizing the essay around 3–4 distinct claims, each with its own paragraph, evidence, and analysis.

Evidence and Credibility

This is the area needing the most attention. Right now, your evidence consists almost entirely of anecdotes: what a Jamaican acquaintance told you, what your friends do, what dealers you know drive. While personal experience can enrich an essay, it cannot *carry* an argument in academic writing—especially claims about drug policy, medical benefits, or addiction rates.

Several claims need actual sourcing:
"It is highly non-habit forming" — this requires a citation to medical/scientific research.
"Marijuana can slow down certain eye ailments" — cite a study or medical source (eg, research on glaucoma).
Claims about government officials "getting a cut" from drug busts are serious accusations that need evidence, not speculation ("I'm not trying to say... but think about it").

I'd recommend researching: peer-reviewed studies on marijuana's health effects, DEA/FBI arrest statistics (to support your point about who gets arrested), and comparative drug policy in countries like Portugal or the Netherlands. This will replace speculation with credible support.

Logical Reasoning

A few arguments rely on weak logic that a critical reader will challenge:
Ad hominem/whataboutism: "Politicians who set these laws up use drugs themselves" doesn't actually address whether marijuana *should* be legal—it's an attack on the people, not the policy.
Appeal to celebrity: Listing rappers, rock stars, and public figures who've used marijuana doesn't prove it's safe or beneficial. This is a common logical fallacy (appeal to popularity/authority).
Anecdotal generalization: "My friends light up before a study session and ace their tests" is a single, unverifiable data point, not evidence of cognitive benefit.

Consider revising these into stronger points: instead of citing that politicians use drugs, you might argue that drug laws are inconsistently enforced, backed by arrest data. Instead of naming celebrities, discuss medical marijuana legislation already in effect in various U.S. states.

Tone and Voice

The essay's conversational tone—rhetorical questions ("Did you know...?"), direct address to the reader ("Do you ever notice..."), and casual phrasing ("mega bucks," "talking out of his head")—suits a blog post or spoken speech more than an academic essay. For a more persuasive, credible tone:
Replace rhetorical questions with declarative statements backed by evidence.
Avoid slang ("mega bucks," "light up," "mellows them out").
Refer to people more formally where possible (eg, avoid nicknames like "Black Mosses" unless directly relevant, and even then, contextualize respectfully).

Organization

Right now the essay follows chronological order (your life story) rather than logical order (your argument's structure). Consider organizing by *reasons* instead: an introduction with your thesis, then body paragraphs on (1) medical benefits, (2) inconsistency/unfairness in enforcement, (3) comparison to other legal substances (alcohol, tobacco), and a conclusion that restates your position and calls for action.

Mechanics

There are recurring grammar issues to proofread for: "their" vs. "there" (used interchangeably), run-on sentences, comma splices, and typos ("cession" for "session," "Moses" misspelled). I recommend reading the essay aloud or using a grammar-checking tool, then having a peer or tutor review it.

Final Note

Your passion and voice are assets—don't lose them in revision. But channel them through stronger structure, credible evidence, and more formal language, and this essay will be significantly more persuasive to an academic audience.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles an interesting and legally complex topic—the protection of journalistic sources through shield laws. You've clearly done substantial research, gathering historical background, legal cases, and statistics. However, the essay currently reads more like a research report than an argumentative essay. To strengthen it, you need to sharpen your thesis, improve organization, and integrate your evidence more purposefully in service of a clear argument. Below are specific areas for revision.

Thesis and Argumentative Focus

Your introduction explains *what* shield laws are and *why* trust matters, but it never states a clear, arguable claim. What exactly are you arguing? Possible theses could be:
"Shield laws should be strengthened and made uniform at the federal level because inconsistent state protections undermine press freedom and the public's right to information."
"Courts should not be permitted to override shield laws except in extremely narrow circumstances, because doing so damages the trust essential to journalism."

State this thesis explicitly at the end of your introduction, and make sure every subsequent paragraph works to support it rather than simply presenting information.

Structure: Separate Background from Argument

Right now, the essay alternates between historical/legal background (Maryland's 1896 law, South Carolina's law, various court cases) and argumentative claims (the trust bond, the importance of protecting sources). This makes the essay feel like two different documents stitched together. Consider this structure:

Introduction – Hook, context, clear thesis.
Background – Brief history of shield laws (Maryland origin, current patchwork of state laws). Keep this tight; you don't need the full text of the 1896 law reproduced—summarize its key provisions instead.
The Problem – Use 2-3 of your strongest case studies (Decker/Susan Smith, Procter & Gamble/Swasy, Kamins/Serrano) to *demonstrate* how courts undermine shield laws or bypass them, connecting each case explicitly back to your thesis.
Counterargument – Acknowledge the opposing view: courts and prosecutors might argue that source anonymity can obstruct justice or protect wrongdoers. Address this directly rather than ignoring it.
Your Argument/Solution – Explain why the trust bond and public's right to know outweigh these concerns, and what should change (federal shield law? stronger state protections?).
Conclusion – Restate thesis with a call to action.

The Watergate and Charles Stuart cases, as currently presented, don't clearly support an argument—they read as unconnected anecdotes. Either tie them explicitly to your claim (eg, "Woodward and Bernstein's refusal to reveal Deep Throat demonstrates that source protection can serve the public interest by exposing government corruption") or cut them for space.

Integrating Evidence

You've included excellent material, but much of it is simply quoted at length without analysis. For each piece of evidence, follow this pattern:
Introduce the source/case and its relevance.
Present the evidence (paraphrase where possible; reserve direct quotes for especially powerful language).
Explain what it proves in relation to your thesis.

For example, after the long quotation about the Maryland law's text, add a sentence explaining *why* this history matters to your argument—perhaps noting how narrow and specific the original law was compared to today's broader debates.

Citation and Mechanical Issues

There are several typographical problems with quotation marks (curly quotes rendering as apostrophes or commas: `,internal policy debates'`, `deep Throat"`). Proofread carefully to fix these.
Some sentences have stray commas or punctuation errors (eg, "In a very recent example,, Twila Decker"). A careful read-aloud proofreading pass will catch many of these.
Ensure consistent citation format (Author page) throughout—you're inconsistent about spacing and placement, eg, "(Gordon 9)" vs. "(Gordon\n6)" (likely just a formatting artifact from copying, but check your final draft).
Long block quotations (eg, the full text of the Maryland law) should generally be shortened or paraphrased unless the exact wording is essential to your point.

Analytical Depth

Right now, several claims are asserted without support:
"The trust bond between a reporter and his/her source can be compared to that of a doctor-patient relationship" — this is a compelling analogy, but you don't develop it. Why is it apt? What are the risks if this trust is broken, beyond a vague reference to guilt or ruined careers?
"In 1989, U.S. faced almost 4,500 subpoenas. Since then the numbers have definitely increased." — Where is this from? What does it prove? Numbers without a clear source and without analysis of their significance won't persuade a skeptical reader.

Push yourself to explain the *significance* of each fact, not just report it.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph is more like a series of slogans ("Protect the Shield Laws," "Don't keep them from doing their job") than a reasoned conclusion. Revise this to synthesize your argument: briefly restate your thesis, summarize your strongest points, and end with a specific recommendation (eg, a call for a federal shield law, or stronger enforcement of existing state laws).

Summary of Priorities for Revision

Write an explicit, arguable thesis statement.
Reorganize so that background information leads into a clear argumentative arc.
Add analysis after each piece of evidence explaining its relevance to your thesis.
Address a counterargument.
Proofread carefully for punctuation and quotation mark errors.
Strengthen the conclusion to be a true synthesis, not a list of appeals.

You have strong raw material here—now it needs a clearer argumentative skeleton to hold it together. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback Report: "Should Women Be Allowed to Attend the Citadel?"

Overall Impression

You've chosen a compelling, debate-worthy topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You raise several legitimate arguments—about tradition, individual variation in ability, public funding requirements, and the value of coeducation. However, the essay would benefit significantly from a clearer structure, stronger evidence, and careful proofreading. Below are specific suggestions organized by area.

Thesis and Structure

Your introduction states the question but doesn't clearly announce your position. Readers should know from the first paragraph that you support women's admission to the Citadel. Consider a explicit thesis statement, such as: "Because the Citadel receives public funding and because ability is not determined by gender, women should be permitted to attend."

Each body paragraph currently addresses a counterargument reasonably well, but the essay would flow better with clearer topic sentences that preview each paragraph's main point. Right now, paragraphs sometimes drift (eg, the tradition paragraph moves from slavery to gender equality to physical strength within a few sentences). Try to keep one central idea per paragraph and use transitions ("Furthermore," "In addition," "However") to connect paragraphs logically.

Argumentation and Evidence

This is the area needing the most attention. Your essay relies heavily on assertions ("as a woman I especially know that is not true") rather than evidence. To strengthen your argument:

Cite the actual legal case. You mention "the courts" but never name *Shannon Faulkner* or the *United States v. Virginia* precedent (VMI case), which is directly relevant. Referencing real legal reasoning will make your public-funding argument much more persuasive.
Support claims with data or examples. For instance, when you argue women can "handle the challenges," cite an example of a woman who succeeded in a similarly demanding program.
Avoid overreaching analogies. Comparing the Citadel's tradition to slavery is a serious overstatement that may weaken your credibility with skeptical readers. A more measured comparison (eg, other institutions like VMI or the Ivy League that once excluded women) would be more persuasive and appropriate.
Address the counterargument more rigorously. You raise the "distraction" argument but dismiss it in one sentence. Explain *why* coeducation works elsewhere—perhaps citing statistics on academic performance or workplace integration—to make this section more convincing.

Paragraph on Public Funding

This is one of your stronger arguments—keep developing it. However, the digression into buses, teachers, and taxes if schools separated by ethnicity is a hypothetical that, while illustrating a point, takes up space without strengthening your core claim. Consider condensing this to one or two sentences and instead spend more time explaining the specific legal standard for gender discrimination in publicly funded institutions (eg, "intermediate scrutiny").

Language and Mechanics

There are numerous spelling, word-choice, and grammar issues that distract from your argument. A few examples with corrections:

"appearred" → *appeared*
"That questions is" → *That question is*
"alao" → *also*
"publically" → *publicly*
"desciniinate" → *discriminate*
"hispanic or if you are poliish" → capitalize proper nouns: *Hispanic*, *Polish*
"excellence discipline" → *excellent discipline*
"eqaul" → *equal*
"Every deserves" → *Everyone deserves*
"learening" → *learning*
"less superior" → *inferior* (this is a logical redundancy—something is either "superior/inferior" or "less/more," not both)

I strongly recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these errors before submission. Because you're writing in a second language, it's also worth asking a native-speaking peer or tutor to proofread a later draft—not to change your ideas, but to polish surface-level errors that might distract readers from your argument.

Sentence-Level Clarity

Some sentences run together ideas that need separation. For example: "Yes, it has been tradition to only enroll males, but as the saying goes traditions are meant to be broken." This is fine, but the following sentences (about slavery and housewives) should be their own supporting point with a transition, not blended into the same flow.

Suggested Next Steps

Write a clear, one-sentence thesis and place it at the end of your introduction.
Reorganize each body paragraph around a single claim with a clear topic sentence.
Add at least one concrete example, case, or statistic per paragraph.
Do a dedicated grammar/spelling pass, ideally with help from a writing center or peer reviewer.
Reconsider the slavery comparison—perhaps replace it with a more directly comparable case.

You have real analytical instincts and a genuine stake in this argument—the next revision should focus on channeling that passion into more precise, well-supported prose. Good work so far, and I look forward to seeing your revision!

Feedback Report

Thank you for sharing this essay on an important and relevant topic. You clearly have strong convictions about the equal value of a stay-at-home wife's contributions, and your personal example about your mother provides a compelling contrast. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: thesis clarity, organization, argument development, and grammar/mechanics. Below is detailed feedback organized by category.

Thesis and Focus

Your opening sentence is confusing—it presents a claim ("wife should literally worship the ground he walks upon") that you then immediately reject ("Not in this day and age"). This creates a jarring start. Consider stating your actual thesis clearly and directly: something like, "A wife who chooses to stay home should be considered an equal partner in marriage, deserving of equal ownership and respect, not a subordinate dependent on her husband's income."

Additionally, your essay drifts between several related but distinct arguments:
Equal ownership of property/possessions
The value of domestic labor compared to paid work
Legal protections (having both names on contracts)
Social judgment of stay-at-home wives by outsiders

Each of these could be a strong supporting point, but right now they appear in a loose sequence without clear transitions showing how they connect back to your central claim. Consider using topic sentences at the start of each paragraph that explicitly link to your thesis.

Organization

The essay would benefit from a clearer structural plan. I suggest:
Introduction: State thesis clearly.
Body Paragraph 1: The domestic value argument (use your mother's example here—it's your strongest piece of evidence).
Body Paragraph 2: The ownership/legal argument (the TV show example and the "put both names on the contract" point).
Body Paragraph 3: The social judgment argument (the grocery store gossip example).
Conclusion: Return to thesis, broaden to a final statement.

Right now, the TV show anecdote appears very early, before you've fully established why ownership matters. Consider moving your mother's example earlier, since it powerfully demonstrates the *labor* argument before you pivot to the *ownership* argument.

Development of Ideas

Some of your strongest potential arguments are underdeveloped:

The minimum wage comparison ($4.25/hour for a housekeeper) is a great start toward quantifying domestic labor's value, but you drop it after one sentence. Expand this: calculate roughly what a housekeeper, cook, and childcare provider would cost combined per week, and compare that to what the wife "earns" implicitly by not being paid.

The legal argument about property titles is genuinely insightful and could be a highlight of your essay, but it needs more explanation. Why does a wife lose the house if her name isn't on it? What legal protections exist or don't exist? A brief explanation of estate law or spousal inheritance rights would strengthen this considerably.

The TV show dialogue references seem to have placeholder brackets () instead of actual quotes. Please fill these in with the specific dialogue, as the anecdote loses persuasive power without concrete details.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are frequent errors that affect readability and should be corrected:

Comma splices and run-ons: eg, "The husband then went on to say that he viewed the car as his because he took care of it, She then took the argument..." Should be a period or semicolon, not a comma.
Quotation mark errors: You frequently write commas *inside* single quotes incorrectly, eg, ',their'," and ',stay home wife"." Standard usage: `"their"` with the comma outside or after, depending on context.
Subject-verb agreement and word choice: "loose the house" should be "lose the house"; "fixing on an automobile" should be "fixing an automobile" or "working on an automobile."
Sentence fragments: "Where as if she were able to stay home..." is a fragment; connect it to the previous sentence.
Consistency: You alternate between "stay home wife" and "stay-at-home wife"—choose one and hyphenate consistently when used as an adjective.

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these mechanical issues before your final draft.

Style Suggestions

The idiom "what's good for the goose isn't good for the gander" is a nice touch, but make sure your audience will understand it, and consider whether it fits your point precisely.
Avoid starting too many sentences with "However"—vary your transitional phrases (eg, "Nevertheless," "Still," "Yet").
Your closing comparison to choosing to be an "electrical engineer" is a strong, concrete image—consider ending on this note rather than burying it mid-paragraph.

Summary

Your essay has a genuine, well-intentioned argument and includes valuable personal experience. Focus your revision on: (1) clarifying your thesis upfront, (2) reorganizing paragraphs around distinct sub-arguments, (3) deepening your strongest evidence (the wage comparison and legal ownership points), and (4) proofreading carefully for grammar and punctuation. With these revisions, this essay could be both clearer and more persuasive.
