Feedback Report: Marijuana Legalization Essay

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a genuinely controversial and interesting topic, and you clearly did research to support your points. You've gathered useful facts and quotes. However, there is a significant gap between your stated purpose and what your essay actually does, and this creates the biggest problem for your argument. Below, I outline the main issues to address in revision, organized by importance.

Contradiction Between Purpose and Execution (Most Important Issue)

In your introduction, you explicitly state: *"The purpose of this passage is not to convince you to legalize marijuana... Rather, its purpose is to inform you on all the facts."* This is a strong, clear thesis for an informative/expository essay.

However, your essay does not stay informative. By the final paragraphs, you are clearly arguing *for* legalization: you claim legalization would "elevate much of the crime," bring "billions of dollars in tax revenue," and you close by saying "there can only be one logical answer" — implying legalization is obviously correct.

This is a major structural problem. You need to choose one of two paths:
Option A: Commit to an informative essay. Present both sides of every issue (arguments for AND against legalization) with equal weight, and remove language that signals a "correct" answer (like "only one logical answer").
Option B: Revise your thesis to openly argue for legalization. This would actually let you use your strongest material (the alcohol/cigarette comparison, the tax revenue, the crime argument) more effectively, since you're already leaning this way.

Given the strength of your evidence, Option B is likely the better choice. Right now you're trying to have it both ways, which weakens your credibility with readers.

Unbalanced Treatment of the "Other Side"

If you choose to write a persuasive essay (Option B), you still need to acknowledge counterarguments — for example, concerns about impaired driving, effects on adolescent brain development, or gateway-drug theories. Right now, opposing views are barely mentioned, which makes the essay feel one-sided. A short paragraph addressing the strongest counterargument (and then responding to it) would strengthen your credibility significantly.

If you choose to write a true informative essay (Option A), you MUST include the case against legalization in similar depth to the case for it.

Citation and Source Problems

Several claims need proper sourcing:
"DEA concluded marijuana is one of the safest..." — cite the specific report/year.
"Psychology and You, by McMahon and Romano" — good that you cite this, but you need a full citation (year, publisher) and it should appear in a Works Cited/References list.
"Department of Agriculture admits..." — again, specify the year and source document.
The claim about NO ONE dying from marijuana ever needs a source, and honestly, this is a difficult claim to defend as literally true — consider softening it (eg, "no confirmed overdose deaths have been recorded").

Action step: Add a Works Cited page and in-text citations for every statistic and quotation.

Logical Leaps and Unsupported Claims

Some connections between evidence and conclusions need more development:
You state legalization would reduce crime, but you don't explain the mechanism (eg, comparing to the end of alcohol prohibition, which you mention only briefly at the end — this comparison could be expanded much earlier and used as a central pillar of your argument).
The hemp/paper statistic is interesting but feels disconnected — connect it clearly to an economic or environmental argument.

Organization

Your paragraph structure (history → physical effects → mental effects → comparison to alcohol/cigarettes → crime/economic issues → conclusion) is logical and easy to follow. Keep this structure, but:
Make sure each paragraph has a clear topic sentence connecting back to your (revised) thesis.
The conclusion should explicitly return to your thesis rather than introducing a new comparison (Prohibition) at the very end. Consider moving the Prohibition comparison earlier and using the conclusion to synthesize your argument.

Language and Mechanics

Since English is your second language, here are patterns to watch for in revision:
Spelling/typos: "goverwnent" → government; "Pgychology" → Psychology; "eanount" → amount; "govermnent" → government. Proofread carefully or use spell-check.
Comma splices/run-ons: "The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States" — this sentence is missing a verb (should be "is the history...").
Capitalization: "Marijuana" and "Alcohol" don't need to be capitalized mid-sentence (they aren't proper nouns).
Word choice: "elevate" crime should likely be "alleviate" or "reduce" — this changes the meaning of your sentence significantly.

Summary of Priority Revisions

Decide your purpose (informative vs. persuasive) and revise your thesis and conclusion to match consistently throughout.
Add balance — either present both sides equally, or acknowledge and respond to counterarguments.
Fix citations — add a Works Cited page and proper in-text attribution.
Proofread for spelling and grammar errors.
Strengthen transitions between evidence and your claims/conclusions.

You have good raw material here — the facts are interesting and the alcohol/cigarette comparison is compelling. With a clearer, more consistent argumentative stance, this essay could be quite persuasive.

Feedback on Your Essay: "Double Standards for Women in Society"

Overall Impression

You've chosen an important and engaging topic, and your passion for gender equality comes through clearly, especially in your concluding paragraphs. However, the essay needs significant revision in several areas: thesis clarity, organization, historical accuracy, and language mechanics. Below are specific suggestions organized by category.

Thesis and Argument

Your essay currently reads more like a general history/opinion piece than a focused argument. You should state a clear, specific thesis early on—for example, rather than "society has a double standard for women," try something like: "Despite decades of feminist progress, media and cultural expectations continue to impose contradictory standards on women, demanding they be both intelligent professionals and passive, beautiful objects." This gives your essay a specific claim to prove rather than a broad topic to describe.

Currently, the essay moves through historical background, biblical reference, childhood socialization, media portrayals, and a "Bill of Rights" list without clearly connecting each section back to a central argument. Each paragraph should explicitly link back to your thesis.

Organization

The essay would benefit from clearer structural signposting. Right now it reads chronologically (past → present) but within that structure, ideas jump around. Consider this framework:

Introduction: State thesis clearly.
Historical background (brief—this is currently too long relative to its importance): Why double standards emerged.
Modern manifestations: Media, socialization of children, dating double standards.
Consequences: Why this matters (psychological effects, unfairness).
Conclusion: Return to thesis, suggest what should change.

Right now, the historical section takes up nearly half the essay, but your strongest, most original material is in the discussion of media and children's stories—expand that section instead.

Missing Content

There is a placeholder  where a quotation should be, and the sentence immediately after it ("This quote clearly describes...") refers to content that isn't there. Make sure to insert the actual quotation before submitting.

Development and Evidence

Your best moments are your specific examples: the discussion of Cinderella and Sleeping Beauty, the description of dating double standards (curfews, chaperones), and the analysis of media's "beautiful and thin" ideal. Develop these further with more specific examples—perhaps name specific TV shows, magazines, or additional fairy tales.

The biblical reference (Adam before Eve) is presented as historical fact ("This is why early society placed women under men") but this is actually one interpretation among many, used *by* patriarchal societies to justify hierarchy—it's not a neutral historical cause. Reframe this as: "Some used religious texts, such as the biblical order of Adam and Eve's creation, to justify..." This shows you understand the difference between a belief people used to justify inequality and objective historical fact.

The "Everywoman's Bill of Rights" list feels disconnected from your argument—it's inserted without much analysis. Choose two or three rights most relevant to your argument and discuss them in depth, rather than listing all ten.

Tone and Style

Your ironic tone ("women started getting a little 'uppity'"; "Things were fine and dandy") is a stylistic choice that can work well for satire, but it's used inconsistently. Sometimes you write ironically to mock old attitudes, other times you write earnestly. Decide which tone you want and apply it consistently, or clearly signal transitions between the two.

Also, be careful with rhetorical questions ("How fair was that?" "Why can't she be chased after because she's intelligent?"). A few are effective, but too many make the essay feel more like a spoken rant than a structured argument. Consider replacing some with direct analytical statements.

Grammar and Language Issues

Since English is not your first language, here are the most important patterns to review:

Repeated word error: "warm" appears to be a typo for "women" throughout (eg, "equality of warm," "what society deems a warm," "In our society, warm are expected..."). Please proofread carefully—this may be a spell-check or OCR error, but it appears often enough to seriously distract readers.
Missing subjects: Sentences like "It stems back from the past when were strictly in the home" and "Being that were believed to be the weaker sex" are missing the subject "women." Check for dropped words throughout.
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past tense (describing history) and present tense; make sure each paragraph maintains logical tense agreement.
Comma splices and run-ons: Several sentences join independent clauses with only a comma (eg, "Women were getting restless, they no longer wanted..."). Use a period, semicolon, or coordinating conjunction instead.
Citation format: The Bloom citation (24) lacks a full reference. Include a Works Cited/References entry with author's full name, title, and publication details.

Next Steps

Write a one-sentence thesis and check that every paragraph supports it.
Cut the historical background section by half; expand the media/childhood socialization sections.
Fill in the missing quotation.
Do a careful proofreading pass focused on the "warm/women" error and dropped subjects.
Add a proper citation for Bloom.

You have real insight and passion here—tightening the argument and cleaning up the language will make that insight much more persuasive. Good luck with your revision!

Areas for Revision

Thesis and Structure

Body 1: Historical/legal background on censorship
Body 2-3: Case studies (school boards, libraries) with analysis of *why* they matter to your argument
Conclusion: Synthesize, don't introduce new points

Case Studies Need Analysis, Not Just Summary
Right now, you *report* the Salvail and Cary cases well, but you don't explicitly *connect* them to your argument. After each case, add 1-2 sentences answering: "What does this prove?" For example, after Cary v. Board of Education, explain why it's troubling that a First Amendment violation resulted in "nothing could be done"—this seems like a powerful point, but you state it without exploring its implications.

Citation and Source Integration
You mention "People of the American Way" (I believe this should be "People for the American Way") and statistics, but there are no citations. For an argumentative essay, especially one using specific numbers, you need to cite your sources (author, publication, year) so readers can verify claims and so you avoid plagiarism concerns.

Paragraph on Authors (Twain, Salinger, Steinbeck, Angelou)
This is a strong paragraph conceptually, but it appears late and briefly. Consider expanding this into its own dedicated section—give one specific example (like Angelou) more depth: what was censored, why, and what was lost by censoring it.

The Vonnegut Reference
This appears in your final paragraph but isn't explained. Who is Vonnegut in this context? Why does his quote matter? If you're going to include a quotation, give it context and unpack its meaning for your argument.

Language and Mechanics

You're a non-native speaker, so I'll focus on patterns rather than every error:

Spelling: "fireedom" → freedom; "Gutenburg" → Gutenberg; "curriculwn" → curriculum; "bum" → burn; "baimed" → banned
Verb tense/agreement: "the go as far" → "they go"; "was provided" → check subject-verb agreement throughout
Punctuation: Watch stray commas and quotation marks (eg, "However,'" — check your opening/closing quotation mark pairs)
Word choice: "restrain" should be "restraint"; "openingly" → "openly"; "opening" → "openly"
Capitalization: "high students" seems to be missing a word (high school students?)

Suggestion: After revising content, do a dedicated proofreading pass focused *only* on grammar/spelling—perhaps reading the essay aloud, which often catches errors your eyes skip over.

Priority Revisions (in order)

Write a clear, explicit thesis statement in your introduction.
Add analytical sentences after each case study explaining its significance to your argument.
Properly cite your sources (PAW statistics, court cases).
Expand the Maya Angelou/authors section—this is your emotional core; give it more room.
Proofread for spelling and grammar in a separate pass.

Final Thought

You have real conviction here, and the raw material for a compelling essay exists in this draft. The next step is to make your *reasoning* as visible as your *evidence*—show readers not just *what* happened in these cases, but *why* it matters and how it proves your point. Good luck with your revision!

Feedback on Your Essay: The Confederate Flag Controversy

Overall Impression

You've clearly done substantial research for this essay, gathering an impressive range of sources and perspectives on this controversial topic. This is commendable. However, the essay currently reads more like a collection of quotations than an argumentative essay. To strengthen this piece, you need to focus on developing a clear thesis and using your evidence to *support your own argument* rather than simply presenting multiple viewpoints.

Major Issues to Address

Unclear Thesis and Argument

An argumentative essay requires a clear position that you defend throughout. Currently, your essay presents many competing views (pro-flag, anti-flag, neutral) without indicating which position you're arguing for until the final paragraph. Your conclusion suggests you believe the flag should remain because of heritage, but this argument appears too late and isn't well-developed. Consider stating your thesis clearly in your introduction, then organizing your evidence to build toward that argument.

Over-reliance on Quotations

Long block quotes dominate this essay—sometimes several in a row with little analysis between them. Ask yourself: what is *your* voice contributing here? For each quotation, add 1-2 sentences explaining its significance and how it connects to your argument. Readers need your analysis, not just a sequence of other people's opinions.

Lack of Synthesis and Transitions

The essay moves abruptly between different sources, surveys, and speakers without connecting them. For example, the paragraph with five different survey respondents (Laverne Thomson, Elena Minor, etc) simply lists opinions without any framing or conclusion. What do these varied responses tell us? Help your reader understand the significance of this diversity of opinion.

The Alexander Stephens Quote

This is a powerful piece of historical evidence—Stephens explicitly stated the Confederacy was founded on white supremacy. However, you don't analyze what this means for your argument. If you're arguing the flag represents "heritage" rather than racism, this quote seems to contradict that position. You need to address this tension directly rather than letting the quote sit unexamined.

Logical Gaps in the Conclusion

Your final paragraph makes several claims that need more support:
"There is no reason to fight over something established so many years ago"—but many would argue historical wrongs still matter today
"If we let these people have their way it will only lead to bigger controversy"—this claim needs explanation. Who are "these people"? What controversy?

Consider revising this conclusion to more directly address the counter-arguments you've presented earlier (like Stephens's speech or the NAACP's position).

Sentence-Level and Mechanical Issues

Citation format: Your citations (Riley 30) need a Works Cited page with full source information. Also, ensure consistency in how you cite sources throughout.
Spelling/typos: Watch for errors like "fory" (forty), "siad" (said), "todl" (told), "Africa-Americans" (African-Americans), "Killingly" (verify spelling), and inconsistent capitalization (eg, "united Daughters," "united States").
Punctuation: There are several comma splices and missing punctuation, particularly around quotation marks. Review comma usage before and after quoted speech.
Awkward phrasing: Sentences like "There is always going to be controversy for since we live in this world" need revision for clarity.

Suggestions for Revision

Write a clear thesis statement in your introduction that states your specific position on the Confederate flag controversy.

Reorganize your evidence so that each section builds your argument, rather than simply presenting a chronological list of different viewpoints.

Add analytical sentences after each quotation explaining its relevance to your thesis.

Directly address counter-evidence, especially the Stephens quote, which seems to challenge a "heritage, not hate" argument.

Strengthen your conclusion by tying it back to your thesis and specifically responding to the strongest opposing arguments you've presented.

Create a Works Cited page with complete source information (author, title, publication, date, page numbers).

A Final Note

You've assembled valuable source material that shows real engagement with this complex issue. Your next revision should focus on transforming this from a report of various opinions into an essay where *your* argument drives the discussion, with sources serving as support rather than substitute for your analysis. I'd be happy to discuss your thesis with you before you begin revising.

Feedback Report

Overall Impression

Your essay tackles a timely and engaging topic—the cost of frivolous lawsuits and their impact on the judicial system and taxpayers. You clearly have a strong opinion and provide vivid examples that make your argument memorable (the underwear lawsuit is a great illustrative detail!). However, the essay would benefit from stronger organization, more credible evidence, and careful proofreading. Below are specific areas to focus on as you revise.

Structure and Organization

Your essay currently reads as a series of related but loosely connected points rather than a tightly organized argument. Consider this revision strategy:

Strengthen your thesis statement. Your introduction ends with a general observation ("Are all of these lawsuits necessary?") rather than a clear claim. State explicitly: *what* you want lawmakers to do, and *why* it matters. For example: "Frivolous civil lawsuits are draining taxpayer resources, delaying justice for legitimate cases, and driving up costs for ordinary citizens; therefore, lawmakers must reform the system to curb this abuse."

Group your evidence into clear categories. Right now you move between inmate lawsuits, staged accidents, workers' compensation, and lawsuits against police/schools somewhat abruptly. Consider organizing body paragraphs around themes: (a) frivolous lawsuits from specific groups (inmates), (b) fraudulent lawsuits (staged accidents), (c) the ripple effects on cost and access to justice (wages, insurance, court backlogs), and (d) your proposed solutions.

Use topic sentences. Each paragraph should open with a sentence that signals its main point. This helps readers follow your logic, especially since some paragraphs currently shift topics mid-way (eg, your third paragraph moves from workers' comp to police lawsuits to school discipline without clear transitions).

Development of Ideas

Your causal argument in the fourth paragraph (fewer civil cases → freed-up courts → faster criminal trials → less jail overcrowding) is a nice chain of reasoning, but it needs support. Right now it reads as a logical assumption rather than a demonstrated fact. Consider:

Adding at least one statistic or source to support your claim that "it now takes three to five years for a civil case to be tried." Where does this figure come from?
Providing evidence for the connection between civil case backlogs and criminal case delays—this is a debatable claim that needs justification.

Evidence and Credibility

This is the area most in need of attention. Several claims are stated as fact without support:

The inmate lawsuit examples (ice cream flavor, hairstyle, underwear) are compelling, but without citations, readers may doubt their accuracy or wonder if they are exaggerated for effect. Even a brief attribution ("According to a 1995 report by...") would strengthen credibility.
"I heard of a case last week" is too informal and vague for academic writing. Avoid personal anecdotes as evidence; instead, cite documented cases or media reports.
The claim about insurance companies "encouraging" fraud by paying claims without investigation is a strong accusation—provide a source or soften the language to "some critics argue..."

Revision tip: For your next draft, try to find at least 3–4 credible sources (news articles, legal studies, government reports) to support your key claims. This will transform your essay from an opinion piece into a persuasive, evidence-based argument.

Tone and Word Choice

Your tone is conversational, which suits some argumentative writing, but for academic purposes, consider adjusting:

Phrases like "everybody is suing everybody" and "ridiculous cases" are informal. Replace with more measured language: "an increasing number of individuals are filing lawsuits" and "cases widely regarded as lacking merit."
Avoid rhetorical questions in academic essays unless used sparingly for effect ("Are all of these lawsuits necessary?"). Convert some into direct statements.

Grammar and Proofreading

There are several typos and word-processing errors that need correction before this essay is ready for submission (eg, "wn t~g," "ft~tes," "bmtes," "lie insurance companies," "corporation" should likely be "cooperation," "payoff" should be two words "pay off"). Please:

Proofread carefully, ideally reading the essay aloud.
Watch for subject-verb agreement ("the trend s cems to be" → "seems to be").
Double-check homophone errors (eg, "then" vs. "than" in "cheaper to pay off a claim then to challenge it").

Conclusion

Your proposed solutions in the final paragraph are practical and specific—this is a strong ending. To elevate the essay overall, focus on: (1) sharpening your thesis, (2) organizing evidence into clear thematic sections, (3) supporting claims with credible sources rather than anecdotes, and (4) polishing grammar and tone. With these revisions, your argument will be more persuasive and professional.

Keep up the good work—your passion for this topic is clear, and with revision, this essay has real potential.

Feedback on Your Essay: The 1995 Government Shutdown

Thank you for sharing this essay on a genuinely interesting and historically significant topic. You've clearly done research into the specifics of the 1995 shutdown, and your passion for the subject comes through. Below is feedback organized around the major areas that will help strengthen your next draft.

Thesis and Argument Clarity

Your opening quotation from Jefferson is a strong hook, but your actual argument gets diffused across the essay. You seem to be making two related but distinct claims: (1) that partisanship inherently damages government function, and (2) that the specific 1995 shutdown unfairly harmed ordinary Americans. Consider which of these is your primary thesis and make it explicit in your introduction—ideally in one clear sentence near the end of your first paragraph. Right now, the essay reads more like a narrative report with commentary sprinkled in, rather than an argument being built and supported.

Suggestion: Try a thesis like: "The 1995 government shutdown demonstrates that Jefferson's warning about partisanship was prophetic: when representatives prioritize party victory over cooperation, ordinary citizens—not politicians—bear the cost."

Structure and Organization

The essay would benefit from clearer paragraph-level organization. Currently, historical background (fiscal year mechanics, the 1962 shutdown, veto process) is interspersed with argument and narrative in a way that makes it hard to follow chronology or logic. Consider organizing into distinct sections:
Introduction with thesis
Background: how government funding works (fiscal year, the thirteen bills)
Narrative: what happened in 1995, step by step
Analysis: why it happened (partisanship) and its costs (economic impact, human impact)
Conclusion: return to Jefferson's warning

This would also help you avoid repetition—right now you explain the veto process and mention job losses more than once in different sections.

Evidence and Sourcing

This is important: you must cite your sources. You provide specific statistics (800,000 workers, exact numbers from Fort Jackson, Charleston Naval Base, etc) and quote Jefferson directly, but there are no citations anywhere in the essay. For an argumentative essay using historical and statistical evidence, this is a serious gap that could raise questions about academic integrity, even if unintentional. Please add in-text citations and a works cited/reference list, and double-check the accuracy of your Jefferson quote—paraphrased or unverified quotations attributed to historical figures should be verified carefully, as many circulate online without solid attribution.

Balance and Fairness in Argument

You state "Neither side is right" and compare Clinton, Gingrich, and Dole to "two kids fighting on a playground." This is a vivid image, but be cautious with the tone—it risks sounding dismissive rather than analytical. Since your essay's strength lies in critiquing *partisanship as a system*, rather than specific individuals, you might strengthen your credibility by analyzing *why* each side refused to compromise (differing philosophies on budget balancing, political incentives before elections, etc) rather than simply saying both were being stubborn. This would also better support your Jefferson-based thesis about structural problems with parties themselves.

Grammar and Mechanics

There are several recurring issues worth addressing in revision:
Sentence fragments and run-ons: eg, "This event is the shutdown of the United States government and it will not end until government officials." (incomplete thought). Read your sentences aloud to catch these.
Verb tense consistency: You shift between past and present tense when discussing 1995 events. Since this is a historical event, past tense throughout is more appropriate ("Congress passed," not "Congress pass").
Comma splices: eg, "This pact will last until December 15, 1995. All the government employees were then reinstated" — some of these need connecting words or should remain separate sentences with proper punctuation.
Proofreading for typos: "govermnent," "figthing," "Thurrnond," "'m seven years" (likely "in seven years"), inconsistent spacing after periods. A careful proofreading pass will catch these.
Capitalization inconsistency: "the American people" is sometimes capitalized inconsistently; "Should be equally divided" has an unnecessary capital S.

Word Choice and Register

Some phrases could be more formal/academic: "who's right?" and "So who won this battle for power?" work well as rhetorical questions but might read better integrated into full analytical sentences rather than standing alone as short questions, especially when they cluster together.

Overall Impression

You have real command of the facts and a clear moral stance, which is commendable. Your next revision should focus on (1) sharpening a single clear thesis, (2) reorganizing for logical flow, (3) adding citations, and (4) a careful grammar/proofreading pass. Strengthening these elements will help your strong factual research and evident conviction shine through more persuasively. I'd be happy to discuss any of these points further in office hours.

Feedback on Your Argumentative Essay: Abortion Rights

Overall Impression

You've chosen a genuinely important and difficult topic, and your passion for the subject comes through clearly. You've also organized your argument around several distinct sub-points (trauma from rape, socioeconomic factors, legal precedent, bodily autonomy, and practical consequences of criminalization), which shows you're thinking about the issue from multiple angles. However, the essay needs significant revision in four key areas: structure and thesis clarity, evidence and sourcing, logical development of arguments, and grammar/mechanics. Below I address each.

Thesis and Structure

Your thesis statement—"Abortion is the right of the people and it should remain so"—is clear, but the essay lacks a strong roadmap. A good introduction should briefly preview the main reasons you'll use to support your argument, so readers know what to expect. Right now, each paragraph jumps to a new point (rape trauma, poverty, family size, legal history, government overreach, unsafe abortions, adoption costs) without clear transitions connecting them back to your thesis or to each other. Consider grouping related ideas—for example, combine the poverty and family-size paragraphs, since they make a similar point.

Additionally, your essay has no conclusion. It ends abruptly on the topic of infant mortality. Every argumentative essay needs a concluding paragraph that restates your thesis and synthesizes your strongest points.

Evidence and Sourcing (Most Important Issue)

This is the area that needs the most attention. You present several statistics and factual claims but provide no sources:

"One in four women experience rape..."
"Lower income families tend to have five children on average..."
"The average cost to support a child is one hundred twenty-five thousand dollars for eighteen years."

In academic writing, especially on a controversial topic, unsupported statistics weaken your credibility rather than strengthen it. Readers may assume you invented these numbers or misremembered them. Every statistic needs a citation (eg, a government report, a peer-reviewed study, or a reputable organization). Please locate real sources for these claims, or remove them if you cannot verify them.

Also, you refer to "Rowe versus Way"—I believe you mean the landmark U.S. Supreme Court case *Roe v. Wade* (1973). This is an important factual error to correct, as misnaming a legal case undermines your argument's authority. You should also briefly explain *what* the case decided, not just that it was a "victory."

Logical Development

Several paragraphs assert a claim without fully explaining the reasoning behind it:

Poverty paragraph: You claim children born into poverty "remain in that socioeconomic level their entire life" and that lower-income families have more children—but you don't explain *why* this supports a right to abortion, or address the ethical complexity of suggesting that abortion should reduce the number of poor children. This argument, as written, risks sounding like you're suggesting poor people shouldn't have children, which is not likely your intention. Revise this section carefully to clarify your actual point (eg, that lack of access to abortion disproportionately harms low-income women who have fewer resources to raise additional children).

"No one should decide for another person" paragraph: This is a strong point about bodily autonomy and slippery-slope concerns about rights, but it needs development. Why does restricting abortion logically lead to restricting *other* rights? Give an example or explain the connection.

Unsafe abortion paragraph: This is one of your stronger arguments—historical evidence about pre-*Roe* "back-alley" abortions is well documented and could be cited effectively.

Grammar, Mechanics, and Word Choice

There are several errors that need correction:

"Hapeo povertyg personal choice" — this phrase in your first paragraph appears to be a typo or corrupted text; please rewrite this sentence clearly.
"Would be able to love him or her?" → missing subject: "Would you be able to..."
"force to give birth" → "forced to give birth"
"eightteen" → "eighteen"
"sky racket alsop" → "skyrocket also"
Comma splices and run-ons appear throughout (eg, "Women deserve the right to have an abortion. they won this right..." should be one sentence with proper punctuation, or two complete sentences with capitalization).
"If abortion were to be outlawed again would this end the practice'?" — awkward phrasing and a stray apostrophe; consider: "If abortion were outlawed again, would this end the practice?"

I recommend reading your essay aloud slowly, or using a grammar-checking tool, to catch these smaller errors before submission.

Suggested Next Steps

Write a clear introduction with a thesis and roadmap.
Find credible sources for every statistic.
Correct the *Roe v. Wade* reference and explain the ruling.
Strengthen the logical connections in the poverty paragraph.
Add a conclusion.
Proofread carefully for grammar and typos.

This essay has a solid foundation of ideas—now it needs stronger evidence, tighter logic, and careful editing to make your argument as persuasive as possible.
